# Kuki Shūzō, *The Problem of Contingency* (偶然性の問題)

## Preface

### The book

Kuki Shūzō's *The Problem of Contingency* (偶然性の問題, *Gūzensei no mondai*), published by Iwanami Shoten in December 1935, is probably the most systematic treatment of contingency in the history of philosophy, as John C. Maraldo has observed (Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo 2011). It begins from a simple definition. Contingency is the negation of necessity: the necessary is what is necessarily so, and the contingent is what happens to be so, an existence that does not hold a sufficient Ground within itself and so contains the possibility of not being. Kuki places contingency at the point of contact between being and nothingness, and for that reason he holds that it is a problem for metaphysics, which alone takes nothingness as its question. Probability theory and quantum mechanics, he argues in the Introduction, deal with contingent cases and acknowledge contingency as a principle, but neither can grasp the contingent as contingent.

The book then divides contingency into three modes, following the three relations of judgement. Categorical contingency (Chapter 1) is the contingency of the individual thing in relation to the general concept: the four-leaved clover, the exception to a law, the mark that does not belong to the essence. Hypothetical contingency (Chapter 2), the longest part of the book, concerns ground, cause and purpose, and it is here that Kuki gives his best-known definition of contingency as "the encounter of two independent elements" (獨立なる二元の邂逅). Disjunctive contingency (Chapter 3) concerns the whole of which each actual thing is one possible part, and it leads Kuki through modal logic, probability, time, wonder, art and fate to the "metaphysical absolute" and to what, following Schelling, he calls primal contingency. In the Conclusion the argument turns from the logical to the existential: contingency is the Encounter of I and thou in the present, and the book ends with an imperative drawn from a Pure Land text, "Having met, do not pass by in vain."

The book draws on an unusually wide range of sources. Aristotle, Leibniz, Kant, Hegel, Schelling, Cournot, Boutroux, Windelband, Rickert, Husserl, Heidegger and Bergson stand alongside the *Book of Changes*, the *Liezi*, Buddhist sutras, the *Man'yōshū*, Japanese newspaper reports and Kuki's own etymologies of the Japanese words for chance. It was the expanded form, about five times as long, of a doctoral dissertation, 偶然性 (*Gūzensei*, "Contingency"), which Kyoto Imperial University accepted in November 1932 with Tanabe Hajime among its readers (Maraldo 2008: 40; Nara 2004: 174). A French translation by Omodaka Hisayuki, *Le problème de la contingence*, appeared in 1966, and Massimo Scaligero reviewed it in *East and West* the following year (Scaligero 1967). Excerpts from the 1932 dissertation are translated in Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo (2011).

### Kuki Shūzō

Kuki was born in Tokyo on 15 February 1888, the fourth son of Kuki Ryūichi, a Meiji official in the Ministry of Education and a diplomat who served in Washington, and who was made a baron in 1896 (Nara 2004: 171; Pincus 1996: 27-28). Okakura Kakuzō (Tenshin), the champion of Japanese art, was close to the family and mentored Kuki in childhood. Okakura and Kuki's mother, Hatsu, had a love affair that broke up both households, and Kuki later called Okakura his "spiritual father" (Pincus 1996: 28-29; Nara 2004: 123 n. 2). He studied at the First Higher School, where he turned from law to philosophy, and from 1909 at Tokyo Imperial University under Raphael von Koeber. In 1911 he was baptised a Catholic (Nara 2004: 171).

In 1921 Kuki left for Europe, where he stayed until the end of 1928. He studied in Heidelberg with Heinrich Rickert, reading Kant's first *Critique* with him in private tutorial, and with Eugen Herrigel. From the autumn of 1924 he lived in Paris, attended courses at the University of Paris and visited Henri Bergson, whom he later called the greatest philosopher of the first half of the century (Pincus 1996: 57; Nara 2004: 125 n. 11). In Paris he wrote poetry, including a poem titled "Contingency", and the first draft of *The Structure of Iki*. In 1927 he went to Freiburg to study phenomenology with Edmund Husserl and Oskar Becker, and at Husserl's house he met Martin Heidegger, whose lectures and seminars on Kant, Schelling, Leibniz and Aristotle's *Physics* he attended at Marburg in 1927 and 1928. Back in Paris in the second half of 1928 he met the young Jean-Paul Sartre, then a student at the École Normale, once a week for some two and a half months to discuss French philosophy from Descartes to Bergson; Sartre later recalled that Kuki "was very enthusiastic about Heidegger". The meetings are often described as language lessons, but Sartre denied having been Kuki's tutor, and Kuki's notebook of their conversations, which mentions Breton's *Nadja*, dates them to after his return from Marburg (Light 1987: 3-4, 12-13, 27 n. 5). Heidegger later told a Japanese visitor that Sartre had come to him with a letter of introduction from Kuki, but Simone de Beauvoir told Light that Sartre had no such letter, and Sartre met Heidegger only once, in 1952 (Light 1987: 27 n. 6). In August 1928, at Paul Desjardins's invitation, he lectured on time at the *décades* of Pontigny, sharing the podium with Alexandre Koyré and Raymond Aron; the lectures were published as *Propos sur le temps* (Pincus 1996: 58-59).

On his return to Japan in 1929 Kuki was appointed to Kyoto Imperial University, after Nishida Kitarō, who had read the Pontigny lectures, recommended him to Tanabe (Pincus 1996: 59; Nara 2004: 173). He taught French philosophy, Husserl, Heidegger, Bergson and contingency, including a class on Boutroux's *De la contingence des lois de la nature* in 1930, and became professor in 1935 (Nara 2004: 173-75). *The Structure of Iki* (いきの構造) appeared in 1930, a study of Heidegger in 1933, *The Problem of Contingency* in 1935 and *Man and Existence* (人間と實存) in 1939. In 1939 he made the tour of Manchuria and China expected of a holder of a Kyoto chair (Pincus 1996: 212). He died of peritonitis on 6 May 1941, at the age of fifty-three, and is buried in Kyoto beside Hōnen-in, under an epitaph written by Nishida (Pincus 1996: 212 n. 5; Maraldo 2008: 37).

### Kuki and Heidegger

Kuki was a year older than Heidegger and had already spent six years studying philosophy in Europe when they met (Parkes in May 1996: 85). Of the Japanese visitors of the 1920s he seems to have made the strongest impression on Heidegger, and his 1933 study was the first book-length account of Heidegger's thought in any language (Parkes in May 1996: 96). Heidegger's "A Dialogue on Language between a Japanese and an Inquirer" (1959) opens with a remembrance of "Count Kuki", his visits and his silence in seminars. Reinhard May has shown, however, that the Dialogue was reconstructed from a 1954 conversation with Tezuka Tomio, who had not known Kuki, and that the gloss on *iki* it gives cannot be squared with Kuki's own account (May 1996: 15-19).

The relation in *The Problem of Contingency* is one of use and departure. Kuki takes up Heidegger's vocabulary, Dasein, care and anticipatory resoluteness, and discusses them directly in Chapter 3. But he refuses Heidegger's grounding of time in the future. For Heidegger contingency appears as the openness of future possibility, which authentic resolve makes necessary; for Kuki the temporal character of contingency is the present, the moment of encounter, and the nothingness that contingency faces is the "deep past" of primal contingency, re-produced in the present encounter with another (Mayeda 2001: 38-41). The emotion that answers to contingency is accordingly wonder, set against the anxiety Heidegger assigns to possibility (Chapter 3, §10). Hiroshi Nara argues that by the end of his years in Europe Kuki's thought had moved closer to Bergson's than to Heidegger's (Nara 2004: 97, 102).

### Kuki and the Kyoto School

Kuki taught alongside Nishida and Tanabe, and he is often counted among the philosophers of the Kyoto School, but most scholars place him at its edge: Maraldo calls him "something of a black sheep" on its fringes (Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo 2011; Maraldo 2008: 40, 53). His interest in French philosophy set him apart from a faculty oriented to German idealism, and he did not write for the department's journal (Pincus 1996: 4 n. 5, 22). The philosophical difference lies in nothingness. For Nishida and Tanabe nothingness is absolute, the ground in which opposites are determined; for Kuki it is relative, the non-existence that every contingent existence holds within it, the possibility of the negation of being (Maraldo 2008: 40; Heisig, Kasulis and Maraldo 2011). Where Nishida and Tanabe look to a self-determining absolute, Kuki asks that contingency be accepted and valued, and where Watsuji Tetsurō stresses communal life, Kuki insists on individuality and on the duality of the encounter. Tanabe, as a reader of the dissertation, criticised it for giving too little place to teleology (Maraldo 2008: 40).

### Politics

Kuki's place in the politics of the 1930s is contested. Leslie Pincus reads *The Structure of Iki* as laying "the groundwork for a Japanese version of national aestheticism", and points to essays of 1937 on the Japanese character, and to a newspaper article urging that the war in China teach "the spirit of Japanese philosophy", as moving towards the official ideology of the time (Pincus 1996: 210, 220-31). Harry Harootunian finds in the theory of contingency "a powerful critique" of identity and necessity that "risked sliding into an argument for necessary exceptionalism", although Kuki "never went so far", and notes that his writings reduce imperial aggression to "incidents" (Harootunian 2000: 249). Hiroshi Nara, Graham Parkes and Jon Mikkelsen dispute the case against him, and Nara concludes that "an incontrovertible basis to inculpate Kuki is lacking" (Nara 2004: 6). Readings of Kuki as a theorist of Japanese uniqueness also need to weigh what he met in Europe. In Paris he wrote "Yellow Face" (黄色い顔, 1926), a satire of European race theory (Marra 2008: 63-65; Marra 2004: 55-57), and Yuasa Yasuo traces *The Problem of Contingency* itself to Kuki's experience of being singled out by race in European society (Yuasa in Parkes 1987: 158). A judgement of his writing on the supposed uniqueness of the Japanese character that leaves out the attitudes of Europeans towards the Japanese in those years is therefore one-sided. Omodaka's introduction to the French translation read Kuki's ethics through bushidō and Nietzsche; Mayeda rejects that reading and finds in the book an ethics of responsibility for chance encounters (Mayeda 2008: 8-9). The record also includes his help in finding Karl Löwith a post in Japan after Löwith had to leave Germany (Pincus 1996: 212). *The Problem of Contingency* itself argues for the individual against the general concept and ends with the ethics of an encounter between I and thou; the essays on the Japanese character followed it within two years.

Pincus calls Kuki's wartime record "ambiguous", and his death on 6 May 1941 ended it before it was tested further (Pincus 1996: 212). He died seven months before Pearl Harbor, and so before the period in which Japanese intellectuals were most pressed to take a public position. Between November 1941 and November 1942 four younger philosophers of the Kyoto School, Nishitani Keiji, Kōsaka Masaaki, Kōyama Iwao and Suzuki Shigetaka, held the three roundtable discussions published in *Chūōkōron* and collected in 1943 as *Sekaishiteki tachiba to Nihon* (The World-Historical Standpoint and Japan), the last of them on "The Philosophy of Total War". In July 1942 Nishitani and Suzuki also took part in the symposium on "Overcoming Modernity" (Calichman 2008: ix-xii). Kuki took part in neither, and how he would have answered that pressure cannot be known. The case against him rests on what he wrote in 1936 and 1937.

### This translation

This is a preliminary translation, made from the fourth printing of 1948. The Translator's Note that follows explains how it was made and the conventions used, and Appendix A records the translation choices for other translators. Page numbers in the margins of the text are those of the 1948 printing; Appendix C relates them to the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū*.

---

## Translator's Note

This is a preliminary translation of Kuki Shūzō's 偶然性の問題 (*The Problem of Contingency*), made from the National Diet Library's digitised images of the fourth printing (Iwanami Shoten, 1948; first published 1935), and much work remains to be done on it. It has not yet been checked against the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū* or against Omodaka Hisayuki's French translation, *Le problème de la contingence* (University of Tokyo Press, 1966). The attributions and dates given in the footnotes still need verifying, the readable version needs a full revision for consistency across its chapters, and the passages Kuki quotes from other authors have been translated from his Japanese and not from the original texts.

This is what I call a vector translation.[^tn-1]

Two conventions are used in the book to identify my changes to the text. In the readable version every footnote is the translator's, since Kuki gives his own references in the body of the text, and each begins "Tr.". To mark anything I have inserted into Kuki's text itself, whether a supplied word, a gloss or a note, I considered Kuki's own sign for contingency, )(, which he introduces as the sign of "the Encounter of two independent elements" (p. 73) and which pictures two pieces meeting at a seam, much as a kintsugi repair does. Instead I chose tortoise-shell brackets, 〔 〕, as Japanese scholarly editions use them for insertions made by an editor or by the person quoting, so that the convention comes from the editorial tradition in which Kuki's own works are published, and because Kuki never uses them himself.

One of the issues in translation is where multiple source terms share a single English word, to deal with this in this translation capitalisation is used to distinguish them. Where his German sources have *Grund*, Kuki writes both 理由 (*riyū*) and 根據 (*konkyo*). For the encounter of independent series of events (Cournot's *séries indépendantes*) he writes both 遭遇 (*sōgū*) and 邂逅 (*kaikō*), and for modality both 樣相 (*yōsō*) and 樣相性 (*yōsōsei*). 理由 is "ground", 遭遇 "encounter" and 樣相 "modality"; 根據 is "Ground", 邂逅 "Encounter" and 樣相性 "Modality". The capital marks the term Kuki uses for his own metaphysical determination. 根據 is the ground that existence has, or lacks, within itself (p. 1), and the word in which he gives Hegel's account of the contingent (p. 324); 理由 is the ground in the relation of ground and consequent on which Chapter 2 is built. At pp. 146 and 148 Kuki writes 遭遇または邂逅, *sōgū* or *kaikō*, and then defines contingency as 獨立なる二元の邂逅, "the Encounter of two independent elements"; 遭遇 is also his word for Cournot's *rencontre* (pp. 10, 179). 樣相性 is modality as a category, and 樣相 a modality or the modalities. Where 根據 or 邂逅 is a verb the English is in lower case, with the Japanese given. Headings are set in sentence case, and no sentence opens with the lower-case term of a pair, so that a capital is never ambiguous.

The whole text has since been read again against the page images, both for mistranslations, which have been corrected, and for other English words that stood for more than one of Kuki's terms. Several of these have been separated. 偶然性 is "contingency" and 偶然, where it stands alone, "the contingent" or "chance"; 現實性 is "actuality" and 現實 "the actual"; 契機 is "moment" and 瞬間 and 刹那 "instant"; 規定 is "determination" and 限定 "delimitation"; and 基礎づける and 基く are "to found" and "to rest on", leaving the verb "to ground" for 理由. 必然 and 可能 are likewise "the necessary" and "the possible", against "necessity" and "possibility". Some overlap remains: "determine" renders both 規定 and 決定 in its causal sense. Appendix A gives the details.

In making it I have relied on the work of others, and I am grateful to them. My greatest debt is to Takeshi Morisato, whose partial translation of the book, circulated as a work in progress, gave me a first English text against which to test my own reading of the Introduction. I have followed his decision to keep Kuki's two pairs of terms apart, rendering 存在 and 非存在 as existence and non-existence and 有 and 無 as being and nothingness, and his marking of the pages of the *Zenshū* fixed the first points of the page concordance. Graham Mayeda's doctoral thesis, *Ethics and Difference: Time, Space and Ethics in the Philosophy of Watsuji Tetsurō, Kuki Shūzō and Martin Heidegger* (University of Toronto, 2001), reproduces Kuki's Japanese alongside references to both Omodaka's French translation and the *Zenshū*, and these made it possible to tie the 1948 pagination to volume 2 of the *Zenshū*. Mayeda's chapter "Is There a Method to Chance?" and John C. Maraldo's "The Contingencies of Kuki Shūzō", both in Victor Sōgen Hori and Melissa Anne-Marie Curley (eds), *Frontiers of Japanese Philosophy 2: Neglected Themes and Hidden Variations* (Nagoya: Nanzan Institute for Religion and Culture, 2008), gave me English versions of passages from the Introduction and the Conclusion against which to check my own, and Maraldo's rendering of Kuki's terms confirmed the choices adopted here. The errors that remain are mine.

David M. Berry, University of Sussex, UK

[^tn-1]: Tr. By a vector translation I mean one made by directing a swarm of AI agents through the text under human guidance, in a manner close to vibe coding. A corpus of existing translations and of scholarship that quotes the text, for this book chiefly Takeshi Morisato's draft, the passages translated by Graham Mayeda and John C. Maraldo, and the *Sourcebook*'s version of Kuki's 1932 dissertation, fixes the key terms and serves as the reference against which the work is checked. The agents work from the page images in parallel under a shared brief, and for each section they produce a literal version, kept close to Kuki's sentences for checking and quotation, and a readable version, which is the translation printed here. Other agents check the readable version against the literal one, against the corpus and against the images, and return their corrections to be applied, so that the text is revised through repeated rounds of checking, a kind of hermeneutic circle. The decisions about terms, register and conventions are mine. The method is set out at https://vector-humanities.github.io/vector-translation/.

---

Literal translation, Japanese to English, published alongside the readable translation as part of its apparatus. It follows Kuki's sentences one by one, keeps the page markers of the 1948 printing, and is the version to quote from. The readable version is `Kuki_Problem-of-Contingency_translation.md`.

Source: 九鬼周造『偶然性の問題』岩波書店, 昭和23年 (1948 printing; first published 1935). NDL Digital Collection pid/1037966. Translated from the page images, not the OCR. A corrected Japanese transcription of the Introduction is in `source/Kuki_Guzensei_transcription.md`; there is none for the other sections.

Conventions:
- The appendices (A, notes on translation choices; B, contents of the 1948 printing; C, concordance with the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū*; D, index of persons and works cited) and the glossary are at the end of the book.
- `[p. N]` marks the start of page N of the 1948 edition. `[コマ N]` gives the image number (`source/guzensei-pages/page_NNN.jpg`).
- Kuki's emphasis marks (圏点) are rendered in italics.
- Kuki's citations are kept in his form. Quotations from Shestov, Trendelenburg, Hegel, Lotze and others are translated from Kuki's Japanese, not from the original-language texts, and should not be cited as translations of those authors.
- Kuki's 甲, 乙, 丙 are rendered A, B, C.
- Translator's notes, glosses and supplied words are set in tortoise-shell brackets, 〔 〕; notes begin "Tr.:". Square brackets inside quotations are those of the quoted text.

## Author's Preface (序) [コマ 3]

On the subject of contingency I stated my own views at the autumn public lecture meeting of Ōtani University in Shōwa 4 〔1929〕. For the lectures of Kyoto Imperial University in the academic year Shōwa 5 〔1930〕 too I chose the same subject. Therefore I had had an interest in this problem from considerably earlier, but circumstances did not permit me to concentrate my thinking on this one point. However, this problem is a problem that touches the core of *Existenz*, and unless at some time it takes some ultimate form, it is a thing that will not let me rest. For the time being I think I will make it public in about this form.

Further, I wish to express deep gratitude to the seniors and friends who gave me sympathy and instruction in the making of this book, and in particular to the two doctors Tomonaga Sanjūrō and Tanabe Hajime.

August, Shōwa 10 〔1935〕. The Author

## Introduction (序説) [コマ 7-12; pp. 1-11]

### 1. Contingency and metaphysics

[p. 1] [コマ 7] Contingency is the negation of necessity. The necessary means what is necessarily so. That is, existence has, in some sense, its Ground within itself. 〔Tr.: Ground: 根據 (*konkyo*). Kuki also writes 理由 (*riyū*) for *Grund*, rendered "ground" in lower case: the ground in the relation of ground and consequent (Chapter 2). See the Translator's Note.〕 The contingent means what happens to be so: existence does not have a sufficient Ground within itself. It is existence that contains negation, existence that is able not to be. In other words, contingency arises when, in existence, an inseparable inner relation with non-existence is witnessed. It is limit-existence, interposed at the surface of contact between being and nothingness. It is the state in which being is rooted in nothingness, the figure of nothingness encroaching upon being.

In contingency, existence faces nothingness. Now, to go beyond existence to nothingness, to go beyond [p. 2] [コマ 8] form to what lies beyond form, is the core meaning of metaphysics. 〔Tr.: 形而上 (*keijijō*), "above form", is the term from which 形而上學, metaphysics, is built; Kuki plays on it here.〕 Metaphysics no doubt takes "true existence" (*ontōs on*) as its problem. But "true existence" forms a problem in the original sense only in relation to "non-existence" (*mē on*). The existence that metaphysics takes as its problem is existence enveloped by non-existence, that is, by nothingness. The difference between metaphysics, philosophy in the eminent sense, and the other disciplines lies precisely in this. The other disciplines take existence, or a fragment of being, and treat it only as given existence and as a given fragment of being. Of nothingness, and of the relation between being and nothingness, they seek to know nothing at all.

The problem of contingency is strictly a problem of metaphysics, in the sense that it cannot be separated from the question of nothingness. It follows that the disciplines other than philosophy as metaphysics do not take contingency as their problem in its proper sense. It may be thought that the theory of probability, which belongs to mathematics, has contingency as its own problem. Probability theory certainly deals with contingent cases. But its aim is not to grasp the contingent as contingent, in its contingency. It does not seek to elucidate the meaning of contingency itself, in itself. The concern of probability theory comes down entirely to [p. 3] the quantitative relation between all the possible cases of the occurrence and non-occurrence of an event and the contingent cases in which that event occurs. Moreover, since a theoretical quantitative relation acquires empirical validity only when the number of observations is made infinitely large, probability theory seeks no more than to determine, macroscopically, the ideal constancy of the quantitative relations in which contingent events occur. The contingent variability that resides in microscopic detail is not touched at all. Yet what makes the contingent contingent lies precisely in the movement of the details. In short, probability theory is not an inquiry into the contingent itself. It cannot even be called a "calculation" of "the contingent". The contingent itself cannot be calculated. It cannot be denied that probability theory, by casting a sidelong glance at contingency from a fixed angle, lets us witness its structure to some degree, but it permits no grasp of contingency as a whole. Some may think that quantum-mechanical theory takes contingency as its problem. But quantum-mechanical theory asserts that, for quantum-mechanical phenomena, the two conditions of position and velocity cannot be determined simultaneously, and so does no more than allow a certain degree of contingency to hold sway. Its so-called uncertainty principle merely acknowledges contingency as a principle. For quantum mechanics itself, [p. 4] [コマ 9] quantum-mechanical contingency belongs, to the last, to an "unknowable dimension". One cannot ask quantum-mechanical theory to reflect, in principle and across the whole of existence, on its own principles. Contingency can become a datum of science in principle, but it has a primordial character by which, precisely through its contingency, science cannot treat it as an object. Only philosophy as metaphysics can take up the contingent as contingent, in its proper character, as a problem.

Yet all disciplines, for the very reason that they seek to investigate the necessary or probable relations of things, cannot in principle be separated from the problem of contingency. Every discipline, when it reflects in principle on its own labours, necessarily confronts the problem of contingency in its proper character, though only then. The reason is simply that every discipline is connected at its foundation with metaphysics. In short, the problem of contingency, insofar as it concerns nothingness, that is, insofar as it can be fully grasped only on the horizon of nothingness, is strictly a problem of metaphysics. [p. 5] Whether this problem is one that can be completely solved is, of course, another question. We must simply pursue contingency to the end as a problem of philosophy. To borrow Shestov's words, we belong to "those who do not wish to give up the hope of discovering in this world something other than statistics and 'necessity'" (L. Chestov, *La Philosophie de la Tragédie*, Paris 1926, Préface, p. XV). And we wish to bring the ontological structure and the metaphysical grounds of contingency to as much clarity as we can.

### 2. The essence of necessity and its three modes

Since contingency is the negation of necessity, to grasp the meaning of contingency we must start by elucidating the meaning of necessity. What, then, is necessity? As already said, it means being necessarily so, that is, the impossibility of the opposite. That the opposite is impossible means having the ground of existence within oneself, [p. 6] [コマ 10] a given self maintaining itself as it is given. When the self maintains itself to the end, it takes the form of self-preservation or self-identity. The concept of necessity, that is, presupposes identity. The form of the law of identity, "A is A", therefore expresses the strictest necessity. The necessary is, in the end, nothing other than the qualitative determination of identity stated from the standpoint of modality. Trendelenburg says: "The necessary is, by its concept, unchangeable, and was therefore already called ἀΐδιον (eternal) by Aristotle and *aeternum* (eternal) by Spinoza; in the necessary, the *identical* appears. Everything necessary is self-identical and, as such, holds fast to itself" (Trendelenburg, *Logische Untersuchungen*, II, 3. Aufl., S. 210). "Behind *identity* stands necessity, back to back" (ibid., S. 175). Hegel says that "*necessity* in itself is the one essence, *identical* with itself but full of content", and that "the *necessary* is in itself absolute relation, that is, the developed process in which the relation also sublates itself into absolute *identity*" (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, [p. 7] 1906, §§ 149, 150).

The determination "identity", and therefore "the necessary", is found in (1) conceptuality, (2) groundedness, (3) wholeness. That is, it is grasped in respect of (1) the relation between concept and mark, (2) the relation between ground and consequent, (3) the relation between whole and part. Lotze holds that, in the relation between A and B, there are three forms that lead to necessary knowledge. (1) We form a generic judgement (see p. 22) and seek a B that is already thought within the generic concept A itself. This B then belongs necessarily to every species of A. (2) We form a hypothetical judgement and show that, when a condition x is added to A, a B arises in that A which would not exist without this condition. This B then holds necessarily of every A on which the same condition acts in the same way. (3) We form a disjunctive judgement: if a problem is reduced to a strict "either the one or the other", we at once have a firm grip on the state of affairs. A single experience then suffices to decide, in each individual case, which of the two predicates, B or C, is posited of A with necessity. The ways [p. 8] [コマ 11] of arriving at necessary knowledge are limited to these three, and there is no other (Lotze, *Logik*, hrsg. v. Misch, Leipzig, 1912, S. 65). Hegel likewise names three absolute relations that embody necessity: (1) the relation of substantiality and accident, (2) the relation of causality, (3) the relation of reciprocal action. For substance to fulfil the concept of necessity, substance must be grasped as cause and the accident as effect; that is, the relation of substance and accident must pass over into the causal relation. And for the causal series to be truly infinite, it must take the form of a circle and not of a straight line; that is, causality must become reciprocal action. In this way Hegel sets out the inner connection of the three kinds of necessity (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, §§ 150-156).

Let us call these three modes of necessity (1) categorical necessity, (2) hypothetical necessity, (3) disjunctive necessity. Distinctions in the relation of judgements are more usually expressed by the terms 定言的, 假言的 and 選言的, but we deliberately use 定言的, 假說的 and 離接的. 〔Tr.: Kuki sets aside the standard Japanese logical terms for "hypothetical" (假言的, *kagenteki*) and "disjunctive" (選言的, *sengenteki*) in favour of 假說的 (*kasetsuteki*, hypothetical in the sense of supposition) and 離接的 (*risetsuteki*, disjunctive in the sense of separation and conjunction). English has a single word for each pair; the distinction is lost in "hypothetical" and "disjunctive".〕

### 3. The three modes of contingency

[p. 9] Since contingency has the sense opposed to necessity, there should be three modes of contingency corresponding to the three modes of necessity: (1) categorical contingency, (2) hypothetical contingency, (3) disjunctive contingency. Only by distinguishing contingency into these three modes, I think, does the meaning of contingency become clear in its multiplicity and its unity. That theories of the contingent always lack clarity is due in part, of course, to the difficulty of the problem itself. But I think it is due in large part to the fact that, at the point where the problem is first posed, the modes of contingency are not clearly distinguished on any principle, and grasping them as a unity is not clearly held in view as a theme.

In fact a division corresponding to these three modes can be found in Aristotle. As will be discussed in detail later, Aristotle's *symbebēkos* (συμβεβηκός, *accidens*) corresponds to categorical contingency; *automaton* (αὐτόματον, *casus*) and [p. 10] [コマ 12] *tychē* (τύχη, *fortuna*) correspond to hypothetical contingency; and *endechomenon* (ἐνδεχόμενον, *contingens*) corresponds to disjunctive contingency. Milhaud, in an essay on chance in Aristotle and Cournot, points out three aspects of chance common to Aristotle's and Cournot's theories. The first is "encounter" (*rencontre*). The second is "rarity" (*rareté*). The third is "a possible" (*un possible*) (Gaston Milhaud, *Études sur la Pensée Scientifique*, 1906, p. 137-158; *Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale*, 1902, p. 667-681). 〔Tr.: Kuki gives the essay title only in Japanese, アリストテレスおよびクールノーに於ける偶然, "Chance in Aristotle and Cournot". The French title has not been checked.〕 Now "encounter" means the encounter of one series of grounds with another series of grounds, and so is nothing other than hypothetical contingency. "Rarity", since it means what belongs only rarely, must be categorical contingency, which lies between concept and mark. And "a possible", since it means one possible among several equally possible disjuncts, is nothing other than disjunctive contingency. Rickert too lists three meanings of the contingent: (1) what is not lawlike, (2) what has no cause, (3) what is not essential (H. Rickert, *Die Grenzen der naturwissenschaftlichen Begriffsbildung*, 3. Aufl. 1921, S. 286-287). As examples of what is not lawlike he gives the fact that Saturn, and not the earth, has a ring, and that Frederick [p. 11] the Great won the battle of Leuthen. He is therefore pointing to the disjunctive isolation of disjunctive contingency, set against the universal inclusiveness of law. What has no cause, as what lies outside causality, is hypothetical contingency. What is not essential, as what does not belong to the essence of the concept, is categorical contingency. I believe these examples make it fairly clear that the division of contingency into categorical, hypothetical and disjunctive accords with the matter itself.

As will be shown later, categorical contingency remains throughout within the conceptual standpoint of logic; hypothetical contingency appears most markedly in connection with causality in the empirical world; and disjunctive contingency stands out especially in relation to the metaphysical absolute. One option, then, would be to name the three from their predominant feature: logical contingency, empirical contingency and metaphysical contingency. But since contingency at its root belongs to logical Modality, naming of this kind is, strictly speaking, inappropriate. 〔Tr.: Modality: 樣相性 (*yōsōsei*), modality as a category. 樣相 (*yōsō*), a modality or the modalities, is "modality" in lower case. See the Translator's Note.〕 In what follows we examine the problem of contingency under three heads: categorical contingency, hypothetical contingency and disjunctive contingency.

---

## Chapter 1. Categorical contingency (第一章 定言的偶然) [コマ 13-30]

### 1. Concept and categorical contingency

[p. 13] [コマ 13] The structure of a concept is based on witnessing some universal identity common to individual representations. And the constitutive content of a concept is the whole of the essential marks abstracted as identity, and its possible content arises by giving access to the non-essential marks placed outside the circle of identity by abstraction. The distinctive feature of an essential mark lies in the fact that, when it is negated, the concept itself is also negated. This is because the constitutive content of the concept and the whole of the essential marks are one and the same. Also, the relation between an essential mark and the concept, insofar as it is determined by such identity, is necessary. In contrast, the relation between a non-essential mark and the concept, as something that depends on whether or not access is given, [p. 14] [コマ 14] is contingent, because it lacks an identity of its own. Essential marks are called necessary marks, and non-essential marks are called contingent marks. Categorical contingency is nothing other than the contingency of contingent marks.

For example, for the concept triangle, being a part of a plane enclosed by three lines is a necessary mark. That is, it forms the constitutive content of the concept triangle, and together with negating this property the concept of the triangle is also dissolved. Because the concept triangle and being a part of a plane enclosed by three lines are joined by identity, we call the relation of the two necessary. In contrast, such things as that an angle is a right angle, or is obtuse, or is acute, merely form the possible content of the triangle. That is, those marks are contingent marks for the concept of the triangle. The concept of the triangle is related to these marks only by giving access to these marks, and because between the two an identity of its own is lacking, we call the relation of the two contingent. Even such a thing as that the sum of the interior angles is equal to two right angles is, insofar as it does not have identity with the concept of the triangle, [p. 15] something contingent for the triangle. That non-Euclidean geometry can think the sum of the interior angles of a triangle as smaller or larger than two right angles is also for this reason.

What has conferred reality on a concept is substance. Substance is what maintains itself unchangingly in time by the law of identity. To borrow Kant's words, "the identity of the substratum" (Kant, *Critique of Pure Reason*, B. 229 〔Tr.: reading uncertain; faint in the scan〕) is the meaning of substance. Hegel too speaks of the "absolute identity" of substance (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 150, S. 202). Over against substance, attributes are thought. Attributes are the various determinations of substance, and mean the mode of existence of a single substance. That is, the relation between substance and attribute corresponds to the relation between concept and mark. Accordingly, essential attributes and non-essential attributes, or contingent attributes, are thought. The striking example that made the concept real and stated the meaning of substance clearly is, needless to say, Plato's Idea. The Idea, as concept, is, in relation to many individual things, "the common" (τὸ κοινόν) and accordingly "the one" (μονάς). Moreover it is "true existence", forming a world independent in itself. The individual thing on the one hand participates in the Idea, [p. 16] [コマ 15] but on the other hand is rooted in "non-existence", understood as space. And contingency means that participation in the Idea is made incomplete by non-existence, that is, the gap that lies between archetype and copy. Aristotle called this kind of contingency "symbebēkos" (συμβεβηκός, accidens). The symbebēkos is "what belongs to something and can also be truly asserted, but is neither *of necessity* (ἐξ ἀνάγκης) nor *in most cases* (ὡς ἐπὶ τὸ πολύ)" (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ 30, 1025a). Thus Aristotle made clear the distinction between "by itself" (καθ' αὑτό, per se) and "by accident" (κατὰ συμβεβηκός, per accidens). What is "by itself" is "the essence", or "everything that belongs to the essence", or "when something has received a thing directly into itself, or into a part of itself" (Ib., Δ 18, 1022a). For example, "man lives *by himself*. For the spirit, in which life directly dwells, is a part of man" (Ib.). That is, "that he is a living being is not *by accident*" (Ib., E 2, 1026b). However, "that a certain man is white is *accidental*. For it is neither *always* (ἀεί) nor *in most cases*" (Ib.). [p. 17] And "matter (ὕλη) is the cause of the accidental" (Ib., E 2, 1027a). Accordingly, "the accidental is something near to non-existence (ἐγγύς τι τοῦ μὴ ὄντος)" (Ib., E 2, 1026b). Further, Aristotle says that the sum of the interior angles being equal to two right angles is, for the triangle, accidental, in a slightly different sense. The contingent of that kind can happen to be "eternal" (ἀΐδιον), and "belongs to its object *by itself*, but is not *in its essence* (ἐν τῇ οὐσίᾳ)" (Ib., Δ 30, 1025a). The concept of this kind of "accident by itself" (καθ' αὑτὸ συμβεβηκός) (Ib., B 1, 995b) contains, so to speak, a contradiction, but it can in a certain sense be compared with such things as what Husserl calls the "contingent a priori" (kontingentes Apriori) (Husserl, Formale und transzendentale Logik, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. X, S. 25-26).

### 2. The contingency of synthetic judgements

In short, categorical contingency means the contingency of contingent marks in relation to a concept. [p. 18] [コマ 16] When concepts are seen as the elements of judgement, a judgement comes to be an unfolded concept. Accordingly, the contingency based on the concept must also appear in the judgement, taking some form. When a certain concept is made the subject of a judgement, in the case where the mark occupying the place of the predicate is a contingent mark of the concept, contingency is seen in the connection between subject and predicate. And since the joining of subject and predicate is the judgement, that contingency exists in the connection of the two is nothing other than that the judgement itself has the character of contingency.

When the distinction between analytic judgement and synthetic judgement is drawn, the analytic judgement has as its basis an analysis within the identity of the subject concept, so the predicate of the judgement must be a necessary mark of the concept. In contrast, the synthetic judgement has as its essence the synthesis of the subject concept with possible content, so a contingent mark of the concept comes to the place of the predicate. Accordingly, the synthetic judgement is something that has the character of contingency.

Kant says in the *Critique of Pure Reason*, "an analytic judgement (affirmative) is one in which the connection of predicate and subject is thought through *identity*" (B. 10), and also in the *Logic* he says, "analytic propositions are propositions whose certainty is based on the identity of the concepts (of the predicate with the notion of the subject)" [p. 19] (Kant, Logik, hrsg. v. Jäsche, 3 Aufl. § 36). That is, the analytic judgement is nothing other than an analysis, within identity, of a concept grasped in identity. "All bodies are extended" asserts that, if the concept body is a×b, extension b is contained within that concept. And insofar as identity forms the basis, we "can be conscious of the *necessity* of the judgement" of this kind (*Critique of Pure Reason*, B. 12). What has reached the limit of the distinctive features of the analytic judgement is the identical judgement, such as "a body is a body", and it has, just as it is, the necessity that accompanies the law of identity, "A is A". In contrast, the synthetic judgement is one in which the connection of predicate and subject "is thought without identity" (B. 10). "All bodies are heavy" asserts that attraction c is also joined to the concept body, a×b. And insofar as the predicate c is something other than the concept a×b, the connection of weight with body is "merely *contingent*" (B. 12). After all, synthesis (σύνθεσις) means leaving self-identity behind and existing *together* (σύν) with another thing, by chance.

[p. 20] [コマ 17] Of course, if one speaks of one particular judgement of perception, the judgement arises from analysing the concrete content of perception given to the perceiving subject, so a judgement such as "this man is yellow" should also be called an analytic judgement, and accordingly the connection of "this man" and "yellow" is necessary. We, under the supposition that regards the subject as a general concept, admit the distinction between analytic judgement and synthetic judgement, and on that basis merely intend to point out that the former has necessity, and the latter, insofar as it means the empirical synthetic judgement, has contingency. That is, in the empirical synthetic judgement "some men are yellow", the connection of the concept "man" and the predicate "yellow" is entirely contingent. In other words, in the constitution of the concept man, the mark of the yellow of skin colour is something abstracted away as a contingent mark. It is something placed outside the conceptual identity man. When the synthetic judgement empirically synthesises man as concept and yellow, it goes without saying that the connection of man and yellow is contingent.

### 3. The contingency of particular judgements

[p. 21] In the case where the subject and predicate of a judgement show a necessary connection on the basis of conceptual identity, the judgement can take the form of a universal judgement. In contrast, in the case where there exists no more than a contingent connection, the judgement should take the form of a particular judgement. The particular judgement shows, on the basis of categorical contingency, contingency in the judgement itself. For example, when one says "all bodies are heavy", strictly speaking, it should be presupposed that weight is a necessary mark of the concept body, that is, that a necessary relation exists between weight and body. In the case where this necessity is denied, it is said by a particular judgement, "some bodies are heavy" or "some bodies are not heavy". And it is declared that weight is no more than a contingent mark of the concept body. Also, since weight is no more than a contingent mark of body, even if one were to say "all bodies are heavy", the universality in that case is not strict universality, but is "merely comparative" (Kant, Welches sind die wirklichen Fort-[p. 22] [コマ 18]schritte, die die Metaphysik etc, Beilagen I, Abschnitt I). Kant used the universal judgement "all bodies are heavy" in the *Critique of Pure Reason*, but in the *Prolegomena*, in order to state the contingency more precisely, he changed it to a particular judgement and says "some bodies are heavy" (Prolegomena, § 2, a).

According to Lotze, the universal judgement (das universale Urteil) only asserts a general fact, and accordingly is merely assertoric (see p. 198). Even if one says "all men are mortal" (alle Menschen sind sterblich), it is only that in fact in the end they are not alive, and the necessity of dying is not grounded (*konkyozukeru*, 根據づける). In contrast, the generic judgement (das generelle Urteil) also shows the Ground of necessary validity, and accordingly can be called apodictic (see p. 198). When one says "man is mortal" (der Mensch ist sterblich), it is asserted that, in the character of man, it is contained that anyone who shares in this character cannot avoid death. In short, the universal judgement cannot express necessity. To express necessity, one must choose the generic judgement (Lotze, Logik, hrsg. v. Misch, 1912, S. 93). That is what he says. However, as Benno Erdmann also argues, [p. 23] it is true that in the universal judgement the subject is thought from extension and in the generic judgement from intension, so there is a need to distinguish these two kinds of judgement, but Lotze exaggerates the distinction between the two too much. It cannot be said that the universal judgement stops at merely asserting a general fact. The fact is only that all men have died until now. That all men are in general mortal is already more than the assertion of a mere fact (Benno Erdmann, Logik, 3 Aufl. S. 456). Therefore, there is no harm in our thinking that necessity is expressed by the universal judgement and contingency is expressed by the particular judgement.

The contingency of the particular judgement is expressed symbolically by the signs of symbolic logic. According to Couturat's notation (L. Couturat, L'Algèbre de la Logique, 2e éd.), the universal affirmative expresses the relation of inclusion between a and b plainly by the sign <.

> a < b

The universal negative "no a is b" 〔Tr.: literally "all a are not b"〕 means "all a are non-b". That is, the universal negative is the same as a universal affirmative having a negative predicate. [p. 24] [コマ 19] Since the negation of a term is expressed by the sign ′, the universal negative is written as follows.

> a < b′

Speaking of the particular judgement, the particular judgement is the negation of the universal judgement. And to negate the judgement as a whole, it suffices to negate the copula <. The negation of the copula is expressed by drawing a bar vertically to strike it out. That is, the sign ≮ is used. Now, the particular affirmative is the negation of the universal negative, so it is expressed as follows.

> a ≮ b′

The particular negative is the negation of the universal affirmative, so it is expressed as follows.

> a ≮ b

Further, if one asks where the basis of the relation of inclusion between a and b lies, it is the relation of identity that exists between a×b, that is ab, on the one side, and a on the other. Therefore the universal affirmative can also be expressed as follows.

> a = ab

The non-inclusion of a and b in the universal negative can be thought as the inclusion of a and b′. [p. 25] Accordingly it can be seen as the identity that exists between a×b′, that is ab′, on the one side, and a on the other. The universal negative is expressed as follows.

> a = ab′

Since the particular judgement is the negation of the universal judgement, the particular affirmative and the particular negative can be expressed as follows.

> a ≠ ab′
>
> a ≠ ab

These two deny, respectively, the identity of ab′ and a, and the identity of ab and a. Since the modality of identity is necessity, the negation of identity is contingency. The categorical contingency that forms the basis of the particular judgement is symbolised most clearly in the form ≠, as the negation of the sign =, which expresses necessity as identity.

Further, the relation between the particular judgement and categorical contingency becomes more and more clear by considering the relation between inference and the particular judgement. Among immediate inferences there is what is called "conversion by accident" (conversio per accidens). In the case where "all A are B", one can say "some B [p. 26] [コマ 20] are A". That this method of conversion is said to be "by accident" is because contingency intervenes between A and B. So long as a universal judgement, "all A are B", is used, there must be between A and B a necessary relation as some relation of identity. However, that necessary relation is the necessary relation a<b, that is a=ab. Since there is no absolute relation of identity, absolute necessary relation, a=b, the relation b<a, that is b=ba, does not hold. One cannot say "all B are A". One can only say, as a particular judgement, "some B are A". The reason is that, as the signs b≮a′ or b≠ba′ express, room for the intervention of contingency remains between A and B. For example, one can say "all members of the yellow race are human", but one cannot say "all humans are members of the yellow race". One can only change the universal judgement to a particular judgement and say "some humans are members of the yellow race". The colour of the skin, which shows racial difference, is a contingent mark of the concept human. So long as this categorical contingency lies hidden, there is no way but to use a particular judgement, as a conversion "by accident".

[p. 27] Also, in mediate inference, what brings a new particular judgement as conclusion is the third figure of the syllogism.

> (Darapti) All Ethiopians are black. All Ethiopians are human. Therefore some humans are black.
>
> (Felapton) No Ethiopians are white. All Ethiopians are human. Therefore some humans are not white.

In this case, the particular judgement of the conclusion shows that, for the general concept human, black (not white) skin is no more than a contingent mark, and that it happens to be in Ethiopians that the mark black (not white) forms the possible content of the concept human.

In short, categorical contingency must seek logical expression through the particular judgement. In other words, the contingency that the particular judgement has is something based on the categorical contingency [p. 28] [コマ 21] of contingent marks.

### 4. The contingent as isolated fact

In the above it has been stated that categorical contingency, as the contingency of contingent marks, forms the basis of the contingency of the synthetic judgement and the particular judgement. Here, further considering the meaning of contingent marks, we can define the contingent as an isolated fact. The reason why a contingent mark is placed, as something non-essential, outside the circle of the identity of the concept is based on its isolation. Thus contingency in general comes to mean the isolated fact over against a system, and accordingly also comes to mean the individual thing.

Cohen says in regard to contingency: "A fact, even if its actuality has been proved, must be regarded as contingent so long as it has not yet been fitted into some general, or rather the most general, connection" (Cohen, Logik der reinen Erkenntnis, 3 Aufl. S. 580). For example, for the general concept body that mathematical natural science has, the organism [p. 29] is nothing other than an isolated fact. "Mathematical natural science, because it is limited to the mechanical, can neither determine the organic as the organic nor even describe it. Therefore *the contingent* stands for the organism. Organic natural forms are, in relation to the mechanical, not only relatively but also conceptually *contingent*" (Cohen, Kants Theorie der Erfahrung, 3 Aufl. S. 708). Windelband too regards the contingent as "the isolated fact" (das vereinzelte Factum) (Windelband, Die Lehren vom Zufall, S. 28). The meaning of this kind of contingency appears well in the following example. "Seen from the historical development of the collecting of works of art too, the interest of art science was extremely secondary, and artistic interest and cultural-historical interest were primary. Apart from the periods in which the desire for decoration, curiosity, religious feeling and the like were primary, what was collected first of all, chiefly, was the remains of ancient art. Only at last in the Renaissance did the works of each period come to be collected. As a result, the need was felt to order the remains of each period systematically, and at last art-historical research arose. However, because the history of the formation of the famous collections of old was extremely personal and *contingent*, there were few cases in which they were ordered and enlarged [p. 30] [コマ 22] according to art-historical interest. That kind of thing was limited only to newly founded collections. The national museums of the various countries at present are things in which, as a result of centralisation, many famous collections were joined in one place, became their foundation, and from there developed gradually and systematically" (Kojima Kikuo, "The Problem of a National Museum", *Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, printed 18 March Shōwa 6). The general concept in this case takes the form "art science" or "art-historical system".

Further, things such as the idea of so-called "occasional crime" also seek to place a certain kind of crime in the position of an isolated fact in relation to a general concept concerning a given personality. The following example is also almost the same kind of case. "The followers of Zihua were all of hereditary families. In white silk they rode in carriages, walked slowly and looked about haughtily. Turning, they saw Shang Qiukai, old in years and weak in strength, his face dark, his clothes and cap untidy; there were none who did not look down on him. Then they treated him with familiarity and contempt, cheated and deceived him, pushed, struck and jostled him; there was nothing they did not do. Shang Qiukai always had no look of resentment, and the guests' tricks were exhausted and they were wearied of jesting and laughing. At length they climbed a high terrace together with Shang Qiukai, and one among the crowd said idly, 'Whoever can throw himself down will be rewarded with a hundred pieces of gold.' The crowd all competed to respond. Shang Qiukai took it to be true, and at length threw himself down first. His form was like a flying bird; he floated down to the ground, and his bones were unharmed. The Fan household took it to be *chance* and did not yet think it strange. Therefore they again pointed to a deep pool at the bend of the river and said, 'In there is a precious pearl; by diving it can be got.' Shang Qiukai again followed them and dived for it. When he came out, he had indeed got a pearl. The crowd then for the first time doubted together, and Zihua then for the first time had him placed in the rank of those who eat meat and wear silk. Shortly afterwards the Fan storehouse had a great fire. Zihua said, 'If you can enter the fire and take out the brocade, I will reward you according to how much you get.' Shang Qiukai went without a look of difficulty, entered the fire, went and returned; the ash did not soil him, and his body was not scorched. The Fan household thought he [p. 31] possessed the Way, and so together they apologised to him, saying, 'We did not know that you possessed the Way and we deceived you; we did not know that you were a divine man and we insulted you. You may take us for fools; you may take us for deaf; you may take us for blind. We dare to ask about your Way'" (*Liezi*, "Huangdi"). The guests of Zihua of the Fan family had made, in regard to the person of Shang Qiukai, a general concept, a man to be scorned. Therefore they thought of an isolated fact that could not be subsumed under that concept as chance. When similar facts came to be repeated several times as actions of Shang Qiukai, they realised that it was not chance, and discarded the former general concept. And in its place they constituted, in identity, the concept of a possessor of the Way, or a divine man.

The isolated fact also comes to mean the individual thing or the individual. Aristotle too holds that "universals (τὰ καθόλου) belong by themselves, but the accidental is not by itself; it is said merely in respect of particulars (τὰ καθ' ἕκαστα)" (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ 9, 1017b). Husserl too says the following. "Individual existence of every kind is, speaking generally, *contingent*. ... However, the sense of this contingency, which means factuality, is delimited of itself in that it has a correlative relation to a necessity. That necessity does not mean the mere factual subsistence of a valid rule that orders [p. 32] [コマ 23] spatio-temporal facts. It has the character of essential necessity, and accordingly has a relation to essential universality. ... In the sense of each contingent thing it is contained that it has precisely an essence, and accordingly an eidos to be grasped purely. ... For example, each tone has in itself an essence, understood purely as a moment to be seen in the individual tone. And at the highest level it has the universal essence tone in general, or rather the auditory in general" (Husserl, Ideen zu einer reinen Phänomenologie, S. 9). Further, according to Husserl, since the phenomenological domain is formed by the transcendental reduction and the eidetic reduction, "in the phenomenological domain the contingent does not exist" (Ib., S. 288). Since Husserl's phenomenology is a science of essences based on the intuition of ideas, seen from its proper task there is no room for admitting the contingent. On the basis of categorical contingency meaning the individual thing or the individual, the reason why contingent attributes are often called individual attributes also becomes clear. Also, the reason why Schleiermacher tried to limit the range of application of the synthetic judgement as "judgement proper" (eigentliches Urteil) to individual facts can be understood (Schleiermacher, Dialektik, [p. 33] Ausg. v. Jonas, § 155, S. 88-89, see 405).

### 5. Exceptional contingency

In the previous section we considered that categorical contingency comes to take the meaning of the isolated fact, or of the individual thing or the individual. Here let us consider the case where the general concept means law and the contingent means exception. After all, the constitutive content of a concept, insofar as it means a universal determination, has the value of a *law* in relation to the individual representations to be subsumed under that concept. Accordingly, possible content that runs counter to the constitutive content is seen as a possible *exception*.

Aristotle gives cold weather in the *doyō* of summer as an example of the accidental (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, E 2, 1026b). We also have, for example, the concept clover. Since being three-leaved is something found in clover "in most cases" or "almost always", we witness it in identity and make it, as an essential mark, the constitutive content of the concept of clover. In the strict sense it cannot be called a necessary mark, [p. 34] [コマ 24] but insofar as it has been caught in identity, we regard it as something "quasi-necessary". And it comes to have the value of a law. Each individual clover depends in actuality on the quasi-universality of three leaves. The determination three-leaved holds of each individual clover as a law. In contrast, being four-leaved can form the possible content of clover, but insofar as it lacks identity with the concept, it is no more than a contingent mark, and is seen as a possible exception to the law. To give one more example, in the case where the weather forecasts of a certain meteorological-observatory technician have a large rate of success, being right up to ninety-nine per cent, "being right" becomes the constitutive content and comes to constitute the general concept, the weather forecasts of that technician. And since that "being right" comes to have the value of a law in relation to individual cases, the possible content "being wrong" is regarded as an exceptional contingency in relation to the law. Categorical contingency in this way sometimes takes the form of exceptional contingency.

Exceptional contingency appears in the following example. "I have seen that in the state of nature it must be a rare fact that some *chance* deviation of structure, for example something like a monstrosity, is preserved, [p. 35] and that, even if preserved at first, it must in general be lost by subsequent crossing with normal individuals" (Darwin, *The Origin of Species*, trans. Koizumi Makoto, vol. 1, p. 266). In the next example it is said that a certain fact is not an exceptional contingency. "Labour disputes do not arise merely because workers harbour grievances about working conditions, nor should they be started for that reason. They arise only when the workers believe they can win if they fight. Seen statistically, disputes are most frequent in the case where a boom is just about to turn into a period of depression, and in the case where the economy has left the period of depression and taken a step into a period of activity. In the former case, the entrepreneurs, sensing unease about the future early on, try to lower working conditions, but the mass of workers, insensitive to the subtle movements of the economy, are apt to misjudge the real situation and try to resist the employers' retrenchment. In the latter case, corporate profits have increased, prices have risen and the cost of living has soared, yet labour income does not readily increase, so the workers lose patience and try to set up disputes. On the side of the entrepreneurs, since they are uneasy about how far the shoots of the boom can grow, they do not readily try to loosen the hand of retrenchment. The frequent occurrence of labour disputes immediately after the Great War corresponds to the former case, and the recent increase of disputes in America and in our country [p. 36] [コマ 25] is probably a suitable example of the latter case. Therefore one must not regard the present labour disputes as *chance* occurrences and lightly overlook them. One should rather resolve that we are coming into an era of labour disputes and make preparations to prevent their ravages in advance" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 14 September Shōwa 9). It is a consideration of the relation between the general concept, era of labour disputes, and the facts of individual labour disputes, and it says that, since at present it is an era of labour disputes, the present labour disputes are not exceptional contingencies. A labour dispute arising in an era of no labour disputes is an exceptional contingency.

After all, categorical contingency as a contingent mark is something that does not take part in the subsuming function of universal identity. And since that subsuming function is carried out with the schema "always" or "almost always", the categorical contingency abstracted away by that function has, as the negation of "always" and "almost always", the structure "sometimes" or "rarely". Also, it is especially in the case of "rarely" that the contingent comes to take the meaning of an exception to a general law. It is also for this reason that there are cases in which words expressing chance have their root in "rarely". There is an old word, *wakuraba ni*, meaning chance; *wakuraba* [p. 37] are leaves of trees that have coloured like autumn foliage in summer and withered at the tips, and are something seen only "rarely". It is the law of summer leaves that they are green. Yet there are some that have rarely coloured. For green summer leaves, being coloured is an exception seen rarely. The word *tamatama* too, together with meaning chance, also has the meaning "rarely". Originally *tamatama* is something that intensifies its meaning by repeating *tama*. And it is said that *tama* has the sense of *tema* (手間). And this *tama* has almost the same meaning as *mama*, that is, *mama* (間間). Accordingly the core meaning must lie in the *ma* common to *tama* and *mama*. What is *ma*? *Ma* is 間. It is an interval. It is a spatial and temporal interval. Also, an interval in turn comes to mean what does not exist except after leaving an interval. Accordingly it means what is *mare* (rare). *Mare* too is in fact a contraction of *ma are* (間有れ) (see the dictionary *Genkai*). Since *ma* is what does not exist except rarely, *ma* also means chance. *Ma ni au* means to fit a chance occasion. *Ma ga warui* means that the chance encountered lacks fitness. *Konna ma ni natta* means that things have turned into this kind of chance state of affairs. In short, on the basis of *ma*, [p. 38] [コマ 26] *mama*, *tama*, *tamatama* and *mare* all form one and the same group of words. And the fact that *wakuraba ni* and *tamatama*, whilst meaning exceptional contingency as "rarely", became words that mean chance in general proves that categorical contingency is easily grasped especially in exceptional contingency.

What is called the "fallacy of accident" (σόφισμα παρὰ τὸ συμβεβηκός, fallacia ex accidente) in the theory of fallacies of formal logic also concerns mainly exceptional contingency. The so-called "simple fallacy of accident" applies a statement concerning a general concept even to exceptional, contingent content. For example, it is the case of thinking, from "a dream (*yume*) has the sense of *i-me* (寢目, sleeping eye), is something seen whilst sleeping, and its visual content is large", that "the dreams of the blind too have much visual content". The fallacy arises in applying a general statement concerning the general concept dream even to the exceptional contingency, the dreams of the blind. That for the blind a dream is not something seen but, as something heard, has much auditory content is clear also in the light of the diaries of the blind and other sources. The "converse fallacy of accident" directly generalises a truth concerning an exceptional contingency. For example, it is the case of asserting, from the fact that "morphine has value as a medicine", that "since the main constituent of opium is morphine, [p. 39] opium is beneficial to health". It is a fallacy that arises from not distinguishing the exceptional, contingent phenomenon, the sick body that needs an anaesthetic or analgesic, from the body in general.

In the above we have considered exceptional contingency, but in short, in the case where a non-essential mark in relation to a general concept is a contingent mark as an exception, non-essentiality, contingency, stands out especially.

### 6. The reason for the existence of categorical contingency

In the *Nāgasena Sūtra* (那先比丘經), Milinda puts the following question to Nāgasena. "The people of the world have heads, faces, eyes, bodies and four limbs all complete. For what reason are there the long-lived and the short-lived, the much ill and the little ill, the poor and the rich, the honoured and the base, the handsome and the ugly, those trusted by others and those doubted by others, the bright and the dull? For what reason are they not the same?" (first fascicle). This question is a philosophical question that holds within it the joys and sufferings of human beings, but in the end it is nothing other than a question concerning the contingency of the individual thing, categorical contingency. It is a question concerning the contingent determinations that individual human beings have insofar as they stand in relation [p. 40] [コマ 27] to the general concept human. Nāgasena, answering this, said, "It is like the many trees bearing fruit: there are the sour, there are the bitter, there are the pungent, there are the sweet." But this is no more than giving another example of categorical contingency.

This kind of doubt concerning the existence of categorical contingency means, in general, a doubt concerning the existence of individual facts and individual things. The doubt concerning the existence of individual things is the question why, besides genera and species, individual things exist. And this question is in the end a question concerning existence itself. For if a given species did not contain individual things within it, it would itself become an individual thing and exist in relation to the genus. That genus too, by negating the particulars within itself, would itself become an individual thing and come to exist in relation to the higher genus. Thus one goes back to the highest genus. And to negate individual things and think only the existence of the highest genus is nothing other than to think an emptiness and an abstraction. Of course, a case is not inconceivable in which, when the universal individuates itself, all the individual forms, on the basis of the identity of the universal, have strict equality among one another. But in that case the individual thing becomes something like what Husserl calls ideal singularity [p. 41] (eidetic singularity), and cannot be called something that has true individuality. In the actual world one must think that Leibniz's principle "the indiscernible is identical" holds sway without exception. Each monad, as a "metaphysical point", must occupy a position at a "mathematical point" and express the universe from its own standpoint. Accordingly, in the universe there are not two things that are completely alike. There are neither two leaves completely alike nor two raindrops completely alike. That individual things show differentiation beyond ideal singularity must be an indispensable condition of the actuality of the actual. Further, insofar as general concepts such as species and genus are things originally constituted, on the basis of individual things, by the operation of identifying abstraction, it is natural that the contingent marks placed outside the circle of identity by abstraction should, as categorical contingency, conversely speak of the existence of individual things.

The doubt concerning the existence of categorical contingency further also contains a doubt concerning the case in which a contingent mark takes the form of an exception and endangers the general concept itself. To this one must answer that a general concept which admits exceptions is not something fixed and static but, as something generative and dynamic, [p. 42] [コマ 28] means rather a movement towards a general concept. It may also be said that this kind of general concept is not something that has the universality of determinant judgement, that is, already-formed universality, but something that has the universality of reflective judgement, that is, universality as a task. Between the general concept and the individual thing there is movement. The individual thing has incommensurability in relation to logic. There lies the possibility of the exception. Hegel too says: "On the surface of nature, so to speak, the contingent acts freely. It should be acknowledged as such. One must not have the demand (sometimes mistakenly attributed to philosophy) to want to find there what can only be so and cannot be otherwise. ... It is entirely correct to hold that the task of science, and more precisely the task of philosophy, lies in general in coming to know the necessity hidden under the semblance of contingency. However, this must not be understood as if the contingent belonged merely to our subjective representation, and accordingly were something to be simply eliminated in order to reach the truth. Scientific efforts that are one-sided in pursuing this direction will hardly escape the justified reproach of being called an empty game, or an inflexible, rule-bound pedantry" [p. 43] (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, S. 195). Also, concerning the relation between law and individual fact, he says: "Law and the administration of justice contain contingency on one side. And the reason is that law is a general determination and must be applied to individual cases. If one were to take a stance opposed to this contingency, one would come to assert an abstraction" (Hegel, Grundlinien der Philosophie des Rechts, hrsg. v. Lasson, 1911, S. 341). This speaks of the movement of the contingent existing within a general determination, specifically with regard to law, but it is also something that can be said in general of natural law and individual instances of the contingent. Boutroux, concerning contingency within natural law, states the famous words: "Laws are the riverbed through which the torrent of facts passes. Facts follow that riverbed, but it was originally the facts that hollowed out the riverbed" (Les lois sont le lit où passe le torrent des faits : ils l'ont creusé, bien qu'ils le suivent.) (Boutroux, De la contingence des lois de la nature, 9 éd., p. 39). Since every law lies in the relation of subsumption between a general concept and individual facts, insofar as the general concept has the character of the universality of reflective judgement, universality as a task, it must be rather natural that contingency as exception accompanies the reverse side of the law. [p. 44] [コマ 29]

### 7. From categorical contingency to hypothetical contingency

The doubt concerning the existence of categorical contingency shows that the problem must presently be unfolded on a new horizon. If there is a chilly day in the *doyō* of summer, it is contingent for the general concept the *doyō* of summer. But for the temperature being low in the *doyō* of summer of a certain given year, there must be a cause in sunspots or something else. Also, in the one particular judgement of perception "this clover is four-leaved", the connection of "this clover" and "four-leaved" has necessity. The connection of "clover" and "four-leaved" is contingent only in the case where a general concept is thought. At the same time as a delimitation is given to "clover" by the demonstrative pronoun "this", the relation between one particular clover and four leaves is no longer contingent. For "this clover" to be "four-leaved", there must be some [p. 45] cause, whether the state of nourishment, or the influence of the climate, or the stimulus of an injury. Everyone acknowledges that such a thing as a snake with two heads on one body is an extremely rare contingent existence. However, according to the experiments of biologists, a snake with two heads on one body can be made artificially by surgery, and so it is thought that even a snake with two heads on one body as seen in the natural world arose because the head part was split in two whilst very young. To think the snake with two heads on one body contingent is only insofar as one sees it in connection with the general concept snake. For "this" snake to have two heads there must be some cause. To think those disabled from birth, idiots and the like contingent is also in connection with the general concept "human". "This" disabled person and "that" idiot must each have embryological or other causes. To think the "handsome or ugly" of outward appearance contingent is also because one sets up the general concept "human" and witnesses the particular in relation to that general concept. For "this" person to be "handsome" and "that" person to be "ugly", there must be some cause, whether the manner of combination of the germ cells of the two sexes, male and female, or the state of health of the mother's body at the time of pregnancy. Likewise, the difference of races by the difference of skin colour is something contingent for the conceptual essence "human". But the relation between one particular race and skin [p. 46] [コマ 30] colour is by no means contingent. For "this" race to have a given skin colour, there must be a cause in light rays, temperature and other things. Also, likewise, that the weather forecast of a certain technician of the meteorological observatory, whose rate of success reaches ninety-nine per cent, should be wrong is an exceptional contingency precisely in relation to the general concept, the weather forecast with a rate of success of ninety-nine per cent, but in "this" particular case there must be some cause, such as a sudden change in the weather or an agitation of the forecaster's mind.

We in this way move from the problem of conceptuality to the problem of groundedness. We move from the problem of categorical contingency to the problem of hypothetical contingency.

〔Tr.: 不具者 (*fugusha*) and 白痴 (*hakuchi*) are period terms, rendered here "those disabled from birth" (生來の不具者) and "idiots"; the readable version renders 白痴 as "those with severe intellectual disability". 土用 (*doyō*) is kept in transliteration; the readable version renders it "dog days".〕

---

## Chapter 2. Hypothetical contingency (第二章 假說的偶然) [コマ 30-99]

### 1. Contingency of ground

[p. 47] [コマ 30] Leibniz called the law of contradiction and the law of sufficient reason the "two great principles" (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 515, 707). The law of contradiction is, after all, nothing other than the law of identity. He says "the axiom of identity or, what is the same, the axiom of contradiction" (Ib., p. 136). Kant too did not distinguish the two (Kant, *Logik*, hrsg. v. Jäsche, S. 58). Then in what relation do these two great principles, the "law of identity" and the "law of ground", stand? In the fragment "First Truths" (*Primae veritates*) Leibniz showed the possibility of reducing groundedness to identity. The predicate or consequent is always inherent in the subject or antecedent (*Semper praedicatum seu consequens inest subjecto seu antecedenti*). And inherence (*inesse*) is nothing other [p. 48] [コマ 31] than identity (*idem esse*). In the end, therefore, groundedness is rooted in identity. The proof of a truth rests, after all, on the law of ground, "nothing is without a ground". If the law of ground did not hold, there would be truths that cannot be proved a priori. Such a truth means a truth that is not resolved into identity. But that is contrary to the nature of truth.
