Truth, whether explicitly or implicitly, is in any case always identical (*Alioqui veritas daretur, quae non potest probari a priori, seu quae non resolveretur in identicas, quod est contra naturam veritatis, quae semper vel expresse vel implicite identica est*). And the first truth is nothing other than the proposition "A is A" (*Opuscules et fragments inédits de Leibniz*, éd. Couturat, p. 518-519). To borrow Couturat's concise formulation: "The law of identity says that all identical (analytic) propositions are true. The law of ground affirms that all true propositions are identical (analytic)" (Couturat, Sur la métaphysique de Leibniz, *Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale*, t. X. 1902, p. 8). Émile Meyerson too points out that the law of ground is based on the law of identity, and says: "This conviction of rationality, this conviction of necessary connection, has its origin solely in the reduction to the identity that exists between antecedent and consequent" [p. 49] (Émile Meyerson, *Du cheminement de la Pensée*, I. 1931, p. 55).

In the sense set out above, ground and consequent have identity. The relation between the two is therefore necessary. By contrast, what does not stand in the relation of ground and consequent is contingent. It can be called contingency of ground, as against necessity of ground. And the *causal* contingency and *teleological* contingency to be explained later, together with this contingency *of ground*, are called by the general name hypothetical contingency. The division of hypothetical contingency into these three rests on the division of the hypothetical relation into the three of groundedness, causality and purposiveness. Schopenhauer divided the law of sufficient reason into four roots. They are the four: the "principle of the sufficient reason of becoming" (*principium rationis sufficientis fiendi*), the "principle of the sufficient reason of knowing" (*principium rationis sufficientis cognoscendi*), the "principle of the sufficient reason of existence" (*principium rationis sufficientis essendi*) and the "principle of the sufficient reason of acting" (*principium rationis sufficientis agendi*). The principle of the sufficient reason of becoming corresponds to the causality that we shall treat later, the principle of the sufficient reason of knowing corresponds to the groundedness just described, and the principle of the sufficient reason of acting corresponds roughly to the purposiveness to be described later. What he calls the principle of the sufficient reason of existence refers to the ground of intuitive existence, that is, to a principle concerning mathematical [p. 50] [コマ 32] ground (Schopenhauer, *Über die vierfache Wurzel des Satzes vom zureichenden Grunde*). And corresponding to the four divisions of the law of sufficient reason, Schopenhauer divides necessity into four: physical necessity, logical necessity, mathematical necessity and practical necessity (Schopenhauer, *Die Welt als Wille und Vorstellung*, Anhang. Kritik der Kantischen Philosophie. Sämtliche Werke. hrsg. v. Deussen, I. S. 549). Whether it is right to distinguish mathematical ground from logical ground, and accordingly to set up mathematical necessity besides logical necessity, belongs among the important problems of epistemology, but we cannot now go deeply into that problem. Here we have simply followed Lotze, who says: "The necessary, whose non-existence is impossible, is only what is conditioned as follows. That is, (1) what is determined by a ground as consequent, (2) what is determined by a cause as effect, (3) what is determined by a purpose as means" (Lotze, *Mikrokosmus*, III, 1864, S. 551). We have distinguished the hypothetical relation into the three of groundedness, causality and purposiveness, and, corresponding to the three of necessity of ground, causal necessity and teleological necessity, have separated the three of contingency of ground, causal contingency and teleological contingency.

[p. 51] There are two kinds of contingency of ground. One is the case where the non-existence of a ground is witnessed negatively with respect to one thing; the other is the case where the existence of some relation other than groundedness between two or more things is witnessed positively. The former can be called negative contingency of ground, and the latter positive contingency of ground.

### 2. Negative contingency of ground

Negative contingency of ground is the case where the non-existence of a ground is witnessed negatively, and the concept of the contingent of this kind appears in the following example. "When I do not know what is true sufficiently clearly and distinctly, if I refrain from passing judgement, I am plainly acting rightly and am not mistaken. But if I either affirm or deny, I do not use free will rightly. If I have turned to the wrong side, I am certainly mistaken. If I have taken hold of the other side, I fall upon the truth *by chance*, but that does not mean [p. 52] [コマ 33] I am without fault. For it is clear by the natural light that the perception of the intellect must always precede the decision of the will" (Descartes, *Meditationes* IV. *Oeuvres*, éd. Adam & Tannery, VII. p. 59-60; cf. *Principia philosophiae*, I. § 44. *Oeuvres*, éd. Adam & Tannery, VIII. p. 21). Since he affirmed at will whilst lacking a clear and distinct ground sufficient to affirm the truth, he fell into the truth by chance. The contingent in this case is negative contingency of ground, as the non-existence of a ground. The following example is the same. "That science finds the highest and most complete expression of its explanation in atomism is due to the very structure of science, and is not the result of a kind of *chance*. That science, as the labour of an understanding that knows clearly and distinctly nothing but number, masters the obscure and confused manifold of the actual is possible only through number" (Arthur Hannequin, *Essai critique sur l'hypothèse des atomes*, Paris, Alcan, p. 129). According to the author, there is a sufficient ground in the very structure of science for science arriving at the consequent called atomism. Since science and atomism are bound by necessity of ground, the contingent as the non-existence of a ground cannot be found. The following case is the same. "When the dialectical [p. 53] structure is kept without the least distortion, being and nothingness, which are relatively opposed to each other, become entirely equal in rank and standing, and we have no way to distinguish precedence and subordination between them; we must abandon the structure of the dialectic according to the usual interpretation, in which being turns into nothingness and that nothingness turns into being, and must think that being and nothingness are both, equally and directly, manifested from within absolute being through its self-delimitation or self-negation. And for that reason, from the standpoint of the absolute, being and nothingness become mutually exchangeable. And to attach a before and after to the arising of the two can only be put down to the *contingent* caprice of the individual who thinks it" (Takahashi Satomi, *Zentai no tachiba* (The Standpoint of the Whole), p. 277). Logically, being and nothingness are entirely equal in rank and standing, and there is no ground for making either first or last. If one makes one of them first and the other last, it is doing so merely at will, without a necessary ground, that is, contingency of ground. In short, negative contingency of ground is a mental product lacking a sufficient ground.

A mental product lacking a sufficient ground is, generally speaking, something irrational. Such irrational negative contingency of ground finds a place of existence with comparative constancy in dreams, in madness and in artistic production. The tendency of the positing of ideas in a dream is [p. 54] [コマ 34] nothing other than wholly contingent fancy. As Bergson too says, in dreams we encounter contingent events countless times. Memory rakes together fragments of recollections of every kind from here and there and offers them, without making them consistent, to the consciousness of the sleeper. The sleeper seeks some meaning in that meaningless heap. He thinks the inconsistency is because there are gaps, and calls up various other recollections to fill them, but these too often appear with the same disorder, so a further new explanation becomes necessary, and the same thing goes on without end. The instability of dreams rests, after all, on not making sensation correspond exactly to recollection and leaving room there for mischief. In dreams, very different recollections of every kind come to fit the same sensation. In Uchida Hyakken's *Hyakkien zuihitsu* there is: "The eighth of August, autumn begins. When I woke once at dawn, I left the shōji and the window wide open and went back to sleep. At the foot of the Denzūin slope, a big yellow horse came walking towards me. Its body was absurdly long, and of its four legs the left hind one was short. No one was leading it, so I thought it dangerous. When I said to Kiikō, who had come with me, [p. 55] 'It's dangerous, shall we run?', the horse turned towards us. Just then I saw that there were two men in black clothes on the far side of the horse. When the horse had gone off somewhere, I woke up. I heard a crow cawing nearby. It went on cawing for a long time in a strangely low voice." The sensory datum is that "a crow went on cawing for a long time in a strangely low voice". It called up the recollection of the gloomy slope road of Denzūin, the recollection of a yellow of strangely low tone, the recollection of a horse with a long body, the recollection of a strange form in which only one of the four legs is short, and the recollection of the theatre's *kurombō* wearing crow-like black clothes. These recollections all chase the sensation around, trying to revive within the sensation. There a contingent product such as a yellow horse arises. It comes walking towards us from the far side at the foot of the Denzūin slope, limping. Again, different recollections cling to the sensation together, or catch up with the sensation in turn, one after another. Just when one thinks there is a man in black clothes on the far side of the horse, this time the yellow horse changes in an instant into the man in black clothes. The dreamer reasons in the dream, as in "No one was leading it, so I thought it dangerous", "It's dangerous, shall we run?", "When I said shall we run, the horse turned towards us", "The horse turned [p. 56] [コマ 35] towards us. Just then", and makes logical connections, trying to remove the absurdity of the dream, but on the contrary makes it all the more senseless. The contingent caprice that a dream unfolds is almost beyond explanation (cf. Bergson, *L'Énergie spirituelle*, p. 91, 114).

The intellectual world of the madman is likewise a tangle of contingent caprice. Descartes describes how "they stubbornly insist that they are kings whilst in abject poverty, say they are clothed in purple whilst naked and will not listen, or else say that their head is earthenware, or else believe themselves to be pumpkins or glass bottles" (Descartes, *Meditationes* I. *Oeuvres*, éd. Adam & Tannery, VII. p. 19). One can also cite the words of Shakespeare's mad King Lear as an example of contingency of ground. "Let us have Regan anatomised. I would have it examined what on earth grows in that wretch's heart. Is there some particular cause that brews such a cruel heart? You, I take into my service as one of my hundred knights. Only I do not like your dress. You will say, I suppose, that it is the Persian fashion, but I would have it changed. Be quiet, quiet. Draw the curtain. So, so. Now, we shall finish supper [p. 57] in the morning" (Act III, Scene 6). Anatomy of the body and examination of a cruel heart, nakedness and dress, the morning and supper. The positing of ideas lacks a sufficient ground.

That art, whilst approaching dream and madness on the one hand, has a difference from them on the other, is often said, but in certain kinds of artistic production negative contingency of ground appears to an extreme degree, and there are some that make one think of dream or madness itself. For example, in Book Sixteen of the *Man'yōshū* there are two "poems to which the mind attaches nothing". 『吾妹子が額に生ひたる雙六の牡牛の鞍の上の瘡』 ("On my beloved's brow there grows / the sugoroku bull's / saddle, the sore upon it.") 『吾背子が犢鼻にする圓石の吉野の山に氷魚ぞさがれる』 ("The round stone my husband makes his loincloth: / on the mountains of Yoshino / icefish hang.") They are poems made as a result of Prince Toneri issuing the order: "If there is anyone who makes a poem with nothing it rests on, I will reward him with coin and silk." "With nothing it rests on" means nothing other than the non-existence of a ground. The "rikai" poem of the *Kakyō hyōshiki*, 『春日山峰漕ぐ船の藥師寺淡路の島の犁の耳』 ("Kasuga mountain, a boat rowing over the peak, Yakushiji, the island of Awaji, the mouldboard of a plough"), is the same. Between word and word there is no logical connection at all. Almost nothing positive can be found, and it remains only that, in the run of the words, the non-existence of a ground is witnessed negatively. Yet we still cannot deny that there is some art there. Shakespeare's *A Midsummer Night's Dream* is an example of the symbolisation of the abstract [p. 58] [コマ 36] nonsense of chance, and at the same time a realistic depiction of the most concrete truth of human life. As Bottom the weaver confesses, it is "a dream wondrous beyond all bounds", "a dream that human wisdom can make nothing of", "a dream such as the eye of man has never heard, nor the ear seen, nor the hand tasted, nor the tongue thought, nor the heart passed on". "Whoever thinks to explain such a dream is a great ass." It is "a great fool". As "Bottom's Dream", "the bottom has gone right out of it" (It hath no bottom). "Having no bottom" is "having nothing it rests on". It is the non-existence of a ground. On the "supramundane play and surreal laughter" of this kind of "meaninglessness", that is, "nonsense", Mr Takahashi Satomi sets out an interesting philosophical consideration (Tōhoku Imperial University, Faculty of Law and Letters, *Tenth Anniversary Philosophical Essays*, p. 53).

### 3. Positive contingency of ground

In positive contingency of ground, some other positive relation that is not a necessary relation [p. 59] through groundedness is witnessed between two or more things. The contingent of this kind often appears in relations of number. For example, if we turn the so-called Archimedean value of π for the ratio of the circumference, 3 1/7, into a decimal, we obtain the following recurring decimal.

> 3.142857142857……

The repetend 142857 is repeated "again and again" (πάλιν καὶ πάλιν), but in this case the property of the number that the approximate value of the ratio of the circumference to its diameter has exists in a relation of necessity of ground, without any relation to the system of numeration. Accordingly, the part corresponding to the first to the sixth decimal place is necessarily conditioned by the property of the number, and likewise the part corresponding to the seventh to the twelfth decimal place is also necessarily conditioned. However, there is the device called decimal numeration, adopted in order to express that approximate value in figures, and on the basis of it 142857 and 142857 are positively witnessed as contingency. Below the twelfth decimal place too, the same relation is repeated infinitely. Further, let us take this number 142857. And on a disc, at the vertices of a hexagon inscribed in a circle, let us arrange the figures of this number in order in the direction in which the hands of a clock move. If this number is doubled it becomes 285714, if tripled [p. 60] [コマ 37] it becomes 428571, if quadrupled 571428, if quintupled 714285, and if sextupled 857142. These numbers have the same figures in the same order as those on the disc. To get the double, one need only take the starting point at 2 and go round in the direction of the hands of a clock. To get the triple take the starting point at 4, to get the quadruple at 5, to get the quintuple at 7, to get the sextuple at 8, and go round likewise in the direction of the hands of a clock. This number 142857 is a number that becomes 999999 when multiplied by seven, and is therefore the integer part of the quotient of one million divided by seven. The necessary relation that exists between 142857 and the numbers that are two, three, four, five and six times it is, after all, the relation of one number corresponding to a common divisor and its various multiples. And on the ground that the common divisor is a multiple of nine, the consequent follows that the various multiples of the common divisor are also multiples of nine. However, that the special relation described above exists besides this rests on regarding each number as something ultimate and thinking of a circular movement that returns from the figure at the right end to the figure at the left end. This kind of coincidence drawn out by circular movement is a contingent relation entirely unconnected with the necessary relation between one number and its various multiples. There, there is one unmoving thing, wholly at rest, which can be read as "is not in the world" [p. 61] (四二八五七一, 428571) and so on, something that does not change in the least even when multiplied by two, three, four, five or six. 〔Tr.: Read by the conventional syllabic values of the digits (4 *yo*, 2 *ni*, 8 *ha*, 5 *i*, 7 *na*, 1 *i*), 428571 gives *yo ni wa inai*, "is not in the world".〕 It is, at any rate, a relation worthy of attention. Positive contingency exists where that relation is positively witnessed.

That in name divination several names come to have a positive relation through the stroke count of their characters is also positive contingency of ground existing where there is no logical relation. Yukio (行雄), Hisanori (久徵), Teisuke (禎介), Takeo (武郎), Senji (宣治) and so on, despite there being no logical relation whatever among them, show a contingent relation in the so-called earth grade, that is, in the total stroke count of the given name being eighteen, and there build one basis of name divination. The logical relation lies between the meaning the name expresses and the name itself. The ground on which a given name arises lies in the meaning that name expresses. By a certain ground the name Yukio follows as consequent, and by another certain ground the name Hisanori follows as consequent. Between Yukio and Hisanori there is no necessary relation through groundedness at all. That the stroke counts are both eighteen is a positively witnessed contingency. The logical relation between name and name must be a relation of meaning. For example, Yukio, Tsugio, Mitsuo, [p. 62] [コマ 38] Yoshio and Yasuo have in common the character 雄, which might be called a common divisor of meaning. The names Yukio, Tsugio, Mitsuo, Yoshio and Yasuo must have arisen with a certain ground, for example a meaning such as "manly", as their ground. In this case each of them, on the basis of being the consequent of the same ground, has a logical necessary relation to the others. But the common point of a total stroke count of eighteen, which is also to be seen besides, is a contingency grasped positively, outside groundedness.

Positive contingency of ground is further to be seen strikingly in the phonological relations of language. That is, it is the case where a phonological relation exists positively where there is no logical relation through groundedness. For example, it is such things as the relation between "鉢" (*hachi*, bowl), "蜂" (*hachi*, bee) and "八" (*hachi*, eight), or the mutual relation of "線香" (*senkō*, incense stick), "詮衡" (*senkō*, selection), "專攻" (*senkō*, specialisation), "潜行" (*senkō*, moving in secret), "鮮紅" (*senkō*, bright crimson), "戰功" (*senkō*, military merit) and "仙公" (*senkō*, an immortal). Between these words there is no logical relation at all by which one, as ground, and the other, as consequent, mutually condition their positing. Not only that, they do not have the logical relation of being various consequents arising from a common ground either. What exists positively is only the phonological relation. For that reason the relation is called contingent. This kind of contingency has a certain value in literature in the forms of alliteration, end rhyme, pivot words (*kakekotoba*), pillow [p. 63] words (*makurakotoba*), acrostics (*oriku*) and palindromes (*kaibun*) (cf. *Iwanami kōza Nihon bungaku*, Kuki, "Nihon shi no ōin", p. 8, p. 119, p. 155). As for pillow words, in "百敷の、大宮どころ" (*momoshiki no, ōmiyadokoro*) and "草枕、旅やどりせす" (*kusamakura, tabiyadori sesu*), the positing of the pillow word and of the following seven-syllable phrase are mutually and necessarily conditioned by a logical relation of meaning. However, those based on alliteration, such as "稻舟の、いなにはあらず" (*inafune no, ina ni wa arazu*) and "うの花の、うきことあれや" (*u no hana no, uki koto are ya*), and those based on pivot words, such as "玉垂の、を(緒、越)智の大野の" (*tamadare no, o 〔緒 cord, 越 crossing〕 chi no ōno no*) and "梓弓、はる(張、春)の山べを" (*azusayumi, haru 〔張 stringing, 春 spring〕 no yamabe o*), all lack true groundedness between the pillow word and the following seven-syllable phrase, and there exists only a contingent relation in sound. Further, the essence of the pivot word is to split one sound into two different meanings and to grasp the contingent coincidence in sound that the two meanings have. As for end rhyme, in the line "洛陽城東桃李花" each word is necessarily conditioned by logical relation. The line "飛來飛去落誰家" is the same. Now between these two lines, through the phonological character that "花" and "家" are both of the level tone, 麻 rhyme, a contingent relation having nothing to do with the groundedness of the meaning of the words is established. "奧つ鳥(i)鴨着く島に(i)我がゐ寢し(i)妹は忘れじ(i)世のことごとに(i)" (*okitsu tori (i) / kamo doku shima ni (i) / waga ineshi (i) / imo wa wasureji (i) / yo no kotogoto ni (i)*), through the contingent relation of one rhyme throughout, [p. 64] [コマ 39] makes a recurring circular movement repeated infinitely. Again, in an acrostic such as "からごろもきつつなれにしつましあればはるばるきぬるたびをしぞおもふ" (*karagoromo / kitsutsu narenishi / tsuma shi areba / harubaru kinuru / tabi o shi zo omou*), on top of the whole poem, permeated by the groundedness of the meaning of the words, another new meaning is further added doubly through a contingent relation in sound. 〔Tr.: Kuki marks the first syllable of each line, *ka*, *ki*, *tsu*, *ha*, *ta*, with emphasis circles; together they spell *kakitsubata*, the iris.〕 A palindrome is the same poem whether read from the top or read from the bottom, as in "なかきよのとおのねぶりのみなめざめなみのりぶねのおとのよきかな" (*nakakiyo no tōno neburi no mina mezame naminoribune no oto no yoki kana*), and in producing, through circular movement, a special contingency besides the logical necessary relation, it has a character similar to the number 142857 cited earlier and the like.

Mr Terada Torahiko, in *Mangekyō*, says: "As I was taught English and German, step by step, I often came across words of the same meaning with sounds very like Japanese. Though I thought this was probably *chance*, it seemed rather difficult to prove that all of them were *chance* coincidences" (p. 296), and gives examples such as beat and butu, flat and filattai, new and nii, fat and futo, easy and yasasi, clean and kilei, angry and ikari, anchor and ikari, tray and tarai, mattress and musiro. There are of course often cases where what was at first thought to be contingency in sound is resolved into necessity [p. 65]. For example, 神 (*kami*, god), 上 (*kami*, above) and 髮 (*kami*, hair) are all venerable things located towards the head. The wolf (狼, *ōkami*) too is a fearsome "*kami*". These are all various consequents conditioned by a common ground. Among them there exists a logical necessary relation based on groundedness. It goes without saying that positive contingency of ground is not seen in things of this kind.

### 4. Causality and purposiveness

Leibniz's so-called law of sufficient reason, as something that contains both the "ground of knowledge" and the "ground of reality", also has the meaning of the law of causality. This is clear also from the words he uses in the fifth letter addressed to Clarke: "This principle is the principle that requires a sufficient reason in order that a thing exist, an event occur, a truth arise" (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 778). There are, indeed, theorists who think that the distinction between ground of knowledge (reason) and ground of reality (cause) is a lasting acquisition for epistemology, [p. 66] [コマ 40] but against such theorists Émile Meyerson says the following: "Perhaps that is so. But our claim lies in the point that the essence of reasoning consists in more or less consciously setting this distinction aside" (É. Meyerson, *Du Cheminement de la Pensée* I, 1931, p. 55). In any case, insofar as the law of ground is reduced to the law of identity, there should be no objection to thinking that the law of causality too is reduced to the law of identity. Meyerson says: "The law of causality is nothing other than the law of identity applied to the existence of things in time" (É. Meyerson, *Identité et Réalité*, 3. éd., p. 38). What we call the cause with regard to some event is something identical found within another event. That water has arisen as the result of the combination of hydrogen and oxygen means that what are called elements, hydrogen and oxygen, maintain themselves identically within the compound as well. Therefore the causal relation takes as its ideal that it can be expressed by an equation. "Cause equals effect" (*causa aequat effectum*) means this. That is, causality too can be reduced to identity. And if the causal relation is nothing other than a relation of identity, the law of causality should also have the necessity that the law of identity has. We think that if hydrogen [p. 67] and oxygen are combined they necessarily become water. Between the combination of hydrogen and oxygen and water there exists a necessary relation. For hydrogen and oxygen maintain themselves identically within water. There, there is something identical. Consequently, there, there is necessity.

Further, the relation of purpose and means can also be seen as a kind of causal relation in the broad sense. In that sense there is the concept of the "final cause" (*causa finalis*) over against the "efficient cause" (*causa efficiens*). That is, Aristotle saw both "the beginning of motion" (ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κινήσεως) and "that for the sake of which" (τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα) as "causes" (αἰτία) answering the question "why" (διὰ τί) (Aristoteles, *Physica*, II, 3; *Metaphysica*, Δ, 2). Likewise Kant speaks of "two kinds of causality", and calls the causal relation in the narrow sense "real causal connection", and the relation of purpose and means "ideal causal connection" or "causal combination through concepts of reason" (Kant, *Kritik der Urteilskraft*, § 65). Only, in the causal relation and in the relation of purpose and means, the relation is reversed. What comes later as result is thought first as purpose, and what comes first as cause is thought later as means. Since the purpose acts as cause and produces the means as its result, what was originally result becomes cause, and [p. 68] [コマ 41] what was originally cause becomes result. Renouvier says: "The means is a kind of result peculiar to the cause called teleological" (Renouvier, *Traité de Logique générale*, 1912, II, p. 164). Therefore, to borrow Hamelin's words, purposiveness is "determination by the future" (Hamelin, *Essai sur les éléments principaux de la représentation*, 2e éd., p. 332). And since the future, which ought to lack actuality, can take on actuality and act as cause only through the fact of the temporal anticipation of consciousness, purposiveness is, strictly, a concept that has validity only within the range of consciousness. To try to acknowledge this concept as a constitutive principle in the natural world as well, beyond the range of consciousness, there is probably no way other than through the mediation of the concept of the unconscious as what Bergson calls "annulled consciousness" (*conscience annulée*) (Bergson, *L'Évolution créatrice*, 25 éd., p. 156). There is a way of thinking which, on the basis of the fact that conscious acts change through habit into unconscious reflex movements, sees the natural world as the limit at which consciousness has become unconscious through habit. Habit is, so to speak, the differential continuity by which will reaches nature. "The history of habit represents the return of freedom to nature, or rather represents the invasion of the domain of freedom by natural spontaneity." "Thus [p. 69] perhaps the rule of knowledge and foresight appears in the domain of nature," says Ravaisson (Ravaisson, *De l'habitude*, nouvelle éd. 1927, p. 62, 16). Kant explains that genius carries out artistic production half unconsciously, "as nature" (Kant, *Kritik der Urteilskraft*, § 46), and his theory of genius is precisely the bond that joins his aesthetics and his philosophy of nature. Kant, whilst permitting the application of purposiveness in the natural world only as a regulative principle, still hints at the concept that would need to be assumed in order to acknowledge it as a constitutive principle, that is, the concept of the unconscious. In any case, the relation of purpose and means is an inverted causal relation.

Now, the relation of purpose and means takes the form "for the sake of A, B must be done", and shows that there is a necessary relation between purpose and means. This necessity comes, in the end, from the causal relation. The relation of purpose and means, "for the sake of A, B must be done", presupposes the relation of cause and effect, "if B is done, A necessarily arises". Consequently there is necessity there. In general, the realisation of a purpose requires that it come about through the causal relation. The purpose must be brought about as the result of some event. For that, the cause must be taken as means. And between cause and effect there is a necessary relation. [p. 70] [コマ 42] Therefore, when a definite result is willed as purpose, it is necessarily required that a definite cause be willed as means. Of course, it can also be said that between the necessity of the causal relation and the necessity of the relation of purpose and means there is a difference that can be expressed by "must" (*Müssen*) and "ought" (*Sollen*), but in being necessity they are the same. When Aristotle, among the three meanings of necessity he lists, speaks of "that without which the good is not" (τὸ οὗ οὐκ ἄνευ τὸ εὖ) (Aristoteles, *Metaphysica*, Δ. 7. 1072b), this is necessity from the teleological standpoint. When Hegel says that "this pure interplay with itself is necessity unveiled, or posited" and that "this truth of necessity is therefore freedom", he too is speaking of the necessity that the relation of purpose and means has (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, §§ 157, 158). Nicolai Hartmann too says the following about the necessity of the ought in the narrow sense, that is, of the ethical absolute ought. "In the essence of the ought there lie urging, tendency, compulsion. That compulsion holds even when the object of the compulsion is something that cannot be realised. Modally speaking, it can mean nothing other than the necessity of the object. And yet that necessity [p. 71] is one in which the possibility of the object's actualisation is lacking. The peculiarity of such ethical necessity does not lie in its meaning, modally, something new of its own, for example, ultimately, a new Modality. The difference of ethical necessity from theoretical, ontological necessity lies solely in the point that this necessity, without regard to possibility, that is, without regard to the actual conditions of the actuality of the object, posits that object as necessary" (N. Hartmann, *Logische und ontologische Wirklichkeit*, Kantstudien XX, S. 21). In short, causality and purposiveness both have necessity on the basis of identity.

### 5. Causal contingency and teleological contingency

Since contingency was the negation of necessity, over against causal necessity and teleological necessity there should be, as the negation of each, two: *causal contingency* and *teleological contingency*. Causal contingency is the contingent that arises through the lack of causality. Teleological [p. 72] [コマ 43] contingency is the contingent that arises through the lack of purposiveness. And it has already been said that these two, causal contingency and teleological contingency, together with the contingency of ground, are considered the three modes of hypothetical contingency (see pp. 49-50).

Among the words that express chance, *yukurinaku*, *hashinakumo*, *futo* (不圖) and the like, which have a negative word as a moment, all have the basis of their formation in the negation of groundedness, or of causality, or of purposiveness. *Yukurinaku* means "without *en* (緣)", that is, "without *en'yu* (緣由)", and so is probably the negation of groundedness. The *hashi* (端) of *hashinakumo* means the head and start, the thread-end, so it may be seen as negating causality, as in "without cause". *Futo* may be a phonetic substitute in characters, but insofar as it can be thought to be related to *fui to* (不意と), there should be no objection to seeing it as the negation of purposiveness. The Greek αὐτόματον too comes from αὐτό (of itself) and μάτην (without reason), and it too arises on the same foundation as these Japanese words. Further, *futo* may be related to the onomatopoeic sound *futto*. *Futto*, *hyotto*, *hyokkori*, *pokkuri* and the like suggest chance, but *futto*, *hyotto* and *hyokkori* are probably based on the sound of breath or of wind, and *pokkuri* on the sound made in rising from under water to the surface. [p. 73] These are extremely apt as auditory symbols of contingency as hypothetical contingency, that is, as the negation of groundedness, causality and purposiveness. If, incidentally, we try to give a visual symbol of contingency, we can, following La Mettrie, say "like those mushrooms that spring up from one day to the next" (*semblable à ces champignons qui paraissent d'un jour à l'autre*) (La Mettrie, *L'Homme machine*, Paris, 1865, p. 98). In Naruse Mukyoku's "Dialogue on Chance" the metaphor "a frog that jumped out from under the veranda" is used (*Dialogue on Chance*, pp. 433, 436). In Greece, when chance, that is, Tychē (τύχη), was worshipped as a goddess, she was given as her attributes a rolling "ball" or "wheel", or a fluttering "feather". The visual symbol of contingency as one of the modal categories is not a matter in which symbolic logic, either, should lack all interest. That I later chose )( as the sign of contingency is nothing other than the symbolisation of contingency as "the Encounter of two independent elements" (獨立なる二元の邂逅) (see p. 196). 〔Tr.: Kuki's sign is printed as two arcs meeting at their midpoints, rotated in the vertical setting; )( is an approximation. Encounter is 邂逅 (*kaikō*), the word of Kuki's definition of contingency (p. 148). 遭遇 (*sōgū*), which he also uses, and which glosses Cournot's *rencontre* (pp. 10, 179), is "encounter" in lower case; see the Translator's Note.〕 Lewis (C. I. Lewis, *A Survey of Symbolic Logic*, 1918, p. 293, 312) (see p. 193) calls the property common to apodicticity (see p. 198), that is, to impossibility (~) and necessity (~-), "strict implication" (*Strict Implication*), and calls the property common to assertoricity, that is, to truth ( ) and falsity (-), "material implication" (*Material Implication*), [p. 74] [コマ 44] and calls the property common to problematicity, that is, to possibility (-~) and contingency (-~-), "consistency" (*Consistency*); to express "strict implication" he uses the sign ⥽, to express "material implication" he uses the sign ⊃, and to express "consistency" he uses the sign ○. Consistency means the property by which A and B can stand together, and refers to the case in which it is possible that something is A and also possible that it is not A (A is contingent). p ○ q expresses -~p (p is possible) or -~-p (it is possible that not p = p is contingent). That the relation between p and q is expressed by ○ shows that whether it will turn out p or q is left as a problem, undetermined. Whether the "ball" symbolised by ○ will roll towards p or towards q is entirely uncertain. This is entirely different in character from "material implication" being expressed by the shape of a hammer, ⊃, and "strict implication" by the shape of an anchor, ⥽. That ○ has thus become the symbol of problematicity in symbolic logic rests on exactly the same psychology by which the Greeks chose the "ball" as an attribute of the goddess Tychē.

Of the three modes of hypothetical contingency, the contingency of ground, as something concerning the so-called "ground of knowledge", [p. 75] belonged purely to the range of logic. By contrast, causal contingency and teleological contingency, as concerning the "ground of reality", leave the abstract range of logic and arise in the empirical domain of the philosophy of nature and the philosophy of mind. Therefore causal contingency and teleological contingency can be called together *empirical contingency*.

In general, the mechanistic view, in its thoroughgoing form, recognises only causal necessity. Consequently there is no room for the existence of causal contingency. But it denies teleological necessity, and as a result acknowledges teleological contingency. The world view of natural science represents this tendency. By contrast, when the teleological view takes a thoroughgoing form, it tries to explain everything solely by teleological necessity. Consequently teleological contingency does not exist. Instead, as a result of the negation of causal necessity, causal contingency can exist. When Christian theology explains everything by the will of God, its recognition of the existence of miracles, which mean causal contingency, rests on this relation. In this sense causal necessity readily combines with teleological contingency, and teleological necessity readily combines with causal contingency. Paradoxical expressions such as "the necessity of chance" rest on the possibility of such a combination. [p. 76] [コマ 45] For example, when Plato says "by chance (τύχη), out of necessity (ἀνάγκη)" (Platon, *Leges*, X, 889), when Leibniz says "trying to attribute everything to the necessity of matter or to some chance" (Leibniz, *Discours de métaphysique*, § 19), and when Engels says "with the necessity immanent in chance" (*Dialectics of Nature*, vol. 2, Iwanami Bunko, p. 28), they refer to the combination of causal necessity and teleological contingency. By contrast, when Thomas Aquinas says "God willed that it should happen contingently" (Thomas Aquinas, *Summa theologiae* I. 19. 8c) or "nothing is so contingent that it does not have in itself something necessary" (Ib., I. 86. 3c), he is thinking of the combination of teleological necessity and causal contingency. Thomas's so-called "law of necessity or contingency" (*lex necessitatis vel contingentiae*) advocates the combination of causal contingency as freedom and teleological necessity as providence. Again, when Lange says in the *History of Materialism*, "There is nothing else that contradicts each other so completely as chance and necessity. Nevertheless there is nothing else that is so often confused as the two" (Lange, *Geschichte des Materialismus*, I. S. 13), this too is because the combination of causal necessity and teleological contingency on one side, and of teleological necessity and causal contingency [p. 77] on the other, arises easily. Let us provisionally name this way of combining "heterogeneous combination". But heterogeneous combination is not the only possible way of combining. There are also cases in which causal necessity and teleological necessity combine to form a philosophy that emphasises necessity, and in which causal contingency and teleological contingency combine to constitute a philosophy that stresses contingency. The Stoics taught a thoroughgoing determinism that starts from teleological necessity and arrives at causal necessity (see p. 292), and Epicurus advocated a univocal indeterminism that sets out from causal contingency and ends in teleological contingency (see pp. 115-116, 127). This way of combining can be named "homogeneous combination".

![Figure 1](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-01-p077.png)

**Figure 1.** Causal and teleological necessity and contingency: homogeneous combination runs vertically, heterogeneous combination horizontally (1948, p. 77). Labels: 因果的必然 causal necessity; 目的々偶然 teleological contingency; 目的々必然 teleological necessity; 因果的偶然 causal contingency; 必然性 necessity; 偶然性 contingency; 因果性 causality; 目的性 purposiveness. Corner labels are set right to left.

```
causal necessity ----------------------- teleological contingency
       |  \                                       /  |
       |     \  causality          purposiveness  /     |
       |        \                         /        |
  necessity        \                 /       contingency
       |              \         /              |
       |                  \ /                  |
       |                  / \                  |
       |              /         \              |
       |          /                 \          |
       |      /                         \      |
teleological necessity ----------------- causal contingency
```

〔Tr.: Kuki's figure on p. 77: a square with causal necessity at top left, teleological contingency at top right, teleological necessity at bottom left and causal contingency at bottom right; the left side is labelled necessity and the right side contingency; the diagonal from causal necessity to causal contingency is labelled causality, the diagonal from teleological contingency to teleological necessity purposiveness. In the original it stands above the sentence that refers to it.〕

Let us express these relations by a figure like the one above 〔Figure 1〕. In this figure, homogeneous combination (the combination of causal necessity and teleological necessity, the combination of causal contingency and teleological contingency) takes place in the vertical direction, and heterogeneous combination (the combination of causal necessity and teleological contingency, the combination of teleological necessity and causal contingency) [p. 78] [コマ 46] takes place in the horizontal direction.

Causal contingency and teleological contingency were in Greece originally both expressed by the word τύχη. Therefore, both when Democritus attacks causal contingency from the standpoint of the mechanistic view and when Plato refutes teleological contingency from the standpoint of the teleological view, both sides use the same word, τύχη. When Democritus says "Human beings have made the idol of chance (τύχη) as an excuse for their own perplexity" (Diels, *Fragmente der Vorsokratiker* II. Demokritos Fr. 119) or "Chance (τύχη) is fond of giving, but cannot be relied upon. Nature (φύσις), by contrast, is sure" (Ib., Fr. 176), the chance here should be understood as causal contingency, opposed to nature, that is, opposed to causal necessity. By contrast, when Plato, criticising the atomists, says "According to what they say, it seems that nature (φύσις) and chance (τύχη) made the greatest and most beautiful things, and art made the lesser things" (Platon, *Leges*, X, 889), or "Not through mind. Nor through any god. Nor through art. But through nature (φύσις) and chance (τύχη)" (Ib.), the chance here [p. 79] is properly understood as teleological contingency in heterogeneous combination with the causal necessity called nature.

Leibniz distinguished *contingence* and *hasard* within chance (Leibniz, *Opera philosophica*, ed. Erdmann, p. 763). *Contingence* is treated as forming one group with freedom and spontaneity, so there is no objection to seeing it as *causal contingency* in heterogeneous combination with teleological necessity. And *hasard* is treated as of the same kind as compulsion and absolute necessity, so it may be seen as *teleological contingency* in heterogeneous combination with causal necessity. In modern times too, Boutroux, following Leibniz, calls causal contingency *contingence* and teleological contingency *hasard*. For example, where, taking the standpoint of opponents of his own theory, he raises the question whether causal contingency is not led into teleological contingency, he says "If the principle of *contingence* is not *hasard*, what in the world can it be?" (Boutroux, *De la contingence des lois de la nature*, 9e éd., p. 140). And as his answer to this he says "If *contingence* did not to some degree reign in the series of determining causes, *hasard* would reign in the series of final causes" (Ib., p. 143).

[p. 80] [コマ 47] However, such usage is not necessarily followed in general. For example, Hamelin calls causal contingency and teleological contingency taken together *hasard*, and calls causal contingency "chance through the non-existence of causality (*hasard par néant de causalité*)" and teleological contingency "chance through the non-existence of purposiveness (*hasard par néant de finalité*)" (Hamelin, *Essai sur les éléments principaux de la représentation*, 2e éd., p. 324, 329). Bergson likewise calls both "the absence of efficient cause" (*absence de cause efficiente*) and "the absence of final cause" (*absence de cause finale*) *hasard* (Bergson, *L'Évolution créatrice*, 25 éd., p. 254-255). Windelband too calls the two together *Zufall*, and for each uses the terms *kausaler Zufall* and *teleologischer Zufall* (Windelband, *Die Lehren vom Zufall*, S. 65).

Although the distinction between causal contingency and teleological contingency is very important, as Bergson too points out, it shows "a peculiar oscillation of the mind" (*le singulier ballottement de l'esprit*), passing from one to the other (l. c. p. 255). Therefore, where the distinction between the two is not expressed in language, it is especially important to recognise the distinction with great care. For example, Lange says: "Against the teleologists, it is only in order to escape the fetters of purpose [p. 81] that we assert the contingency of events. But as soon as the law of sufficient reason becomes the problem, we at once give up that same contingency again" (Lange, *Geschichte d. Materialismus*, I, S. 14). He says "the same contingency" (*dieselbe Zufälligkeit*), but in fact it is not the same. The former is teleological contingency, the latter is causal contingency. Again, for example, Jaspers says: "Human beings seek to escape alternately from the one by means of the other. By the thought of necessity they try to escape from arbitrary chance, and by the thought of the possibility and opportunity of chance they try to escape from merciless necessity" (Jaspers, *Philosophie*, II, S. 217). His meaning is that by the thought of teleological necessity they try to escape from teleological contingency, and by the thought of causal contingency they try to escape from causal necessity. In other words, by the thought of the heterogeneous combination of teleological necessity and causal contingency they try to escape from the threat of the heterogeneous combination of causal necessity and teleological contingency.

As already said, causal contingency arises through the negation of causality, and teleological contingency arises through the negation of purposiveness. And in causal contingency there are the case in which, with regard to one event, the non-existence of causality is witnessed *negatively*, and the case in which, between two or more events, [p. 82] [コマ 48] the existence of a relation other than causality is witnessed *positively*; but in either case there is no difference in the point that it is the negation of causality. Likewise in teleological contingency there are the case in which, with regard to one event, the non-existence of purpose is grasped *negatively*, and the case in which, between two or more events, the existence of a relation other than purpose is grasped *positively*, and in either case they have in common the negation of purposiveness. In short, there exist four kinds: negative causal contingency, positive causal contingency, negative teleological contingency and positive teleological contingency. Adding the two kinds treated earlier, negative contingency of ground and positive contingency of ground, six kinds of contingency are to be considered in hypothetical contingency.

![Figure 2](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-02-p082.png)

**Figure 2.** The six kinds of hypothetical contingency (1948, p. 82). Labels: 假說的偶然 hypothetical contingency; 理由的偶然 contingency of ground; 經驗的偶然 empirical contingency; 目的々偶然 teleological contingency; 因果的偶然 causal contingency; 積極的 positive; 消極的 negative.

```
hypothetical contingency
├── contingency of ground
│     ├── negative
│     └── positive
└── empirical contingency
      ├── causal contingency
      │     ├── negative
      │     └── positive
      └── teleological contingency
            ├── negative
            └── positive
```

[p. 83] We try, in the elucidation of contingency, to proceed step by step from the subjective to the objective. First of all we treated categorical contingency, which has its basis in the logical structure of general concept and mark. Next we treated the contingency of ground, which likewise belongs to the purely logical dimension. Now, as we leave the logical domain and turn to the elucidation of empirical contingency, it must be the natural order to begin with teleological contingency, which is comparatively subjective, and to move afterwards to causal contingency.

### 6. Negative teleological contingency

Negative teleological contingency grasps the non-existence of purpose negatively, and within it there are further two cases: the contingent as *purposelessness* and the contingent as *counter-purposiveness*. The contingent as purposelessness is the case in which purposiveness is simply negated; the contingent as counter-purposiveness is the case in which, while the purpose that ought to be realised is affirmed, the non-realisation of that purpose is witnessed in a particular instance.

[p. 84] [コマ 49] What belongs to the first case is, for example, the case in which teleological contingency is asserted of the whole universe as the other half of mechanistic determinism. In this case the idea of teleological contingency is applied across the whole of existence through heterogeneous combination with causal necessity. It appears, for example, in La Mettrie's *L'Homme machine*. "Man has perhaps been thrown *by chance* onto some point or other of the surface of the earth. ... Now the pantheist asks a question. Is chance, then, so great a geometer that it varies at its own pleasure, in this way, the many works of which chance is thought to be the author, and yet such variety cannot prevent them from attaining the same end? ... This is certainly all the most favourable things that can be said for the existence of a God. ... But the atheist can oppose to it things equally strong and absolutely contrary. For if we listen further to what the natural scientists say, they will say this. The same cause that, in the hands of the chemist, through various chance mixtures, made the first mirror, made, in the hands of nature, clear water, and this serves merely a nameless shepherdess. ... That mirrors and water are not made so that people can reflect their figures in them [p. 85] is no different from all other bodies with smooth surfaces that have the same property. The eye is in fact a kind of wall mirror in which the soul can view the figures of objects just as they are presented by those bodies, but it has not been proved that this organ was in fact made specially for this viewing, nor has it been proved that it was specially placed in the eye socket. ... That the eye sees is only because it is organised as it now is and placed in such a place as it now is, and once we suppose, for the production and development of the human body, the same laws of motion that nature follows, it cannot be that this marvellous organ should have another organisation and be placed in another place" (La Mettrie, *L'Homme machine*, trans. Sugi Toshio, Iwanami Bunko, pp. 100-104). In this case, what is called chance is the first case of negative teleological contingency, means the contingent as purposelessness, and simply negates purposiveness. The same meaning of chance also appears in the following two examples, but as a standpoint it is of course, contrary to La Mettrie, the standpoint that denies teleological contingency. "To think of something like a soul of plants, as Fechner does, may be fanciful. But unless we think, in some sense, [p. 86] [コマ 50] of something conscious, for example something like an unconscious consciousness, we can only think of a living thing as a *chance* combination of physical forces, and cannot think of anything such as its own existence" (Nishida Kitarō, *Fundamental Problems of Philosophy*, p. 134). "The skin in every part is something that does not develop sufficiently until about three months after birth have passed, but the tactile sense of the skin and mucous membrane in the region of the lips alone is an exception, and already shows its function one day after birth. That is, as soon as an infant is born it searches for the mother's breast, which is to supply the sole nourishment of life, and thereby, in the place important for maintaining its own life, the sense of touch shows an early and excellent development. This is truly not *chance*" (Nagai Hisomu, *The Boundary between Biology and Philosophy*, p. 231).

Next, the second case is negative teleological contingency in the sense of the contingent as counter-purposiveness, that is, in which a fact contrary to a purpose that ought to be realised is witnessed; a person with severe intellectual disability is one example of this. If the existence of thinking activity means, for a human being, a purposiveness that ought to be realised, then such a person, since this means the non-existence of that thinking activity, is thought to be something contingent. The phenomenon of a flower becoming double can also be seen as teleological contingency. Where the function of the flower is taken to be that which governs the reproduction of the plant body, such a thing as stamens changing into petals [p. 87] is contrary to the purpose, so in botany the double flower is regarded as a malformation, that is, a contingent phenomenon. Again, where having three leaves is regarded as one of the purposes that ought to be realised for clover, the four-leaved clover lacks the realisation of the purpose, and so is something contingent. For a snake with one body, realising one head is purposive, so a snake with two heads on one body is a counter-purposive, that is, contingent, existence. Aristotle called this kind of teleological contingency "contrary to nature" (παρὰ φύσιν) (Aristoteles, *Physica*, II, 6, 197b), and Hegel attributed it to "the impotence of nature" (*Ohnmacht der Natur*) (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 250). And behind the declaration that something is contrary to nature there is the teleological view that "nature makes nothing without reason (μάτην)" (Aristoteles, *De caelo*, I. 4, 271a). Again, in order to judge nature impotent, one has in advance imposed on nature the type, as conceptual determination, as the purpose of the completion of the particular.

This kind of "contrary to nature" sometimes coincides with what, within categorical contingency, we specially called exceptional contingency (see Chapter 1, section 5). This second mode of negative teleological contingency arises where the general concept in categoricity is regarded as a purpose and the realisation of the purpose is demanded. [p. 88] [コマ 51] Because Plato's Idea, as "the Idea of the Good", is valid in the character of an ought, the actual exposes its own "impotence" by presenting exceptions "contrary to nature". Categoricity and purposiveness stand, after all, in an inseparable inner relation. That the general concept can be seen as something valid in itself as a purpose is clear in the light of its universality as reflective judgement, as a task. Boutroux too says: "The logical order, that is, the operation that subordinates facts to concepts, perhaps conceals the spontaneous activity of an inner reason or of final causes. The concept would be no more than the logical sign of the final cause. Thus individuals would have their reason for existence in the species" (Boutroux, *De la contingence des lois de la nature*, 9 éd., p. 168).

### 7. Positive teleological contingency

Positive teleological contingency is the case in which the existence of a relation other than purpose between two, or more than two, events is positively witnessed. It is, for example, a case such as that in which, while one is digging [p. 89] a hole in order to plant a tree, treasure comes out of the ground. This example is one that has often been cited as an example of chance ever since Aristotle gave it in Book V of the *Metaphysics* (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ 30, 1025a) and Boethius later commented on it (Boethius, De interpretatione, Ed. II. lib. III. Migne p. 491, 506). Because planting the tree was the purpose and obtaining treasure was not included in the purpose, obtaining the treasure is called chance. There is, on the one hand, the series of actions in which the gardener digs the ground and plants the tree, and, on the other, the series of actions in which a thief or someone of the kind hid treasure in the ground, and between these two series, each independent, some positive relation other than purposiveness has been established. Again, for example, it is said that the seventeenth-century chemist Brand, trying in accordance with alchemy to obtain a substance that would turn silver into gold, evaporated urine and heated it strongly, and as a result obtained an element that emits light; of this it is said that he discovered phosphorus by chance. Here too it is called chance because obtaining phosphorus was not included in the purpose. In this case too there is, on the one hand, a series of actions as the carrying out of an alchemical design, and, on the other, a chemical series in which phosphorus was liberated from phosphate. And between these two series another relation, which is not purposiveness, has been positively established.

[p. 90] [コマ 52] In general, the greater part of what is called chance in everyday life is this positive teleological contingency. Let us give two or three examples. "On the fifteenth of December, *by chance*, Major-General Kondratenko, the enemy's commander of the land-front defence, was killed in action at the North Fort of East Jiguanshan. Major-General Kondratenko was, it seems, not only excellent in the tactics of fortress defence but also enjoyed very great trust, and there had been a rumour from before that, because Kondratenko was in charge, the garrison could fight with confidence; we did not know whether that major-general had come to the North Fort of East Jiguanshan on the fifteenth of December or not, but our twenty-eight-centimetre shell *by chance* struck there and he was killed" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 4 March 1935). Our shells had as their purpose the striking of the enemy, but they did not take Major-General Kondratenko in particular as their target; that the shell was fired towards the North Fort of East Jiguanshan and that the same major-general came to that fort on that day are two independent series, and so the death in action of the same major-general is called chance. Again: "Mr Masuda Jirō, president of Shōwa Electric Power, was summoned to the Tokyo District Court on the morning of the twenty-sixth and, after undergoing a lengthy examination by Prosecutor Kanazawa, further underwent detention questioning by Preliminary Judge Yamaguchi, and at six in the afternoon, on a charge of official corruption, he was committed to Ichigaya Prison under compulsory measures prior to [p. 91] indictment. It is reported that, when he was first examined by Prosecutor Nakajima, in charge of the Shōden case, as a witness in the Shōden scandal that the Metropolitan Police Board and the Public Prosecutor's Office had for some time been investigating, his deep involvement in the decorations-buying affair was *by chance* grasped by the same prosecutor, and for that reason he was sent on anew to the examination room of Prosecutor Kanazawa, in charge of the decorations-buying affair" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 27 September 1929). The prosecutor who first examined him had as his sole purpose the investigation of the Shōden case. Therefore the discovery concerning the decorations-buying affair, as something wholly outside the purpose, is called chance. The investigation of the Shōden case on the one hand and the investigation of the decorations-buying affair on the other formed two entirely independent teleological series. Between these two series a new positive relation, which had not at all been aimed at, was established. To give one more example: the entertainment broadcasts in the radio programme of the Tokyo Broadcasting Station on 10 September 1934 were all relays from the Osaka Broadcasting Station, and this was discussed in that day's newspaper as "this programme born of chance". First the facts are stated. "Today, beginning with the afternoon 'Solo Song and Orchestral Music', and including the evening 'Gidayū' and 'Rakugo', the entertainment broadcasts are all relays from Osaka, so that it takes the form of 'AK literary and arts department closed today'; the AK authorities say that this programme was arranged entirely *by chance*, [p. 92] [コマ 53] but since the programme arrangement committee decided it thus, nothing can be done about it." Next there is an explanation of how it came about. "Today's entertainment broadcasting is, for AK, a programme arrangement wholly without precedent; why was such an irregular thing born? Since the reorganisation of the Broadcasting Corporation, the programmes of each station are decided, one month's worth half a month ahead, at the programme arrangement meeting held once a month; when today's programme was decided, the gidayū from eight in the evening was scheduled as a relay broadcast from the Bunrakuza, and because the broadcasting time this required was uncertain, the next entertainment item was entrusted to BK; further, since light music was wanted for the afternoon, 'Solo Song and Orchestral Music' was put in, and it is said that this *by chance* originated from BK. That is, the arrangement committee says that it is not that it does not consider the originating station, but because it considers chiefly the type of broadcast, this result came about; but voices of criticism have been raised that this is a blunder of the programme arrangement meeting." That is, because it is said that "for a regional station it might be another matter, but this is as if AK had entirely lost face as the parent station", the secretary-general of the programme arrangement meeting excuses himself: "That the entertainment all ended up originating from BK was entirely a result of *chance*, and of course we wish to take care [p. 93] that such a thing does not happen again" (*Tōkyō Asahi Shinbun*, 10 September 1934). According to the explanation, that the evening entertainment broadcasts "Gidayū" and "Rakugo" are both BK is a matter entrusted to BK because of the uncertainty of the broadcasting time they required, and there is no chance whatever in it. What is called chance is that the afternoon entertainment broadcast "Solo Song and Orchestral Music" was also BK. Why is it chance? Because in the arrangement committee, with regard to the afternoon entertainment broadcast, only the type of broadcast had been taken into the series of purpose. Adopting something originating from BK was not included in the purpose. Because the teleological series that decided the evening entertainment programme as originating from BK and the teleological series that decided the afternoon entertainment programme as "Solo Song and Orchestral Music", although they were decisions of the same arrangement committee, constituted two independent series, an unexpected chance manifested itself.

〔Tr.: Kuki's Shōwa-era newspaper dates are converted to the Western calendar. Kuki writes 買勳事件, "decorations-buying affair"; the 1929 scandal is usually called 賣勳事件, the sale of decorations. 意圖 is rendered "design" to keep it apart from 故意, "intention".〕

### 8. Purpose without purpose

As to what is positively witnessed in positive teleological contingency, [p. 94] [コマ 54] it is something that, although it was not set up as a purpose in that case, is nonetheless such as could be a purpose. In positive teleological contingency, in particular, a kind of "purpose without purpose" usually casts a strong shadow. To borrow Bergson's words, it presents the appearance of a "good genius" (un bon génie) or an "evil genius" (un mauvais génie) being at work there (Bergson, L'Évolution créatrice, 25 éd., p. 254; Les deux sources de la morale et de la religion, 7 éd., p. 152-157). To speak of the death in action of Major-General Kondratenko, for our army it seems like a purpose aimed at by a good genius, and for the Russian army like a purpose aimed at by an evil genius. And to speak of the example of the broadcast programme, for the Tokyo Broadcasting Station it is thought of as like the purposive act of an evil genius, and for the Osaka Broadcasting Station as like the purposive act of a good genius. Of course, in negative teleological contingency too, insofar as the non-existence of purpose is asserted, there is some "purpose without purpose", and over against it the condition of being "without purpose", the non-existence of purpose, is especially asserted. However, insofar as in that case all the emphasis is placed on grasping the non-existence of purpose itself, teleological contingency appears in negativity. By contrast, in positive teleological contingency the existence of "purpose without purpose" in particular is positively emphasised [p. 95] and witnessed. In other words, in the two-limbed structure "purposeless / purpose", negative teleological contingency especially emphasises the upper limb, and positive teleological contingency places its stress rather on the lower limb. 〔Tr.: Kuki writes 目的なき ("purposeless") and 目的 ("purpose") joined by a long dash; the slash replaces his dash. The upper limb (上肢) is "purposeless", the lower limb (下肢) "purpose". 目的々行爲 is rendered "purposive act".〕

There was a newspaper article like the following. "Yamada Isuzu, star of the First Film Company, who, invited by the captain of the Isuzu of the Invincible Fleet, visited the cruiser Isuzu, in port at Osaka, on the nineteenth and was moved at being entertained by Captain Yamada and the young officers, lent her ear at the radio microphone to the gallant scene of Captain Yamada of the Isuzu commanding the landing force of the First Fleet at the naval review at the Osaka Castle East parade ground on the twentieth; on the afternoon of the same day she signed and sent to Captain Yamada of the Isuzu a letter of thanks for the hospitality she had received and the photograph of the commemorative picture taken with Captain Yamada on board, and she has decided to hang an enlargement of this commemorative photograph in her home and long commemorate this strange tie" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, Kyoto edition, 21 October 1934). In this case, the motive for which Captain Yamada was appointed captain of the cruiser Isuzu did not aim at the combination of Yamada and Isuzu. Yet through the combination of Yamada and Isuzu the same name as the film actress Yamada Isuzu appeared. It is no more than a teleological contingency, but it has an all too striking "purpose without purpose". If [p. 96] [コマ 55] there had been no actress called Yamada Isuzu, the contingency that Captain Yamada is the captain of the Isuzu would have almost no resonance. Through the existence of the actress Yamada Isuzu a resonance box was provided, and it gave forth a powerful sound. We can say that the essence of positive teleological contingency lies in grasping "purpose without purpose" by positively emphasising it.

As a result of the emphasis on "purpose without purpose", chance comes to present an aspect as if it were not chance, and to take on an intentional structure of self-negation. "With the rise of the question of a silver loan for the purpose of relieving the price of silver in America, there being a faction at home that responded to it, the situation fell into one in which nothing could be done about the wavering of the plan for reforming the currency system through the import of silver. That at this time, at the urging of Mr Rajchman, the invitation of Mr Salter, head of the economic and financial section, and Mr Haas, head of the communications section, came about, it would not necessarily be arbitrary to see as having come from a deep consideration to prevent a split in the Nanjing government and to preserve unity in the loan plan. That two days after Mr Haas entered the capital, and two days before Mr Salter's arrival, the fall of Mr Hu Hanmin, a sympathiser of the silver loan, came about was an event that was *by chance and yet not by chance*" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 12 March 1931). In Mushanokōji Saneatsu's *His Sister* [p. 97] there is also the line: "I have a feeling that my going to your place that day *was not by chance*" (Act III). "By chance and yet not by chance" or "cannot be thought chance" are forms in which, through excessive emphasis on the lower limb of "purposeless / purpose", the upper limb has almost lost its resonance.

The psychologist Henri Piéron, in an essay entitled "Essay on chance: the psychology of a concept" (Henri Piéron, Essai sur le hasard: la psychologie d'un concept), stresses this aspect of contingency in particular. According to him, if one is passing along a lakeside and there is a boat there, one does not especially call it chance. Only if one had, at that instant, a strong desire to row a boat for pleasure does one call it a "happy chance". If one is walking along the road and a cat crosses in front, one does not especially treat it as chance. If a cyclist, just as he tried to avoid a car, ran into a cat and ran it over and killed it, then for the first time he hates the "unhappy chance" that placed the cat in the road. Piéron thus holds that in chance "subjective interest" (intérêt subjectif) forms an important moment, and emphasises the "subjective side" (côté subjectif) or the "human element" (élément humain) of the phenomenon of chance; as the theory of a psychologist it is of interest [p. 98] [コマ 56] (Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale, 1902, p. 688-691).

Further, as something belonging to the psychological interpretation of chance, there is the idea that chance is that in which a minute difference in the cause brings about a large difference in the effect. For example, Renan says "chance is what does not have a moral cause proportioned to the effect" (ce qui n'a pas de cause morale proportionnée à l'effet) (Ernest Renan, L'Avenir de la Science, p. 24), and Poincaré too says concerning chance: "the difference in the cause is imperceptible, and the difference in the effect is for me of very great importance" (La différence dans la cause est imperceptible, et la différence dans l'effet est pour moi de la plus haute importance) (H. Poincaré, Science et Méthode, p. 71). When we think the effect is large in comparison with the cause, the standard of that evaluation lies mainly in purposiveness, and so the so-called "grave effect" is, in the end, often nothing other than "purpose without purpose". This is clear also in the light of the fact that, as examples of grave effects, Renan gives "Gustavus Adolphus struck by a cannonball at Lützen" (l. c. p. 494) and Poincaré gives "a terrible catastrophe" (l. c. p. 68). It is also for this reason that Renan says "moral" in particular, and Poincaré says "for me". 〔Tr.: 精神的, rendered "moral" in line with Renan's *morale*, means "mental" or "spiritual". The page number for Poincaré's "terrible catastrophe" is hard to read in the scan: 68 or 69.〕

### 9. Intention and chance

[p. 99] When, in positive teleological contingency, the emphasis on the lower limb of "purposeless / purpose" reaches an extreme, cases also arise in which, as a practical matter, it is hard to distinguish whether something is *chance* or *intention*. "The preliminary Anglo-Japanese negotiations between Ambassador Matsudaira and the British Prime Minister Mr MacDonald held a further, third meeting on the twenty-eighth, but . . . meanwhile in America, in the preliminary Japanese-American negotiations being conducted between Mr Stimson and Ambassador to the United States Debuchi, Mr Stimson too, like the British Prime Minister, has presented to Ambassador Debuchi in writing a proposed solution as his private plan, and, whether by *intention* or by *chance*, it appears that, as with Britain, he asks for an explicit statement of the number of ships in place of Japan's seventy per cent demand" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 2 December 1929). In Natsume Sōseki's *Kokoro* too there are the words: "The mistress very seldom went out. Even on the rare occasions when she was away from the house, she never went off leaving the young lady and me alone together. Whether that too was *chance* or *intention*, I do not know" (Part III, 14) [p. 100] [コマ 57].

In general, distinguishing intention from chance, as what makes clear where legal responsibility lies, has decisive meaning in the constitution of a crime. In the "Announcement to Kang" chapter of the *Book of Documents* there are the words: "The king said: Oh, Feng! Be reverent and clear in your punishments. If a man has a small crime which is not a mischance but is carried through to the end, doing of himself what is unlawful, and doing it so, then though his crime is small he must be put to death. If a man has a great crime which is not carried through to the end but is only a mischance and misfortune, happening by chance, and he has fully declared his guilt, then he is not to be put to death"; here "適" (*tamatama*), that is, chance, stands opposed to "終", that is, carrying out with intention, and is nothing other than teleological contingency. 〔Tr.: Kuki glosses 適 with the reading たまたま (*tamatama*, "by chance") and takes 終 to mean carrying out with intention.〕 The contingency that becomes a problem in law in particular is positive teleological contingency. What fully constitutes a crime is intention as the will to the crime, that is, criminal intent. Article 38 of the Criminal Code, saying "An act done without the intention of committing a crime is not punished", constitutes crime in the eminent sense only in the domain of teleological necessity. So-called "negligence" is positive teleological contingency. Concerning negligence there is also the explanation: "Negligence has inattention as its core. In this respect it must be distinguished from the fact of chance (*force majeure*)" (Makino Eiichi, *Revised Japanese Criminal Law*, p. 178). But the concept of chance is a higher concept that can include extensionally both a certain kind of negligence and *force majeure*. There is no reason why chance must be interpreted in a particularly narrow sense and identified [p. 101] with *force majeure*. The difference between negligence and *force majeure* is that the former is the case in which the chance could have been removed, the latter the case in which the chance could not have been removed. When chance is seen quantitatively, the contingency in negligence is small and the contingency in *force majeure* is large. The difference between the two lies in a difference in the degree of contingency (see pp. 212-213). When the bullet a hunter fired does not hit the deer but hits a woodcutter who was nearby, there is of course no *force majeure* there, but there is inattention. Negligence is constituted where, through inattention, the facts of the crime were not recognised. However, even if it is not *force majeure*, one cannot say that it is not chance. The teleological necessity of the hunter's activity of will connects the hunter and the deer. That the bullet unexpectedly hit the woodcutter constitutes, for the teleological necessity, a positive teleological contingency. That the proviso to the first paragraph of Article 38 of the Criminal Code, "Provided that this does not apply where the law has special provisions", lays down that negligence is punished in cases of negligent homicide and injury and others, is because, with regard to an unfortunate chance, it emphasises the point that the chance could have been removed, the point that the quantity of contingency is minute, and asks for responsibility arising from inattention. The cases in which positive teleological contingency means *force majeure* and consequently has [p. 102] [コマ 58] no relation whatever to crime are cases such as the following. "Where, because a chance circumstance that could not be foreseen has newly been added, a chance causal connection has been formed, the result cannot, in criminal law, be attributed to the activity of will in question. For example, in cases such as that in which a person who has suffered a slight wound is crushed to death by a structure that collapsed in a storm while being carried to hospital for treatment, or is burned to death in a fire while in hospital, it cannot be recognised that there is a causal relation in criminal law between his act of injuring and the death by burning" (Motoji Shinkuma, *Japanese Criminal Law: General Part*, 43rd ed., p. 306). When A has intentionally inflicted violence and injury on B, that B was crushed to death or burned to death by a natural disaster while being carried to hospital or while in hospital constitutes, for the teleological necessity of A's criminal activity of will, a positive teleological contingency, as a "concurring chance circumstance". "The concurrence of chance circumstances" and "negligence" either have no relation whatever to crime or merely receive punishment exceptionally as so-called crimes of negligence. This is because it is only through positive teleological contingency that whether or not a crime is constituted barely becomes a problem. By contrast, when a crime is willed on the horizon of teleological necessity, but the completion of the crime is prevented by a chance obstacle, it is natural that, as an "attempt frustrated by an obstacle", it should in principle be treated as equivalent [p. 103] to the completed offence. "When the completion of a crime has not been accomplished because of a chance circumstance, the court has the power, according to the facts and the manner of the act, to apply the sanction laid down for the completed offence" ("Italian" draft of 1921, Article 16) (Makino Eiichi, *Revised Japanese Criminal Law*, pp. 277-278). It is clear that an event that happens to act as an obstacle is a positive teleological contingency with respect to the teleological necessity of the criminal will.
