For that reason, crimes of this kind do not necessarily receive a mitigation of punishment.

That the chance which becomes a problem in law is positive teleological contingency is clear also when we look at the theory of crimes concerning gambling and lotteries. Under Article 185 of the Criminal Code "a person who has engaged in gaming or betting with property on a chance winning or losing" is punished, and "the reason for punishing acts of gambling and lotteries is that, by letting the speculative spirit run unchecked in public morals, they paralyse sound economic thinking and, at worst, threaten to obstruct the functioning of the national economy. The reason such acts are deemed unlawful is that, whereas the gain and loss of property ought in general to have a proper cause, taking a mere chance circumstance as the cause runs counter to the idea of justice in economic society" [p. 104] [コマ 59] (Ono Seiichirō, *Lectures on Criminal Law*, p. 460). It is insofar as gambling and lotteries constitute a positive teleological contingency with respect to the teleological necessity of economic activity of will that the law forbids them as harming the functioning of the national economy. The concept of "chance winning or losing" is first made capable of radical understanding by the concept of disjunctive contingency, to be discussed later (see Chapter 3, section 8), but it is no harm to say that, in the domain of the law's concern, the concept of positive teleological contingency suffices. In short, positive teleological contingency is the dimension to which chance as spoken of in law belongs, and through its "purpose without purpose" it is sometimes placed in an outward proximity to "intention".

〔Tr.: 過失 is rendered "negligence", 不可抗力 "*force majeure*", 犯意 "criminal intent", 障礙未遂 "attempt frustrated by an obstacle", 既遂 "completed offence", 輸贏 "winning or losing". The legal quotations are translated from Kuki's Japanese.〕

### 10. Automaton and tychē

We have stated that, through the mediation of "purpose without purpose", positive teleological contingency can take even a form that is hard to distinguish from "intention". Aristotle's distinction between "*automaton*" (αὐτόματον) and "*tychē*" (τύχη) too can be understood from this point.

[p. 105] Plato did not yet make this distinction. He uses "by *tychē*" (τύχῃ) and "by *automaton*" (ἀπὸ τοῦ αὐτομάτου) in exactly the same meaning. For example, in the *Protagoras*, a virtue such as justice is "neither by nature nor by chance" (οὐ φύσει οὐδ' ἀπὸ τοῦ αὐτομάτου) but comes from education, and its possessor acquired this virtue through effort. By contrast, defects such as being ugly, being small or being weak, and the merits opposite to them, he treats as things "by nature or by chance" (φύσει ἢ τύχῃ) (Platon, Protagoras, 323). Here nature and chance appear twice as a pair, but in the case of "by chance" he says "by *automaton*" on the one side and "by *tychē*" on the other. That is, the two are used in exactly the same meaning.

By contrast, Aristotle in the "Physics" set up the distinction between *automaton* and *tychē*. According to what he teaches, the point the two have in common lies in this: it is the case where an event belonging to the domain of purposiveness did not occur with what was in fact brought about as its purpose, but had some other cause (efficient cause or final cause). In other words, both are [p. 106] [コマ 60] the "cause by *symbebēkos*" (αἰτία κατὰ συμβεβηκός) of an event belonging to the domain of purposiveness. The "cause by *symbebēkos*" refers to a mere "purpose without purpose" that has no necessary relation whatever to the true system of cause and effect or of purpose and means. It refers to the mere "purpose without purpose" of the event that was in fact brought about. 〔Tr.: The repetition is in the printed text.〕 This "purpose without purpose", in relation to the true causal necessity or teleological necessity present in that case, has the contingency (*symbebēkos*) of a contingent attribute in relation to the essence (see p. 16). It is for this reason called the "cause by *symbebēkos*". For example, the cause of a statue is the sculptor. The "cause by *symbebēkos*" is a white man or a musician. Being a white man or a musician has, in relation to the sculptor who is the essential cause, no more than the contingency of a contingent attribute. The sculptor happened to belong to the white race, or happened to have a talent for music. For this reason the white man or the musician is the "cause by *symbebēkos*" of the statue. In short, *automaton* and *tychē* alike, as the "cause by *symbebēkos*" of an event belonging to the domain of purposiveness, have no more than a mere "purpose without purpose". The point common to the two is also, in truth, the negation of teleological necessity. That is, both are teleological contingency (Aristoteles, Physica, [p. 107] II. 5).

As to where the point of difference between the two lies: *tychē* refers to the case where the contingent event relates to something that can act by "design" (προαίρεσις), whereas *automaton* is a concept that holds also in cases where this is not so. That is, *automaton* is, in extension, a superordinate concept that includes *tychē*. *Tychē* is *automaton*, but *automaton* is not necessarily *tychē*. Consequently, too, *automaton* exists in animals and in inanimate things, but *tychē* is limited to those who can design purposive action. That is, *tychē*, through the mediation of the two concepts "good fortune" (εὐτυχία) and "happiness" (εὐδαιμονία), is tied to "action" (πρᾶξις) and made to belong to the practical domain. Consequently inanimate things, animals and children, which have no design, do not know *tychē* (l. c. II. 6). In other words, in *tychē* whether there was "intention" or not can become a question in practice, and in *automaton* this question does not arise, by its essence.

The example of *tychē* that Aristotle gives in the "Physics" is the following. A went to a certain place for some purpose other than collecting money. But there he happened to be able to collect money. [p. 108] [コマ 61] Collecting the money was not included in the purpose for which A went to that place. Collecting the money is the "cause by *symbebēkos*", that is, the "purpose without purpose". This example of *tychē*, insofar as it concerns something that can act by design, is one in which intention or chance can become a question. The case in which A went to that place "intentionally" in order to collect the money can also be conceived.

Four examples of *automaton* are given. The first example is the case where a tripod fell down but stood on its legs so that it could be sat on; the second example is the case where a stone fell and struck a man; the third example is the case where a horse came this way and that horse was thereby saved. That the tripod fell from a high place and its three legs stood is mere causal necessity, and that it came to be able to be sat on cannot be the purpose of the tripod's falling. Likewise, that the stone fell to a certain fixed place is mere causal necessity, and that the stone struck a man cannot be the purpose of the stone's falling. Again likewise, that the horse came this way is mere causal necessity, and that the horse was saved cannot be the purpose of the horse's coming this way. [p. 109] The tripod, the stone and the horse cannot act by design, and so these are examples of *automaton*. Moreover, a purpose is, by the nature of a tripod, a stone or a horse, absolutely impossible, and so these are cases in which there is no possibility at all of the "purpose without purpose" being understood as "intention". Further, these three examples also become examples of positive causal contingency, to be discussed later, but that will be stated later (see pp. 139-140).

As the fourth example of *automaton*, the case where something occurred contrary to nature (παρὰ φύσιν) is given. This example is the case where, after assuming according to the teleological view that teleological necessity governs the natural world, a purpose has not been realised, as an anti-natural phenomenon. This is what we earlier treated as the case of "counter-purpose" within negative teleological contingency (see p. 87). As already said, even in negative teleological contingency, insofar as the non-existence of a purpose is asserted, some "purpose without purpose" is latent (see pp. 94-95), and there is a point at which negative contingency and positive contingency are, in the end, hard to separate sharply. This fourth example too concerns a natural purpose, but does not concern a practical human being who can act by design. Such a thing as having "intentionally" taken counter-purpose as one's purpose cannot be thought. [p. 110] [コマ 62] That is what makes *automaton* *automaton*. However, this fourth example, when compared with the first, second and third examples, differs entirely in the way the negated purpose exists. In the first, second and third examples the purposes "in order to sit", "in order to strike a man", "in order to be saved" are negated, but such purposes occupy a position outside (ἔξω) the causal series. By contrast, in the fourth example the purpose that ought to be realised by nature is negated, and such a purpose occupies a position within (ἐντός) the natural causal series. In the first, second and third examples something that ought not to exist is asserted not to exist, and in the fourth example something that ought to exist is asserted not to exist, and by this counter-purposiveness is pointed out. But all of them, insofar as there is no possibility of their being designed "intentionally", are examples of *automaton*.

Further, regarding the difference between *automaton* and *tychē*, according to an example given by Alexander of Aphrodisias, famous as a commentator on Aristotle, in the case where a horse that ran away happened to meet its master, for the horse it is *automaton*, and for the master it is *tychē*. The possibility of the "purpose without purpose" being replaced by "intention" the horse [p. 111] does not have, but the master has. Whether the possibility of "intention" exists or does not exist must be the main point of the distinction between *tychē* and *automaton*. And, insofar as *tychē* and *automaton* both concern purposiveness, whether or not there is a possibility of that purposiveness being posited with design in the domain of practice must be a rather important difference.

Let us say a word about the translated terms for *automaton* and *tychē*. In Latin, *casus* was assigned to *automaton*, and *fortuna* to *tychē*. Translating *automaton* literally, it was also called *per se vanum*. *Per se* is the translation of αὐτό (of itself), *vanum* the translation of μάτην (without reason). *Tychē*, according to Aristotle, is called "good fortune" (εὐτυχία) when its result is a great good, and "misfortune" (δυστυχία) when it is a great evil (Aristoteles, Physica, II. 5, 197a). What is common to "good fortune" (*secunda fortuna*) and "misfortune" (*adversa fortuna*) is "fortune" (*fortuna*), and so in Latin *tychē* was translated as *fortuna*.

The translations in modern languages do not agree. There are roughly three different approaches. (1) The Latin translations *casus* and *fortuna* are made into modern-language words as they are. Hamelin is an example of this, and translates *automaton* [p. 112] [コマ 63] as *hasard* and *tychē* as *fortune* (Aristote Physique II, Traduction et Commentaire par O. Hamelin). Wicksteed and Cornford likewise translate *automaton* as *chance* and *tychē* as *fortune* or *luck* (Aristotle The Physics, with an English Translation by P. H. Wicksteed and F. M. Cornford). *Hasard* and *chance* both share their etymology with *casus*. (2) One translates directly from Greek into a modern language. Milhaud translates *automaton* as *spontané* and *tychē* as *hasard* (G. Milhaud, Le hasard chez Aristote et chez Cournot, Études sur la Pensée Scientifique, pp. 137-158). Gomperz likewise translates *automaton* as *das Spontane* and *tychē* as *Zufall* (Gomperz, Griechische Denker, III, Aristoteles und seine Nachfolger, Kap. X. Von Zufall und Notwendigkeit). Hardie and Gaye are the same. They translate *automaton* as *spontaneity* and *tychē* as *chance* (Aristotle Physica translated into English by R. P. Hardie and R. K. Gaye). What should be noted is that, between the case where the Latin translation was made modern and the case where a modern-language translation was made directly from Greek, a reversal of the translated terms is seen. In the former, *hasard*, *Zufall* and *chance* are assigned to *automaton*, and in the latter to *tychē*. The case where the modern-language translation is made directly from Greek [p. 113] is on the whole superior. However, that in translating *automaton* the weight is placed on αὐτό (of itself), it is translated "spontaneity", and μάτην (without reason) is in effect disregarded, is inappropriate. Insofar as contingency appears clearly in the word μάτην, it ought not to be slighted. (3) One translates the Greek strictly and literally. Prantl translates *tychē* as *Zufall* and *automaton* as *das grundlos von selbst Eintretende* (Aristoteles' Acht Bücher Physik. Griechisch und Deutsch herausgegeben von C. Prantl, 1854). This translation of *automaton*, insofar as it translates strictly and literally, as they are, the various moments that the original word contains, must be called, the skill or clumsiness of the translation being a separate question, the most appropriate translation with regard to the philosophical content of the word. Prantl himself also states the necessity of translating literally in this way (Ib., S. 483-484). That in Latin too there was *per se vanum* as a literal translation has already been said. The half that was slighted by translating *automaton* as "spontaneity" regains its rights through the literal translation.

### 11. From teleological contingency to causal contingency

[p. 114] [コマ 64] The above has discussed teleological contingency. Now, contingency of this kind on the teleological horizon too, seen from the standpoint of causal relation in the narrow sense, should be considered to have arisen as the effect of some cause and to possess causal necessity. Speaking of negative teleological contingency as "purposelessness", the idea that human beings were thrown by chance onto the surface of the earth is nothing other than one half of the mechanistic view that human beings were necessarily produced by the laws of motion. Speaking of negative teleological contingency as "counter-purpose", being severely intellectually disabled must have some cause in the tissue of the cerebrum, above all in the hereditary material of the cells. 〔Tr.: 白痴 (*hakuchi*), a period term, as in Chapter 1, section 7.〕 That a flower becomes double must have a determinate cause in the stimulating action of parasitic fungi or insects and in the reaction of the flower to it. If we speak of positive teleological contingency, that one dug the ground and obtained treasure has its cause in the inertia possessed by the object that lay at rest in the opaque earth, and in the mechanical action of the hoe and the human force applied to it. [p. 115] Again, that phosphorus was obtained by evaporating urine and heating it strongly was caused by the phosphate that had been contained in the urine as the end product of physiological combustion, and the chemical action applied to it. That the investigation of the Shōden case happened to come to have a relation to the decorations-buying affair must have had its root cause in the personal character and the social circumstances of the person who was examined. In this way teleological contingency, taking the same direction as categorical contingency, is reduced to causal necessity, at least to the problem of causality in the narrow sense.

### 12. Negative causal contingency

On the horizon of causality the problem of causal contingency remains. Let us first discuss negative causal contingency, which negatively witnesses the non-existence of causal necessity. Epicurus, who adopted atomism, thought the original motion of the atoms to be vertical motion directed from above downwards, but taught that there are atoms that, without any cause (ἀναιτίως), of themselves (*sponte*), deviate very slightly from the vertical line [p. 116] [コマ 65]. 〔Tr.: P. 116 begins with the last words of this sentence, る原子のあることを説いた, "taught that there are atoms".〕 Lucretius called this slight departure from the vertical "inclination" (*clinamen*). "The mind itself is not, in each of its actions, constrained as if bound by an inner necessity to endure and suffer. This is owing to the *minute* (*exiguum*) *inclination* (*clinamen*), determined neither spatially nor temporally, that the primary bodies have" (Lucretius, De rerum natura II. 289-293). This Epicurean indeterminism was advocated against the determinism of the Stoic school, but it also shows, on the problem of determinacy, a character directly opposite to the atomism of Democritus. *Clinamen* is indeed the type of negative causal contingency. Causal contingency in this sense is nothing other than *spontaneity*. And, as is shown by the word "without a master" (ἀδέσποτος) (Diogenes Laertios, X. 133), it also comes to mean *freedom*.

That negative causal contingency can mean *freedom* is a point to be noted. "This right to be able to enjoy *contingency* unhindered within certain fixed conditions, people have hitherto called *personal freedom*" (Marx and Engels, "Die deutsche Ideologie", trans. Miki Kiyoshi, Iwanami Bunko, p. 116). Again: "The dialectical materialism of scientific socialism, which makes the natural-scientific methodology that excludes every teleological standpoint [p. 117] the sole principle of concept formation, ignores the typicality of the historical concepts of the human sciences, and seeks to put the construction of concepts of order in place of understanding by types, cannot escape coming to take away the room in which the universal of reflective judgement, which makes possible teleology as the regulative principle of the logic of dialectic, arises, to block the place where *freedom* or *contingency* should be admitted, and to seek to construct history solely by the logic of an entirely necessary dialectic. The world of dialectical movement that arises in this way is in fact nothing other than a kind of nature, and its particular falls into a particular that is merely an instance of a concept of law, and can no longer demand to be, with a unique individuality, an object of historical knowledge" (Tanabe Hajime, "Hegel's Philosophy and Dialectic", pp. 383-384). Again: "That dialectic turns spiritual life into logical necessity is something rightly done only under the priority of the actual; conversely, it does not determine the whole of the actual exhaustively, extending even into the future that has not yet occurred, and so there must necessarily be the *contingent* relative to dialectic there. The positive content that appears as what fills this negatively remaining place is *freedom*" (ibid., p. 278).

Causal contingency, when it means indeterminate spontaneity, is expressed in Japanese by the word "*onozukara*", [p. 118] [コマ 66] and when it is joined with teleological necessity and takes on the meaning of freedom, the word "*mizukara*" is used. 〔Tr.: *Onozukara*, "of itself", and *mizukara*, "by oneself", can both be written 自ら.〕 "Now I love my lonely dwelling, my hut of one room, *of myself* (*mizukara*). Though, when *on occasion* (*onozukara*) I go out to the capital, I am ashamed that I have become a beggar, yet when I return and am here, I pity others for running after the dust of the world" (*Hōjōki*). Further, the word "*onozukara*" means "nature" and, as a result of that, can on the contrary stand opposed to causal contingency. But in that case, insofar as on the other side it means teleological contingency, it should be thought to express a union of nature and chance. In "My View of Life" (p. 242) in the special issue of *Risō*, "The Philosophy of the View of Life", I used the word "*onozukara*" in particular in this sense, that is, in the sense of standing opposed to causal contingency as "nature". "Even without deliberately learning and imitating, the things that catch the ear and the eye of a person with even a little talent must *naturally* (*jinen ni*) be many. And so she dashes off *mana*, and in letters between women, where it is not fitting, crams them in until more than half is filled; how distasteful, one wishes she were gentler in this respect. Though in her heart she may not mean it so, it *of itself* (*onozukara*) comes to be read out in a stiff voice, and seems affected" (*Genji monogatari*, Hahakigi). That in this case "*jinen ni*" (自然に) and "*onozukara*" are entirely synonymous [p. 119] rests on the same Ground as Plato's regarding nature and chance as of one kind (see p. 78). In short, the indeterminate spontaneity of "*onozukara*" as causal contingency turns, by heterogeneous combination, in the direction of teleological necessity and becomes the freedom that "*mizukara*" has, and conversely, by homogeneous combination, runs in the direction of teleological contingency and becomes "*jinen*", that is, nature. Freedom and nature thus show a directly opposed opposition. "If at that time the seed of Korean ginseng is sought out by your order, it can be procured *freely* in any quantity. If you have ten or twenty thousand seeds sown in a year and continue for ten years, the seeds sown first will bear fruit from four or five years on, so that within ten years they will be cultivated step by step. Then, when the seed has become plentiful, if it is sown and left here and there in the mountains, afterwards Korean ginseng will grow *naturally* in the mountains, just as the present Japanese ginseng does" (*Hiraga Gennai zenshū*, published by the Hiraga Gennai Sensei Kenshōkai, vol. 1, pp. 194-195).

> Causal contingency = *indeterminate spontaneity* (*onozukara*)
>
> - (result of heterogeneous combination) teleological necessity = *freedom* (*mizukara*)
> - (result of homogeneous combination) teleological contingency = *nature* (*jinen*)

Hegel's distinction between freedom (*Freiheit*) and arbitrariness (*Willkür*) too sets up, on the one side, "*mizukara*", in which teleological necessity is extremely emphasised, as "freedom", and on the other side calls the result of the homogeneous combination of the causal contingency [p. 120] [コマ 67] that "*onozukara*" has with the teleological contingency that "*jinen*" has "arbitrariness". According to Hegel, the will has the contingent within itself merely as a sublated moment, in the form of arbitrariness. Arbitrariness is the will in the form of the contingent, and freedom is the will in the form of the necessary. When the freedom of the will is spoken of, often merely arbitrariness is thought of. Arbitrariness, as the capacity to determine oneself to one thing or another, is of course without doubt an essential moment of free will. But it is not freedom itself, merely formal freedom. The truly free will holds within itself the causal contingency of arbitrariness as sublated, but is clearly self-conscious of its own content as its own, as teleological necessity. By contrast, the will that remains at the stage of arbitrariness, even when it has determined itself to what is true and right in content, has not escaped the contentless emptiness that it could also have determined itself to something else if only it pleased it. Hegel thus opposes the teleological necessity that freedom as "*mizukara*" has to the causal contingency that arbitrariness as "*onozukara*" has, but further points out that arbitrariness contains contradiction in that it has teleological contingency, and [p. 121] sharpens its opposition to freedom. In arbitrariness, form and content are separate. Arbitrariness is self-contradictory in that in form it has causal contingency, but in content it has teleological contingency and, as the other side of that, causal necessity. The content of arbitrariness, insofar as it is something given, is not something the will itself (*mizukara*) has founded within the will, but something founded of itself (*onozukara*) in external circumstances. Freedom arises with regard to such content only in the form of choice, but such formal freedom is merely a freedom one imagines to be freedom. For if one analyses in detail, it becomes clear that the cause of the will's determining itself precisely to B and not to A does not lie in the self, but that the cause of the self-determination lies in the externality of the circumstances that form the basis of the content the will found as given. "*Onozukara*" as the causal contingency of arbitrariness, when analysed in detail, is found, insofar as it is joined with teleological contingency, to be no more than the causal necessity of "*jinen*". Because what was thought to be causal contingency is, as a result of analysis, seen as causal necessity, arbitrariness contains contradiction. Hegel's view is that arbitrariness, which is at the low stage of the will, must be developed into freedom, which is at the high stage (Hegel, En-[p. 122] [コマ 68]cyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 145, Zusatz, S. 194).

We can consider the concept of freedom by dividing it into two, negative freedom and positive freedom. Then negative freedom corresponds to indeterminacy and spontaneity as causal contingency, and positive freedom corresponds to what Hegel calls freedom, as teleological necessity. According to Nicolai Hartmann's study of Modality, negative freedom is the possibility of acting one way or the other, and is the predominance of possibility over necessity. However, the ought, which is the essence of morality, is the predominance of necessity over possibility, and so activity conditioned by the ought cannot be said to be free in the negative sense. It is in the positive sense that it can be said to be free. Negative freedom is "freedom of possibility" (Freiheit der Möglichkeit), that is, "freedom from necessity" (Freiheit „von" der Notwendigkeit), but positive freedom must be, as "freedom from possibility" (Freiheit „von" der Möglichkeit), "freedom of necessity" (Freiheit der Notwendigkeit). He holds that the freedom of necessity is necessity gushing forth freely. And he arrives at the paradoxical concept of "free necessity" (freie Notwendigkeit) (see N. Hartmann, Logische und ontologische Wirklichkeit, [p. 123] Kantstudien XX, S. 23-24; N. Hartmann, Ethik, 1926, S. 587-589). The "free necessity" of which Hartmann speaks is positive freedom as teleological necessity. Causal contingency, as indeterminate spontaneity, constitutes the concept of negative freedom.

Further, in Eastern thought there is a marked tendency for freedom and nature to be seen, not in a disjoined opposition, but as fused and one. It may be possible to see this as a stage in which the teleological necessity that "*mizukara*" has and the causal necessity that "*jinen ni*" has are sublated in the spontaneity of "*onozukara*". An interesting problem is contained in this point, but now is not the occasion to discuss it. On this point, Mr Abe Yoshishige's article "On Nature", printed in *Risō* no. 17, is rich in suggestion.

Above, we have briefly considered the relation of causal contingency as indeterminate spontaneity, on the one hand to freedom in the positive sense, and on the other to nature. As a result, it cannot be denied that in the domain of the philosophy of spirit there is a tendency to seek to replace the concept of causal contingency with the concept of teleological necessity. However, teleological necessity makes no sense unless at its origin it depends on causal contingency. Causal contingency must be the starting point of teleological [p. 124] [コマ 69] necessity. In other words, negative freedom as indeterminacy and spontaneity constitutes the indispensable condition of positive freedom as determinacy and necessity. Not only that: if positive freedom as teleological necessity is understood as "liberation from obstacles", then from the process by which the consciousness of freedom arises, that one on the contrary has the negative meaning, and negative freedom as absolute spontaneity even comes to have the positive meaning. Mr Amano Teiyū's "Personality and Freedom" makes this point clear. "The empirical process by which we have the consciousness of freedom begins from the negative side. Only where there are obstacles do we become conscious of freedom. If there were no obstacles whatever, the consciousness called freedom could not arise. This is why freedom is understood as liberation from obstacles. The word freedom originally has this negative meaning. ...... And since in human beings action is thought to be based on the will, though the obstacles that bind are many and various, what is bound always comes down to the will. A human being comes to be free when the will is not bound. Here the meaning of freedom turns to the positive side, that a person can both will an action and not will it. [p. 125] But this does not mean that the meaning of freedom changes. For the negative meaning too has the positive meaning as its Ground. Though freedom is said to be liberation from obstacles, it is not the liberated state that is freedom; the possibility of activity, which through liberation has become able both to submit to the obstacles and not to submit, is freedom" (Iwanami Lectures on Philosophy, Amano Teiyū, "Personality and Freedom", p. 4. See also p. 38. Further, in the same work personal freedom is discussed with philosophical passion in connection with "the comprehension of the intelligible world".)

The important role of causal contingency in the domain of the philosophy of spirit is as described, but on the other hand it cannot be denied that natural science, as its general tendency, tries by every means to exclude the concept of causal contingency by the concept of causal necessity. Even if concepts such as chance error, chance generation and chance variation arise, they are soon conquered by causal necessity. These concepts originally arose in connection with causal contingency. That is, chance error with regard to the cause of error in the measurement of quantities, chance generation with regard to the cause of the generation of organisms, and chance variation with regard to the cause of changes occurring in the hereditary material: in each case one sought to witness contingency in some sense. [p. 126] [コマ 70] However, for chance error, by determining something like what conforms to the causal law as the quantity to be measured; for chance generation, by bringing the boundary between inorganic and organic matter closer by way of chemical synthesis; and for chance variation, by necessitating the causes of variation experimentally by using radiation and other means, it replaces contingency with necessity in every case. It may be said without objection that the ambition of natural science lies precisely in this point. If natural science makes causal necessity its ultimate idea, and the philosophy of spirit postulates causal contingency as an indispensable condition of teleological necessity, it must be only natural that a grave philosophical problem unfolds around the concept of causal contingency.

Whether causal contingency exists in the strict sense is one of the ultimate problems for natural scientists and for philosophers alike. On the side that affirms its existence there is, for example, Boutroux. According to Boutroux, the causal law, as something abstract, can be a practical maxim of science, but in the concrete actual world it is not strictly applied. All measurement is merely approximate. To attain absolute precision is [p. 127] in principle impossible. Experimental verification comes down in the end to compressing the value of the measurable element of phenomena between limits brought as close together as possible. What we see is, so to speak, merely the container in which the thing has been put. It is not the thing itself. And a *minute indeterminacy*, to a degree that exceeds the range of validity of our crude methods of measurement, can be immanent in phenomena. That is precisely causal contingency (contingence) as the non-existence of causal necessity. Thus Boutroux sought to give the "philosophy of freedom" advocated by Maine de Biran and Ravaisson a foundation through the concept of the contingent. Further, whereas in the indeterminism of Epicurus causal contingency and teleological contingency form a homogeneous combination, in Boutroux causal contingency forms a heterogeneous combination with teleological necessity. Epicurus on the one hand acknowledged the existence of the gods, but on the other thought that these gods dwell in the void between the worlds (μετακόσμια, intermundia), rest content in self-sufficient bliss, and make no interference whatever with the world or with human beings. Boutroux's so-called "contingency of the laws of nature", by contrast, depends in the end on the teleological necessity of the universe (Boutroux, De la contingence des lois de la nature, 9e éd., p. 155-157). Boutroux speaks of "necessity in the form of duty", that is, of "the end conceived as necessary", [p. 128] [コマ 71] and says that in God "freedom is infinite" and at the same time there is "practical necessity". He also expounds God's "unceasing providence" and "particular providence", and says that "the contingency shown by the hierarchical organisation of the various forms and general laws of the universe is explained by this doctrine of the freedom of God". And the quotation from Aristotle's *Parts of Animals* that he placed at the head of his book as a motto, "Here too there are gods", indicates his standpoint as one that points to the teleological necessity lurking behind causal contingency, and at the same time hints that his thought is close to Thomas Aquinas's concept of the "law of necessity or of contingency" and to Malebranche's occasionalism. Émile Borel too affirms causal contingency in his book *Le Hasard*. According to his claim concerning physical phenomena, on the one hand "the necessity of a certain total phenomenon is not incompatible with the freedom of the partial phenomena". On the other hand, "even a determinism thought to be absolute with regard to the partial phenomena does not permit the total phenomenon to be foreseen with absolute rigour". Absolute determinism is in any case difficult to support. "For, however small the interference of freedom within the universe may be, between the universe in which such interference exists [p. 129] and the universe from which such interference is excluded, an abyss lies" (Émile Borel, Le Hasard, 1920, p. 290-295).

However, there will be not a few who say with Poincaré: "We have become absolute determinists" (H. Poincaré, Science et Méthode, p. 65). When a cone is stood on its apex, which direction it falls in seems indeed to be by chance. But for the direction of its fall to be determined there must necessarily be some cause. That is, if the cone lacks symmetry even very slightly, it inclines, however slightly, to one side or the other. And however slight that inclination may be, it determines the direction in which the cone falls. And even if the symmetry were perfect, it is inclined by some seconds of arc by an extremely minute vibration. And that slight inclination determines the direction in which the cone falls. In short, "a *minute* cause that escapes our notice determines a considerable effect that we cannot fail to see, and then we say that the effect happened by chance" (ib., p. 68). Poincaré sharply distinguished determinism and freedom in the Kantian manner and sought to harmonise the domain of science with the domain of morality. And he says: "Just as it is impossible for a human being, [p. 130] [コマ 72] when doing science, not to reason as a determinist, so it is impossible for a human being, when acting, not to act as a free human being" (H. Poincaré, Dernières Pensées, Paris, Flammarion 1926, p. 246).

The point at issue between indeterminism and determinism concerning causality lies in whether to acknowledge a minute indeterminacy within determinacy or to acknowledge a minute determinacy within indeterminacy. The problem comes down to the single point of the "minute". It is concentrated on the single point of Lucretius's exiguum. And so long as the minute indeterminacy that Boutroux and Borel assume is something human beings cannot measure, this problem may be, as Poincaré says, a problem that is "difficult to resolve clearly" (H. Poincaré, La Valeur de la Science, p. 248).

What attitude does recent natural science show on this problem? Let me quote here the words of a natural scientist. "The principle of what is usually called the causal law, that is, of the necessary succession of events, has been regarded as an absolutely necessary category for the establishment of natural science. For if no such necessary relation existed among events, we would not have been able to set up there any fixed law or principle whatever. [p. 131] However, the development of physics gradually came to doubt the absolute validity even of this category. First, in thermal phenomena the contingency of molecular motion came to be acknowledged, and accordingly it was made clear that the laws of thermodynamics, for example, are merely something that appears as the result of a transition from a state of contingent distribution of this molecular motion to another state of greater probability, and are by no means of absolute necessity, but permit many contingencies contrary to them. However, in this case the motion of each individual molecule was still regarded, as to its laws, as following necessary mechanical principles, and thermodynamically contingency results because, instead of seeing individual molecules 'microscopically', we see their great aggregate 'macroscopically', that is, statistically. According to the results of the recent development of the quantum theory of the electrons within the atom, however, it has been made clear that the law determining such a state of the electron (the wave function of the electron) is not likewise given necessarily, but can be expressed only with a certain contingent probability. This must truly be called an astonishing new fact. [p. 132] [コマ 73] It is a matter concerning the ultimate elements of matter. Moreover, that it can be determined only with contingency overturns at its root the old conception of necessary causality with respect to the basic principles of matter. We could, of course, by comparison with the example of molecular motion in thermal phenomena, interpret that in this case too some necessary law is hidden behind the contingency of the state of the electron, and that we merely observe its statistical results. However, the so-called necessary law behind it is, like the aforementioned form or orbit of the electron, in the end nothing other than a certain imagining, and goes beyond the domain of actual experience. Accordingly, we could there, with entirely equal right, assume the negation of necessary law and imagine, for example, something like the free will of the electron itself. For what goes beyond the domain of experience is already not an object of natural-scientific research" (Ishihara Jun, "The Supra-materialist Tendency of Modern Natural Science", *Shisō*, no. 100, commemorative special issue, pp. 84-85). "When we consider that what is discussed here is the most basic phenomenon of physics, we cannot but realise how deeply contingency lies at the very root of natural science" (Ishihara Jun, *Outline of Natural Science*, p. 88).

[p. 133] The concept of negative causal contingency, as this quotation shows, has recently come to be affirmed as so-called quantum-mechanical contingency based on the uncertainty principle, and natural science has come to show a tendency to give the advantage to the indeterminism that asserts a minute indeterminacy. And theorists who stress how far-reaching its philosophical outlook is have gradually increased in number (cf. Ed. Le Roy, Ce que la Microphysique apporte ou suggère à la Philosophie, Revue de Métaphysique et de Morale, 1935, esp. p. 345-355). However, not only is it not the case that the uncertainty principle necessarily gains the recognition of every influential natural scientist, but it should also remain somewhat doubtful to what degree it is compatible with the essence of natural-scientific thinking itself (cf. É. Meyerson, Du cheminement de la pensée I, p. 63-64, III, p. 763-765, 768-769). On the other hand there are also theorists who claim that these problems belong to the sphere of "*quantum metaphysics*" (métamicrophysique = ultra-microphysics), distinct from the domain of the former *metaphysics* (métaphysique = ultra-physics) (G. Bachelard, Noumène et Microphysique, Recherches philosophiques I, p. 55-65), so that the state of affairs still lacks clarity on philosophical ground. Those who philosophise should perhaps not think the value of so-called quantum-mechanical contingency itself too exaggeratedly. Since the state of affairs is [p. 134] [コマ 74] like this, we shall for the time being leave as a problem the question of the existence or non-existence of negative causal contingency in the so-called "domain of objects". Then does the contingent not exist concerning causality in some other sense? Here, in contrast to negative causal contingency, there is further the concept of positive causal contingency.

〔Tr.: 量子上學 (*ryōshijōgaku*), rendered "quantum metaphysics", is coined on the model of 形而上學 (*keijijōgaku*), metaphysics, replacing "form" (形而) with "quantum" (量子). 超唯物的 is rendered "supra-materialist". 偶然發生 is rendered "chance generation".〕

### 13. Positive causal contingency

Positive causal contingency is, as already said, the case of positively witnessing the existence of a relation other than causality between two or more events. For example, if a tile falls from a roof and bursts a rubber balloon that was rolling under the eaves, we think that chance. Again, when a volcano erupted, if it was at the time of a solar eclipse, we call that chance. The tile, with some cause such as loss of adhesion through the decay of the roof or detachment by the force of the wind, as a result followed the law of falling and fell to a certain place. The rubber balloon, with the minute impulse it first received, the elasticity of the rubber, the spherical shape of the balloon and [p. 135] the slope and unevenness of the ground as causes, as a result followed the laws of motion and rolled to a certain place. We call it chance that two events with different causal series were placed in a certain positive relation. Likewise, the volcano erupted because the tension of underground hot water turning into steam reached a certain degree, and as a result according to the law of pressure. The sun, owing to the cause of being obscured by the moon, as a result followed the simplest laws of astronomy and presented the phenomenon of darkness. One causal series and the other causal series are wholly independent. No causal relation is recognised between the two series. The two series are not bound by necessity. The contingency of this case too lies in a non-necessary mutual relation between one causal series and another causal series. For the similar relation existing among many events, we may consider the following example. "Let us consider a white ball. Just as a machine moves according to necessary laws, the ball too would roll on a smooth flat board according to laws known to us. If we roll countless such white balls, in great numbers, on one flat board, various spotted patterns of white balls that were not at all foreseen must surely appear on the board. Whatever pattern we find in each individual case, [p. 136] [コマ 75] all is chance" (Ishihara Jun, "God Loves Chance", magazine *Serupan*, June 1935 issue, p. 7). However, even where positive contingency is a relation among many events, two or more, it still has at its basis the relation between two events as its prototype. The contingent of this kind is very important. Stuart Mill too says: "It is not correct to say that some phenomenon was produced by chance. But one can say that two or more phenomena were combined by chance, that they merely exist simultaneously by chance or that one follows the other. That is, one can say so in the sense that they are not related in any way by causality" (It is incorrect to say that any phenomenon is produced by chance; but we may say that two or more phenomena are conjoined by chance, that they co-exist or succeed one another only by chance; meaning that they are in no way related through causation) (J. Stuart Mill, A System of Logic, III, ch. XVII, § 2. New Impression 1925, p. 345).

This positive causal contingency founds both categorical contingency and teleological contingency in their principal forms. That a certain clover is four-leaved was seen as categorical contingency, [p. 137] but this has causal necessity, such as the stimulus of an injury. But this causal necessity probably has its Ground further in positive causal contingency. That is, that the leaf of a certain clover received an injury at the early stage of its morphogenesis must rest on a positive causal contingency such as a strong wind's happening to strike sand against that part. Again, the case in which a weather forecaster whose success rate is ninety per cent or more happens to make a wrong forecast is a categorical contingency, but it had as its causal necessity facts such as a sudden change in the weather or the excitement of the forecaster's mind. Yet such causal necessity too presupposes at its root some positive causal contingency. For example, we can think that a positive causal contingency, such as air current A and air current B happening to collide, or to pass each other, on a line of discontinuity, became the basis and brought on the sudden change in the weather. Again, at the root of the fact of the forecaster's mental excitement there lies hidden a positive causal contingency, in which the act of making the forecast on the one hand and an event that would bring on mental excitement on the other combine by chance. "Just the other day something like that happened; a child had a fever, but [p. 138] [コマ 76] *as ill luck would have it*, the forecast begins at eight in the evening. *Just as it was about to begin*, it reached forty-three degrees, and there was a fear that it might be diphtheria" (Fujiwara Sakuhei, *Talks on Astronomy and Meteorology*, p. 58). In short, positive causal contingency founds categorical contingency. Further, when the general concept is seen as a purpose to be realised, categorical contingency became negative teleological contingency (see pp. 87-88), so the present examples of the four-leaved clover and of the error of the weather forecast can also be seen as examples of negative teleological contingency, and it follows that behind negative teleological contingency too we cannot but think some positive causal contingency. In positive teleological contingency this is still clearer. If, in digging the ground to plant a tree, treasure came out unexpectedly, we said that this is positive teleological contingency. Since the sole purpose lay in planting a tree, obtaining the treasure is teleological contingency. Now, as said before, purposiveness is the reverse of causality: it first aims at the result that comes later as a purpose, and afterwards chooses the cause that precedes that result as a means. The relation of purpose and means presupposes the relation of cause and effect. Then, as to what causal relation exists in this case in the shadow of purposiveness: on the one hand, the [p. 139] mechanical action of A's spade became the cause, and the treasure was buried in the ground. On the other hand, the mechanical action of B's spade became the cause, and the earth was dug over. There, there were two independent causal series, and those two causal series were by chance placed in one relation. In short, positive causal contingency lies hidden at the root of positive teleological contingency.

Now here there is one phenomenon worth noting. We said earlier that teleological contingency is accompanied by "purpose without purpose", and that in negative teleological contingency its upper member, "without purpose", is emphasised, whilst in positive teleological contingency its lower member, "purpose", is emphasised. This character of teleological contingency is reflected in positive causal contingency too. And so positive causal contingency also comes to have a kind of imagined "purpose without purpose". In the fact that the roof tile fell and just hit the rubber balloon, it is felt as though some mischievous purpose were hidden; in the volcano's eruption and the solar eclipse occurring at the same time, as though some mysterious purpose were lurking. But these are nothing more than the projection, through a kind of empathy, of the "purpose without purpose" of teleological contingency onto positive causal contingency. And [p. 140] [コマ 77] from this standpoint one can also assert that the distinction between Aristotle's *automaton* and *tychē* is the distinction between positive causal contingency and positive teleological contingency (see p. 109).

〔Tr.: 上肢 / 下肢, "upper member" / "lower member", refer to the two halves of the phrase 目的なき目的, "purpose without purpose": in the Japanese word order "without purpose" (目的なき) comes first, "purpose" (目的) second. 感情移入 is the standard Japanese rendering of *Einfühlung*, "empathy". 假想的 is rendered "imagined".〕

### 14. Compound contingency

Various kinds of the contingent sometimes combine with one another to constitute a single compound contingency. For example, a case such as that in which a person going to gamble unexpectedly finds a four-leaved clover by the roadside on the way is compound contingency. Not only is the four-leaved clover itself categorical contingency, but that a person going to gamble happened to find on the way a four-leaved clover, which is regarded as a sign of good luck, is positive teleological contingency. It was said that positive causal contingency lies hidden at the root both of categorical contingency and of teleological contingency, but here, further, categorical contingency and positive teleological contingency show a state of compounding. To give one more example, an article appeared in a newspaper (19 April 1935, Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun, Kyoto edition) saying that, because a car with the number 1564 had run a person over, "the driver [p. 141] went pale and applied for a change of number", and this too is compound contingency. Between 1564 and "murder" (*hitogoroshi*) a positive contingency of ground holds through a phonic relation. And that a car with such an "uncanny" number happened to run a person over is positive teleological contingency. Through positive contingency of ground and positive teleological contingency presenting a compound state, contingency is emphasised.

Further, what should be noted is that the basis of the compoundness of compound contingency is itself formed by contingency. In the clover example, between the four-leaved clover, which is categorical contingency, and the positive teleological contingency of having found it, there is a positive contingency, namely that the four-leaved clover is a symbol of good luck and that the finder was on the way to gamble, wishing for good luck, and this forms the basis of the compoundness. In the car example too, between the positive contingency of ground through the phonic relation and the positive teleological contingency of unexpectedly running a person over, there is a positive contingency, namely that the sound of the car's number corresponds to murder and that the chance that the car encountered was a killing, and only this [p. 142] [コマ 78] makes compound contingency in the true sense hold. I shall discuss the compoundness of successive contingency later (see pp. 159 ff.).

〔Tr.: The numerals 1, 5, 6, 4 can be read *hi(to)*, *go*, *ro(ku)*, *shi*, giving *hitogoroshi*, 人殺し, "murder", "killing". 凄い (*sugoi*) is rendered "uncanny".〕

### 15. The general character of positive hypothetical contingency

Above, we have considered each of the three kinds of hypothetical contingency by dividing it into two, negative contingency and positive contingency. Negative contingency is the case in which the lack of a hypothetical antecedent (ground, cause, purpose) with regard to one event is grasped negatively. Positive contingency is the case in which a relation other than a hypothetical relation is witnessed positively between two or more events. Since in negative contingency the lack of an antecedent is merely grasped with regard to one event, in that sense it can be called *absolute contingency*. Since in positive contingency the relation among two or more events is judged to be contingent, it can be called *relative contingency*. In short, positive contingency, whether positive contingency of ground, positive teleological contingency or positive causal contingency, possesses [p. 143] positivity and relativity.

What should be noted here, however, is that every negative absolute contingency in the end has positive relative contingency at its root. Relative contingency in general has a negative aspect, that of lacking a hypothetical relation, and a positive aspect, that some relation other than the hypothetical relation holds. So-called negative contingency is the case in which only its negative aspect is witnessed, and positive contingency is the case in which, going further, the positive aspect is also witnessed together with it. However, since the contingent that in reality wholly lacks the positive aspect cannot exist, there must be some positivity there even if the positive aspect is not consciously grasped. That positivity may be of a nature to be witnessed in some other dimension, but in any case, unless some positivity is witnessed there, it cannot be called the true contingent. In that sense it can be said that all the contingent is positive relative contingency.

For example, positive contingency of ground lies hidden at the root of negative contingency of ground. Earlier, [p. 144] [コマ 79] we gave as an example of negative contingency the case of "falling by chance into the truth" without ground. In that case the non-existence of ground is witnessed negatively, but insofar as one says "falls into the truth", some relativity must lie hidden there. That is, on the one side there is the series of necessary relations of ground and consequent called the knowledge of truth, on the other side there is the series of necessary relations of the free will that affirms or denies, and insofar as a non-necessary relative relation holds between the two series, truth emerges as positive contingency. Therefore, at the root of the negative contingency of lacking a ground of knowledge with respect to the establishment of truth, there exists the positive contingency that the series of decisions of the will, while lacking a ground of knowledge, fell into the series of the knowledge of truth. Since it lacks a ground of knowledge, that series of decisions of the will could also have fallen into a logical series in which error holds. The positive existence called truth is something that suddenly floated up where two independent series were placed in a contingent relation. As for the fact that positive contingency lies hidden at the root of negative teleological contingency, I have already discussed it in another connection with regard to the case of "counter-purpose" (see p. 138). That is, when a purpose that should be realised is [p. 145] not realised, a purpose that is realised in many cases is in that case in particular not realised, so that for this we could not but think some positive causal contingency on the reverse side. Even with regard to the case of negative teleological contingency as "purposelessness", insofar as one asserts the negation of purposiveness negatively, some "purpose without purpose" must be witnessed on its reverse side, even if unconsciously, and purposiveness must be denied with regard to that purpose without purpose. For example, insofar as one claims that the eye is not made for the purpose of looking at objects, in the way that clear water is not made for the purpose of reflecting a person's figure, some purpose without purpose concerning the structure and function of the eye is positively grasped, and on top of that purposiveness is refused with regard to that purpose without purpose. And as to what that purpose without purpose originates from, we must think that it is based on a contingent relative relation among two or more causal series. Therefore negative teleological contingency, even in the case where it is mere "purposelessness", conceals some positive relative contingency at its root, just as in the case of "counter-purpose". Finally, the same holds also for negative causal contingency. If we think about concepts such as chance error, [p. 146] [コマ 80] chance generation and chance variation, it is clear. In chance error, unpredictable changes in the atmosphere or sudden minute changes in the instruments form positive relative contingency. In chance generation, it is assumed that, for living things to arise from non-living things, some positive causal contingency was established between matter and the state of the environment at a certain period in the history of the formation of the earth. In chance variation too, the changes occurring in the hereditary material acknowledge the existence of some positive relative contingency between the organism on the one side and temperature, light and so on on the other. Where negative causal contingency strictly means spontaneity, at least the relative relation of positive contingency of ground between the plane of the non-actual and the plane of the actual must be thought at its root.

In short, at the root of negative absolute contingency regarding one event there lies latent positive relative contingency among two or more events. Schopenhauer too says that "the contingent is always only relative" (Das Zufällige ist immer nur relativ) (Schopenhauer, Sämtliche Werke, hrsg. v. Deussen, I, S. 550). And the positivity and relativity of positive relative contingency take on, as dynamic relativity, the meaning of encounter (遭遇) or Encounter (邂逅). 〔Tr.: Kuki pairs two words for encounter: 遭遇 (*sōgū*), a meeting that befalls one, rendered "encounter", and 邂逅 (*kaikō*), a chance meeting, rendered "Encounter".〕 [p. 147] Cournot too, defining chance, says that it is "an encounter between facts each rationally independent of the other" (une rencontre entre des faits rationnellement indépendants les uns des autres) (Cournot, Traité de l'enchaînement des idées fondamentales dans les sciences et dans l'histoire, nouvelle éd. 1922, p. 67), and also says that it is "a concurrence of two orders of rational facts independent of each other" (concours de deux ordres de faits rationnels, indépendants l'un de l'autre) (Cournot, Matérialisme, Vitalisme, Rationalisme, p. 227). This definition of chance by Cournot resembles Mill's definition cited earlier (see p. 136), but whereas Mill's definition has a range of application only to empirical chance, Cournot takes chance in the purely logical domain into consideration too. Cournot too at first took only empirical chance into view and gave the definition "events brought about by the combination or encounter of other events belonging to series independent of each other" (Les événements amenés par la combinaison ou la rencontre d'autres événements qui appartiennent à des séries indépendantes les unes des autres) (Cournot, Essai sur les fondements de nos connaissances, nouvelle éd. 1912, p. 39), but later he found its range of application too narrow and revised it. The definition thus revised includes not only teleological [p. 148] [コマ 81] chance and causal chance but chance of ground too, and is apt. In this point lies the superiority of Cournot's definition. In any case, chance is defined as encounter (遭遇) or Encounter (邂逅). The 偶 of 偶然 has the meaning of pair, facing, side by side, union. It has the same meaning as 遇 and means meeting. An even number (偶數) is a number based on one and one meeting and becoming two. The 偶 of 偶然 is the 偶 of 偶坐 (sitting face to face) and the 偶 of 配偶 (spouse). The core meaning of contingency is the Encounter of A and B, which negates the necessity of the law of identity, "A is A". We can, I think, define contingency as "the Encounter of two independent elements" (獨立なる二元の邂逅). Aristotle too sometimes uses the word "Encounter" (σύμπτωμα) in place of chance (Aristoteles, Phys. II, 8, 199a; De caelo II, 8, 289b; Rhet. I, 9, 1367b, etc.). And, as Schopenhauer too pointed out (Schopenhauer, Sämtliche Werke, hrsg. v. Deussen, I, S. 550, III, S. 196), beginning with σύμ-πτωμα, συμ-βεβηκός, con-tingens, ac-cidens and Zu-fall all set out clearly through their prefixes the contact of two elements. τύχη comes from τυγχάνειν, that is, "to hit". chance comes from cadentia, hasard from casus; that is, both have their source in cadere. And cadere (to fall) has the structure of in......cadere (to fall onto ......), as in "a stone falls onto the head of a passer-by", [p. 149] and, like Zu-fall, makes sense only through relativity. I have already said that Descartes too uses the expression "to fall by chance into the truth" (casu incidam in veritatem) (see p. 51). This in-cidere is the etymon of incident, and, needless to say, comes from in-cadere. That an apple *fell* by chance into Newton's field of vision, and that, when the Eitai Bridge collapsed, Miyokichi *fell* by chance into the boat of Shinsuke of Chijimiya, must be said to have a symbolic significance. Again, fortuitus, like fortuna, is based on fors and comes from ferre. This ferre (to bring) too shows the structure of in......ferre (to bring to ......) and expresses relativity. Further, chance (偶然) is also called 適然, and 適 is glossed "相當 is called 適", so that 適 clearly speaks of relativity. Other words concerning chance, such as "yukiatari-battari", "meguriawase", "shiawase" and "magure-atari", hint at relative positive contingency. 〔Tr.: 適然 (*tekizen*): chance, literally "fitting so". 相當曰適: "to correspond (相當) is called 適". *Yukiatari-battari*: hit-or-miss, happening upon; *meguriawase*: coming round together, fortune; *shiawase*: fortune, happiness (literally a doing-together); *magure-atari*: a fluke, a lucky hit.〕

That contingency takes a marked form in the Encounter of the two elements A and B is what Plato in the *Symposium* and Schopenhauer in "The Fate of the Individual" alike recognised. The legend of the Kojiki, that the two gods Izanagi [p. 150] [コマ 82] and Izanami said "You go round from the right and *meet* me, I will go round from the left and *meet* you" and "went round the heavenly pillar and *met*", is, quite apart from whether or not it is a rite performed since ancient times in India, a primitive event extremely rich in symbolism. Genji and Utsusemi too "*met by chance*" (wakuraba ni yuki-au) at the Ōsaka barrier. "I asked K whether he had gone out together with the young lady. K answered that it was not so. He explained that he had *met* them *by chance* at Masago-chō and so they had come back together" (Natsume Sōseki, *Kokoro*). In short, as the general character of positive hypothetical contingency, and more broadly as the character of the contingent itself in general, the meaning structure of the *Encounter* of *two independent elements* is witnessed.

### 16. The Book of Changes and chance

The guiding principle of the *Book of Changes* (*Zhouyi*) also lies in the contingent as Encounter (邂逅). The dualism of yin and yang is its essence throughout, and the unexpected Encounter of line A and line B is the foundation of the whole of the *Changes*. The line (爻) means [p. 151] crossing (交); it means nothing other than Encounter. The *Changes* is exchange (交易). That the *Xici zhuan* says "The sages had the means to see the movements under heaven and to observe their meeting and interpenetration (聖人有以見天下之動、而觀其會通) .... therefore these are called lines (是故謂之爻)" states that the *Changes* observes, in its concreteness, the contingent crossing of two elements (二元) as dynamic relativity within the changes and movements of heaven, earth and human life. "The two modes give birth to the four images" (兩儀生四象) is nothing other than the four kinds of contingent Encounter: yang line with yang line (old yang ⚌), yin line with yin line (old yin ⚏), yang line with yin line (lesser yang ⚎), yin line with yang line (lesser yin ⚍). Plato, besides recognising ⚌ and ⚏, recognised the Encounter of ⚊ and ⚋ as arising from the "o-me" (ἀνδρόγυνος), and so describes three ways of Encounter in all (Platon, Symposium, 191-192), but he does not go so far as to distinguish ⚎ from ⚍. That the *Changes* starts from the possibility of four kinds of combination shows a rigour sufficient for the Encounter of two elements to form, as guiding principle, the foundation of the whole system. "The four images give birth to the eight trigrams" (四象生八卦) is the eight kinds of chance, Qian, Kun, Zhen, Xun, Kan, Li, Gen and Dui, that arise when the four images again Encounter either a yang line or a yin line. Further, the eight trigrams and the eight trigrams cross one another by chance and give birth to the sixty-four hexagrams. In the light of the fact that the complete hexagram (大成の卦), formed by piling Encounter upon Encounter from the first line to the sixth, is called the "encountered hexagram" (遇卦), one can see how basic a meaning relative contingency has. In determining good and ill fortune (吉凶), [p. 152] [コマ 83] these relative contingencies have decisive meaning: which line, yin or yang, chances to fall into each line position of the hexagram; which line comes round to meet which line in the two corresponding trigrams, inner and outer; and above all, what lands squarely in the "centre" (中) of the trigram, that is, its middle place. The structure of the *Changes* is in every respect extremely simple, as is characteristic of a product of genius. The *Changes* is "simple change" (簡易). That is, it seeks to explain individual phenomena by the combination of the unchanging principles of yin and yang. That the *Changes* is "the unchanging" (不易) rests on the unchangeability and necessity of the abstract rule of the combination of yin and yang. Insofar as the question is how the two elements of yin and yang combine to produce individual concrete actualities, the *Changes* is "change" (變易). And the life that lies hidden deep at the root of the unchanging and change, and that makes the *Changes* breathe as the *Changes*, is "exchange" (交易). The *Tuan zhuan* says: "When heaven and earth do not interact, the ten thousand things do not flourish" (天地不交而萬物不興). The ambition "The *Changes* is commensurate with heaven and earth, and so can comprehend and order the way of heaven and earth" (易與天地準、故能彌綸天地之道) brings contingency as its consequence. It is in Xian, Heng and Weiji, and above all in the hexagrams Shihe and Gou, that the *Zhouyi* is clearly conscious of its own structure as positive relative contingency. Shihe, with Zhen below and Li above, has the figure ䷔. To understand this hexagram we must start from its resemblance to the hexagram Yi. Both are hexagrams concerned with the oral cavity. Yi, with Zhen below and Gen above, has the figure ䷚. [p. 153] That is, it is the shape of moving the jaws (頤) up and down to bite things. Shihe differs from Yi in that a hard yang line has come by chance into the fourth place. The *Tuan zhuan* says: "When there is something between the jaws, it is called Shihe" (頤中有物曰噬嗑). It goes on to say: "Biting through, and so success; the firm and the yielding are divided; movement and brightness; thunder and lightning unite and become manifest" (噬嗑而亨、剛柔分、動而明、雷電合而章). "Biting together" (噬嗑す), "the firm and the yielding are divided" and "thunder and lightning unite" are all determinations based on the contingent Encounter of Zhen and Li. "Biting together" is based on the visual impression of ䷔. "The firm and the yielding are divided" because Zhen is a yang trigram and firm, and Li is a yin trigram and yielding. "Thunder and lightning unite" because Zhen is thunder and Li is fire, and fire is interpreted as lightning. Further it says: "The yielding attains the centre and moves upward" (柔得中而上行). This "yielding" refers to the yin line in the fifth place, and it goes smoothly upward on the basis that this yin line has landed squarely in the centre, that is, the middle place, of the outer trigram. Again, that it says "not in its proper place" (不當位) means that, although the fifth place is a yang position, a yin line has come there by chance. The line statements say: "Six in the third place: biting dried meat, one meets poison" (六三、噬腊肉、遇毒). That biting meat one runs into poison is an interpretation of the contingency that, although the third place is a yang position, a yin line happens to have come there. "Nine in the fourth place: biting dried meat on the bone, one gets a metal arrow" (九四、噬乾胏、得金矢) means that on biting 乾胏, that is, dried meat on the bone, an arrowhead had remained in the bone. This contingency is an interpretation of the contingency that a hard yang line has come into the fourth place, [p. 154] [コマ 84] but it is not only that a yang line is in a yin position: the impression that the figure ䷔ makes on sight itself endorses this interpretation. The hexagram Shihe itself places the whole of its weight on the contingency of the nine in the fourth place. Further, "Six in the fifth place: biting dried meat, one gets yellow gold" (六五、噬乾肉、得黃金) is also an interpretation of the contingency that a yin line has come into the fifth place, a yang position, which is the centre of the trigram Li. Next, Gou, with Xun below and Qian above, has the form ䷫. The *Tuan zhuan* says: "Gou is encounter; the yielding encounters the firm" (姤遇也、柔遇剛也). And since "to meet without expecting it is called encounter" (不期而會曰遇), in the hexagram Gou a single yin by chance encounters pure yang. One yielding line happens to encounter (*kaikō*, 邂逅) five firm lines. Gou (姤) is none other than the 逅 of Encounter, *kaikō* (邂逅). However, the meaning of Encounter is not limited to this hexagram Gou alone. The Encounter of two elements is, as the essence of every hexagram, the foundation of the *Changes* as a whole. The *Tuan zhuan*'s "Heaven and earth encounter each other, and all kinds of things become manifest" (天地相遇、品物咸章也) must have that meaning. It is the same with the *Xici zhuan*'s saying: "Heaven and earth intermingle and the ten thousand things are transformed and refined; male and female join their essences and the ten thousand things are transformed and born" (天地絪縕、萬物化醇、男女構精、萬物化生). Things such as the so-called corresponding lines (應爻) are also simply one manifestation of "heaven and earth encounter each other", "heaven and earth intermingle" or "male and female join their essences". For example, the contingent relation between the third line and the top line is a correspondence of lines. In the hexagram Kui it says: "Nine at the top: opposed and alone. One sees a pig covered in mud, a cartload of ghosts. First one draws the bow, afterwards one unstrings the bow. No robber, a suitor for marriage. Going on, one meets rain, and then good fortune" (上九、睽孤、見豕負塗、載鬼一車、先張之弧、後說之弧、匪寇婚媾、往遇雨則吉). [p. 155] Kui, with Dui below and Li above, takes the figure ䷥. The nine at the top and the six in the third place originally came round to each other by chance. Because it is by chance, the nine at the top somehow harbours doubt towards the six in the third place. On the way from the nine at the top to the six in the third place, it happens to come round to the trigram Kan ☵. Kan is water, and is rain. "Going on, one meets rain, and then good fortune" means that it is good fortune that the nine at the top meets rain by chance on the way to the six in the third place. Rain washes away even doubt and dissolves it. "The many doubts vanish" (群疑亡也). This correspondence of lines is no more than one example, but the *Zhouyi*, in its concrete structure, whatever case one takes, shows throughout the structure of positive relative contingency as Encounter. The Encounter of the two elements yin and yang must be the whole life of the *Zhouyi*.

〔Tr.: Kuki prints the digrams and the stacked trigram pairs as figures in the line of text. They are given here by the Unicode characters whose names match his labels (⚌ greater yang, ⚏ greater yin, ⚎ lesser yang, ⚍ lesser yin; ䷔ Biting Through, ䷚ Jaws, ䷫ Coming to Meet, ䷥ Opposition); the stroke order of the printed figures has not been checked line by line.〕

### 17. Simultaneous contingency and successive contingency

Of positive relative contingency, positive contingency of ground belongs purely to the logical domain and so has no relation to temporal determination, but the other two, the empirical positive contingencies, are correlations lacking causality and purposiveness, and so the temporal moment has decisive meaning for them. [p. 156] [コマ 85] It is for this reason that chance is called "a bounce of *time*" (時のはずみ). That the *Xici zhuan* says "The six lines intermingle; they are only the things of their *time*" (六爻相雜唯其時物也) also says that the contingency of the six lines meeting together depends on time. In the Old Testament book of Ecclesiastes too it says: "Again I turned myself about and looked under the sun: the swift do not win the race, the strong do not win the battle, the wise do not obtain food, the quick-witted do not obtain treasure, the knowledgeable do not obtain favour. All that befalls human beings is a thing of *time*, a thing of *chance*. And human beings do not know their time. As fish are caught in the evil net and birds are caught in the bird net, so the people of the world too, when the *time* of misfortune comes upon them unforeseen, are caught by that misfortune" (chapter 9, verses 11-12). In Natsume Sōseki's *Kōjin* too it says: "When I began to write this letter, your brother was sleeping soundly. Now, as I finish writing it, he is again sleeping soundly. I think it strange that I began writing *by chance* at a *time* when your brother was asleep and finish writing *by chance* at a *time* when your brother is asleep. Somewhere I have the feeling that if he never woke from this sleep, how happy he would be" (*Kōjin*, "Jinrō", 52). It should not be thought strange that the *Kojiki*, in expressing contingency, has no more than the word "time". For example: "So, having been driven out, he descended to the land of Torikami on the upper reaches of the Hi in the land [p. 157] of Izumo. At this very *time*, chopsticks came floating down that river. Thereupon Susanoo, thinking there must be people upstream, went up in search, and there were an old man and an old woman, the two of them, weeping with a young girl set between them" (vol. 1). "Then Haya-Susanoo drew the ten-fist sword that he wore at his side and cut the serpent to pieces, and the river Hi turned to blood as it flowed. Now, at the *time* he cut its middle tail, the edge of his sword broke. Thinking this strange, he pierced and split it with the point of his sword and looked, and there was the great sword *tsumugari*. So he took this great sword and, thinking it a wondrous thing, reported it to Amaterasu" (vol. 1). "So at the *time* he arrived at the village of Kohata, he met a beautiful maiden at the crossroads. Thereupon the Emperor asked the maiden, 'Whose child are you?', and she answered, saying, 'I am the daughter of Wani no Hifure no Ōmi, and my name is Miyanushi Yakawaehime'" (vol. 2).

Empirical positive contingency, being on the horizon of time, has *simultaneity* or *succession* as its constitutive determination. That Mill said "coexist, or one follows the other, by chance" is for that reason (see p. 136). The *Kojiki* examples are all three simultaneous contingency. There is also, besides, successive contingency. For example, that a white fish leapt into the boat when King Wu of Zhou reached Mengjin and crossed [p. 158] [コマ 86] the river, and that a sea bass leapt into the boat on the sea on the way when Kiyomori went to Kumano, are a successive contingency. There is a short story by Satomi Ton entitled "An Unfortunate Chance". When a certain nervous woman threw a glass bottle out of a train window, the train was just coming onto a bridge over a busy street. She thought she heard a baby cry out in the street below. One or two months later, when she had gone to the public bath, the glass of the skylight in the ceiling broke and the shards of glass very nearly fell on her naked body. The plot is that from then on, with worry over whether the glass bottle she had thrown from the train window had injured someone as its origin, the woman went mad. That she threw a glass bottle from the train window and that the glass window in the ceiling broke are a successive contingency. Besides these, the correspondence (符合) between dream and the actual is also a successive contingency. For example, a case such as dreaming one night of a person one had never thought of at all, and that person coming to visit the next day, is nothing other than a successive contingency.

However, insofar as the meaning of positive relative contingency lies, as dynamic relativity, in encounter and Encounter (*sōgū kaikō*, 遭遇邂逅), the meaning of the contingent appears more clearly in simultaneity than in succession. [p. 159] Not only that, successive contingency always forms a compound that has simultaneous contingency as its basis. Even looking independently only at the fact that a white fish leapt into King Wu's boat, it is contingent. Between the course of King Wu's boat and the white fish's leaping in there is a simultaneous contingency. It is precisely because it is contingent that the *Shiji* says "King Wu bent down, took it, and offered it in sacrifice" (武王俯取以祭). Again, that a sea bass leapt into Kiyomori's boat is already by itself contingent. To let the *Heike monogatari* say it, it is "the blessing of the Gongen" (權現の御利生). It is where these two simultaneous contingencies become the basis and are further placed in a relative relation to each other that a successive contingency is constituted. In the example of the woman who went mad too, that, at the time she threw the glass bottle from the train window, the train was just coming onto the bridge over the crowded street, is already by itself an "unfortunate chance". A minute or two faster or slower, and that thing would not have happened. That, when she had gone to the public bath, the glass window in the ceiling just then broke is also an "unfortunate chance". And it is where these two simultaneous "unfortunate chances" became the basis and formed a further compound that there was one successive "unfortunate chance" that led that woman to madness. In the dream example too, that one dreamed of a person one had never thought of at all is a teleological contingency for the dreamer. [p. 160] [コマ 87] Again, that a person one had never thought of came to visit is a teleological contingency for the master of the house. These two simultaneous contingencies become the basis, and one successive contingency is constituted in the form of the correspondence between dream and the actual.
