Let us make the role of simultaneous contingency in the constitution of successive contingency clearer still. The two or more simultaneous contingencies that are the constitutive moments of a successive contingency must show a marked similarity in the character of the events. Successive contingency rather takes the form of something like a mere repetition of one and the same simultaneous contingency. And it is where one simultaneous contingency is offered repeatedly that there is the particular impressive force possessed by the contingency of successive contingency. Speaking of the present examples, the single contingent "a fish leapt into the boat of a ruler or a general" was repeated twice with identity. Likewise, the single contingent "glass was broken in a dangerous place" was repeated twice with identity. Again, the single contingent "the appearance of an unexpected person A" was repeated twice, in dream and in the actual. In Naruse Mukyoku's "Dialogue on Chance" too an example of this kind of successive contingency is given. In Arashiyama in Kyoto he met an actress by chance. That actress's friend, an actress, was again an actress [p. 161] he had once got to know by chance at a certain dance hall. Then shortly afterwards, again by chance, a certain book came up there, and the man to whom that book is dedicated was the man who wrote a book entitled "The Actress" (Naruse Mukyoku, *Gūzen mondō* 444-450). The single contingent "ran into an actress" is repeated three times with identity.

Now, when the simultaneous contingencies that are the constitutive moments of a successive contingency have in themselves a fairly strong impressive force, it is not necessary that the number of repetitions required to constitute the successive contingency be large. That two or three repetitions suffice can be known from the examples above. However, when the simultaneous contingencies that are the constitutive moments have in themselves no more than a weak impressive force, a large number of repetitions is required to constitute the successive contingency. For example, if we look at the great earthquakes of the last ten-odd years, the Kantō earthquake was on 1 September, the Tajima earthquake on 23 May, the Tango earthquake on 7 March and the Izu earthquake on 26 November. The sum of nine and one, the sum of five, two and three, the sum of three and seven, and the sum of one, one, two and six are all ten. That the Kantō earthquake occurred on 1 September, that is, on a day whose month and day sum to ten, is [p. 162] [コマ 88] certainly a simultaneous contingency, but as a single isolated fact it is something hardly worth notice. By contrast, when the single contingent "an earthquake occurs on a date whose month and day sum to ten" is repeated over several times with identity, a successive contingency is constituted there with a fairly strong impressive force. Again, suppose that a certain person set out on a journey on the fourth of the month. Suppose that when he boarded the train the station platform was platform no. 4. The carriage he boarded happened to be carriage no. 4. The fellow passengers were four in all. Suppose the porter's number was also number 4. In this case, "person A runs into the number four" is something contingent of weak impressive force, but where that single simultaneous contingency is repeated successively over several times, the contingent is constituted embodying sufficient impressive force. Consequently, in a case like this, where one simultaneous contingency alone has no more than weak force and becomes forceful as a successive contingency only after being repeated several times, it cannot be denied that the successiveness of the contingent comes to have a relatively independent meaning, but even so the indispensable condition for a successive contingency to be constituted is that its individual events are simultaneous contingencies. If all the factors had lacked contingency [p. 163] of their own, successive contingency could not be constituted either. In the present example, if the person had chosen the number four in every case by his own intention, it goes without saying that there would no longer be any room for any successive contingency to arise.

Such contingency, in that a single thing is repeated several times with identity, is, to let Proust, the author of *In Search of Lost Time*, say it: "They exist simultaneously in the present and the past, not actual but real, not abstract but ideal. .... An instant (*setsuna*, 刹那) freed from the order of time creates again in us the human being freed from the order of time, and makes us feel that instant (*setsuna*, 刹那)" (Marcel Proust, *Le Temps retrouvé*, II, p. 16). This kind of successive contingency may be said to be the contingency possessed by the recurring decimal 142857, 142857 ..., by personal names of stroke count eighteen, eighteen, eighteen ..., by rhyme held throughout on one sound, i, i, i, i, and so on, which we gave earlier as examples of positive contingency of ground (see pp. 59-63), appearing within time. Fujitsubo, Utsusemi, Yūgao, the Rokujō Lady, Murasaki, Suetsumuhana, Gen no Naishi, Oborozukiyo no Naishi, Aoi, Hanachirusato, the Akashi Lady, the Saigū Consort, Asagao and Tamakazura are all, for Hikaru Genji, "happy chances" repeated with identity. The twelve [p. 164] [コマ 89] initials V, Aⁿ, Aᵈ, M, Mⁱ, Aˡ, Aⁱⁿᵉ, Aᵖᵍ, Mᵈᵉ, C, G, A, written with a stick on the sand of the shore of Lake Albano, are all, for Stendhal, "unhappy chances" repeated with identity. Again, recurrent metaphysical time such as transmigration may also be considered a successive contingency constituted by a single simultaneous contingency being repeated an infinite number of times with identity, "again and again" (πάλιν καὶ πάλιν) (see Kuki, "Metaphysical Time", in the collection of essays commemorating Dr Tomonaga's sixtieth birthday). Successive contingency is in fact recurrent contingency. Further, the contingent of this kind, through the identity that repetition has, takes the mode of "the necessity of chance", and moreover has a prospect of pressing close to the concept of fate.

Cournot gives the following as an example of chance (Cournot, *Traité de l'enchaînement des idées fondamentales dans les sciences et dans l'histoire*, nouvelle éd., 1922, p. 67-68). I thought of weighing a pebble that my foot happened to strike, but had only a kilogram balance to hand. When I weighed it with that, it was found to be between three kilos and four kilos. I wrote the figure 3 on a slip of paper. I bought a hectogram balance and weighed the part that could not be weighed in kilograms, and it came to 3. To the right of the earlier figure 3 I wrote 3. It is clear that there is no [p. 165] necessary connection whatever between the figure obtained earlier and the figure obtained now. For there can be no connection whatever between, on one side, the cause that gave this pebble the weight it has at this instant when I am weighing it and, on the other, the reason that made the French legislators fix the kilogram as the unit of weight and divide it by the decimal system. Therefore, in fixing the figure for hectograms, I do not expect to find any figure in particular among the ten figures of decimal numeration. Even if I obtain the figure 3 again, that is certainly a strange combination, but there is no necessary link in it and the encounter is merely contingent. I can go further and fix the figure for decagrams. And again, going on to grams, decigrams, centigrams, I can fix figures as far as milligrams in the end. And as a result I obtain a series of seven figures, but I do not expect to encounter any regularity in it. If I obtained the figure 3 all seven times, then precisely that extremely rare case has occurred, which has only the possibility of happening just once in 60,480 times. It is like putting 60,480 different Chinese characters all jumbled together [p. 166] [コマ 90] in one jar and happening to pull out the Chinese character one wants. That is a very strange case, but since there can be no connection whatever between the weight of this pebble picked up by the roadside and the establishment of the units of weights and measures and of the decimal graduation, however strange it is, it is chance. In this example of chance from Cournot, the combination of seven 3s is in itself a positive contingency of ground, but for the person weighing it appears within time as a successive contingency, three, three, three, three, three, three, three. Cournot gives a case where this contingency can be necessity just as it is. This time it is not that one's foot stumbles on a pebble: it is the case where a vessel filled with mercury has come into one's hands. Even if the weight of the mercury were as strange as that of the pebble earlier, this time a completely different conclusion is drawn. Since by arithmetic's count one third, converted into a decimal, becomes the recurring decimal 0.3333333 ..., one concludes that this mercury was one third of 10 kilograms of mercury. Probably some physicist somewhere weighed 10 kilograms of mercury very exactly and divided it into three equal parts in order to carry out some comparative experiment. What he put aside as one of them [p. 167] must have come into my hands. Then the combination of seven 3s becomes something necessary. Successive contingency, that is, recurrent contingency, is thus something that has the possibility of reversing into necessity.

Bergson, in his book *Laughter*, discusses the comic quality that repetition has. "Suppose I meet in the street one day a friend I have not seen for a long time. That situation has nothing comic about it at all. But if on that same day I meet him again, and further meet him a third and a fourth time, in the end the two of us will come to laugh together at that 'coincidence' (符合)" (Bergson, *Le rire*, p. 91). This is the cheerful laughter of divine wisdom at the play of successive contingency. However, is meeting in the street, quite unexpectedly, a friend one had not expected at all, in itself something that "has nothing comic about it at all"? Is there not already a "coincidence" there, and a certain degree of comic quality? And it must be that the slight comic quality that accompanies a simultaneous contingency increased in geometric progression through successive repetition.

In the above, I think, we have made clear the relation between simultaneous contingency and successive contingency, and in particular that simultaneous contingency has a basic meaning and takes part in the constitution of successive contingency. Further, on the basis of this [p. 168] [コマ 91] point it would also be possible to discuss the fact that the temporal character of contingency is presentness, but let us not deal with that problem here and leave it until later (see chapter 3, section 9).

### 18. Contingency and spatio-temporal delimitation

We have made clear that the principal moment of positive relative contingency in the empirical world is simultaneity. And simultaneity, by its essence, suggests spatiality. In other words, contingency in the eminent sense lies at the point of intersection of two, or more than two, causal series, and is constituted in "here and now" (hic et nunc). It is delimited to the spatial "here" and the temporal "now". Looking at the passages of the *Kojiki* quoted earlier too, temporal delimitation coexists with spatial delimitation. Spatial delimitations such as "Torikami on the upper reaches of the Hi in the land of Izumo" or "the village of Kohata" cannot be dispensed with in order to constitute empirical contingency.

Further, the role of spatio-temporal delimitation in empirical positive contingency is also easily understood by looking at the structure of so-called directional divination. To judge good and ill fortune by direction is nothing other than to try to understand the good and ill fortune that one is to encounter by reducing it [p. 169] to the contingency of direction. But the concrete content of direction in that case is not direction itself. It is direction delimited by time. Moving towards a certain space at a certain time is judged good or ill for a certain particular person. Therefore, even in a case where moving to a certain direction has been judged unlucky, provided only that a change is given to the time, a change comes about in the concrete content of the direction, so there is no harm in moving to the same direction. So long as no change is given to the time, it is required to choose, at the same time, a direction having other content. In short, directional divination constructs positive contingency purely intentionally, with space and time. After that, it mobilises all conceivable good and ill fortune and seeks the fulfilment of meaning of this positive contingency constructed a priori. For example, it is said that Prince Itō Hirobumi's moving in early January of Meiji 41 from the Sōrōkaku in Ōiso to the Onshikan in Ōmori was a direction of great misfortune and brought about his killing in Harbin the following year. The reason is as follows. Prince Itō was born in the old ninth month of Tenpō 12. Tenpō 12 is *kanoto-ushi*, and its year star is Six White. Again, the old ninth month of *ushi* has Six White as its month star. That is, Prince Itō's natal star is Six [p. 170] [コマ 92] White for both the year star and the month star. Now, January of Meiji 41 still belongs to the old twelfth month of the previous year. Therefore it is Meiji 40 that is in question. But Meiji 40 is a year of Three Jade in the central palace. Again, this year is *hinoto-hitsuji*, and the old twelfth month of *hitsuji* is a month of Three Jade in the central palace. From Ōiso, Ōmori falls to the north-east, that is, to Gen. But in a year of Three Jade and a month of Three Jade, Six White has come to Gen. By moving house towards the Six White of the year star and the Six White of the month star, Prince Itō committed the great misfortune of *honmeisatsu*. Moreover Gen, as the direction diametrically opposite the direction (south-west) in which *hitsuji*, the branch of that year, lies, is a direction of great misfortune called *saiha*. In short, that Prince Itō, whose natal star is Six White, moved house to such a direction in January of Meiji 41, that is, in the old twelfth month belonging to Meiji 40, amounts to committing *honmeisatsu* and *saiha* on top of each other. Even if he moved house in the same year and the same month, it would have been fine had he chosen a direction other than Gen. Again, since the concrete character of the direction Gen is delimited by time, even if he moved house to the same Gen, he would not have touched great misfortune if only he had changed the time of the move. The contradiction in principle and the basic delusion of directional divination lie in trying to construct primordial fact a priori, but insofar as it regards space and time as the sole delimiters of empirical positive contingency, [p. 171] it may also be said to touch one side of the truth.

Further, the decisive meaning of spatiality and temporality in empirical positive contingency will also be clearly recognised from the following case. "As driver Kosaka Masao and assistant Ri Fukuhō, carrying the liquor merchant Kawamura Hiroshi in Hyōgo 4252, were on their way towards Higashi-Naniwa, at the *instant* (*setsuna*, 刹那) they were about to cross the Hanshin railway level crossing (*about 130 metres west of Amagasaki stop*), an up two-car train, nos. 307 and 361 (driver Yamada Tetsuzō), happened to come charging along and crashed into the side of the car; the car was wrecked, driver Kosaka was killed outright with his forehead crushed and assistant Ri with the back of his head crushed, and the passenger Kawamura was injured in the knee joint of his left leg" (*Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun*, 15 May 1930). With a difference of one metre, a difference of one second, this unfortunate chance need not have occurred. It is for that reason that Aristotle says of contingency "at some place and at some time" (καὶ ποὺ καὶ ποτέ) (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ. 30, 1025a 22).

〔Tr.: The Nine Star (九星) terms are given in English or in romanised Japanese: 年家 year star, 月家 month star, 六白 Six White, 三碧中宮 Three Jade in the central palace, 本命 natal star, 本命殺 *honmeisatsu*, 歲破 *saiha*, 對中 diametrically opposite, 艮 Gen (north-east). The readings of the names 李馥法 (Ri Fukuhō) and 川村廣 (Kawamura Hiroshi) are not certain.〕

### 19. History and chance

[p. 172] [コマ 93] What are called irrationality and contingency within history are based on the concrete delimitation of time and space. As Poincaré says, that King John Lackland passed by here at some time is, for Carlyle, who is a historian, a precious reality that could not be exchanged for all the theories in the world. But for Bacon, the natural scientist, since John Lackland will never pass by here again, it is a matter of no consequence (Poincaré, *La Science et l'Hypothèse*, p. 168). Natural science places its concern only on the rationality and necessity, or else the probability, of law. On the contrary, for history the irrationality and contingency of mere fact possess a life that cannot be thrown away. Even with regard to something like a storm, natural science seeks some lawlike necessity or probability. As causes, the relation of the distribution of land and sea, changes in weather, temperature and air pressure, and so on, are taken into account. And if these definite conditions are fulfilled, the same storm phenomenon is produced, any number of times, necessarily or with the greatest [p. 173] probability. Even in a case where the centre of the storm always lies at a fixed point and the time of its occurrence is also confined to a fixed season, the concern of natural science lies in the abstract periodicity of the storm phenomenon. The necessity and universality of the law must be made clear abstractly. There, prediction of the future is possible, and defensive installations can be considered. Even if we try saying probability instead of necessity, there is no difference whatever in idea. In contrast, for history the storm that arose in such-and-such a year, such-and-such a month, at such-and-such a place, is in question in its once-only character, just as it is in its full concreteness. That a storm arose near Takashima in Hizen on the first day of the intercalary seventh month of Kōan 4 is, for history, a matter of concern in its full concreteness. At the time of the Mongol invasion, the Retired Emperor Kameyama presented a votive prayer to the Ise Shrine and sought to take the national crisis upon his own person. Hōjō Tokimune beheaded the Yuan envoys at Tatsunokuchi and showed his firm resolve. That a storm arose near Takashima on the first day of the intercalary seventh month of Kōan 4 and that, as a result, the enemy army of several tens of thousands was destroyed in the sea, is a chance worthy of special mention in history as "the divine wind of Ise". The irrationality of history is based on the once-only delimitation of space and time, and it is against the background of this concrete delimitation that contingency appears. "By chance, on the day of a great festival in the town, among [p. 174] [コマ 94] the crowds of people gathered there, at the time when the two of us met for the first time, we were at once, just as we were, bound together most closely. And since then nothing has been as close to the two of us as we were to each other" (Montaigne, *Essays*, I, trans. Sekine Hideo, pp. 347-348). What may be seen as the extreme limit of historical irrationality is the following example. "The man called Murakoshi Sanjūrō is the man who happened to be passing just in front when the famous Akechi Mitsuhide was stabbed with a bamboo spear. ...... Although he was resigned to being human refuse with nothing whatever to do with history, because he was by chance the bystander of a certain event, he unexpectedly came to be noticed by history" (Tatsuno Yutaka, *Riyan*, p. 201).

Hegel, on the one hand, presupposes as truth that an ultimate purpose rules in history, that there is reason in history, and under that presupposition he says that "philosophical contemplation has no intention other than to eliminate the contingent" (Hegel, *Vorlesungen über die Philosophie der Weltgeschichte*, hrsg. v. Lasson, I, S. 5). On the other hand, however, he also recognises the right of chance. "With regard to spirit and its activity too, we must guard against being led astray by the well-meant effort of rational cognition into pointing out as necessary phenomena that have the character of contingency, [p. 175] or into trying to construct them, as it is called, a priori. For example, although language is, so to speak, the body of thought, in language chance undoubtedly plays a marked role. The same holds for law, art and other forms" (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 145, Zusatz, S. 195). "It is reason itself that acknowledges that contingency, contradiction and semblance have their own sphere and right, though a restricted sphere and right" (Hegel, *Grundlinien der Philosophie des Rechts*, hrsg. v. Lasson, S. 173). According to Cournot, the distinction between history and science, between the historical element and the scientific element, is more essential than Bacon thought. The distinction does not depend on the fact that two different faculties, memory and reason, exist within the human mind. Even if human beings had never used memory to write history, and never used reason to do science, in the course of phenomena there are, on the one hand, constant laws, so that systematic coherence is possible, and, on the other hand, room is left for the products of chance, that is, for the products of the contingent conjunction of various causal series independent of one another. The concept of chance has, after all, its foundation in nature [p. 176] [コマ 95] itself, and does not depend solely on the weakness of the human mind. The same holds for the distinction between the historical datum and the theoretical datum. Even an intelligence able to trace the series of phases through which the solar system has passed much further back than we can would encounter, as we do, primitive, capricious, contingent facts. And it would have to acknowledge such facts under the name of the historical datum. The historical datum means the result of the contingent concurrence of causes that acted in a still more distant past (Cournot, *Essai sur les fondements de nos connaissances*, Chap. XX, *Du contraste de l'histoire et de la science*, nouvelle édition, p. 460).

〔Tr.: Section 19 begins at the top of p. 172 (right page of コマ 93), not on p. 173.〕

### 20. The objectivity of chance

Chance is what brushes past in "this place" and winks in "this instant". What Hegel calls "indifferent" (*gleichgültig*) (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 250) is sublated into a fleeting concern. Contingency is nothing other than the apex character of concern at [p. 177] the point of intersection of indifference with indifference. What structure and what existence does this apex-like point of intersection possess?

That Poincaré considers "the complexity of causes" (Poincaré, *Science et Méthode*, pp. 73-76) to be the principal meaning of contingency is because he assumes that the point of intersection can be completely explained by the converging causal series. Spinoza says that "a thing is called contingent only because of *the imperfection of our knowledge* (*defectus nostrae cognitionis*), and for no other reason" (Spinoza, *Ethica*, I. 33. Schol. 1). Hume ascribes chance to "*our ignorance* (our ignorance) of the real causes of some event" (Hume, *An Enquiry concerning Human Understanding*, VI). Laplace regards chance as "the expression of the *ignorance* in which we are of the true causes (*l'expression de l'ignorance*)" (Laplace, *Essai philosophique sur les probabilités*, I, Paris, Gauthier-Villars, 1921, p. 2). All of these presuppose that the point of intersection is in reality not a point of intersection of indifference with indifference but a point of intersection brought about by concern, and presuppose a complete knowledge of that point of intersection. In this way they came to produce the theory that chance is merely something subjective, nothing other than a confession of insufficient knowledge.

[p. 178] [コマ 96] However, there are two points that we must consider against this. First, it is of course the case that a certain point of intersection, even though it looks like a point of intersection of indifference with indifference, has in reality been brought about by causal concern. But should we therefore think that every point of intersection depends in advance on concern, in the way that all the knots in all the threads of a whole cast net converge on the single point at its crown? That is, can one so casually argue that everything called chance is two or more causes acting *together* and producing the point of intersection necessarily, or that there is no chance whatever so long as we know the *connection* of the causes of phenomena? Where the special case, in which what was thought to be chance in fact arose from a necessary conjunction of causes, is casually generalised and the inference is drawn that all chance is the product of the necessary conjunction of causes, is there not, on the contrary, an insufficiency of knowledge or an immaturity of thought? We must consider that there can also be cases in which wholly independent series intersect at a point of intersection. Cournot asks whether the word chance corresponds to an idea with a density of its own, whether that [p. 179] idea has an object outside us and has consequences that we cannot escape, or whether it is rather no more than a "breath of the voice" (*flatus vocis*), a deception to dress up our ignorance, which does not know the true causes. And he answers "no". "No, the word chance is not something unrelated to external actuality. The word chance expresses an idea that has a manifestation within observable phenomena and has an efficacy taken into account in the governance of the world. This idea is an idea based on reason. ...... That is, it is the idea of the actual independence (*indépendance actuelle*) and of the contingent encounter (*rencontre accidentelle*) of various chains or various series of causes" (Cournot, *Matérialisme, Vitalisme, Rationalisme*, p. 222). Suppose that a meteorite falls in a white-hot state and makes a powder magazine explode. In that case chance has a share in the explosion of the powder magazine. For there is no connection whatever between, on the one hand, the causes that determine the direction in which the meteorite advances towards the earth and its falling at a certain definite point on the earth's surface, and, on the other hand, the cause that determined that explosives should be stored at exactly the point where the meteorite would in future fall. Instead of a powder magazine, we may suppose that a meteorite fell on a natural solfatara or an oil source [p. 180] [コマ 97] and set it alight. There is no connection whatever between, on the one hand, the work by which nature brought solfataras and oil sources into existence and, on the other hand, the forces that determined the direction of the meteorite's advance and the point at which the meteorite touches the earth (Ib., pp. 47-48). In short, chance is nothing other than the conjunction between systems of causes and facts, each unfolding independently in its own series. Where an intelligence greater than the human differs from the human on this point is merely that it makes fewer errors than human beings, or that it never makes errors. It is merely that such an intelligence never, in any case, thinks of series that in truth influence each other as independent, or, on the contrary, imagines a linkage between causes that are in truth independent. Such an intelligence recognises with strict precision what, in the course of phenomena, belongs to the domain of the necessary and what belongs to the domain of chance. Chance is by no means something subjective. Chance has objectivity. "In this sense it is true to say that chance governs the world, or rather that chance has a part, and a notable part, in the governance of the world" [p. 181] (*Il est vrai de dire en ce sens que le hasard gouverne le monde, ou plutôt qu'il a une part, et une part notable, dans le gouvernement du monde.*) (Cournot, *Essai sur les fondements de nos connaissances*, nouvelle édition, p. 47). We thought of chance as the apex at which indifference and indifference intersect. To acknowledge the indifference of chance in the strict sense is the reason for admitting the objectivity of chance.

Second, even if we suppose that a point of intersection is a point of intersection brought about by the concern of causality, we must consider that a complete explanation and an adequate knowledge of that point of intersection are merely an idea. The causal series that determine a point of intersection are in fact innumerable. When Aristotle says that the contingent attributes of an individual are "unlimited" (ἄπειρος) (Met. E. 2, 1026b; Phys. II. 5), or that the contingent cause is "indeterminate" (ἀόριστος) (Met. Δ. 30, 1024b; Phys. II. 5) 〔Tr.: Kuki's reference is as printed. Book Δ, chapter 30, on the accidental, is at 1025a (1025a14-34); 1024b falls within Δ 29.〕, or that there is no science of the contingent (Met. E. 2), this is based on the innumerability of the causal series converging on the point of intersection and, connected with this, on the innumerability of the points of intersection themselves. When Leibniz speaks, concerning contingent truths, of "detail without bounds" (*détail sans bornes*) or of "the infinite" (*infinité*) (Leibniz, *Monadologie*, 36), it is for the same [p. 182] [コマ 98] reason. The complete explanation of one point of intersection would probably presuppose the complete explanation of an infinite number of points of intersection. And the complete explanation of an infinite number of points of intersection must be a total emptiness that leaves not a single point of intersection. If it is said that the contingency of points of intersection depends wholly on the subject, this is nothing other than to subjectivise everything, and we must arrive at the conclusion that nothing any longer has objectivity.

### 21. From hypothetical contingency to disjunctive contingency

Let us try standing on the standpoint opposite to the one taken so far. Let us try thinking about the formation of the point of intersection while excluding chance as far as possible. Where A and B intersect at point C, for example where air current A and air current B rub against each other at a point on a line of discontinuity, or where man A and man B meet at a certain spot on the street, let us think that A has A′ as its cause and A′ has A″, that B has B′ as its cause and B′ has B″, and that A″ and B″ have S as their common cause. Then [p. 183] the Encounter (邂逅) of A and B cannot be called chance in the strict sense. However, S itself in turn means the point of intersection of M and N. There is room for chance there. But the causal series M M′ M″, which includes M, and the causal series N N′ N″, which includes N, can be thought to have a further common cause, T. However, that T too means the point of intersection of one causal series with another causal series. There is room for chance there. But these two series too can be thought to have a common cause. In this way we go back to x. What, then, is this x? Whilst assuming, within the domain of experience, the rule of necessity throughout, we pursued x as an idea "without end". However, when we have been able to grasp the idea beyond the "infinite", we must know that that idea is "primal contingency". As Schelling says, "of it one can only say that it *is*, and not that it *necessarily is*" (*man kann von ihm nur sagen, dass es Ist, nicht, dass es notwendig Ist*). It is "the oldest primal contingency" (*der älteste Urzufall*) (Schelling, *Sämmtliche Werke*, 1856-1857, II. 1. S. 464; II. 2. S. 153).

[p. 184] [コマ 99] The aforementioned bhikkhu Nāgasena asked Milinda in return, "Why are these trees not the same?" Milinda answered the bhikkhu, and answered himself, by saying, "What is not the same is so because the original plantings each differ." However, the contingency of the trees has merely been transferred, by the law of causality, to the contingency of the seedlings. Again, when the *Madhyama Āgama* (fascicle 44, the *Parrot Sutra*) says, "Know that this deed has such a retribution", and when the *Tattvasiddhi* (fascicle 10, chapter on the causes of deeds) says, "The ten thousand things arise from deeds as cause", it is as if they gave contingency a causal explanation, but in reality they merely extend contingency, unresolved, infinitely as far as "primal contingency". Thus the problem is transferred from the empirical domain of hypothetical contingency to the metaphysical domain of disjunctive contingency.

---

## Chapter 3. Disjunctive contingency (第三章 離接的偶然) [コマ 99-166]

### 1. The meaning of disjunctive contingency

[p. 185] [コマ 99] Disjunctive contingency concerns the relation between whole and part. The whole, by the very character of being a whole, has absolute identity. Insofar as it is determined as something complete, the whole is self-identical through and through. Therefore necessity accompanies the existence of the whole. By contrast, the part, by the very character of being a part, lacks absolute self-identity. The part, insofar as it is a part, presupposes other parts. The part has within itself the character of being able to be this part or that part. The contingency of the part lies in that point. And since each part, while enveloped within the whole, stands in a disjunctive relation to the others, the contingent of this kind is called disjunctive [p. 186] [コマ 100] contingency. For example, a triangle is an acute triangle, or a right triangle, or an obtuse triangle. Insofar as no other triangle can be conceived, the triangle is regarded as something extensionally complete as one whole. And the acute triangle has contingency in relation to the triangle as a whole in the point that, instead of being an acute triangle, it could also be a right triangle or an obtuse triangle. Water is liquid, or solid, or gas. Insofar as no other state can be thought, water, viewed extensionally, can be considered a whole containing parts. And water as liquid has contingency in relation to water as a whole in the point that, instead of being liquid, it could also be solid or gas. Of course the part must have a sufficient ground for its own existence. But instead of that part existing by that ground, it is also *possible* that another part exist by another ground. Disjunctive contingency lies in that point. The word *tamatama* is sometimes written with the character 儻, and 儻 means "a word of what may be so" (或然之詞), that is, "or" or "perhaps". 〔Tr.: 儻 (Chinese *tang*), "if, perchance". 或然之詞 is a classical Chinese lexical gloss.〕 Therefore the contingent that the character 儻 means must be the contingent of the case "A, or B, or C, or D", that is, disjunctive contingency. [p. 187] For example, in the case "In my heart I do not forget you, yet the days when *tamatama* (儻) I do not see you are many, and the months have passed" (Man'yō, book 4), the year as a whole contains three hundred and sixty-five days as its parts. Suppose they met on a certain day of a certain month: that day corresponds to one among the three hundred and sixty-five parts, and so has the possibility of having been another day as another part. It could have been day A, or day B, or day C, or day D. Yet it was *tamatama* (儻) a certain day of a certain month. That it was a certain day of a certain month has contingency in relation to the year as a whole. The contingent here is nothing other than disjunctive contingency.

Let us give a few more examples of disjunctive contingency. "Though *wakuraba ni* I am human, though I too have been made like other men 〔Tr.: 作 is glossed *nare*, "become", so: "have been born as others are"〕, on my shoulders I throw only rags, a sleeveless jacket of cloth without cotton padding, hanging in tatters like sea-pine, ...... Is it so helpless as this, the way of the world?" (Man'yō, book 5). That *wakuraba ni* in this case means disjunctive contingency is clear if it is compared with, for example, "If only I can be merry in this world, in the life to come I would gladly become an insect or a bird" (Man'yō, book 3). "This nembutsu gate is spoken solely for those people who, though they are companions who should think of the life to come again and again with no other mind, turn to pointless mistaken causes 〔Tr.: printed 僻胤, read as 僻因〕, practise miscellaneous practices without regard to the time or to themselves, and, though this time they were *tamatama* born into the human realm so hard to receive, throw away the vow of Amida so hard to meet, return again to the old home of the three evil paths, turn through the wheel of birth and death, and do not know the sorrow of passing [p. 188] [コマ 101] many hundreds of thousands of kalpas" (Wago tōroku, Nenbutsu taii). This too, like the previous example, is disjunctive contingency concerning the whole of life, which has insect, bird, human and so on as its disjuncts. In the disjunctive relation of either insect, or bird, or human, that the disjunct human was posited is disjunctive contingency. "To begin with, the teachings of the Buddha's whole lifetime are divided into exoteric schools and esoteric schools, Great Vehicle and Lesser Vehicle, provisional teachings and real teachings, the treatise masters and the commentators, sections and eight schools, and their doctrines run on in ten thousand differences. Some expound the purport that all dharmas are empty, some make clear the meaning that all dharmas are the true aspect, some set up the doctrine of the five distinct natures, some discuss the principle that all have Buddha-nature; each school contends for the ultimate and supreme doctrine, and each argues for its school of the most profound and correct doctrine. All these are true words of the sutras and treatises. Moreover, they are the golden words of the Tathāgata. Sometimes he preached them adjusting to the capacities of his hearers, sometimes he taught them reflecting on the times. Which is shallow and which deep? It is hard to discern right and wrong in either. That too is teaching, this too is teaching; do not hold to one-sided attachment against each other. ...... All alike are paths of liberation from birth and death. Yet now those who study that envy this, and those who recite this disparage [p. 189] that. The dull are easily confused by this, and those of shallow talent find it hard to discern. If one *tamatama* goes to one dharma and tries to accumulate merit, the disputes of the various schools at once come upon one in turn. If one thinks to range widely over all the teachings and discuss their doctrines, the life of one lifetime soon draws to its close" (Shūi Wago tōroku, Tozanjō). In this case it is the disjunctive contingency that each of the eight schools, that is, each of the eight disjuncts, has in relation to Buddhism as a whole. "With which embryo cell (A or a) the two kinds of pollen cell (A and a) combine depends entirely on *chance*" (Mendel, *Zasshu shokubutsu no kenkyū*, trans. Koizumi Makoto, Iwanami Bunko, p. 74). In this case it is the disjunctive contingency that characterises each of the four disjuncts A|A, A|a, a|A and a|a. "Buffon first of all emphasised that the probability that the angle between the orbital planes of the various planets and the plane of the ecliptic should naturally, entirely by *chance*, lie within seven and a half degrees (that is, one twenty-fourth of the greatest possible inclination of 180 degrees) is extremely small. This had already been pointed out earlier by Bernoulli. For one planet the probability of turning out so by *chance* is only one in twenty-four. Accordingly the probability that the five planets known at that time should all be so is 24⁻⁵, that is, the small figure of about one in eight million" (Arrhenius, *Shiteki ni mitaru kagakuteki uchūkan no hensen*, trans. Terada Torahiko, Iwanami [p. 190] [コマ 102] Bunko, pp. 138-139). In this case the contingent is, if we speak of one planet, the disjunctive contingency that each of twenty-four disjuncts has in relation to the whole, and, if we speak of five planets, the disjunctive contingency that each of the roughly eight million disjuncts, twenty-four to the fifth power, has in relation to the whole.

From the above examples it will have become clear that each part within a whole, as a mere possible disjunct, constitutes disjunctive contingency in relation to the necessity of the whole.

### 2. Modality in general

It is clear that in disjunctive contingency the problem of contingency touches deeply on the problem of possibility. The positing of one *possible* disjunct is contingent, and because it is contingent, the positing of another is also *possible*. Up to now we have treated the contingent as the opposite concept of the necessary, but what relation, then, does the contingent stand in to the possible? Before investigating the relation between the contingent and the possible, let us first consider Modality in general. And in considering Modality [p. 191] we cannot but take as our point of departure Aristotle's classical consideration (Aristoteles, De Interpretatione 12, 13; Metaphysica Δ 5, 12).

We think the non-actual or non-existence over against the actual or present existence 〔Tr.: 現存在〕. The actual is what actually is, the non-actual is what actually is not. And "what actually is" is considered as divided into "what is not able not to be" and "what is able not to be". That is, within "the actual" there are "the necessary" (ἀναγκαῖον, necessarium) and "the contingent" (ἐνδεχόμενον, contingens). Again, "what actually is not" is considered as divided into "what is able to be" and "what is not able to be". That is, within "the non-actual" there are "the possible" (δυνατόν, possibile) and "the impossible" (ἀδύνατον, impossibile). However, in determining the four Modalities "necessary", "contingent", "possible" and "impossible" just now, we provisionally determined them from the standpoint of possibility, taking "is able to ......" and "is not able to ......" as the standard; but the four Modalities should be determinable from the standpoint of any one of the Modalities. Let us list them next.

(1) Determination from the standpoint of *possibility*

> Possible = to be is possible (δυνατὸν εἶναι, possibile esse)
> [p. 192] [コマ 103] Impossible = to be is not possible (οὐ δυνατὸν εἶναι, non possibile esse)
> Contingent = not to be is possible (δυνατὸν μὴ εἶναι, possibile non esse)
> Necessary = not to be is not possible (οὐ δυνατὸν μὴ εἶναι, non possibile non esse)

(2) Determination from the standpoint of *impossibility*

> Impossible = to be is impossible (ἀδύνατον εἶναι, impossibile esse)
> Possible = to be is not impossible (οὐκ ἀδύνατον εἶναι, non impossibile esse)
> Necessary = not to be is impossible (ἀδύνατον μὴ εἶναι, impossibile non esse)
> Contingent = not to be is not impossible (οὐκ ἀδύνατον μὴ εἶναι, non impossibile non esse)

(3) Determination from the standpoint of *necessity*

> Necessary = to be is necessary (ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι, necesse esse)
> Contingent = to be is not necessary (οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι, non necesse esse)
> Impossible = not to be is necessary (ἀναγκαῖον μὴ εἶναι, necesse non esse)
> Possible = not to be is not necessary (οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον μὴ εἶναι, non necesse non esse)

(4) Determination from the standpoint of *contingency*

> Contingent = to be is contingent (ἐνδεχόμενον εἶναι, contingens esse)
> [p. 193] Necessary = to be is not contingent (οὐκ ἐνδεχόμενον εἶναι, non contingens esse)
> Possible = not to be is contingent (ἐνδεχόμενον μὴ εἶναι, contingens non esse)
> Impossible = not to be is not contingent (οὐκ ἐνδεχόμενον μὴ εἶναι, non contingens non esse)

Among those who determine Modality from the first standpoint, that is, the standpoint of *possibility*, there is for example Trendelenburg (Trendelenburg, Logische Untersuchungen II, 3 Aufl. S. 177-227). Among those who determine it from the second standpoint, that is, the standpoint of *impossibility*, there is Lewis (C. I. Lewis, A survey of symbolic logic, 1918, p. 292). In his symbolic logic ~ is the sign of impossibility (impossibility), and - is the sign of simple negation (negation). Expressed with these signs, Modality becomes as follows.

> ~p ...... p is impossible
> -~p ...... p is not impossible (that is, p is possible)
> ~-p ...... that p is not, is impossible (that is, p is necessary)
> -~-p ...... that p is not, is not impossible (that is, p is not necessary)

By contrast, Oskar Becker (Oskar Becker, Zur Logik der Modalitäten, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. [p. 194] [コマ 104] phän. F. XI. S. 510) holds that viewing from the third standpoint, that is, the standpoint of *necessity*, makes the connection of the whole clearer and more positive than viewing from the standpoint of impossibility. One need only put •-p in place of ~p. • is the sign of necessity. He uses a dot-and-stroke notation (Punkt-Strich-Notation), which expresses with • and -.

> •p ...... p is necessary
> -•p ...... p is not necessary
> •-p ...... that p is not, is necessary (that is, p is impossible)
> -•-p ...... that p is not, is not necessary (that is, p is possible)

The main difference between Lewis and Becker is which modality has come to stand in the position of the complex one by having three signs. In Lewis, -~-p (that p is not, is not impossible = p is not necessary = p is contingent) has three signs; in Becker, -•-p (that p is not, is not necessary = p is possible) has three signs. The important meaning of this will become clear later (see pp. 226, 231). Further, both Lewis and Becker, adding the following two cases,

> [p. 195] p ...... p is true
> -p ...... p is false

that is, what corresponds from our ontological standpoint to "the actual" and "the non-actual", regard them together as the whole of Modality.

That there is in fact no one who tries to view from the fourth standpoint, that is, the standpoint of *contingency*, is probably due to the fact that contingency itself has a non-logical character; but for a logic rooted in life it would not be impossible to reduce to some degree the distance between the domain of logic and the immediacy of experience. Determining the other Modalities from the standpoint of contingency is something often seen in ordinary usage. "For critical philosophy to be completed as a system, ...... teleology is required as the division that makes clear the foundation of purposiveness. That what answers this requirement is the *Critique of Judgement* of 1790 needs no saying anew. That this so-called third Critique, as the crown of the system of critical philosophy that unifies and completes Kant's world-view, has given great influence both to the Romantic philosophy immediately after Kant and to the idealist philosophy of the present day is *not by chance*" (Tanabe Hajime, *Kanto no mokuteki ron*, pp. 2-3 [p. 196] [コマ 105]). "That he, who, trying to experience as far as he could the unfree condition of the blind, sometimes seemed almost to envy the blind, became in later years truly blind, is in fact because such an attitude of mind since his boyhood had its influence, and when one thinks of it, it is *not by chance*" (Tanizaki Jun'ichirō, *Shunkinshō*, p. 27). In these, "is necessary" can be put in place of "is not by chance", and so they determine necessity from the standpoint of contingency. Again, when one says "That there are *no* factories in this neighbourhood *is by chance*", this means "It is also *possible* that there be factories", and so it determines possibility from the standpoint of contingency. Again, when one says "Since facilities for scholarly research institutes are insufficient in our country, it is *not by chance* that the problem of unemployment among the intellectual class is *not* solved", this means "That it be solved is *impossible*", and so it determines impossibility from the standpoint of contingency.

If we now provisionally use )( as the sign of contingency (see p. 73), its relations to the other modalities become as follows.

> )(p ...... p is contingent
> [p. 197] -)(p ...... p is not contingent (that is, p is necessary)
> )(-p ...... that p is not, is contingent (that is, p is possible)
> -)(-p ...... that p is not, is not contingent (that is, p is impossible)

If to the above four kinds we add the two kinds

> p
> -p

six kinds of Modality are obtained.

Now, contingency negates the necessity of existence, and necessity negates the contingency of existence, so the necessary and the contingent stand in contradictory opposition. Impossibility negates the possibility of existence, and possibility negates the impossibility of existence, so the possible and the impossible also stand in contradictory opposition. On the other hand, impossibility asserts the necessity of non-existence, and necessity asserts the impossibility of non-existence, so the necessary and the impossible are considered similar. The feature common to the necessary and the impossible here is called "apodicticity" (apodictica). Contingency affirms the possibility of non-existence [p. 198] [コマ 106], and possibility affirms the contingency of non-existence, so the possible and the contingent are considered similar. The feature common to the possible and the contingent here is called "problematicity" (problematica). And the horizon of the actual and the non-actual, or present existence and non-existence, is called "assertoricity" (assertoria) over against apodicticity and problematicity. With regard to distinctions in the modality of judgements, problematica is usually translated 蓋然的, assertoria 實然的 or 正然的, and apodictica 確然的, 必當然的, 必然的, 成然的 and so on, but we choose the words 問題的, 言明的 and 確證的 for these three modalities. 〔Tr.: The usual terms mean roughly "probable", "actually so" or "rightly so", and "certainly so" or "necessarily so". Kuki's 問題的 (*mondaiteki*), 言明的 (*genmeiteki*) and 確證的 (*kakushōteki*) are built on "problem", "statement" and "confirmation", and are rendered here "problematic", "assertoric" and "apodictic".〕 If the above consideration is represented in a figure, it becomes as above 〔Figure 3〕.

![Figure 3](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-03-p198.png)

**Figure 3.** The first system: the modalities on the horizons of actuality and non-actuality, with apodicticity, problematicity and assertoricity (1948, p. 198). Labels: 必然 necessary; 偶然 contingent; 可能 possible; 不可能 impossible; 現實 actual; 非現實 non-actual; 確證性 apodicticity; 問題性 problematicity; 言明性 assertoricity. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

〔Tr.: Figure, printed at the head of p. 198: a square. Corners: top left 必然 (necessary), top right 偶然 (contingent), bottom left 可能 (possible), bottom right 不可能 (impossible). The top side is labelled 現實 (actual), the bottom side 非現實 (non-actual). A dotted diagonal joining necessary and impossible is labelled 確證性 (apodicticity); a dotted diagonal joining contingent and possible is labelled 問題性 (problematicity); a dotted vertical line through the centre is labelled 言明性 (assertoricity).〕

### 3. Two systems of Modality

That there are two different principles for constructing a system of Modality has become evident of itself through [p. 199] the above consideration. The first principle of construction lies in the negation of the *predicate*. That is, with regard to the same subject, the predicate is affirmed or denied. First, "existence" is placed as subject: to affirm it as possible is the possible in the narrow sense (*the possible*), and to deny existence as possible is the impossible in the narrow sense (*the impossible*). Next, "non-existence" is placed as subject and is affirmed or denied as possible. To affirm non-existence as possible is *the contingent*, and to deny non-existence as possible is *the necessary*. When one follows this first method of construction, the system of Modality presents the combinations (1) the actual, the non-actual; (2) the possible, the impossible; (3) the necessary, the contingent. The opposition of the actual and the non-actual may be thought of as "existence" being placed as subject and the predicate affirming or denying it directly.

The second principle of construction lies in the negation of the *subject*. That is, the same predicate is posited of mutually contradictory subjects. First, existence and non-existence are placed as subjects and are "affirmed" as possible. To affirm existence as possible is *the possible* in the narrow sense, and to affirm non-existence as possible is *the contingent*. Next, again, existence and non-existence are placed as subjects and this time are "denied" as possible. To deny existence as possible is *the impossible*, and [p. 200] [コマ 107] to deny non-existence as possible is *the necessary*. Following this second method of construction, the system of Modality shows the combinations (1) the actual, the non-actual; (2) the possible, the contingent; (3) the necessary, the impossible. The opposition of the actual and the non-actual in this case may be thought of as existence and non-existence being placed as subjects and these being directly "affirmed".

As to the formation of the two systems of Modality, one may also think as follows. First set up the possible of existence and the necessary of existence, and consider two kinds of opposite for each of them. The possible of existence has two kinds of opposite: (A) "that existence is not possible", that is, the impossible of existence; (B) "that non-existence is possible", that is, the contingent of existence. The necessary of existence likewise has two kinds of opposite: (A) "that existence is not necessary", that is, the contingent of existence; (B) "that non-existence is necessary", that is, the impossible of existence. And in the first system of Modality the opposites of kind A are taken, and combinations are made such as the possible and the impossible, the necessary and the contingent. In the second system the opposites of kind B are taken, and the combinations the possible and the contingent, the necessary and the impossible are made. And in each system the pair the actual, the non-actual is added.

[p. 201] Let us compare these two systems of Modality. In the first system, in each combination the pair is viewed from the point of difference. Each pair is nothing other than *contradictory opposition*. The law of contradiction and the law of excluded middle are both valid. One cannot be actual and at the same time non-actual. And one must be either actual or non-actual. Again, on the horizon of the actual, one cannot be necessary and at the same time contingent. And one must be either necessary or contingent. Likewise, on the horizon of the non-actual, one cannot be possible and at the same time impossible. And one must be either possible or impossible.

In the second system each pair is viewed from the point of similarity. Each combination is formed on the basis of a point of agreement such as assertoricity, problematicity or apodicticity. And with regard to the necessary and the impossible, the law of excluded middle is not valid but the law of contradiction is valid, so they stand in the relation of so-called *contrary opposition*. There is, after all, no such thing as having to be either necessary or impossible. The problematic can be in between as a third. That is, the law of excluded middle is not valid. Here lies the limit of apodicticity. However, [p. 202] [コマ 108] one cannot be necessary and at the same time impossible, so the law of contradiction is valid. Again, with regard to the possible and the contingent, the law of contradiction is not valid but the law of excluded middle is valid, so they stand in the relation of so-called *subcontrary opposition*. One cannot say that something cannot be possible and at the same time contingent. The possible and the contingent can hold at the same time. For what presently exists by chance is able to presently exist. That the possible does not become actual but remains within the non-actual is merely by chance. In short, between the possible and the contingent the law of contradiction is not valid. However, if the standpoint of problematicity is extended and necessity is thought of as the limit of possibility, and impossibility as the limit of contingency, one can say that something must be either possible or contingent. Therefore the law of excluded middle is valid. Further, the combination the actual, the non-actual was viewed in the first system from the side of difference, as contradictory opposition, but in the second system it may be thought of as viewed especially from the side of similarity, as assertoricity.

Further, though it need not yet be taken up directly as a problem here, the relation of the necessary and the possible, and the relation of the impossible and the contingent, may both be seen as standing in the relation of so-called *subalternation* [p. 203]. As said just now, necessity is thought of as the limit of possibility, and impossibility as the limit of contingency (see pp. 220-221). Let us represent these relations in the figure on the left 〔Figure 4〕.

![Figure 4](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-04-p203.png)

**Figure 4.** The square of opposition of the four modalities (1948, p. 203). Labels: 必然 necessary; 不可能 impossible; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent; 反對 contrary; 小反對 subcontrary; 大小 subalternation; 矛盾 contradictory; (現實) (actual); (非現實) (non-actual). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

〔Tr.: Figure on p. 203: a square of opposition. Corners: top left 必然 (necessary), top right 不可能 (impossible), bottom left 可能 (possible), bottom right 偶然 (contingent). The top side is labelled 反對 (contrary), the bottom side 小反對 (subcontrary), and each vertical side 大小 (subalternation). The diagonal joining necessary and contingent is labelled 矛盾 (contradiction) with (現實) (actual); the diagonal joining impossible and possible is labelled 矛盾 with (非現實) (non-actual).〕

Further, speaking especially of the contingent alone, in the first system "the contingent and the necessary" stand opposed, forming contradictory opposition, and in the second system "the contingent and the possible" construct a relation of subcontrariety on the common ground of problematicity. And it may be said that to see the contingent and the necessary as a pair is a static, abstract standpoint, and to see the contingent and the possible as a pair is a dynamic, concrete standpoint.

Kant's categories of Modality correspond to the first system just described, but they also show a connection with the second system. Kant, in the "table of categories", divided Modality as (1) the possible, the impossible; (2) present existence, non-existence (that is, the actual, the non-actual); (3) the necessary, the contingent, and attempted its application also in the chapter on "the postulates of empirical thought in general". But the table of categories is derived from the division [p. 204] [コマ 109] of judgements in formal logic. That is, he discovered (1) the possible from the problematic judgement, (2) present existence from the assertoric judgement, (3) the necessary from the apodictic judgement. And next he assigned to each of them the concept of its contradictory opposite, making a pair. That is, (1) to the possible he assigned the impossible, (2) to present existence non-existence, (3) to the necessary the contingent. Deriving the possible, present existence and the necessary from the table of judgements as the first stage, and assigning to each of them the concept of its contradictory opposite as the second stage, were done from separate standpoints. Had Kant kept consistently to the principle of division by the table of judgements, the possible and the contingent would have formed one set as having the problematicity of the problematic judgement, and the necessary and the impossible would have formed one set as having the apodicticity of the apodictic judgement. Kant's table of categories, in its ultimate, decisive form, belongs to the first system of Modality, but in the genetic process at its point of departure it shows a connection with the second system.

The second system appears in Leibniz as well. "Truths of reasoning (raisonnement) are *necessary*, and their opposite is *impossible*. And truths of fact (fait) are *contingent*, and their opposite is *possible*" (Leibniz, Opera philosophica, ed. Erd- [p. 205] mann, p. 707). Leibniz grasped the necessary and the impossible in the apodicticity of reasoning, and understood the contingent and the possible in the problematicity of fact. Hegel too thinks broadly in accordance with this second system. "Possibility and contingency are the two moments of actuality" (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 145). And pure impossibility, as the impossibility of impossibility, reverses into necessity (Ib, S. 193, Anm. 1).

There is, after all, a marked difference of standpoint according to whether the actual is viewed statically or dynamically. If the actual is viewed statically, the actual has the stillness of assertion and is something that does not move. Speaking of the figure of Modality shown earlier on p. 198 〔Figure 3〕, the vertical line of assertoricity joining the actual and the non-actual is thought of as the starting point. And within the actual, that whose non-existence is impossible is the necessary, and that whose non-existence is possible is the contingent, so the necessary and the contingent are thought of as opposed. Again, within the non-actual, that whose existence is possible is the so-called possible and that whose existence is impossible is the so-called impossible, so the possible and the impossible are placed in a relation of opposition. This is the standpoint of the first system, and in such a standpoint everything is, so to speak, static and decided.

[p. 206] [コマ 110] By contrast, if one stands at the standpoint of the second system and views the actual dynamically, the actual is thought of as not assertoric but problematic. The actual is something with thickness. It is pregnant with problems. The problem must be unfolded. Accordingly, in the earlier figure, one grasps as the starting point the problematicity that joins the possible and the contingent. Assertoricity has lost its own self-evidence and turned into problematicity. Now contingency, insofar as it means the possible of non-existence, is something that, while occupying a position in existence, is rooted in non-existence. Possibility, insofar as it means the possible of existence, is something that, while occupying a position in non-existence, is directed towards existence. And the actual, insofar as it is constituted by possibility and contingency, contains the two moments of existence and non-existence within its thickness and constantly unfolds problems. According to Hegel, possibility is the inner and contingency is the outer. And actuality is nothing other than "the identity of the inner and the outer". Possibility as inner actuality is essentiality, and contingency as outer actuality is the immediate. When abstract possibility acts on immediate actuality and immediate actuality acts on abstract possibility, in other words when the inner makes an immediate self-transposition into the outer and the outer into the inner, [p. 207] real possibility arises there and unfolds into necessity. Necessity is nothing other than "unfolded actuality" (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, §§ 142-148; Wissenschaft der Logik, hrsg. v. Lasson, II. S. 169-184).

In the standpoint that thus views the actual dynamically and considers the development of Modality, the figure on p. 198 earlier 〔Figure 3〕 should probably be altered as on the left 〔Figure 5〕.

![Figure 5](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-05-p207.png)

**Figure 5.** The second system: the square of Figure 3 turned through forty-five degrees on the line of problematicity (1948, p. 207). Labels: 必然 necessary; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent; 不可能 impossible; 現實 actual; 確證性 apodicticity; 問題性 problematicity. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

〔Tr.: Figure on p. 207: a square standing on one corner. Top 必然 (necessary), left 可能 (possible), right 偶然 (contingent), bottom 不可能 (impossible). A solid horizontal line joins possible and contingent and is labelled 問題性 (problematicity). The upper triangle, drawn in solid lines, is labelled 現實 (actual); an arrow labelled 確證性 (apodicticity) rises from the horizontal line to necessary. The lower sides, running to impossible, are dotted.〕

To obtain this figure, one need only rotate the whole figure by forty-five degrees about the straight line of problematicity that joins the possible and the contingent in the earlier figure. "The impossible" is shown with dotted lines because it was seen as negative necessity. If the earlier figure reflects the first system of Modality, this figure is one that arises on the basis of the second system.

### 4. The relation of similarity between contingency and possibility

[p. 208] [コマ 111] Contingency and possibility, when seen over against the apodictic completeness of necessity, cannot help being regarded as problematic, incomplete modalities of existence. And on this point contingency and possibility are often witnessed as things extremely similar to each other. That existence is possible at the same time also means the possible of non-existence. For the point in which what is merely possible of existence differs from what is necessary of existence lies precisely in the fact that its non-existence too is possible. Therefore possibility and contingency are often seen as one and the same thing.

Aristotle too, whilst on the one hand distinguishing the possible and the contingent, on the other hand tries to see the two as one and the same. He says, "What is capable of being cut or of walking is also capable of not walking or of not being cut", and "the *possible of being* and the *possible of not being* are the same" (Aristoteles, De interpretatione, 12, 21b), [p. 209] and further says, "From the *possible* of being follows the *contingent* of being. And the latter can be converted with the former" (τῷ μὲν γὰρ δυνατὸν εἶναι τὸ ἐνδέχεσθαι εἶναι, καὶ τοῦτο ἐκείνῳ ἀντιστρέφει) (Ib., 13, 22a). He also says, "Therefore the *possible* means, in one sense, as was just said, that whose being false is *not necessary*; in another sense it means the true (*what is*); and in another sense it means what is true and *contingently is*" (τὸ μὲν οὖν δυνατὸν ἕνα μὲν τρόπον, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, τὸ μὴ ἐξ ἀνάγκης ψεῦδος σημαίνει, ἕνα δὲ τὸ ἀληθὲς [εἶναι], ἕνα δὲ τὸ ἐνδεχόμενον ἀληθὲς εἶναι) (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Δ 12, 1019b). Here possibility is determined (1) from the standpoint of necessity as "what is not necessary", (2) from the standpoint of actuality as "what is", and (3) from the standpoint of contingency as "what contingently is". It is a passage extremely rich in implication, but it should be noted that it identifies the possible and the contingent.

Boethius assigned possibile to δυνατόν (possible) and contingens to ἐνδεχόμενον (contingent), but said "being contingent and being possible mean one and the same thing, and there is no difference whatever" (contingens esse et possibile esse idem significat, nec quidquam discrepat), and said that [p. 210] [コマ 112] if one insists on stating a difference, it is merely a formal difference: for the negative form of the one there is the word impossibile, whereas as the negative form of the other there is no word incontingens (Boethius, De interpretatione, Ed. II. lib. V. Migne p. 582-583). Abélard too said, "the possible and the contingent mean entirely the same thing" (possibile quidem et contingens idem prorsus sonant) (Abélard, Ouvrages inédits, publ. par Cousin, Dialectica, p. 265).

Spinoza too thought of the contingent and the possible as extremely close to each other. Concerning the explanation of the contingent he says the following. "Now I shall briefly explain how we should understand the contingent. But before that I wish to explain how we should understand the *necessary* and the *impossible*. A thing is called *necessary* either in relation to its own essence or else in relation to its cause. For the existence of a thing follows necessarily either from its own essence and definition, or else from a given efficient cause. Also, for the same reason, a thing is called *impossible* either because its essence or definition involves a contradiction, or else because no external cause exists that is determined so as to produce such a thing. In contrast, a thing is called contingent only because of the imperfection of our knowledge, and not for any other reason. [p. 211] For a thing of which it is not known to us whether its essence involves a contradiction or not, or of which we know for certain that its essence does not involve a contradiction but about whose existence we can assert nothing with certainty because the order of its causes is hidden from us, never seems to us either necessary or impossible, and for that reason we call it *contingent* (contingens) or *possible* (possibilis)" (Spinoza, Ethica, I, 33, Schol. I). However, it is not that Spinoza did not distinguish the contingent and the possible at all. He expresses their relation as follows. "When we keep in view only the essence of individual things, insofar as we find nothing that necessarily posits their existence or necessarily excludes it, I call individual things *contingent*. When we keep in view the causes by which individual things must be produced, insofar as we do not know whether these causes are determined so as to produce them, I call the same individual things *possible*. In the first part, Scholium 1 to Proposition 33, because there was no need to distinguish strictly, I did not distinguish between the *possible* and the *contingent*" (Ib., IV, def. 3, 4).

Further, Nicolai Hartmann, MacColl and others can also be considered as standpoints that identify the possible and the contingent, but let us discuss this later (see pp. 224-227) [p. 212] [コマ 113]. In short, the above is the standpoint that sees the contingent and the possible in a relation of similarity, as things that share the character of problematicity.

〔Tr.: 確證的 (apodictic) and 問題的 (problematic) are Kant's modal terms; see the glossary (apodicticity, problematicity). At p. 211-212 Kuki's cross-reference "二二四-二二七頁參照" breaks across the page after 參; only 照) falls on p. 212.〕

### 5. The relation of proximity between contingency and impossibility

In the above we have seen the relation of similarity between contingency and possibility, but on the other hand what is extremely important is to turn our eyes to the relation of opposition between contingency and possibility, or the relation of proximity between contingency and impossibility. For that consideration let us take the necessary and the impossible as the starting points. Possibility is born through the negation of impossibility. Possibility grows gradually from the one point at which it was born. And the limit of the increase of possibility coincides with necessity. Next, the negation of necessity gives birth to contingency. Taking the one point of its birth as its beginning, contingency gradually increases. And the limit of the increase of contingency coincides with impossibility. Here the oppositional relation of possibility and contingency, and the relation of proximity between contingency and impossibility, are made clear. As possibility increases, contingency decreases, and as contingency increases, [p. 213] possibility decreases. The extreme of the increase of possibility coincides with the extreme of the decrease of contingency. That is necessity. And the extreme of the increase of contingency coincides with the extreme of the decrease of possibility. That is impossibility. The consideration just stated can be represented by the figure on the left 〔Figure 6〕.

![Figure 6](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-06-p213.png)

**Figure 6.** The increase and decrease of possibility and contingency between necessity and impossibility (1948, p. 213). Labels: 必然 necessary; 不可能 impossible; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent.

```
                necessary
            .------*------.
          .'   ↗    (      '.
         /           )       \
        |  possi-    (  contin- |
        |  ble        )  gent   |
         \           (   ↙     /
          '.          )      .'
            '------*------'
               impossible
```

〔Tr.: Approximate rendering of Kuki's figure: a circle divided by an S-shaped curve running from the top point, labelled 必然 (necessary), to the bottom point, labelled 不可能 (impossible). The left half is labelled 可能 (possible), with an arrow curving up towards necessity; the right half is labelled 偶然 (contingent), with an arrow curving down towards impossibility.〕

From this point the special position held by exceptional contingency, treated in Chapter 1, section 5, is understood. Exceptional contingency is one in which the mark has non-essentiality, the contingent with the smallest content of possibility. Accordingly it is the contingent that is most conspicuous. Conversely, what is regarded as an essential mark is the extreme of the increase of possibility, something approximating to necessity, and as the constitutive content of the concept it has the value of a law. That clover has three leaves is that such an essential mark is present. Being three-leaved cannot, strictly speaking, be called necessary for the concept of clover, but as the limit of the increase of possibility it is something quasi-necessary. In contrast, that clover has four leaves [p. 214] [コマ 114] is an exception, and accordingly the contingent containing extremely little possibility. It is something that grazes the domain of the impossible. Therefore the four-leaved clover stands out especially as something contingent.

Meinong too says the following. "It deserves attention that a chance is said to be the more remarkable, not the larger the possibility contained in the chance, but the smaller it is. It is of course not that there is no contingency at all in two colleagues running into each other inside the office, but one thinks of it as a special chance when they run into each other in a town far away from where the office is located. The possibility in that case is far smaller than in the former case" (Meinong, Über Möglichkeit und Wahrscheinlichkeit, 1915, S. 243). When the *Book of Changes* says "biting dried meat, one meets poison" (噬腊肉、遇毒), since dried meat is old meat that has been dried, the possibility that it has gone bad and harbours poison is fairly large. That one ate meat and was poisoned has some contingency, but it is not much of a chance. This is because the possibility is large. In contrast, when it says "biting dried meat on the bone, one gets a metal arrow" (噬乾胏、得金矢), that there is a metal arrowhead in dried meat on the bone is of course not impossible if the animal was shot with a bow at the time of the hunt, but if it were a golden arrow [p. 215] or the like, it would be something of extremely small possibility. Instead, the contingency is extremely large. Once, on a train of the Tōkaidō line going from Kyoto to Tokyo, I entered the dining car a little later than the set time. Only one empty seat remained, so I took that seat. The man next to me called out, and when I looked it was my younger brother-in-law, who lives in Miyazaki Prefecture. At that instant I was struck by the strangeness of it. But when I thought about it, it was the day before the memorial service for the first anniversary of my father-in-law's death, in Tokyo. Also, the train was the limited express Tsubame. That the two of us should take the train of that day, and also that we should board the Tsubame, have a fairly large possibility. The contingency lies rather in the fact that, although there were several dinner sittings, the two of us had without planning chosen the same sitting, and that, although the dining car was full, only the seat next to my brother-in-law was empty. As I came to see that a fairly large possibility was contained in it, the contingency too gradually decreased, and accordingly I came no longer to think it so very strange. If it had not been an express, or if we had had no common business in Tokyo, then, in proportion as the possibility decreased, the contingency would have been greater. Also, immediately after the World War, I became acquainted with a Russian named S, an exile in Japan, a young man skilled at the piano, [p. 216] [コマ 115] and associated with him on fairly close terms. He also once appeared as accompanist for Russian dancing at a theatre in Kamakura. However, after he left for Harbin to join the fighting under a White general, there was no news of him at all. I had thought he might have been killed in battle. It was several years after that, when I was staying in Freiburg in Germany. I went, accompanying someone who was receiving treatment for neuralgia, to the electrotherapy room of the university hospital. I was not a little surprised that the assistant there was S. He had wandered from place to place and at last come to Freiburg, studied medicine, and was now an assistant at the university. At first I received the impression of a great chance, but since it also, after all, had a fairly large possibility, when I thought about it carefully the contingency diminished. The language in which I had spoken with him in Japan was German. He greatly regretted having had to interrupt his studies because of the revolution. He also said that, although Japan too had its own distinctive culture, he missed the culture of Europe. From these circumstances there is considerable reason for his having gone to Germany and completed his studies. In his and my meeting again in Germany, therefore, a good deal of possibility is contained. The contingency hangs on the fact that, without planning, it was a small town in South Germany like Freiburg, [p. 217] and also that he happened to be the assistant in the electrotherapy room. If it had been something like the boy who brought breakfast when I stayed a night at a hotel in a remote rural place on a journey in Africa turning out to be S, then, just as the possibility is smaller, the contingency would have been greater.

The next example is in Wilhelm von Scholz's *Der Zufall*, but I quote it from Mr Naruse Mukyoku's "Dialogue on Chance". "They say this is a true story about a certain person. In the spring of 1914, the wife of this person, who was then living in the Schwarzwald, took photographs of their eldest son, who was turning four. It seems it was a small-format film of six exposures. The wife needed to undergo a small operation and went to Strassburg. She intended to have the photographs developed there, but for some reason her return was suddenly brought forward, so she asked a Russian woman student she knew to have them developed, naming the photographer's shop, and left. However, the war began soon after, and she had no further chance to meet that woman student, and in the end she never got the photographer's receipt either.
