A few days before the outbreak of the war the husband, having gone to Strassburg, dropped in at that photographer's shop and said that, though he had no receipt, he was such-and-such a person and had asked for developing, [p. 218] [コマ 116] but the photographer would on no account hand over the film. It seems the wife was terribly disappointed. While things remained like that, in 1916 the wife bought a film, I think it was in Frankfurt, took it to a spa called Soden, and there took photographs of their daughter, who was a year and a half old. When she had it developed, it turned out that all the pictures were double-exposed. Thinking that could not be, she held the film up to the light and looked closely, and to her astonishment the figure of the eldest son appeared, hazy, like a dream, against a background of the snow of the Schwarzwald. Overlapping it, the naked figure of the little sister with her curly hair, sunbathing, could be seen; brother and sister had become one in the film." The explanation of this chance is that "in the confusion of the war the former photographer sold the film, mixed up, to the next photographer. Or perhaps a third party was involved in between." But the great chance lies in that "going to that shop without any particular thought (if she had been a step later, someone else might have bought it) that very film came into her hands, just at the right time" (Naruse Mukyoku, Dialogue on Chance, pp. 451-452, Wilhelm von Scholz, Der Zufall, 1924, S. 21-24). The possibility contained in this chance is extremely small, almost [p. 219] equal to the impossible. Accordingly the contingency, again, is extremely great. That there are cases in which chance is understood as something in contact with the impossible also appears in the following words. "Mr Huth, in his book *Consanguineous Marriage*, included a long quotation by a Belgian. It said that he had carried on the breeding of domestic rabbits very closely interbred over very long generations, without seeing the least harmful result. That account was published in a most reputable journal, namely the bulletin of the Royal Academy of Belgium, but I could not help entertaining doubts about it. If it was not the result of some kind of *chance*, the reason for it was not clear to me, and from my own experience of breeding animals I considered that such a thing was *something that could not be*" (Francis Darwin, *Charles Darwin's Autobiography, His Religious Views and Reminiscences*, trans. Koizumi Makoto, Iwanami Bunko, p. 107). Also, when it is said "*by chance* born into the human realm, *so hard to obtain*" (*Wago tōroku*, "Nenbutsu taii"), chance is seen as the possible close to the impossible.

It may be permissible to think that Kant too perceived the proximity of chance and the impossible. Kant, concerning the table of categories, pointed out that in every case the third category is always a synthesis of the first category and the second category, and as one example of this he says, "Necessity is nothing other than existence given through possibility itself" [p. 220] [コマ 117] (Kant, Critique of Pure Reason, B. 111). Now since the contradictory opposite of necessity is contingency, and the contradictory opposite of possibility is impossibility, if Kant's claim is applied in this case too, it can be said that "contingency is nothing other than existence (non-existence) given through impossibility itself". In any case, that contingency, whilst opposed to possibility, approaches impossibility without limit is a point that should be watched closely.

〔Tr.: 白系 (*hakkei*): "White" in the sense of the anti-Bolshevik Russians. 義弟 (*gitei*): younger brother-in-law; 岳父 (*gakufu*): father-in-law. Kuki's ハス is rendered "Huth"; see the editor's notes. The dashes around 一足おそければ他の人が買つてしまつたかも知れない in Kuki's quotation from Naruse are rendered by parentheses.〕

### 6. The third system of Modality

If, on the basis of emphasising the relation of opposition between contingency and possibility, one stresses the proximity of contingency and impossibility and the proximity of possibility and necessity, a new method of construction concerning the system of Modality is here thought out. The combination (1) the actual, the non-actual, (2) the necessary, the possible, (3) the impossible, the contingent must be the third system of Modality. What forms the foundation of this system is the concept of the limit. Necessity is thought as the limit [p. 221] that possibility can approach without limit, and impossibility is thought as the limit that contingency can approach without limit. Accordingly, as was also said earlier (see pp. 202-203), it may be said that the relation between the necessary and the possible, and the relation between the impossible and the contingent, form the so-called relation of subalternation.

The thought of Nicolai Hartmann (N. Hartmann, Logische und ontologische Wirklichkeit, Kantstudien. XX. S. 1-28) too can be understood from this standpoint. According to Hartmann's view, that Kant placed Modality in the order possibility, actuality, necessity is because, from the standpoint of the logic of knowledge, he took the degree of certainty of knowledge as the standard. When, from the standpoint of the logic of existence, the degree of existence of the object is taken as the standard, the order must be possibility, necessity, actuality. Epistemologically, actuality is superior to possibility in point of certainty and inferior to necessity. Therefore it is located between the two. But ontologically actuality must be thought as the synthesis of possibility and necessity. Therefore it occupies the last position, as something higher than both. And actuality and non-actuality on the one side, and necessity and possibility on the other, belong to entirely different modal dimensions.

[p. 222] [コマ 118] What is important for our problem is that Hartmann thought of possibility and necessity as one pair. Possibility and necessity stand in a special relation of opposition. The lack of possibility directly means a kind of necessity. For impossibility is nothing other than negative necessity (negative Notwendigkeit). Also, the lack of necessity directly means negative possibility (negative Möglichkeit). Non-necessity (Nicht-Notwendigkeit) is nothing other than the possibility of non-existence (Möglichkeit des Nichtseins). In this way possibility and necessity are not such that the one is directly the opposite of the other, but the contradictory opposite of possibility (impossibility) belongs directly under the superordinate concept necessity, and the contradictory opposite of necessity (non-necessity) belongs directly under the superordinate concept possibility. This is evidence that possibility and necessity stand in a close relation of proximity. Then, if one asks what original correlation they have, there exists the relation that the whole of possibility coincides entirely with necessity, and that this coincidence is actuality. To speak in detail, what is called actuality is constituted from various conditions. Each of these conditions is, for actuality, necessary and cannot be lacking. But if one looks at each condition separately, it merely means the possibility [p. 223] of the conditioned. It does not yet mean its actuality. Only when the conditions are all fulfilled and work in agreement does the conditioned become something actual. That is, at that time the conditioned is not merely possible but also necessary. At that time the conditioned can no longer not exist, and is absolutely required by the whole of its conditions. Such a *coming together* (Zusammentreffen) of possibility and necessity in the conditions of a concrete form constitutes the actuality of that form. Possibility is nothing other than the modal expression for what, as a constitutive moment, is called a condition. And necessity refers to the whole of the conditions. Hartmann points out that this relation between possibility and necessity already appeared in the correlation of Aristotle's potentiality (δύναμις) and actuality (ἐνέργεια). That is, the coming together of the various potentialities necessarily results in actuality.

In order to establish what we call the third system of Modality, besides setting up the correlation of possibility and necessity, one must further set up the correlation of contingency and impossibility. Hartmann has not done this. I [p. 224] [コマ 119] think that there lies a great defect of Hartmann's. Hartmann founds the correlation of possibility and necessity indirectly through the contradictory concept of each. That is, the contradictory opposite of possibility is a kind of necessity, and the contradictory opposite of necessity is a kind of possibility; there, he said, lies the close relation of the two. Therefore, alongside expounding the correlation of possibility and necessity, he ought naturally to have expounded the correlation between the contradictory opposite of possibility and the contradictory opposite of necessity. The contradictory opposite of possibility is impossibility, and the contradictory opposite of necessity is non-necessity, that is, contingency. However, that Hartmann does not set up a special correlation of contingency and impossibility is for the reason that contingency, that is non-necessity, is nothing other than possibility. Non-necessity is the possibility of non-existence. And, he says, since in the modality of possibility the existence and the non-existence of one and the same thing do not exclude each other but constitute two coexisting possibilities, non-necessity is directly negative possibility but at the same time indirectly also positive possibility. For that reason Hartmann does not acknowledge non-necessity as an independent modality. As a result he sets up on one side the three modalities necessity, possibility, impossibility, and on the other side the two modalities actuality, non-actuality, and as a whole [p. 225] sets up five basic modalities. That in the domain of the one oppositional series possibility occupies the middle is because, in existential value, impossibility is the smallest and necessity the greatest. On the other side, since the two modalities form an oppositional domain of their own, in existential value non-actuality is the smallest and actuality the greatest. And in this domain no middle term exists. Further, there are two points to be noted with regard to existential value between the one domain and the other. The first point is that non-actuality, being less negative than impossibility and more negative than possibility, should be located modally between the two. The second point is the relation of position between actuality and necessity: whereas, as seen in mathematical connections, there are necessities that lack actuality, actuality, as the synthesis of possibility and necessity, always has possibility and necessity within itself, sublated as moments. Therefore actuality must be placed higher than necessity. According to Hartmann, the modalities show an ascending relation of rank like the above 〔Figure 7〕.

![Figure 7](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-07-p225.png)

**Figure 7.** Hartmann's ascending order of the modalities (1948, p. 225). Labels: 現實性 actuality; 非現實性 non-actuality; 必然性 necessity; 可能性 possibility; 不可能性 impossibility. The labels are set right to left.

```
  ↑                   actuality
  |   necessity
  |   possibility
  |                   non-actuality
  |   impossibility
```

〔Tr.: Approximate rendering of Kuki's figure: an upward arrow, with necessity, possibility and impossibility on one side and actuality and non-actuality on the other, each placed at its level by short rules.〕

[p. 226] [コマ 120] As already said, that contingency does not occupy a position in this table of modalities is an extreme defect. Hartmann on the one hand says that, as said earlier, non-necessity is directly negative possibility and at the same time indirectly also positive possibility, and so does not form one modality independent of possibility. But that is a prejudice coming from the overweighting of formal logic, as is common to all theorists who identify contingency and possibility. Lewis (see p. 194) too regards non-necessity (-~-p), which has three signs, as a "complex truth-value", and does not include it among the five truth-values (five truth-values) of his so-called "system of strict implication" (system of strict implication). But that too is because he stands entirely at the standpoint of formal logic alone. Lewis, whilst on the one hand acknowledging that -~-p is "irreducible" (irreducible) and "a distinct and recognizable idea" (a distinct and recognizable idea), on the other hand excludes it as "seldom needed in logic or in mathematics" (seldom needed in logic or in mathematics) (C. I. Lewis, A Survey of Symbolic Logic, 1918, p. 292). MacColl too is the same. He merely acknowledges [p. 227] five basic modalities, "true" (true), "false" (false), "certain" (certain), "impossible" (impossible), "variable" (variable), expresses them respectively by τ, ι, ε, η, θ, and takes the last, θ, that is "variable", as containing "not certain" (not certain) and "not impossible" (not impossible) (H. MacColl, Symbolic Logic and its applications. 1906). After all, that the proposition "it is possible that A is B" is compatible with the proposition "it is possible that A is not B" is what formal logic always teaches. That formal logic does not set up a distinction between the possible and the contingent may perhaps be called natural. However, in the logic of existence, possible existence and possible non-existence are by no means one and the same. That is, the possibility "existence is possible", on the horizon of non-existence, and the non-necessity "non-existence is possible", on the horizon of existence, are by no means one and the same. The possibility "existence is possible" has an existential-logical structure that is to unfold towards the necessity "existence is necessary". In contrast, the non-necessity "non-existence is possible" shows an existential-logical structure that is to retreat towards the impossibility "non-existence is necessary". Non-necessity is no doubt negative possibility, but it is precisely because there is something that should be positively distinguished from possibility that there is the concept "contingency". The [p. 228] [コマ 121] possible and the contingent do not both stand in a relation of subalternation to some neutral limit. The possible stands in a relation of subalternation particularly to the necessary, and the contingent stands in a relation of subalternation particularly to the impossible. However, since this relation is something that can be well grasped only in the logic of existence, it may be unreasonable to seek a difference between the possible and the contingent in formal logic itself. If the world of formal logic were everything, the concept of contingency might perhaps not have arisen. However, the actual demanded the birth of the concept of contingency, even to the point of resisting the neutral claim of formal logic. Just as in the actual before sunrise and after sunset are not the same, in the logic of existence possibility and contingency are not the same. That Hartmann, whilst standing at the standpoint of ontology, did not acknowledge the modality of contingency on formal-logical arguments must have some further Ground besides. Hartmann in fact, on the other hand, rejects contingency from the standpoint of ontology itself. According to him, chance is "the logical place of ignorance" (locus logicus ignorantiae), which, in both the causal and the teleological case, does not reach knowledge of the necessary connection. [p. 229] It is no more than the expression of the logical fact that knowledge of actuality exists even without knowledge of necessity. It does not bring the conclusion that there is actuality that does not contain necessity. In this way Hartmann, standing on "the Kantian world-picture, governed without remainder theoretically by causal connection and practically by purpose", limits the meaning of contingency to a result of insufficient knowledge. Furthermore, against indeterminism, which understands chance strictly ontologically, Hartmann reproaches the standpoint of indeterminism as atomising the events of the world and dissolving a unified stream into unconnected individual occurrences, and points out that a chance that does not recognise its condition in another, as something having its condition in itself, postulates the concept of "self-cause" (causa sui). However, even supposing that all the events of the universe are strictly conditioned with necessity, we considered earlier that there is, at an infinite remove, the idea of "primal contingency" as the beginning of the conditions. We cannot but acknowledge a kind of self-cause in primal contingency. Not only that, but can the *coming together* (Zusammentreffen) of conditions, which Hartmann himself acknowledges, really be understood with the concept of necessity alone? [p. 230] [コマ 122] Is not the concept of chance, which Hegel too acknowledged as "external actuality", required there? Even if, supposing, everything could be understood as necessary through the category of reciprocal action, insofar as the absolute is thought as the whole of all events, one goes back to the concept of primal contingency or self-cause as the chance over against the sum of necessity. Furthermore, if the concept of disjunctive contingency, which is the theme of this chapter, is taken into consideration, it will become clear how unreasonable it is to reject the concept of contingency. That Hartmann, in the correlation the contingent and the impossible, acknowledged only the impossible, the one term, as a Modality, and identified the contingent, the other term, with the possible and denied its modal independence, must be a marked defect seen from the standpoint of ontology.

On that point Oskar Becker (Oskar Becker, Zur Logik der Modalitäten, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. XI) seems to think more in keeping with the state of affairs. Becker starts from the way of thinking of the first system of Modality, passes through the second system, and closes in on the third system. Insofar as Becker treats the problem of Modality purely formally from the standpoint of symbolic logic, he has of course not sufficiently elucidated the concept of the contingent. However, he decidedly opposes Lewis's [p. 231] not regarding non-necessity

> -~-p

as one of the basic forms. He also reduced the signs from three to two, making it -•p. And under the name "non-necessary" or "possibly false" he treats the contingent as one basic modality, and accordingly sets up six irreducible Modalities (sechs irreduktible Modalitäten). Becker expresses the mutual relations of Modality by six abbreviations: N (notwendig, necessary), W (wahr, true), F (falsch, false), U (unmöglich, impossible), M (möglich, possible = nicht unmöglich, not impossible), M′ (möglicherweise falsch, possibly false = nicht notwendig, not necessary). And he gives the following combinations as dividing the whole domain of Modality.

> (1) W and W̄ = F
>
> (2) N and N̄ = M′
>
> (3) U and Ū = M

Since this corresponds to the combination (1) the actual, the non-actual, (2) the necessary, the contingent, (3) the impossible, the possible, [p. 232] [コマ 123] it means the first system of Modality. Its relation is clearly expressed in the figure on the left 〔Figure 8〕 too.

![Figure 8](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-08-p232.png)

**Figure 8.** Becker's six modalities on a single line between + and - (1948, p. 232). Becker's letters, no Japanese: N notwendig (necessary); W wahr (true); M möglich (possible); M′ möglicherweise falsch (possibly false); F falsch (false); U unmöglich (impossible).

```
          N        W        M
   + -----+--------+--------+----- -
          M′       F        U
```

But again, since he holds that N (•p) and U (•-p) form symmetrical modalities (symmetrischer Modus) by changing the sign, and that M (-•-p) and M′ (-•p), and also W (p) and F (-p), likewise form symmetrical modalities by a change of sign, if in this figure one takes WF as the central axis and considers symmetry to left and right, the standpoint of the second system appears there. Also, if one takes the modalities above the horizontal line as one set and those below as one set, the standpoint of the third system appears there. Becker further also uses a figure like the following 〔Figure 9〕 to make these relations clear. And from the standpoint of the second system he opposes "the logic of necessity" and "the logic of possibility", grasping on the one hand N and U in the similarity of apodicticity, and on the other hand grasping the common domain (das gemeinsame Gebiet) of M×M′ in problematicity. This domain is "indeterminate" (unbestimmt), and is "neither necessary nor impossible" [p. 233] (weder notwendig noch unmöglich). Also, standing at the standpoint of the third system, he grasps on the one hand the relation of proximity

> N < W < M

and on the other hand the relation of proximity

> U < F < M′

Further, concerning positivity and negativity, he sees the series of Modality as follows.

> +NWM     M′FU-

![Figure 9](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-09-p233.png)

**Figure 9.** Becker's figure of the positive and negative modalities (1948, p. 233). Letters as in Figure 8.

```
           |<-------------- M -------------->|
       +---------+-----------+-----------+---------+
   +   | N       W           |           F       U |   -
       +---------+-----------+-----------+---------+
                 |<-------------- M′ -------------->|
```

〔Tr.: Approximate rendering of Kuki's figure: a rectangle with + at its left and - at its right, divided by three vertical lines; N in the leftmost compartment, W at the first line, F at the third, U in the rightmost compartment. A brace above labelled M spans from the left edge to the line at F; a brace below labelled M′ spans from the line at W to the right edge.〕

This grasps on the one hand the necessary, the actual and the possible in positivity, and on the other hand the impossible, the non-actual and the contingent in negativity. The above is Becker's excellent consideration. Only, it is regrettable that, because he kept throughout to the standpoint of formal logic, he has not sufficiently elucidated the contingent as contingent. That is, the trace of a thinking that, despite his insisting on six *irreducible* Modalities, still saw the contingent as "possibly false", as a kind of possibility, is clear from the mere fact that he used the abbreviation M′ against M.

[p. 234] [コマ 124] In short, the core of the third system of Modality lies in witnessing, on the one hand, the correlation of subalternation of the necessary and the possible, and on the other hand the correlation of subalternation of the impossible and the contingent. Necessity is sometimes called "super-possibility" as the limit of possibility, but it is also not impossible to call impossibility "super-contingency" as the limit of contingency. The standpoint of the third system can be represented in a figure as on the left 〔Figure 10〕.

![Figure 10](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-10-p234.png)

**Figure 10.** The third system: necessity as circumference, impossibility as tangent, contingency at the point of tangency S (1948, p. 234). Labels: 必然性(可能性) necessity (possibility); 偶然性 contingency; 不可能性 impossibility, its characters split along the tangent, 不可 towards T and 能性 beyond S.

〔Tr.: Approximate rendering of Kuki's figure: a circle labelled inside 必然性 (可能性), necessity (possibility), with arrows running round it anticlockwise. A straight line touches the circle at the point S, lower right, and runs up to the right to T; the characters of 不可能性, impossibility, are written along it on either side of S. At S is written 偶然性, contingency. A second straight line runs from S through the point S′, higher on the circle, and dotted lines fan out from S between SS′ and ST.〕

In this figure the circumference represents *necessity*, and the arc represents *possibility*. The tangent ST represents *impossibility*, and the point of tangency S represents *contingency*. The point of tangency S, as the producing point of the curve, sets out from itself, advances in the direction of the arrow, and comes to rest when it has finished producing the whole circumference. The circumference and the point of tangency S grasped in the state of rest represent *actuality*, and the arc and the tangent ST thought in the state of motion represent *non-actuality*. [p. 235] The limit of the arc representing possibility, increased in the direction of the arrow, becomes the circumference representing necessity. When the arc gradually decreases, S′ approaching S without limit in the direction opposite to the arrow, the line SS′ approaching the line ST without limit, and finally, as its limit, the line SS′ coincides with the line ST and S′ coincides with S, then S as contingency emerges. In other words, contingency is S as the point of tangency at which the tangent ST representing impossibility touches the curve representing possibility. Contingency is something close to nothingness, having its position on the straight line of impossibility. And just as the negation of the circumference is the point, contingency is the negation of necessity. Also, just as on the circumference an infinite number of points of tangency besides the point S can be thought, contingency is no more than one disjunct of the totality of disjunctive possibility. Moreover, contingency, once it becomes aware that it is itself the producing point, is the force of the actual that, setting out from infinitesimal possibility, fills the curve continuously and can finally unfold possibility as far as the circumference of necessity. Contingency is the driving force that makes possibility become aware, from the virgin possibility that is possible by chance, of the maternal possibility that is always possible. Contingency merely fastens, frailly, an extreme-point existence to one point of the actual, but as the productive principle of reality it has the passion to bear the whole of productive activity.

[p. 236] [コマ 125] The same state of affairs can also be expressed by the figure on the left 〔Figure 11〕.

![Figure 11](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-11-p236.png)

**Figure 11.** Contingency as the apex of the triangle of possibility, on the line of impossibility (1948, p. 236). Labels: 必然性 necessity (on AB); 可能性 possibility; 不可能性 impossibility (on A′B′, split either side of the apex); 偶然性 contingency (at the apex). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

```
   A ========== necessity ========== B
      .                            .
        .                        .
          .     possibility    .
            .                .
              .            .
   A′ . . . . . . . ● . . . . . . . B′
                impossi- | -bility
                    contingency
```

〔Tr.: Approximate rendering of Kuki's figure: an inverted triangle. Its upper side AB is a thick line labelled 必然性 (necessity); its two other sides are dotted and meet at a lower apex, marked with a black dot and labelled 偶然性 (contingency); 可能性 (possibility) is written inside. A dotted horizontal line A′B′ runs through the apex, labelled 不可能性 (impossibility), the characters spread on either side of the apex.〕

Contingency, at the same time as being one point within the straight line representing impossibility, is the apex in the triangle representing possibility. Contingency is reality as well as being nihility. The apex, which is nihility qua reality, is the force that, as producing point, bears the existence of the whole triangle. The base of the triangle represents necessity, in the state of completion as the end of developmental production. Contingency, whilst itself being infinitesimal impossibility, by seizing infinitesimal possibility in the peril of the extreme point, gives "I" to "Thou" and receives "Thou" into "I", conceives possibility upon possibility, and finally coincides with necessity. That necessity and impossibility are represented by horizontal lines of the same length, AB and A′B′, is in order to symbolise the apodicticity common to the two. In contrast, that contingency and possibility issue from one point and only at their limit coincide with the two points of AB is in order to symbolise the problematicity common to contingency and possibility. That the one [p. 237] point of contingency and the line of necessity are drawn black and thick is in order to represent the actuality common to the two, and that possibility and impossibility are drawn with dotted lines is in order to express the non-actuality common to the two. Actuality is the character common to necessity as "developed actuality" and contingency as actuality that has been given birth. And just as in birth one lets out the first cry, actuality declares itself with a loud cry in contingency. That contingency has actuality despite its nihility is a marked character. In contrast, possibility, whilst being in the non-actual, yearns, on the basis of its rightful claim to reality, for the passage to the actual. What makes impossibility, which is eternally dead in the non-actual and in nihility, leap towards the actual is the supernatural power that contingency has.

〔Tr.: 大小對當 (*daishō taitō*): the relation of subalterns in the traditional square of opposition, rendered "subalternation". 會合 (*kaigō*) renders Hartmann's *Zusammentreffen*, "coming together". 虛無 (*kyomu*) and 虛無性 (*kyomusei*) are both rendered "nihility", following the binding change to the glossary; 無 is "nothingness". 實在 / 實在性: reality. 尖端的存在: "extreme-point existence". 神通力 (*jinzūriki*): supernatural power, a Buddhist term. At p. 231 Kuki writes Becker's reduced sign as -•p; at p. 232 the signs are •p, •-p, -•-p, -•p, p, -p; kept as printed. At p. 230 Kuki joins 偶然 and 不可能 with a dash; rendered "the contingent and the impossible". 追力 at p. 235 is read as printed ("driving force").〕

### 7. Summary of the three systems

Looking back on the above considerations, let us summarise the distinctive features of the first, second and third systems of Modality.

[p. 238] [コマ 126] In the *first* system, necessity and contingency are seen as a pair of modalities, as things that form contradictory opposition on the horizon of actuality, and possibility and impossibility are seen as a pair, as things that form contradictory opposition on the horizon of non-actuality. In the *second* system, necessity and impossibility, while constituting contrary opposition, are seen as a pair of modalities, as things standing in a relation of similarity in the character of apodicticity, and possibility and contingency, while constituting subcontrary opposition, are seen as a pair, as things standing in a relation of similarity in the character of problematicity. In the *third* system, necessity and possibility are seen as a pair of modalities, as things standing in the relation of proximity of subalternation in the dimension of reality, and contingency and impossibility are seen as a pair, as things standing in the relation of proximity of subalternation in the dimension of nihility. 〔Tr.: 矛盾對當, contradictory opposition; 反對々當, contrary opposition; 小反對々當, subcontrary opposition; 大小對當, subalternation. 實在性 is rendered "reality" and 虛無性 "nihility"; 現實性 remains "actuality".〕

The three systems can be represented by the figures on the left 〔Figures 12 to 14〕. 〔Tr.: In the vertical setting, "on the left" means "following".〕 The abbreviations and signs are as on the left.

- R: actuality (*Realitas*)
- R′: non-actuality (*Non-Realitas*)
- N: necessity (*Necessitas*)
- [p. 239] C: contingency (*Contingentia*)
- P: possibility (*Possibilitas*)
- I: impossibility (*Impossibilitas*)
- `+`: reality = being
- `-`: nihility = nothingness

**(I)**

![Figure 12](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-12-p239.png)

**Figure 12.** (I) The first system of modality (1948, p. 239). Letters and signs, no Japanese: R actuality; R′ non-actuality; N necessity; C contingency; P possibility; I impossibility; + reality = being; - nihility = nothingness.

| | left: R | right: R′ |
|---|---|---|
| above the line: `+` | N | P |
| below the line: `-` | C | I |

〔Tr.: Figure (I): a large circle divided by a horizontal diameter, with R at its left end and R′ at its right, `+` above the diameter and `-` below. Inside it are two equal circles, side by side and touching at the centre; the left one holds N above the diameter and C below, the right one P above and I below.〕

**(II)**

![Figure 13](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-13-p239.png)

**Figure 13.** (II) The second system of modality (1948, p. 239). Letters and signs as in Figure 12.

| | left: large circle | right: small circle |
|---|---|---|
| above the line: R | N (`+`) | C (`-`) |
| below the line: R′ | I (`-`) | P (`+`) |

〔Tr.: Figure (II): a large circle divided by a horizontal diameter, R above and R′ below. Inside it a large circle on the left (N above the diameter, I below) and a small circle on the right (C above, P below) touch at a point on the diameter to the right of centre. Straight lines run from this point of contact to the top of the left circle, marked `+`, and to its bottom, marked `-`, and to the top of the small circle, marked `-`, and to its bottom, marked `+`.〕

**(III)**

![Figure 14](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-14-p239.png)

**Figure 14.** (III) The third system of modality (1948, p. 239). Letters and signs as in Figure 12.

| | left of centre: `+` | right of centre: `-` |
|---|---|---|
| above the line: R | N (large circle) | C (small circle) |
| below the line: R′ | P (small circle) | I (large circle) |

〔Tr.: Figure (III): a large circle divided by a horizontal diameter, with `+` at its left end and `-` at its right, R above and R′ below. On the left a large circle (N) contains a small circle (P); on the right a large circle (I) contains a small circle (C); all four touch the centre. The upper arcs of N and C and the lower arcs of P and I are drawn solid; the lower arcs of N and C and the upper arcs of P and I are dotted.〕

Let me briefly explain the figures on the right 〔Figures 12 to 14〕. 〔Tr.: That is, the figures above.〕 (I) First system. Above and below the horizontal line form contradictory opposition. The upper part represents reality (`+`), the lower part represents nihility (`-`). The left side represents *actuality* (R), the right side represents *non-actuality* (R′). *Necessity* (N) and [p. 240] [コマ 127] *contingency* (C) stand in contradictory opposition on the side of actuality, and necessity belongs to the domain of reality, contingency to the domain of nihility. *Possibility* (P) and *impossibility* (I) stand in contradictory opposition on the side of non-actuality, and possibility belongs to the domain of reality, impossibility to the domain of nihility.

(II) Second system. Above and below the horizontal line represent *actuality* (R) and *non-actuality* (R′). The relation of contradictory opposition appears in the opposition of left and right. However, within the range of actuality, the left side represents reality (`+`) and the right side represents nihility (`-`), and within the range of non-actuality, the reverse of that, the left side represents nihility and the right side represents reality. *Necessity* (N) and *impossibility* (I) form contrary opposition, and *contingency* (C) and *possibility* (P) form subcontrary opposition. The large circle of contrary opposition represents apodicticity, and the small circle of subcontrary opposition represents problematicity.

(III) Third system. From the centre of the circle, the left side represents reality (`+`) and the right side represents nihility (`-`). The upper part of the horizontal line represents *actuality* (R), the lower part represents *non-actuality* (R′). The large circle of *necessity* (N) subsumes the small circle of *possibility* (P) and forms subalternation in the domain of reality, and the large circle of *impossibility* (I) subsumes the small circle of *contingency* (C) and forms subalternation in the domain of nihility. Since necessity and contingency are things that form contradictory opposition within the range of actuality, in the range of non-actuality, [p. 241] that is, the part below the horizontal line, the large circle of necessity and the small circle of contingency represent non-belonging by dotted lines. Likewise, since impossibility and possibility are things that form contradictory opposition within the range of non-actuality, in the range of actuality, that is, the part above the horizontal line, the large circle of impossibility and the small circle of possibility represent non-belonging by dotted lines. The above is the explanation of the figures.

Further, if we speak of the relations in the three systems with regard to contingency alone, contingency is seen in the first system as the actual that contradicts necessity, is seen in the second system as something problematic that is similar to possibility, and is seen in the third system as something of nihility that is about to be subsumed by impossibility. If this relation of contingency is shown in a figure following the figure on p. 198 〔Figure 3〕, it becomes as above 〔Figure 15〕. 〔Tr.: The figure stands above the closing columns of this paragraph on p. 241.〕

![Figure 15](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-15-p241.png)

**Figure 15.** Contingency in the three systems of modality (1948, p. 241). Labels: 偶然 contingent; 必然(第一體系) necessary (first system); 可能(第二體系) possible (second system); 不可能(第三體系) impossible (third system); 現實性(矛盾) actuality (contradictory); 問題性(小反對) problematicity (subcontrary); 虛無性(大小) nihility (subalternation). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

```
necessary (first system) ───── actuality (contradictory) ───── contingent
                                                              ╱ │
                                 problematicity              ╱  │  nihility
                                 (subcontrary)              ╱   │  (subaltern)
                                                           ╱    │
                                                          ╱     │
                              possible (second system)          impossible (third system)
```

〔Tr.: Three lines run from a point at the upper right marked 偶然 (contingent): leftwards to 必然 (necessary), labelled 現實性 (actuality) with 矛盾 (contradictory); straight down to 不可能 (impossible), labelled 虛無性 (nihility) with 大小 (subaltern); diagonally down to the left to 可能 (possible), labelled 問題性 (problematicity) with 小反對 (subcontrary). Each end point carries its system in parentheses: 第一體系, 第二體系, 第三體系. The horizontal labels are set right to left.〕

Further, the three kinds of systems of Modality are things that each see the modalities from a different standpoint, and each system, all of them, has [p. 242] [コマ 128] its distinctive features. They are by no means of a nature such that one should be taken and the others discarded. The structure of contingency too is completely grasped only when it is witnessed synthetically from these three points of view. One should emphasise the cooperation of the three systems.

### 8. The play of chance and the concept of probability

Up to this point we have considered contingency mainly on the basis of its relation to possibility or impossibility, but such a consideration means standing behind the given actual or existence and glimpsing the tendency of development at its starting point. For that, the actual or existence must be seen from the disjunctive standpoint. As in "A is either B, or C, or D", disjunctive possibility, and further also impossibility, is made the background of the actual.

Let us consider the case where, taking in the disjunctive possibility that the faces of a die possess, one plays the *play* of chance. That a particular face shown by a die is in general taken as having the character of the contingent is because it is thought against the background of the possibility of six [p. 243] [コマ 128] faces. It is because it is thought that it could also have been any of the other five. Certainly probability theory will seek some constancy there and try to exclude chance. However, what is that so-called exclusion of chance? What is probability? 〔Tr.: 確率 (*kakuritsu*), the mathematical term; Kuki's 蓋然性 (*gaizensei*) is also rendered "probability", per the glossary. Where the two need to be told apart the Japanese is given.〕 The probability of the occurrence of an event means the ratio of the number of cases favourable to the occurrence of that event to the number of all possible cases. That is, the quotient obtained by dividing the number of favourable cases by the number of all possible cases is the probability. Therefore, what probability theory determines with regard to the play of dice is no more than the quantitative relation that exists between all the possible cases of the appearance and non-appearance of a particular face of the die and the "favourable" contingent cases in which that face appears. There is a difference in that between the case where the die is completely homogeneous and symmetrical and the case where its perfection is somewhat lacking, but if it is assumed to be perfect, the law of probability set up as a priori probability is only that there is a probability of 1/6 for each face of the die to appear. It is only that appearing once in six times is probable. Moreover, this theoretical quantitative relation is ideally valid in practice only in the sum of innumerable cases. [p. 244] [コマ 129] If the number of times is small, it can even be extremely lopsided. Again, if, as a result of experimenting with that die over, say, ten thousand throws, it were found that the probability of the one face is 9/60 and the probability of the six face on the opposite side is 11/60, that is the so-called empirical probability. In this case the empirical probability has more objective value than the earlier a priori probability 1/6, but in the end it is of the same nature. Regardless of whether probability is a priori or empirical, the law of probability holds on the so-called macroscopic horizon, so that on the microscopic horizon the contingent variability, of which face comes up in each case, still strictly remains.

Further, let us consider a little the meaning of *probability* 〔Tr.: 蓋然性〕, the basic concept of probability theory. It is usual to divide probability into two, philosophical (qualitative) probability and mathematical (quantitative) probability. What does not reach certainty is called probability, and of probability one says that it is much or little. If one thinks that saying much or little is a quantitative matter, then probability is in essence all quantitative probability. However, there are cases that permit strict quantitative determination and cases that do not. The case that does not permit [p. 245] strict quantitative determination is called philosophical or qualitative probability, and the case that permits strict quantitative determination is called mathematical or quantitative probability (see W. Wundt, Logik, I, 4 Aufl. S. 419-422; Cournot, Théorie des Chances et des Probabilités, 1843, p. 427-428, 437-440; R. Berthelot, Un romantisme utilitaire I, 1911, p. 311-312). Philosophical probability appears best in inference by analogy and inductive inference, whereas mathematical probability forms the theme of probability theory.

Further, probability contains in its concept the moment of approach to certainty. This is clear also in the light of the fact that inference by analogy and inductive inference draw near from probability to certainty. Together with this character, probability in the narrow sense is a concept applied especially only to cases greater than half of certainty. Accordingly, for cases smaller than half of certainty the concept of improbability arises. Kant's definition of probability is of that narrow kind. According to Kant, "When one says probability, it is to be understood as an acknowledgement on insufficient Grounds. However, in that case, the ratio of the insufficient Grounds to the sufficient Grounds is greater than the ratio of the Grounds of the opposite to the sufficient Grounds." Accordingly, "probability is greater than half of certainty (mehr, als die Hälfte der Gewissheit)" (Kant, [p. 246] [コマ 130] Logik, hrsg. v. Jäsche, 3 Aufl. S. 90-91). Further, on the ground that "only the mathematician can determine the ratio of insufficient Grounds to sufficient Grounds" (Ib.), Kant argued that the concept of probability properly ought to belong to the domain of mathematics. Probability in the domain of mathematics, that is, the probability of which probability theory speaks, is probability in the broad sense, valid for the whole that comprehends probability in the narrow sense and improbability.

According to Laplace, "the ratio of the number of favourable cases to the number of all possible cases is the measure of probability, and probability is nothing other than a fraction whose numerator is the number of favourable cases and whose denominator is the number of all possible cases" (Laplace, Essai philosophique sur les Probabilités, Paris, Gauthier-Villars, 1921, p. 6). In Cournot's way of expressing it, probability is "the ratio of the number of chance occasions favourable to the event to the total number of chance occasions" (Cournot, l. c. p. 24). In short, probability 〔Tr.: 蓋然性〕, insofar as it has a quantitative relation as its essence, is nothing other than probability 〔Tr.: 確率〕 as quantity of probability 〔Tr.: 蓋然量〕. Mises, defining probability, says: "The limiting value of the relative frequency with which a given mark appears, provided that this limiting value must be insensitive to place selection, such a limiting value is called the probability of the appearance of this mark within the collective under consideration" [p. 247] (Richard von Mises, Wahrscheinlichkeit, Statistik und Wahrheit, 1928, S. 29). Relative frequency (relative Häufigkeit) corresponds to the fraction Laplace speaks of, but Mises, from the standpoint of empirical probability, that is, statistical probability, makes his main aim the determination of the limiting value of relative frequency. That the limiting value is insensitive to place selection (gegen Stellenauswahl unempfindlich) means that the limiting value receives no influence whatever from place selection. Place selection is the selecting out of only a certain place, or a certain part, in a series. As an example of a case in which the limiting value is influenced by place selection, the case is given of a highway on which a small milestone stands every hundred metres and a large milestone every thousand metres. If one walks along that highway over a long distance, one finds that the limiting value of the relative frequency of coming upon a large milestone in the series of milestones is 1/10. However, if one made a selection in the series of milestones and decided, for example, to attend only to every other small milestone, the relative frequency of the large milestones would no longer be 1/10 but would change to 1/5. That is, in this case, when by free selection a part of the elements is specially taken out of the whole and only that is attended to, the relative [p. 248] [コマ 131] frequency's limiting value receives its influence. In the case of a die there is never any such thing. Even if one throws a die and takes account only of every other throw, if one goes on for a considerably long time there is no change in the frequency. The collective that probability theory takes as its problem must be a collective that satisfies the requirement that the limiting value of the frequency is unchanged even if, by arbitrary place selection, only a part of the elements is taken out of the whole and attended to. That a change is brought about in the limiting value of the frequency is because there is lawfulness in the series, and that no change is brought about is because there is irregularity (Regellosigkeit) in the series. Probability theory, discovering a determinate quantitative relation with regard to irregularity of this kind, determines the degree of probability.

After all, since the quantity of probability has as its essence that it is a fraction, it comes to be smaller than 1 and greater than 0. That is, the maximum is 1 and means certainty or the necessary, and the minimum is 0 and means the impossible. That Kant, regarding the constitution of probability, says that the ratio of the insufficient Grounds to the sufficient Grounds is greater than the ratio of the Grounds of the opposite to the sufficient Grounds, and also says that probability is greater than half of certainty, is because he thinks of 1/2 [p. 249] and above as probability. Accordingly, as already said, below 1/2 can be called improbability. Such probability of 0.5 and above is probability in the narrow sense, but the probability that probability theory deals with is probability in the broad sense, valid for the whole between 1 and 0. Further, insofar as probability occupies a position midway between necessity and impossibility, its relation to possibility and contingency must be considered. Possibility and contingency stand, so to speak, back to back, at any degree whatever of probability in the broad sense. Possibility faces towards 1, and contingency faces towards 0.

![Figure 16](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-16-p249.png)

**Figure 16.** Probability between necessity (1) and impossibility (0), with the relations of opposition (1948, p. 249). Labels: 必然 necessary; 不可能 impossible; (可能) (possible); (偶然) (contingent); 矛盾 contradictory; 反對 contrary; 小反對 subcontrary; 大小 subalternation; (狹義の)蓋然 probable (in the narrow sense); 非蓋然 improbable; 蓋然 probable. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

> [Figure: a vertical scale from 0 (bottom) to 1 (top), marked in tenths, with 0.5 labelled. A brace on the right spans the whole scale from 0 to 1: 蓋然 (probable). Two inner braces: from 0.5 to 1, (狹義の)蓋然, (narrow-sense) probable; from 0 to 0.5, 非蓋然, improbable. To the left of the scale: 必然 (necessary) at the top, level with 1; 不可能 (impossible) at the bottom, level with 0; (可能) (possible) and (偶然) (contingent) side by side a little below 0.5, joined by a dotted bracket labelled 小反對 (subcontrary). An arrow runs from (可能) up to 必然, labelled 大小 (subaltern); an arrow runs from (偶然) down to 不可能, labelled 大小. A dotted line labelled 矛盾 (contradictory) joins 必然 and (偶然); a second dotted line labelled 矛盾 joins (可能) and 不可能; an outermost dotted line labelled 反對 (contrary) joins 必然 and 不可能.]

Let us represent the above relations by the figure above 〔Figure 16〕. As for the relations of opposition, they have already been shown in a figure on p. 203 〔Figure 4〕. Possibility and contingency in subcontrary opposition occupy, in the figure above, a position below 0.5 for convenience, but it goes without saying that they can be located at any point between 1 and 0.

[p. 250] [コマ 132] It is in the fact that probability theory, while discovering laws of probability on the macroscopic horizon with regard to irregularity, cannot exclude that irregularity, that is, contingent variability, from individual cases on the microscopic horizon, that the radical powerlessness of probability theory with regard to the philosophical posing of the problem of contingency lies.

Of course, seen microscopically, the face that a die shows in each individual case will probably be necessarily determined by physical properties such as the die, the manner of throwing, the resistance of the air, and the plane onto which it is thrown. However, it is in the point that one can think, from the ultimate standpoint, that another causal series of necessity also could have been taken, in the point that one can think that the causal series now given as the actual does not necessarily have absoluteness, that is, in the point that one thinks there is a discrepancy between possibility and the actual, that contingency exists. Poincaré says the following with regard to the play of roulette. "Let us divide a disc into a hundred sectors, colour them alternately red and black, and then think of a needle being spun around a spindle. If the needle stops on a red sector it is a win; if not, it is a loss. Clearly everything lies in the first push that we give the needle. For example, supposing the needle turns ten or twenty times, according to whether one pushed it strongly or weakly at first, [p. 251] the manner of stopping becomes earlier or later. Merely by the first pushing force differing by one thousandth or one two-thousandth, the needle stops either on a black sector or on the next red sector. This is because of a slight difference that cannot be perceived by the muscular sense, and that cannot be measured even with a more delicate instrument. Accordingly, how the needle just set in motion will move cannot be foreseen. That is precisely why we wait, with pounding heart, leaving everything to chance" (Poincaré, Science et Méthode, p. 70-71). The reason the contingent is in general contingent lies in the point that, when the needle has stopped on red, one thinks that it could also have stopped on black, and when it has stopped on black, one thinks that it could also have stopped on red. However, thinking so is not necessarily due to a reason such as Poincaré states. It is not only from the subjective reason that the slight difference in the first pushing force cannot be measured even with a delicate instrument, and that accordingly how the needle will move cannot be foreseen. Contingency does not have its Ground only in a lack of knowledge regarding the facts. The true contingency that makes the player's heart pound lies rather, regarding the first pushing force itself, in the point that the determinate strength given as fact does not necessarily have absolute necessity, in the point that one thinks it could also have been another strength, [p. 252] [コマ 133] that it lies. That is, it lies in the point that, even if one thinks it to be another strength, no contradiction whatever exists.

Let us further take horse racing as a concrete example and consider it. That horse racing is one kind of so-called "winning and losing by chance" cannot be disputed. Of course it cannot be said that there is no tendency to try to exclude chance from horse racing. According to Kikuchi Kan's "My Philosophy of Betting Tickets" (carried in *Hanashi*, published by Bungei Shunjū-sha, Shōwa 10, May issue), "The dividend that is the result of good judgement, regardless of whether the amount is large or small, its satisfaction is great. The dividend of a *fluke hit*, even if it be two hundred yen, is *speculative*, and is not something a proper betting-ticket fan should count to his credit." "Even a long shot of a hundred and twenty or thirty yen may, in terms of credit, be equal to two hundred yen. Even a dividend of two hundred yen may, in terms of credit, be worthless. Taking two hundred yen on a new horse by a *fluke hit* is not much different from merely *picking up money*." "There are people who bet on tickets without even knowing what a thoroughbred is; this is to be lamented. To bet on tickets without studying the horses' bloodlines, records and so on at all is to be a *gambler*." The writer appears to be opposed to enjoying horse racing as a play of chance. "Examine carefully the results of each race at the same meeting. Thanks to that, one can take one or two big long shots [p. 253]." The writer tries to set up laws of probability by statistics and to exclude chance as far as possible from winning and losing itself. This attitude also agrees with the attitude that tries to exclude chance from the size of the dividend. "The size of the dividend on a *place* bet depends mostly on how the other favourites cross the line. In that respect it is *more contingent*. One had better aim for a big long shot on a win bet." When the writer stands on the one hand on the standpoint of the "judge of form" and on the other on the standpoint of the betting-ticket buyer who expects a profit, it is natural that he should plan the exclusion of chance from horse racing. But will he succeed in it? The writer says at the end: "In buying betting tickets, winning is extremely hard. Only to enjoy betting tickets at a sacrifice that does not strain oneself, this would be the proper way of the racing fan. Not only has no racing fan built a storehouse, but it is said that a person who goes in and out of the racecourse for two or three years in a row is a person of very considerable means. ... Buying betting tickets is a pastime. It is squandering money. If one truly wishes to make money, one had better apply oneself to a proper family trade." This probably tells that the macroscopic character of the law of probability does not succeed in removing microscopic chance. Accordingly, in spite of the emphasis on "judgement", in spite of the rejection of "gamblers", this is something that acknowledges that at least one half of "the charm of horse racing" still lies in the play of chance, in "winning and losing by chance" [p. 254] [コマ 134].

Then, of what character is the chance immanent in horse racing? As in the case of the die, taking microscopic chance as something that holds only on the macroscopic horizon, one could perhaps also say that on the microscopic horizon the order of finishing of the horses all has causal determination. If so, contingency as a practical problem would dissolve into something subjective that depends on the incompleteness of knowledge. Such contingency differs subjectively in degree according to the degree of knowledge of the spectators, and is something that merely constitutes, in connection with the whole mental mechanism, the object of psychological research into contingency. And yet, independently of such subjective chance, philosophically there exists objective contingency. When horse A has taken first place, horse A's first place does not necessarily have absolute necessity. So long as it contains no logical contradiction to think of the case in which horse A is second or third, contingency exists in the fact of horse A's first place. If causal determination existed in every detail, and if it were all, supposing so, known, then the order of finishing too would be known entirely in advance, and so in that case horse racing would probably not take place as a temporal course. [p. 255] However, even if the contingency that should first be seen at the finishing line is lost, contingency still exists at the starting line. When the horses have lined up at the starting line, both the order of finishing and the margins are already decided by that line-up, so there is no need to run the race itself. The finishing line has come to coincide with the starting line. The duration of time that intervenes between the starting line and the finishing line has been completely eliminated. And yet the contingency of disjunctive contingency still exists in the point that each determination in that case could also have been another determination.

Such disjunctive contingency is not infrequently despised as empty contingency or empty possibility. According to Hegel, possibility of this kind is a mere abstraction over against concrete actuality, and so long as it does not contradict itself, everything becomes possible. This form of not contradicting itself is a form that can be given to any content whatever, provided only that the content is separated from concrete connection. Accordingly, even the most irrational things come to be thought possible. Just as a stone thrown into the air falls, it is also possible that the moon will fall to the earth tonight. That the King of Turkey converts to Christianity and becomes a Catholic priest, [p. 256] [コマ 135] and becomes Pope of Rome, is also possible. The words "such and such is possible", merely formally, are shallow and empty. "Especially in philosophy, there must be no such thing as saying that such and such is 'possible', or that 'something else too' is possible, or, as people often say, that such and such is 'thinkable'" (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 143, S. 191-192; Wissenschaft der Logik, hrsg. v. Lasson, II, S. 171). But we can also think the opposite. Even without completely cutting the content off from concrete connection, as Hegel says, we can think disjunctive possibility, and accordingly disjunctive contingency, while keeping a certain moderate concreteness. Even without thinking that the King of Turkey becomes Pope of Rome, it is not necessarily irrational to think of the case in which Ibrahim Pasha, who besieged Missolonghi, happened to be struck by a cannonball and fell, the Turkish army raised the siege, and Byron did not die abroad. Again, conversely, in cases where irrationality arises because one stops halfway without fully leaving the concrete behind, one can dissolve the irrationality by leaving the concrete further and going to the abstract. That the moon will fall to the earth tonight is very markedly encumbered by the concreteness of time and space. Even if we think of the case in which, in the formation of the universe, the moon [p. 257] was not made, it is not necessarily irrational. Even if we think of the case in which the earth too was not made and man did not arise, it cannot be said that it is irrational. Astronomy teaches that the moon is too large in relation to the earth and is rather an exception as the satellite of a planet, and also teaches that our solar system, in which the fixed star did not become a multiple star and the planets revolve around a single sun as centre, forms an exception to the law of the formation of fixed stars. In the background against which the theory of evolution explains the evolution from animals to man, too, it must also be thinking of something like the possibility that the evolution to man did not take place and the anthropoid apes formed the summit of biological development. Further, even when philosophy carries out a consideration of "nothingness", it must presuppose the disjunctive relation according to the law of excluded middle, or some kind of supra-logical possibility of thought.

Husserl too says the following. "The cognitive acts that found experience posit the actual individually. They posit it as existing spatially and temporally. They posit it as something that is in this temporal position and has this duration and a determinate actual content. And that actual content is something that, according to its essence, could also have been in any other temporal position. Again, they posit it as something that is in this place in a determinate physical form [p. 258] [コマ 136]. And that same actuality could, according to its essence, also have been in any arbitrary place whatever, and also with any arbitrary form whatever. Likewise also, that actuality, even if in fact it does not change, could have changed. Or it could have changed in a way different from the way it in fact changed. ... It could, according to its essence, also have been something else. Certainly some determinate natural law may hold. And according to that natural law, if such and such an actual state of affairs in fact exists, such and such a determinate consequence must in fact exist. But such a law merely expresses a factual determination. That determination itself could also have been stated entirely otherwise. And that determination, as something belonging in advance to the essence of the objects of possible experience, already presupposes that the objects governed by that determination, if seen in themselves, are contingent" (Husserl, Ideen zu einer reinen Phänomenologie, S. 8-9). Benno Erdmann too says likewise: "Actuality has necessarily become the state it is in, according to causal connection. However, this necessity was by no means anywhere the only possible [p. 259] one" (Benno Erdmann, Logik, 3 Aufl. S. 488). August Faust too says: "If the concrete filling-out of the content of all intra-worldly existence is still to be understood conceptually in some way, it becomes clear that an abstract concept extremely poor in content, such as possibilitas logica (logical possibility), is precisely indispensable" (August Faust, Der Möglichkeitsgedanke, II, S. 59).

From the ultimate standpoint that admits logical possibility, and accordingly disjunctive contingency, that this clover has four leaves and not three is contingent, and that Mount Asama is a volcano, neither a fault-block mountain nor a fold mountain, is contingent. That Toyotomi Hideyoshi was born in Nakamura in Owari, neither in Kyōto nor in Ōsaka nor anywhere else, is also contingent. Leibniz, stating that innumerable worlds were possible, holds that there could have been a world in which Sextus Tarquinius was born in Thrace and became the happiest of men as the object of the veneration of the whole people, there could have been a world in which he was born in Corinth and grew old in mediocrity while loved by the citizens, there could have been a world in which he was born in Rome, violated another man's wife and became an exile, and besides these, innumerable worlds containing Sextuses of every kind and in innumerable fashions (Sextus de toute espèce, et d'une infinité de fa- [p. 260] [コマ 137] çons) (Leibniz, Théodicée, III. §§ 414-416, Opera philos. ed. Erdmann, p. 622-624). In actual fact we could also have been American, or French, or Ethiopian, or Indian, or Chinese, or of any other nationality. That we are Japanese is contingent. We could also have been insects or birds or beasts. That we are human, neither insect nor bird nor beast, is contingent. The *Zō agon gyō* 〔Tr.: 雜阿含經, the *Saṃyuktāgama*〕 (fascicle 15) skilfully compares the contingency of being born human. A blind turtle of immeasurable lifespan, hidden in the great ocean, puts out its head once in a hundred years. Again, a floating log with a single hole drifts in the sea, east and west as the wind takes it. It says that being born human is like this blind turtle, when it raises its head, happening to meet the hole in this log. If one thinks that one disjunct of disjunctive possibility has been thrown out like the face of a die, one can find a profound meaning also in the fact that the Sāṃkhya-Yoga doctrine or the philosophy of the Vedānta school thought that the unattached and free Brahman creates and transforms for the sake of *play*. Heraclitus too said, "Time is a child who is playing the *play* of shōgi" (Diels, Die Fragmente der Vorsokratiker I, Herakleitos Fr. 52).

[p. 261] In short, contingency of this kind is the relation that, when one disjunct of disjunctive possibility has been posited before our eyes as the actual, that actual has to possibility as a whole, and accordingly also to impossibility. And in the speed of the transition by which disjunctive possibility, pushing through the opposition of impossibility, slides into actuality, the streamlined form of the play of chance is felt.

### 9. The temporal character of contingency

That the logic of Modality has a deep relation to temporality has also been noted by Oskar Becker (Oskar Becker, Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. XI, S. 539), and for us it is of particular importance to elucidate the temporal character of contingency. The temporal delimitation of the contingent, in particular the simultaneity and successiveness of hypothetical contingency, was discussed earlier (see Chapter 2, section 17). But what special character, in general, does the contingent have on the temporal horizon over against the other modalities? By considering the relation between the other modalities and contingency on the horizon of time, [p. 262] [コマ 138] we can make the structure of contingency still clearer.

First, possibility has, through the schema "in advance", the temporal character of the *future*. Among philosophers who stress the connection between possibility and the future there is Heidegger (see Iwanami Kōza Tetsugaku, Kuki, "The Philosophy of Existence", pp. 54-55, 59-77). The central point of Heidegger's philosophy is the idea of care as projection (Entwurf) and of resoluteness as running ahead (Vorlaufen). Projection is throwing possibility, as possibility, in advance ahead of oneself. The way in which Existenz is its possibility as possibility is care in the mode of projection. Running ahead is running ahead into possibility. In running ahead, possibility must be understood as possibility, formed as possibility, and held out as possibility. To anticipate possibility as possibility, to run ahead into possibility in advance, is the running-ahead character of resolve. And insofar as it rests on the existence of care itself that care is originarily modalised, through anticipatory resoluteness, in the form of understanding projection, the principal moment of care must be "being ahead of itself" (Sich-vorweg-sein). When the ontological meaning of care is interpreted as temporality, the principal moment of temporality also accordingly lies in the future. The primary phenomenon of temporality [p. 263] is the future. Time has its starting point in the future. The direction of time goes, on the basis of "coming towards" (zu-kommen) oneself from the future, the "future" (Zu-kunft), towards the past that has been. The self-futurity of possibility makes possible the running-ahead character of resolve. That possibility is "futural" is because the temporal character of possibility is "of the future".

Against this, necessity has, through the schema "already", the *past* as its temporal character. Aristotle called the necessary essence "what it was to be" (τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι). The necessary essence is not merely something that "is" (ἐστί). It is something that already "was" (ἦν) in order to be actually. To ask about essential necessity it is not enough to ask "what is it" (τί ἐστι). It must also include the question "what was it" (τί ἦν). Necessity has continuance from the past as its temporal moment. Plato's Idea is the type of essential necessity, but to grasp the Idea one must look back over the infinite past and recollect (ἀνάμνησις) the intuition in a former life. The Idea, because it is the necessary, already existed in the far past too, [p. 264] [コマ 139] in its unchanging form. To recollect what had been forgotten and return to the far past is the only way of witnessing the necessary. "In the beginning was the Word" (ἐν ἀρχῇ ἦν ὁ λόγος) (Gospel of John 1:1) is the same. The necessity of the Logos is thought to have been present already at the beginning of all things and to be unchanged even now. It is clear that the temporal character of necessity is of the past. Further, even from the standpoint that sees necessity as the unfolded actual, the past character of necessity is not lost. Someone may say: if necessity is the unfolding of the actual, does not necessity occupy a place in the future? However, if necessity is the unfolding of the actual, then, because it is the unfolding of the actual, necessity as the unfolded actual must carry a recollection of the past. Just as possibility always has a prospect onto the future, necessity must always have a retrospect onto the past. All unfolding unfolds from future possibility towards past necessity. Because, in the form not yet unfolded, the unfolding in the future is anticipated "in advance", possibility is called futural. Because, in the form "already" unfolded, the course of unfolding in the past [p. 265] is looked back upon, necessity is called past.

Whereas the temporality of possibility is the future and the temporality of necessity is the past, the temporality of contingency is the *present*, which has "now" as its schema. In general, the possible of the future passes through the actual into the necessary of the past. The possible, from great possibility down to the infinitesimal possibility that borders on impossibility, becomes actual through the possibility of the possible. The actual unfolds into the necessary. And in general the case in which the possible *meets* the plane of the actual is the contingent in the broad sense. Even what has great possibility takes on more or less the character of the contingent insofar as it meets the plane of the actual. Hegel too says: "The contingent is the actual that is at the same time determined as merely possible" (Hegel, Wissenschaft der Logik, hrsg. v. Lasson, II, S. 173). Further, the contingent in the eminent sense is nothing other than the case in which the smallest possibility, or impossibility, meets the plane of the actual. And insofar as actuality means, temporally, the present, the temporality of contingency too must be the present. The "now" in what is said in the preface of the Kyōgyōshinshō, "hard to meet, *now* I have been able to meet it", is the temporal schema of contingency. In general, the contingent is created in presentness. And the contingent in the eminent sense, as the limit of the diminution of future possibility, [p. 266] [コマ 140] gushes forth suddenly from the nothingness of an impossibility without future, passing through the non-existent single point of the present.

That the contingent is an Encounter in the present may be thought, without objection, to mean the "falling" of presence, as "being alongside" (Sein-bei). It may also, without objection, be thought of as "falling off" as its movement. But it goes without saying that to construe "chance" (Zufall) as "falling" (Verfall) or as "falling off" (Abfall) is to be permitted only on the condition that every axiological point of view is excluded from the words "falling" and "falling off".

Further, primal contingency may be thought to bear the eternal past, as the "oldest" contingent (see p. 283). It may also be thought to have futurity, as what is pregnant with all the contingent that is to come. But when primal contingency is recollected in pastness, primal contingency is modalised into necessity. And when it is anticipated in futurity, primal contingency is modalised into possibility. That primal contingency is contingent must lie in the presentness by which it is contingent in the given instant of the "now". What is pursued in pastness is not the contingent. [p. 267] What is expected in futurity is not the contingent either. The contingent must be something triggered only in presentness. That successive contingency has its basis in simultaneous contingency, 〔Tr.: The text prints 同時的偶然た基礎を, apparently a misprint for 同時的偶然に基礎を.〕 and that from that point too the temporal character of the contingent can be determined as the present, I said in a word earlier (see pp. 167-168), but even apart from that structural fact, and even taking successive contingency by itself alone, we can grasp the presentness of the contingent. If, deceived by the word "succession" in successive contingency, we represent something enduring, then it is already not the contingent, and turns into something belonging to the necessary or the possible. What makes successive contingency contingent must depend on its present character, which coincides, in the given instant of the "now", with what was "already" given. The present in which contingency comes about is the present that "passes in a point" (in puncto praeterit) (Augustinus, Confessiones XI, § 28). It is a present equal to nothingness. Where contingency comes about precariously in a present equal to nothingness, the nihilistic character that contingency shares with impossibility is felt.

As Becker too points out (l. c. S. 539), actuality is the "direct mode" (modus rectus). Possibility, which takes its steps towards the actual, and necessity, which advances its steps from the actual, are both the "oblique mode" (modus obli- [p. 268] [コマ 141] quus). Brentano in his theory of time made the present the "direct mode" and the future and the past the "oblique mode" (F. Brentano, Psychologie vom empirischen Standpunkt, hrsg. v. Kraus, II, S. 225). Whereas presentness as the actual is to be "looked at straight on", the futurity that possibility has and the pastness that necessity has can be brought into the field of vision only when one who stands in the actuality of the present "looks obliquely" to the right and to the left. The temporal priority of contingency also follows from this. If we intuit states of affairs bodily and originarily, contingency is neither experienced as the negation of necessity nor understood in connection with possibility. To look straight at the contingent as the actual in the present is the original, primary, primal fact. Then, secondarily, we can think of the case in which one looks obliquely at the futural possible as a tendency towards the future and looks obliquely at the past necessary as continuance from the past. In other words, possibility and necessity are, by their temporal character itself, things that can be witnessed only obliquely. The only modality that can be looked at straight on is contingency, which is present at a point. That contingency is determined as the negation of necessity, or as the correlate of possibility, is in the domain of logic, which has already departed from the immediacy of experience. In the immediacy [p. 269] of experience, the contingent, as the mode of straight looking, as the direct mode, insofar as it has its position in the present, holds temporal priority. It is, moreover, nothing other than the pulse of the eternal present as the instant.

On the basis of the results of this consideration, the temporal characters of the possible, the necessary and the contingent can be represented by a figure like the above 〔Figure 17〕.

![Figure 17](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-17-p269.png)
