Labels: 過去 past; 未來 future; 現在 present; 現實 actual (現 above the axis, 實 below); 必然 necessary; 可能 possible; 偶然(不可能) contingent (impossible). Horizontal labels are set right to left.

```
                         actual
                            | . . . . . . . . . . . +
                            |                    /  |
                            |                 /     |
                            |              /        |
                            |           /           |
                            |        /  possible    |
                            |     /                 |
                            |  /                    |
past <----------------------+-------------------------> future
    |                    /  | present
    |                 /     | contingent (impossible)
    | necessary    /        |
    |           /           |
    |        /              |
    |     /                 |
    |  /                    |
    + . . . . . . . . . . . |
```

〔Tr.: The figure stands at the top of p. 269, between this sentence and the next paragraph. Kuki's figure: a horizontal axis from 過去 (past, left) to 未來 (future, right, arrowhead), crossed by a vertical line labelled 現 above and 實 below (現實, the actual). The point of intersection is labelled 現在 (present) and 偶然 (不可能), the contingent (the impossible). A straight line runs through that point from lower left to upper right. To the right, the hatched triangle between the axis and the rising line is labelled 可能 (possible), closed by a dotted line to the vertical line; to the left, the hatched triangle below the axis is labelled 必然 (necessary), closed by a dotted line to the vertical line. A short arrow runs from the origin leftwards towards the necessary.〕

In the above, we have grasped presentness as the temporal character of contingency, and I think that at the same time the close relation between contingency and *actuality* has also been made clear. In the first system of modality, contingency was determined as standing in contradictory opposition to necessity on the static horizon of *actuality*. In the second system, the *actual* was problematised dynamically, and contingency and possibility were witnessed in the relation of subcontrary opposition as constituting the actuality of problematic actuality. In the third system, contingency, while it was grasped in the dimension of nihility as standing in the relation of proximity of subalternation with impossibility, was nonetheless [p. 270] [コマ 142] understood as proclaiming its own actuality as the birth of the *actual* (see p. 237). Meinong too points out that, in relation to necessity, it is an indispensable condition of contingency that contingency is not merely the negation of necessity but that "despite the lack of necessity, factuality is nonetheless extant" (Meinong, Über Möglichkeit und Wahrscheinlichkeit, 1915, S. 240). In relation to possibility, as Hegel says, contingency is nothing other than "immediate actuality" (unmittelbare Wirklichkeit). The contingent is precisely "a determinate being" (ein Dasein). It is "an existence" (eine Existenz). It is "an immediate" (ein Unmittelbares) (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 146 u. Zusatz). In short, contingency is the actuality of the actual as mere actual. As Schelling says, the contingent is what "one can only say that it Is" (nur sagen, dass es Ist). It is "what happens in the eminent sense" (das Geschehene κατ' ἐξοχήν) (Schelling, Sämmtliche Werke, 1856-1857, II, 1, S. 464, II, 2, S. 153). And presentness, as the temporal character of contingency, is also in truth based on the actuality of the contingent as the mere actual.

### 10. Contingency and the emotion of wonder

[p. 271] In connection with the temporal character of contingency, we can understand the affective equivalent of contingency. 〔Tr.: 感情當價 (*kanjō tōka*), literally "feeling-equivalent", the feeling that answers to a modality.〕 Let us begin by considering the affective equivalents of possibility and necessity. Possibility, on the basis of futurity, its temporal character, has the feeling of *anxiety* as its equivalent. That is also why Heidegger's philosophy of possibility takes the fundamental-ontological state-of-mind to be anxiety. Necessity, on the basis of pastness, its temporal character, has the feeling of *calm*. When the possibility of the future passes over into the necessity of the past, the tense feeling called anxiety changes into the relaxed feeling called calm. Impossibility, as negative necessity, is nothing other than the negative half of necessity. The feeling of anxiety that possibility has takes the form of the pleasure of *hope* or the displeasure of *worry*, according to the nature of the event that is possible in the future. When possibility has passed over affirmatively into necessity, hope relaxes into the feeling of *satisfaction*, and worry relaxes into the feeling of *gloom*. If, again, possibility has turned negatively into negative necessity, [p. 272] [コマ 143] that is, into impossibility, hope relaxes into the opposite feeling of *disappointment*, and worry into the opposite feeling of *relief*. Whereas hope and worry, the affective equivalents of possibility, are both feelings that have the tension called anxiety, relief, satisfaction, disappointment and gloom, the affective equivalents of necessity (positive and negative), are all feelings of calm in some state of relaxation. And the tension of feeling rests on the futurity of possibility, and its relaxation on the pastness of necessity.

What kind of feeling is the feeling equivalent to contingency? As the existence of words such as *kigū* and *kien* shows 〔Tr.: 奇遇, a strange meeting; 奇緣, a strange tie〕, the affective equivalent of contingency is the emotion of *wonder*. That necessity has the subdued feeling called calm is because the problem has "already" been solved with analytic clarity. Against this, that contingency arouses the excited feeling called wonder is because the problem is thrown out "before our eyes" still unsolved. The emotion of wonder rests on presentness, the temporal character of contingency. In short, the necessary, because of its past, decided apodicticity, has nothing but static, weak feelings of relaxation and subsidence, whereas the possible and the contingent, because of their problematicity, bring dynamic, strong feelings of tension and excitement. And the main difference between the tense feeling of anxiety that the possible has [p. 273] and the excited feeling of wonder that the contingent has is that the former concerns the future and the latter concerns the present. Possibility is the modality in which nothingness awaits being in the future. Contingency is the modality in which being, embracing the present, beholds nothingness.

Let us represent the modalities and their affective equivalents by the figure above 〔Figure 18〕. The impossible is regarded as negative necessity and represented by dotted lines. The dotted part may be seen as folded back upwards so that the vertex "impossible" coincides with the vertex "necessary".

![Figure 18](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-18-p273.png)

**Figure 18.** The modalities and their affective equivalents (1948, p. 273). Labels: 必然 necessary; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent; 不可能 impossible; 平穩 calm; 不安 anxiety; 驚異 wonder; 緊張 tension; 興奮 excitement; 弛緩 relaxation; 沈靜 subsidence; 靜的 static; 動的 dynamic. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

```
                  relaxation   subsidence
                         ^       ^
                          \ (calm) /
                           \  |  /
                            necessary
                          /   |   \
                        /   static  \
                      /       |       \
   tension  (anxiety) possible ------ contingent (wonder)  excitement
                      .    dynamic    .
                        .     .     .
                          .   .   .
                           impossible
```

〔Tr.: The figure stands at the top of p. 273 (left page of コマ 143), before the paragraph that follows. Kuki's figure is a rhombus: 必然 (necessary) at the top, under a circled 平穩 (calm); 可能 (possible) at the left, beside a circled 不安 (anxiety) and the label 緊張 (tension); 偶然 (contingent) at the right, beside a circled 驚異 (wonder) and the label 興奮 (excitement); 不可能 (impossible) at the bottom. The upper sides are solid, the lower sides dotted. A vertical line from 必然 to the horizontal diagonal is labelled 靜的 (static); the horizontal diagonal from 可能 to 偶然 is labelled 動的 (dynamic). Two long arrows rise from 緊張 and from 興奮 to the labels 弛緩 (relaxation) and 沈靜 (subsidence) above 平穩. The arrows in the ASCII version are approximate.〕

The emotion of wonder is the θαυμάζειν of which Aristotle speaks, and the admiration of which Descartes speaks. Descartes defines wonder as follows: "Wonder is a sudden astonishment of the mind, which makes it attentively consider things that seem to it rare and extraordinary [p. 274] [コマ 144]" (Descartes, Les passions de l'âme, Art. 70, Oeuvres, éd. Adam & Tannery, XI, p. 380). And "wonder is the first of all the passions" (Ib., Art. 53, Adam & Tannery, XI, p. 373). Wonder, the affective equivalent of contingency, is the metaphysical emotion that we harbour, at the present instant at which one of the possible disjuncts has been posited, towards the absolute ground of the positing. That Plato in the *Symposium* says that if A "meets by chance" (*kaikō*, 邂逅; ἐντύχῃ) B, "they are struck with wonder" (θαυμαστὰ ἐκπλήττονται) (Platon, Symposium, 192) is a notable example of θαυμάζειν as philosophical wonder. Philosophy is truly born from wonder at chance. And ontological feeling can be seen as unfolding from the wonder of contingency, through the anxiety of possibility, to the calm of necessity.

Further, this wonder ought to accompany the positing of any disjunct whatever, but it appears especially markedly when the positing feels as if it were a teleological positing, that is, when a "purpose without purpose" is present. In a statement made when a certain politician who had once organised a new party returned again to his original party, he said: "After we had resolved on the merger, a *tsujiura* came out. 〔Tr.: 辻占, a fortune slip, often a verse, of the kind sold wrapped in sweets.〕 It said, 'Others are others; come home soon, says the old man.' It is a truly *strange* thing." [p. 275] And a certain senior colleague who had called him a runaway son said, "I was *strangely* moved by a *strange* *tsujiura* like that" (Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun, 7 July, Shōwa 4). Again, under the title "A Strange Tie in Court", an article appeared in the newspaper reporting that "the hearings of two lawsuits of closely similar content, which had arisen for widows over their late husbands' children after the husbands' deaths, were, *strangely*, held on the same day in the same court (the Ōsaka Court of Appeal, Court No. 2)" (Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun, November, Shōwa 4). 〔Tr.: The court name is set off by dashes in the original.〕 This "strange" or "odd" 〔Tr.: 奇 (*ki*) and 妙 (*myō*)〕 is nothing other than wonder at the "purpose without purpose" of chance.

What should further be noted is that wonder at chance from the human standpoint can become the comic of chance from the divine standpoint. And since the difference of standpoint is decided mainly by the relative relation of the magnitudes of subject and object, it is usual that, towards a chance of small existential significance, the subject, being relatively large, feels the comic of divine intelligence (see pp. 58, 167), and that, towards a chance of large existential significance, the subject, becoming relatively small, feels wonder as a human feeling. Something like the comic of a pun is nothing other than the laughter of intelligence at a positive contingency of ground of extremely small existential significance. The laughter that laughs at a chance of large existential significance is the clear laughter laughed by the overman Za- [p. 276] [コマ 145] rathustra. It must laugh at the pettiness of a humanity that wonders. In short, the comic of chance is a feeling that accompanies the divine self-reflection that takes the feeling of wonder as its object.

### 11. Chance and art

That literature values chance rests mainly on the emotion of wonder. Aristotle too said in the *Poetics*: "What is to be wondered at is delightful" (Aristoteles, de poetica 24, 1460a; Rhetorica, 3, 2, 1404b). Literature, that is, poetry in the broad sense, has contingency as an important moment on the two sides of content and form. As to content, in the *Asagao nikki*, for example, contingency forms the backbone of the whole. Moreover it is not merely one chance. The whole is nothing other than a set of variations with chance as its main theme. Again, the Nō play *Semimaru* is brought to life as a Nō play precisely because the mere fact that Semimaru and his elder sister meet by chance at the Ōsaka barrier has sufficient dramatic effect. And that Miyuki, who encounters (*kaikō*, 邂逅) by chance [p. 277] Komazawa, a traveller, at the inn at Shimada, has wept her eyes out and become a blind woman, and that Semimaru is a blind man, symbolise, just as the blindness of the tortoise of immeasurable life that meets the hole in the floating log does, nothing other than the character of coincidence and the blindness of chance that are expressed in such words as "chance coincidence" and "blind chance". The "purpose without purpose" of chance, because it is blind, gives birth to tragedy and comedy. That is why Novalis too says: "Everything poetic must be fairy-tale-like. The poet worships chance" (Novalis Schriften, hrsg. v. Minor, III, S. 4). In our country too, recently, Mr Nakagawa Yoichi has put forward the claim that "against the necessitarianism of Marxism, unless we place the Ground of our thinking in the theory of chance, our literature will wither and die". "Truth without the marvellous is no more than an empty dream. That is, the realism of today must be something that pursues the marvellous of truth, the truth of the marvellous, that stands on the theory of chance. ... I once said, 'The realism of the present is to pursue to the end the *marvellous* that *truth* has.' But these words change at the same time into the claim of romanticism. For this proposition becomes romanticism if one stresses the marvellous, and becomes realism if one stresses truth. 〔Tr.: 不思議 (*fushigi*), the mysterious or marvellous; a different word from Kuki's 驚異, wonder.〕 [p. 278] [コマ 146] But to speak more precisely, today these two isms have come to have to be strongly bound together in the theory of chance. ... Thus has not literature now reached a period of transformation? It seems meaningless, whatever its concern, political, rhetorical or human, so long as, sunk in stillness, it does not have the will to unravel the yarn-ball of chance. ... We must judge our literature once again by the quantity of chance events, choose its material by the truth of chance, and make the power of imagination that lies in human life beat its wings once again. I firmly believe that what will revive the literature of today depends solely on the transformation of this basic thinking" (Tōkyō Asahi Shinbun, 9-11 February, Shōwa 10, Nakagawa Yoichi, "The Yarn-Ball of Chance"; see Shinchō, July issue, Shōwa 10, Nakagawa Yoichi, "On the Literature of Chance").

Further, as to form, that contingency as phonetic coincidence is brought to life artistically in literature I have already set out at length, as an example of positive contingency of ground (see Chapter 2, section 3). It is also an interesting fact that the centre of gravity of the legends about the origin of rhyme lies in chance coincidence. According to a Persian legend, a king called Behram dearly loved a serving-woman called Dilara- [p. 279] m. When the king spoke with her he always ended his words by saying "Dilaram". She in response always ended her words by saying "Great lord Behram". Thus, it is said, the art of rhyme arose. It is that the chance coincidence that the king's name and the serving-woman's name had the same end-rhyme became the basis, and the art form of rhyme arose. Paul Valéry speaks of the "twin smiles" (sourires jumeaux) that exist between one word and another word (Valéry, Aurore), and he is comparing phonetic coincidence between word and word to the contingent relation between twins. Further, Valéry, defining poetry from the formal point of view, calls it "a pure system of the chances (fortunes) of language" (Valéry, Variété, p. 159), and speaks of the "philosophical beauty" (Ib., p. 67) that rhyme has. Again, Oskar Becker says that "transience" (Fragilität) and "fragility" (Zerbrechlichkeit) are basic characteristics of the aesthetic (Oskar Becker, Von der Hinfälligkeit des Schönen und der Abenteuerlichkeit des Künstlers. Jahrbuch f. Philos. u. phän. F. Husserl-Festschrift, S. 27), but there is nothing so edge-like, so transient and so fragile as chance. There lies, again, the beauty of chance. To take contingency into the form of poetry as a glance exchanged between sound and sound, as a passing-by between word and word, means to symbolise the pulse of life in poetry. [p. 280] [コマ 147] And that the meaning of contingency latent in the belief in *kotodama* is actualised as a transient, fragile art form must speak of the richness of the power of poetry. In short, the notable meaning that contingency has in the content and form of literature lies mainly in metaphysical wonder and in the "philosophical beauty" that accompanies it.

Further, let me say a word about the inner relation that art in general has to contingency. It can be considered from two points. First, the structural character of art itself is contingent. Second, art likes to take chance as its object-content. As to the first point, it cannot be denied that science and morality have contingency as a driving force both at their point of departure and in the process of their unfolding, but in spite of that they are things that pursue, in possibility, the necessity of the whole as an infinite task. Science and morality are things that, on the possible path of necessary connection, never know their thirst to be quenched at last. "The noble man thereby makes himself strong without ceasing" (*Book of Changes*, Xiang commentary), or "Only by being active and not resting even for a while is one a man" (Goethe, *Faust*, Part I, 1759): this is the domain of science and morality. Against this, art fulfils and completes itself partially and in isolation. [p. 281] A work of art, as a completed state, is cut off from connection with others in a so-called "microcosmic structure". And it is intuited independently as a whole. There lies what is contingent in the structural character of art itself. Art, so to speak, has contingency as its final form. Whereas science and morality are "futural" cultural forms, as possibility that pursues necessity without end, art is a "present" cultural form that contemplates itself in the contingency before it. To call out to the "instant" (*setsuna*, 刹那) of the present, "Stay, for you are so beautiful" (Goethe, *Faust*, Part I, 1700; Part II, 11582), or to sigh "the maidens' figures, let me keep them a while" (Archbishop Henjō), is the realm of art. Again, freedom in art is freedom *from* all necessity. In art, absolute spontaneity appears suddenly and vanishes abruptly, and there lies the contingency of so-called inspiration and adventure. The genius is one who, reflecting the contingency of primal contingency, opens wide eyes of wonder at their own making. "This impulse, which develops in the direction of the density, intensity and depth of feeling, radiates each artistic individual, through the influence it exerts on the instinct of individuation, in response to the developmental process of feeling. Consequently, for one who stands on this impulse, [p. 282] [コマ 148] artistic creation is rather *chance*" (Abe Jirō, *World Culture and Japanese Culture*, pp. 88-89). That ink painting and colourless film force on the viewer the contingency of total colour-blindness, and that radio drama forces on the listener the contingency of the blind, are all, if one traces them to their root, based on the isolated, contingent structural character of art itself.

The second point, that art likes to take chance as its object-content, is, I think, based on the fact that chance is accompanied by a feeling of life. Contingency in the world of living things is typical. Even meteorological contingency is compared to living things (Fujiwara Sakuhei, *Weather and Human Life*, pp. 32-33). The contingency of natural phenomena is what is hard to foresee, what cannot be caught by law. In it individuality and freedom appear. The licence of life and the play of caprice appear. That life, that play, is beautiful. Wonder at its lively deviation moves us. Lipps too says: "The contingent, as the free, richly varied play of natural forces not fitted into law, is beautiful" (Theodor Lipps, Ästhetik, I, 2 Aufl. S. 205). It is also good to consider that contingency occupies an important place in the appreciation of Eastern ceramics. In the making of ceramics, the fire in the kiln takes part in the making while keeping a certain degree of independence from the maker's [p. 283] intention. From this, distortions arise in the shape, and flavour seeps out in the colour. So-called *yōhen* 〔Tr.: 窯變, kiln transformation〕 is nothing other than contingency as artistic beauty and natural beauty. Among contemporary French painters there are some who leave the choice of the objects to be painted on the canvas of a still life to chance. On the way, hit or miss, they add to the canvas, each time, whatever catches their eye. To take the chance of life into the efficient cause of artistic making, one must have considerable conviction about the meaning that chance has in life and in art and about their connection. The meaning of contingency in the making of *renku* has been pointed out by Mr Watsuji Tetsurō. "In *renku* each verse has an independent world. And yet between them there is a subtle connection, and while one world unfolds into another world, it also has a unity as a whole. Since this unfolding between verse and verse is usually carried out by different authors, the unity that one author's power of imagination has is deliberately thrown away, and the direction of the unfolding is left rather to 'chance'. Consequently the unity as a whole is the product of 'chance', but moreover, for that very reason, the whole becomes on the contrary richer, and gives rise to turns that could not be expected of a single author [p. 284] [コマ 149]" (Watsuji Tetsurō, *Fūdo*, p. 325). Besides this, the fact that stringed instruments have a flavour that keyboard instruments do not have is probably also based mainly on contingent variability. On the relation between chance and the life of art, let me quote the words of an artist himself. "People feel great wonder at chance things. ... Especially for us painters, who are always in direct contact with nature, this wonder of chance is precisely good fortune. Whatever misfortune it may be, one comes to feel like accepting it, on one side, with enjoyment. That painters are mostly people who leave things as they are, or are capricious, is because they dislike expecting things. ... Only, depending on one's way of thinking, all everyday things may perhaps be chance. Life changes even according to whether or not one is receptive to chance" (Kawaguchi Kigai, "Chance", Ōsaka Asahi Shinbun, 30 April, Shōwa 8). I said that the inner relation art has to contingency is, first, that the structural character of art itself is contingent, and second, that art loves contingent things as its object-content; but, as the passage just quoted shows, the two points probably put down deep roots in common in the human existentiality of the artist.

*Section 12 begins at the top of p. 285 (left page of コマ 149). Section 13 begins at the top of p. 300 (right page of コマ 157). Section 14 begins partway down p. 313, where this part ends.*

### 12. Chance and fate

[p. 285] [コマ 149] Metaphysical wonder at the contingent sometimes takes the form of wonder at *fate*. When the contingent has a core, whole-personal meaning for the existentiality of the human being, the contingent is called fate. And contingency as fate, through heterogeneous combination with *necessity*, shows the structure of "the necessary-contingent" 〔Tr.: Kuki joins 必然 and 偶然者 with a long printed rule〕, and confronts the whole existence of the human being sternly, with transcendent power.

The structure of "the necessary-contingent" is also seen in Heraclitus's idea of fate (εἱμαρμένη, fatum). He says "in any case it is *fated* (ἔστι εἱμαρμένα)" (Diels, Fragmente der Vorsokratiker, I. Herakleitos, Fr. 137) and stresses necessity, and at the same time he also says "the most beautiful world is a heap of rubbish piled up *at random* (εἰκῇ)" (Ib., Fr. 124) and stresses the contingency of the world. Kant, in the "Lectures on Metaphysics", holds that among the connections of things what is absurd is blind necessity (die blinde Notwendigkeit), that is, fate [p. 286] [コマ 150] (Schicksal), and blind chance (das blinde Ungefähr), that is, chance (Zufall), and says that the former is "what is founded neither on the essence of the matter nor on other causes", and the latter is "what is contingent in every respect" (Kant's Vorlesungen über die Metaphysik, 2 Aufl. hrsg. v. Schmidt, S. 52 f.). In the "Critique of Pure Reason" too he says that the proposition "nothing happens through blind chance", that is, "in the world there is no chance" (in mundo non datur casus), and the proposition "no necessity in nature is blind", that is, "there is no fate" (non datur fatum), are both a priori laws of nature. And he holds that both belong to the dynamical principles, only that the former, as a consequence of the principle of causality, is to be understood according to the categories of relation, and the latter, which adds the concept of necessity to the determination of causality, is to be interpreted according to the categories of modality. Further, when Kant here says "there is no chance" or "there is no fate", he is only saying that there is none "in the world". And that "world" is nothing other than the so-called "world of appearances" or "nature", which is the object of mathematical natural science. He says: "These principles agree in that they permit nothing that would bring interruption and hindrance to the understanding and to the continuous connection of all appearances, that is, to the unity of the concepts of the understanding, [p. 287] in *empirical synthesis*" (see Kant, Critique of Pure Reason B. 280-282). Kant seems to understand fate as necessity and to set it against contingency, but that is only on the surface of the words; in fact he sees a deep inner connection between the two. Contingency on the one side and necessity as fate on the other agree, both of them, in blindness. Blind means "that by which one can see nothing" (das, wodurch man nichts sehen kann) (Kant's Vorlesungen über die Metaphysik. 2 Aufl. hrsg. v. Schmidt, S. 52). Blind contingency, which is contingent in every respect, and blind necessity, which is founded neither on the essence of the matter nor on other causes, are in the end one and the same. In short, for Kant too the concept of fate is seen in the structure of "the necessary-contingent".

Schopenhauer, in his essay concerning "the fate of the individual", describes the "necessary-contingent" connection of contingency as fate. "However purely contingent the course of things may appear, it is nevertheless at bottom not contingent. Rather, all these chances themselves, τὰ εἰκῇ φερόμενα (what is brought about at random), are embraced by one deeply hidden necessity, εἱμαρ- [p. 288] [コマ 151] μένη (fate). Chance itself is a mere instrument of that necessity" (Schopenhauer, Sämtliche Werke hrsg. v. Deussen, IV. S. 228). "At the root of this mundus phaenomenon (world of appearances) ruled by chance there will lie, wholly and everywhere, one mundus intelligibilis (intelligible world) that governs chance itself" (Ib., 232). "The deep-lying root-unity of necessity and contingency" (Ib., 235), "the final unity of necessity and contingency" (Ib.), is the concept of fate. The same sense is expressed in the words "If *by chance* you obtain practice and faith, rejoice far back in the *conditions of past lives*" (Kyōgyōshinshō, Preface). The fatalism and the acausalism of the six teachers, too, have a point of agreement that is hard to tell apart. To say "all is made by former destiny" and to say "all is without cause and without condition" is in the end the same thing (Madhyama Āgama, fascicle 3, Du jing). Based on the existence of such an inner relation between chance and fate, Wilhelm von Scholz too gives his book "Chance" the other name "A Preliminary Form of Fate" (Eine Vorform des Schicksals). Kunikida Doppo too, in "The Fatalist", in response to A's saying "I am not a fatalist", has B press him with "Then are you a believer in chance?" This pressing is no more than mere irony, and it presupposes that fate and chance are ultimately one and the same. In Mushanokōji [p. 289] Saneatsu's "The Man Who Plays Go with Fate" there are the words "Fate is something of which one cannot tell where it is lying in wait", and it is a place where one might well say "chance" instead of "fate". That successive contingency, as recurrent contingency, shows the combination of the contingent and the necessary and in some cases presses close to the concept of fate, I have already mentioned in a word (see p. 264).

One of those who grasped the connection between chance and fate most deeply is Schelling. According to Schelling, primal contingency is "unprethinkable destiny" (unvordenkliches Verhängnis). It is unprethinkable because consciousness cannot think or recollect what was before it. It is destiny because the will, seeing a result it did not intend, is struck with wonder, and believes that, while it is in actuality, it is the same as when it was still in possibility. What is primal contingency for consciousness in pure substantiality is fate for actual consciousness. Actual consciousness is not itself conscious of the act by which it came to be. For by that act it has become something wholly other and has been cut off from its former state. To recollect the former state, there must be identity between the one who recollects and the object of recollection. That a sleepwalker cannot, after waking, recollect the acts done in sleep is because [p. 290] [コマ 152] the person in sleep and the person when awake are different persons. Through primal contingency consciousness is thereafter subject to "inescapable fate" (unabwendliches Schicksal), but for consciousness that has now become actual and has separated from itself, that primal contingency sinks necessarily into a bottomless depth. Primal contingency is the "first-born fortune" (Fortuna primigenia) from which all else that is contingent springs (Schelling, Sämmtliche Werke II, 2, 1857, S. 153-154).

Kant says: "There are usurped concepts (usurpirte Begriffe) such as fortune (Glück) and fate (Schicksal). They circulate with almost general indulgence, but they are sometimes required to answer the question 'what is the right?'. Then people fall into no small perplexity over their deduction" (Critique of Pure Reason B. 117). However, if a "subjective deduction" can be made, there is no problem of an "objective deduction". If the subjective reality of these concepts can be shown, there is no need "to prove their objective reality" (Ib.). Even if they are "usurped concepts", if they are borrowed from the humanity of the human being, that is enough.

According to Spengler's view, fate belongs to the ultimate lived experience of the individual soul and of the whole culture, [p. 291] and is something that can be communicated only through religion and art, not through concepts and proofs. What grasps what it is, is lived experience, not scientific experience. It is intuition, not calculation. What comprehends what it is, is depth (Tiefe), not intellect (Geist). It is organic logic, not the logic of the inorganic. Fate is the word for a certain inner certainty that cannot be described (Schicksal ist das Wort für eine nicht zu beschreibende innere Gewissheit) (Spengler, Der Untergang des Abendlandes, I. 1924, S. 152, 153, 180). To describe clearly what fate is may be impossible, as Spengler says, but I think it is possible to make clear to a certain degree how the concept of fate arose, what relations it keeps with other similar concepts, and what moments it contains within itself. The concept of fate probably arose in cases where teleological contingency overawes the whole existence of the human being. Teleological contingency readily enters into heterogeneous combination with causal necessity. Further, through homogeneous combination, causal necessity combines with teleological necessity. And this complex of "necessity-contingency", having acquired transcendence of the actual through the enormity of the matter itself, is nothing [p. 292] [コマ 153] other than fate. In that case the homogeneous combination of causal necessity and teleological necessity is made in the following sense. Purposiveness, as what determines the present through the future, presupposes consciousness, as I said before (see p. 68). But between consciousness and the unconscious there opens a graded passage. Consciousness turns into the unconscious through this passage. In this way teleological necessity combines with causal necessity, and causal necessity combines with teleological necessity. Diogenes Laertius says, as the doctrine of the Stoics: "All things come about by fate (εἱμαρμένη). And fate is the *cause* (αἰτία) of existents, or the *reason* (λόγος) by which the world proceeds" (Diogenes Laertios, VII, 149). In this case there is no harm in seeing two moments contained in fate: teleological necessity as "reason" and causal necessity as "cause". At any rate, causal necessity and teleological necessity enter into homogeneous combination. Further, as to what it means that in the concept of fate teleological contingency and teleological necessity are combined: the former concerns purposiveness on the horizon of human *Existenz*, and the latter concerns transcendent purposiveness. In short, fate is teleological contingency that combines with causal necessity, further combines through that mediation with teleological necessity, and, as "the necessary- [p. 293] contingent", is pushed away into the infinitely distant and drawn into the infinitely near. The concept of fate may perhaps be a "usurped concept" for the logic of the understanding, but it is a concept rooted deeply in human experience and provided originally, so to speak pre-conceptually, by the humanity of the human being. Not to speak of Sophocles' "Oedipus the King" and Shakespeare's "King Lear", anyone who opens Mokuami's "Chijimiya Shinsuke" too will refuse to give up the acquired right of the concept of fate.

Kant himself, at the opening of the preface to the first edition of the "Critique of Pure Reason", shows a typical use of the concept of fate. "Human reason has a peculiar *fate* in one kind of its cognitions: that is, it is troubled by questions that it cannot dismiss, but that it also cannot answer. It cannot dismiss them because the questions are imposed on reason by the nature of reason itself, and it cannot answer them because they transcend every capacity of human reason." What is it if not fate, the "necessary-contingent" into which the lover of wisdom 〔Tr.: 愛智者, glossed in ruby as フィロソフォス, *philosophos*〕 was born as the child of Poros and Penia, bearing a future of eternal suffering? Kant, in the [p. 294] [コマ 154] first chapter of the "Principles of Moral Philosophy" 〔Tr.: 道德哲學原論, Kuki's title for the *Grundlegung zur Metaphysik der Sitten*〕, too says as follows. "Even if, through a special disfavour of *fate* or through the scant provision of a stepmotherly nature, this will should wholly lack the capacity to carry through its own purpose, and even if, striving with all its might, nothing at all were accomplished by this will and only the good will remained, it would still, like a jewel, as something that holds its whole value within itself, truly shine alone with a brilliant light of itself." Let me give, besides, one or two examples of the application of the concept of fate. "At each instant we are *face to face* with the absolutely irrational. We are face to face with absolute fact; there we are not act but sense; there we *touch* the fact of primordial history; it is what should be called the content of our *fate*" (Nishida Kitarō, The Self-Conscious Determination of Nothingness, pp. 187-188). "There must be somewhere a place where the power of man and the work of man, everything human, at some point comes to an end, and we *come up against* a divine reality that confronts us with absolute authority. In revelation, every resistance becomes vain for a person, every like and dislike is set at nought, and with his whole personality he is, willy-nilly, carried off into *unexpected* light, truth, blessedness and life. ... For the great [p. 295] religious figures, revelation was not necessarily a path they liked or a work they relished. ... For them revelation was *fate*, holding infinite glory and joy, at any rate *fate*" (Hatano Seiichi, Philosophy of Religion, pp. 32-33).

I said that fate is teleological contingency that, combining on the one hand with causal necessity and on the other with teleological necessity, has been expanded to the infinitely large and at the same time contracted to the infinitely small. And in that case, if one forces an analysis, the combination of teleological contingency with causal necessity appears as "blind fate", and the combination of teleological contingency with teleological necessity appears as "providence". According to Hegel, the πεπρωμένον and εἱμαρμένη of the ancients is blind fate, and Christianity, through the introduction of purposiveness, teaches the providence of God. The former is "necessity that has not cast off its veil" (unenthüllte Notwendigkeit), the latter "necessity that has cast off its veil" (enthüllte Notwendigkeit). The former is a blind and comfortless standpoint, the latter a foreseeing and consoling standpoint (Hegel, Encyklopädie, hrsg. v. Bolland, § 147, Zusatz, S. 197-200). However, I think the distinctive feature of the concept of fate lies rather in not permitting a sharp distinction between two kinds of fate in this way. As I have already said, between consciousness and the unconscious there is a graded, gradual [p. 296] [コマ 155] passage. Spengler too explains the inner connection of fate and chance, and expresses the relation of antiquity and the West to the two in the following words. "If we may understand chance, for the Western soul, as fate of small content, then conversely, for the soul of antiquity, fate may be seen as chance raised to the enormous" (l. c. S. 189). Further, on the point that the difference between fate and chance is a difference of degree concerning content, Spengler says "That Goethe came to Sesenheim is felt as chance, that he came to Weimar is felt as fate. The former is seen as an episode (Episode), the latter as an epoch (Epoche)" (l. c. S. 179), and he further stresses the opposition of the two. But fate should be seen as what has taken the structure of "the necessary-contingent" as a result of contingency's heterogeneous combination with necessity, and to stress too much the relation of opposition between fate and chance hinders the proper understanding of both fate and chance.

I have pointed out that teleological contingency enters into diagonal combination with teleological necessity (see the figure on p. 77 〔Figure 1〕) because the former is teleological contingency for human existence and the latter is teleological necessity in transcendent existence. Teleological necessity in transcendent existence, as something external to the purposiveness of human existence itself, [p. 297] still does not cease to be teleological contingency. Yet here, under the concept of fate, there are cases where teleological contingency and teleological necessity combine dynamically on the horizon of human existence. Teleological contingency, as a sublated moment, is within teleological necessity and conditions it. Such cases can be seen as fate in the eminent sense. In that case, that the combination and the conditioning are made, so to speak, "under the aspect of eternity" goes without saying, since it is fate. In the combination of teleological contingency and teleological necessity, in the ordinary concept of fate teleological necessity is thought to condition teleological contingency, but in the concept of fate in the eminent sense, on the contrary, teleological contingency conditions teleological necessity. Or, in a certain sense, they condition each other reciprocally. Therein lies the difference between the ordinary concept of fate and the concept of fate in the eminent sense. Fate in the eminent sense appears, for example, in Heidegger's concept of fate too (see Iwanami Philosophy Series, Kuki, "The Philosophy of Existence", pp. 81-82). Fate becomes fate only when it is immanent in anticipatory resoluteness. Fate is the authentic happening of Dasein. Accordingly it must be thrownness (Geworfenheit) and at the same time projection (Entwurf). It must be handing oneself down to oneself in a possibility that is inherited and yet [p. 298] [コマ 156] chosen. Fate lies in the combination of the superior power (Übermacht) and the powerlessness (Ohnmacht) of self-handing-down resoluteness. The powerless superior power that passionately hands itself down in the face of the contingency of the disclosed situation is the place of fate. Fate is an existent that is futural and at the same time originally having-been taking over its own thrownness while giving possibility to itself in finitude. Jaspers's concept of fate is likewise. In Jaspers's way of putting it, fate is the case where "chances are inwardly appropriated" (Die Zufälle sind innerlich angeeignet) (Jaspers, Philosophie, II. S. 218). "I and the situation have become one. Because historical consciousness is deeply aware of the self and the particularity of its own Dasein as identical, fortune and misfortune are no longer grasped merely as other, that is, merely as what comes from outside, but are grasped, in the deeper thought called fate, as what belongs to me. I sink myself into my historical determinacy. Within this historical determinacy I affirm my Dasein as it is. It is not that I affirm Dasein merely as empirical objectivity, but I affirm [p. 299] Dasein as existentially saturated objectivity. ... In this immersion I grasp fate not merely as something external but as my own in 'love of fate' (amor fati). Since I become existentially aware only within fate, I love my fate as I love myself. In good contrast to the fading of the merely universal and the whole, I experience existence existentially within what is objectively limitation. Historical consciousness as consciousness of fate is to think concrete Dasein seriously" (Ib., II. S. 218-219). "Historical determinacy in the limit situation, instead of being merely chance, becomes the appearance of a certain existence. That certain existence is an existence that my understanding does not grasp, but that is confirmed to me as the eternal in time. The lover says to the beloved, 'You were my sister or my wife in a former life'" (Ib., II. S. 217).

In short, the concept of fate in the eminent sense must be such that, with passionate self-awareness, the self sinks itself into contingency and thereby brings itself to life originally. And the contingent as fate is not rarely grasped as the eternal present of recurrent metaphysical time.

〔Tr.: 實存 is rendered *Existenz* and 實存性 "existentiality", apart from 存在, "existence". 現存在 is rendered "Dasein" in both the Heidegger and the Jaspers passages. 勝義の is rendered "in the eminent sense", as in "philosophy in the eminent sense". 偶然論者 is rendered "a believer in chance". Kuki's ellipses (......) are rendered "...". Kuki's printed rules in Kant's sentence (before といふのは) and in Hatano's (between 運命 and 兎に角運命) are rendered with a colon and a comma.〕

### 13. The metaphysical absolute

[p. 300] [コマ 157] Fate is one of the disjuncts, thrown out like the pips of a die, being grasped in lived experience into the core of *Existenz* while it shakes the whole breadth of *Existenz*. A disjunct presupposes the whole of the disjunctive possibilities. Yet the whole of possibilities leads, ultimately, to the concept of the metaphysical absolute. The absolute, because it is the absolute, is thought as absolutely one. And because it is absolutely one, it is thought as absolutely necessary. This absolute necessity can be called metaphysical necessity. This concept was already set out by Aristotle in the "Metaphysics" (Aristoteles, Metaphysica, Λ. 7. 1072b). The "unmoved mover", because it is unmoved, "never has the contingency of being otherwise" (οὐκ ἐνδέχεται ἄλλως ἔχειν οὐδαμῶς). Accordingly it is "what exists of necessity" (ἐξ ἀνάγκης ὄν) (see p. 193). Everything other than this "unmoved mover" is moved by another, and so "has the contingency of being otherwise" (ἐνδέχεται ἄλλως ἔχειν). That is, it is metaphysical contingency as the [p. 301] negation of metaphysical necessity. The concepts of this metaphysical necessity and metaphysical contingency are nothing other than the application of disjunctive necessity and disjunctive contingency to the metaphysical horizon; in the Middle Ages they were adopted by Moses Maimonides, a venerator of Aristotle, and were put to use as a moment in the "cosmological proof" of the existence of God. Thomas Aquinas too, following Maimonides, made the inference from the contingency of the world to "something that is necessary through itself" (aliquid quod est per se necessarium) (Thomas Aquinas, Summa theologiae I. 2. 3). The relation between metaphysical necessity and metaphysical contingency also took the form of the opposition between "aseity" (aseitas) and "abaleity" (abalietas). Spinoza's so-called "cause of itself (causa sui)" (Spinoza, Ethica, I, def. 1) is also nothing other than metaphysical absolute necessity. "God exists necessarily (Deus necessario existit)" (Ib., I, 11). In contrast to this, "so long as I find nothing that necessarily posits its existence or necessarily excludes it, I call individual things contingent (contingentes)" (Ib., IV, def. 3). Leibniz too, in the "Theodicy" and the "Monadology", spoke of God as "the necessary existent (Être nécessaire)" and of the events of the world as "contingent existents (êtres contingents)" (Leibniz, Opera philosophica, ed. Erdmann, p. 506, 708).

[p. 302] [コマ 158] We notice that the meanings of the necessary and the contingent are directly opposed according to whether they are on the empirical horizon or on the metaphysical horizon. The empirical horizon, by the way of going "from below", goes back infinitely along the series of causal necessity and arrives at primal contingency (see p. 183). The metaphysical horizon, by the way of going "from above", obtains, as the negation of absolute necessity, the concept of the contingent held captive within causality. In other words, being determined by causality is called necessary from the empirical standpoint, and is called contingent from the metaphysical standpoint. Again, when the beginning of the causal series is grasped as an idea, it is called primal contingency from the empirical standpoint, and the same idea is called absolute necessity, in the form of disjunctive necessity, from the metaphysical standpoint. That is, within necessity there is the distinction between "absolute necessity" and "hypothetical necessity". Leibniz too made the distinction between "absolute necessity" (nécessité absolue) and "hypothetical necessity" (nécessité hypothétique) (Leibniz, Opera philosophica, ed. Erdmann, p. 763); according to Christian Wolff, "Necessary existence is that whose existence is *absolutely* necessary (absolute necessaria). Contingent existence is that which has the ground of its own existence outside itself." Accordingly, "the existence of contingent existence is merely *hypothetically* necessary (hypothetice necessaria)" (Christian Wolff, Ontologia, [p. 303] § 309f., § 316). Kant too, in the "Lectures on Metaphysics", makes this distinction. That is, absolute necessity (absolute Notwendigkeit) is the case where the existence of a thing is known a priori *simply* (simpliciter), and hypothetical necessity (hypothetische Notwendigkeit) is the case where the existence of a thing is known a priori *according to something* (secundum quid) (Kant's Vorlesungen über die Metaphysik, 2 Aufl. hrsg. v. Schmidt, S. 27). And this metaphysical contingency, which is merely hypothetically necessary, can also not exist, for the reason that, as Aristotle already showed, it exists only through some cause other than itself. That is why Thomas Aquinas calls the contingent "what is possible not to exist (possibilia non esse)" (Thomas Aquinas, Summa theologiae I. 2. 3). Metaphysical contingency is, after all, nothing other than empirical necessity, but even empirical necessity, insofar as it is witnessed over against the metaphysical necessity of the absolute, takes on the character of the contingent.

We have seen that the relation of opposition between empirical necessity and primal contingency holds, as the relation of cause and effect, at the hypothetical standpoint, and that the relation of opposition between metaphysical contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity holds, as the relation of whole and part, at the disjunctive standpoint. Next we must consider in more detail the relation between primal [p. 304] [コマ 159] contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity, and the relation between empirical necessity and metaphysical contingency.

First, what is the relation between primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity? Primal contingency is a concept obtained on the hypothetical horizon as the primal beginning of the causal series. When we went back infinitely along the causal series of empirical necessity, we arrived, as an idea, at the concept of primal contingency. Primal contingency is the "primal event" (Urereignis), the "beginning of history" (Anfang der Geschichte) (Schelling, Sämmtliche Werke II, 2, 1857, S. 153). However, the absolute beginning of the necessary series conditioned by the causal chain can also be thought as the whole that implicitly contains as parts all the empirical necessities that are the members of the series. On the other hand, absolute metaphysical necessity is the whole that has as its parts all metaphysical contingencies on the disjunctive horizon. And since absolute necessity as the whole can also be seen as conditioning the positing of the disjuncts as parts, it can also be thought as the absolute beginning of the causal series. Insofar as the serial view and the extensional view can be exchanged for one another in this way, primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity must be one and the same. Further, on the hypothetical horizon [p. 305] the infinite causal series was represented in the form of a straight line, and primal contingency was thought as its ideal beginning, but since true infinity is represented in the form of a circle in which the last of the series returns to the first, absolute metaphysical necessity is thought as the whole circle that comprehends each part of the circle. From this point too it is clear that primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity are one and the same. Whether one says primal contingency or absolute necessity, it is in the end the metaphysical absolute. Something like the Great Ultimate of the Changes too can be thought, as the metaphysical absolute, both as primal contingency and as absolute necessity. In short, absolute metaphysical necessity and primal contingency are no more than two sides of one thing. Spinoza's "cause of itself" and Schelling's "contingent through itself" (das durch sich selbst Zufällige) too in the end unite in one. The absolute must be thought as possessing the contradictory character of "the necessary-contingent". As Jakob Böhme says, it is "Yes" (Ja) and at the same time "No" (Nein). "'Yes' and 'No' are simply one thing, only it separates itself into two beginnings and makes two centres" (Jakob Böhme, Sämtliche Werke 6, S. 597). Primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity, while being one thing in the absolute, still make "two centres". Primal contingency is what was caught as the last idea from the empirical-hypothetical standpoint, which is [p. 306] [コマ 160] the way of going "from below", and absolute necessity is what was set up as the first concept from the metaphysical-disjunctive standpoint, which is the way of going "from above". Therefore absolute metaphysical necessity expresses, so to speak, the affirmative character of the absolute, and primal contingency expresses, so to speak, its negative character. Absolute metaphysical necessity is the in-itself of the absolute. Primal contingency is the otherness within the absolute. The possibility of thinking absolute metaphysical necessity as divine reality, and primal contingency as the beginning of the world or as a fall (Zufall=Abfall), also arises here. There is no harm in thinking that absolute necessity is the static side of the absolute and primal contingency its dynamic side. The words of the pre-critical Kant, "The first of things is the absolutely necessary. The first of states is the absolutely contingent" (Das erste der Dinge ist das schlechterdings notwendige. Das erste der Zustände ist das schlechterdings zufällige.), are worth savouring (Kant, Reflexionen zur Metaphysik, Akademie Ausgabe XVIII. S. 142).

If we think in this way, the close relation between primal contingency as the absolute beginning of the causal series and metaphysical contingency posited as [p. 307] a disjunct also becomes clear of itself. The a priori Ground of the possibility of a disjunct's being posited as a contingent can be thought to lie in the contingency of primal contingency. The contingency of primal contingency is nothing other than the absolute Ground that determines metaphysical disjunctive contingency, within variability, as contingency. Likewise, the close relation between metaphysical necessity as the whole of the parts and empirical necessity as a member of the causal series is also clear. As already said, the relation of the two is the relation of absolute necessity and hypothetical necessity.

Further, it is necessary to elucidate, in another relation, the character of the absolute as "the necessary-contingent". Whole and part in general have meaning in correlation. Without parts there is no whole, and without a whole there are no parts. Accordingly the necessity of absolute metaphysical necessity cannot be thought without the contingency of metaphysical contingency. The contingency of metaphysical contingency also cannot be thought without the necessity of absolute metaphysical necessity. The necessity of absolute necessity is the necessity possessed by a whole that has contingency as its parts. Necessity conditions contingency, and contingency conditions necessity. In short, the absolute, insofar as it is not an empty abstract whole [p. 308] [コマ 161] but a fulfilled concrete whole, is neither the merely necessary nor the merely contingent, but "the necessary-contingent", which has meaning in the correlation of the necessary and the contingent. And since the absolute is what can be the absolute only through the relative, finite thing, in other words what acquires the concrete meaning of the absolute whole only by existing as other as contingent parts, there will be no harm in thinking the absolute as "the necessary-contingent" as the stage of in-and-for-itself in the dialectic of the absolute. Further, it goes without saying that, insofar as primal contingency can be thought as the whole that has each member of the causal series as its parts, exactly the same consideration can be made concerning the relation between primal contingency and empirical necessity as concerning the relation between metaphysical necessity and metaphysical contingency. In the above I believe I have been able to make clear that primal contingency and absolute metaphysical necessity are two sides of the absolute as "the necessary-contingent".

Next, what is the relation between empirical necessity and metaphysical contingency? Empirical necessity on the hypothetical horizon appears on the disjunctive horizon as metaphysical contingency. Leibniz, in the "Discourse on Metaphysics", in trying to set up the distinction between the necessary (nécessaire) and the certain (certain), [p. 309] says: the former is that whose opposite contains a contradiction, and the latter is that whose opposite does not contain a contradiction. And since only the absolutely necessary (absolument nécessaire) is *necessary* in the strict sense, he holds that the hypothetically necessary (nécessaire ex hypothesi) is no more than *certain*. For example, that Caesar did not stop at the river Rubicon but crossed this river, and that he did not lose but won at the battle of Pharsalus, are not necessary but certain. And he explains that, because it is certain but not necessary, the matter is contingent. Further, he Grounds the reason why it is certain and yet not necessary, that is, the reason why it is contingent, in the will of God, that is, in the freedom of choice (Leibniz, Discours de métaphysique, § 13). In short, Leibniz's concept of the "certain" seeks to express empirical necessity, that is, metaphysical contingency. At any rate, each member of the series, insofar as it is conditioned by the absolute beginning, reflects the character of the absolute beginning, and in each part, insofar as it is a part of the whole, the character of the whole is projected. The character of the absolute as "the necessary-contingent" comes to take, in each member and each part, the form of fate as "the necessary-contingent". Kant's so-called "lawfulness of the contingent" (Gesetzlichkeit des Zufälligen), that is, the concept of purposiveness (Kant, Kritik der Urteils- [p. 310] [コマ 162] kraft, § 76), is also nothing other than the necessary-contingent character possessed by a part determined by the whole.

Further, Kant divides contingency into two kinds, empirical contingency (empirische Zufälligkeit) and intelligible contingency (intelligibele Zufälligkeit); what Kant calls empirical contingency corresponds to what we call empirical necessity, and what Kant calls intelligible contingency corresponds to what we call metaphysical contingency. Concerning "empirical contingency" Kant says as follows. "From the various changes in the world, empirical contingency, that is, that change depends on empirically determining causes, was inferred, and an ascending series of empirical conditions was obtained" (Critique of Pure Reason B. 486). It is also concerning empirical contingency that he says such things as "What is conditioned in existence in general is called contingent, and what is unconditioned is called necessary" (Ib., B. 447), or "We know contingency from the fact that some event can exist only as the effect of some cause. Therefore, if something is assumed to be contingent, that it has some cause is an analytic proposition" (Ib., B. 291). "Intelligible contingency" Kant also calls "contingency according to concepts of the pure understanding" (Zufälligkeit nach Begriffen des [p. 311] reinen Verstandes). Concerning this kind of contingency he says "what is contingent in the pure sense of the category is that whose contradictory opposite is possible". A case such as "at the same time as there was motion, there could have been rest instead of motion" is intelligible contingency. When A exists, the case where it is also possible that non-A exists instead of A is intelligible contingency. That is, he calls metaphysical contingency on the disjunctive horizon intelligible contingency. The difference between empirical contingency and intelligible contingency may be said to be the difference between the case where the opposite is possible *successively* and the case where it is possible *simultaneously*. And Kant says that to move from empirical contingency to intelligible contingency is wholly impossible, as "a transition to another kind" (μετάβασις εἰς ἄλλο γένος) (Ib., B. 486-488). Insofar as one is confined, at the standpoint of criticism, to the knowledge of the world of appearances, it can be said to be natural to distinguish sharply these two kinds of contingency and to make the passage from one to the other impossible. However, to make empirical causal necessity disjunctively contingent and see it as metaphysical contingency, that is, through a conversion of dimension to see "empirical contingency" and "intelligible contingency" as one and the same at the root, must be something that is to be permitted insofar as one stands on the metaphysical horizon. [p. 312] [コマ 163] Kant's so-called empirical contingency is conditioned causally, and so, within that limit, it is something that should naturally be called *empirical necessity*. Schopenhauer too, in the "Critique of the Kantian Philosophy", says that to think that everything contingent has a cause is "an evident contradiction", but, as Schopenhauer also states, that idea did not begin with Kant but already has its source in Aristotle (Schopenhauer, Sämtliche Werke, hrsg. v. Deussen, I, S. 552-553). At any rate, in order to call *empirical necessity empirical contingency*, it should probably be thought that, at least in the motive of that naming, one has unawares strayed onto the metaphysical-disjunctive horizon and made the necessary contingent. Empirical necessity, as hypothetical necessity, is necessary only *through the conditioning of another*, and so it is called contingent; but for it to be called contingent, it is said so by thinking of absolute necessity, which is necessary *through itself*, and [p. 313] over against that. Moreover, what is necessary only through the conditioning of another is contingent because, if only the conditioning of another is removed, its contradictory opposite is possible. In short, to see empirical necessity as empirical contingency, a making-contingent carried out in the metaphysical-disjunctive dimension must be presupposed at the root. In the above I believe I have made clear that empirical necessity and metaphysical contingency are the finite seen in different dimensions.

Let us represent the above considerations in the figure on the right 〔Figure 19〕.

![Figure 19](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-19-p312.png)

Its labels read: top, 絕對者 (the absolute); bottom, 有限者 (the finite); top left corner, 絕對的(形而上的)必然; top right, 原始偶然; bottom left, 形而上的偶然; bottom right, 經驗的(假說的)必然.
