Outside the square, 形而上的離接的見地 (metaphysical-disjunctive standpoint) on the left with an arrow pointing down, and 經驗的假說的見地 (empirical-hypothetical standpoint) on the right with an arrow pointing up. 存在 is rendered "existence" and 存在者 "existent" throughout this section; 他在 is rendered "otherness", 卽自態 "in-itself" and 卽自且對自 "in-and-for-itself". 有限者 is rendered "the finite". 易の大極 is printed with 大 for the usual 太.〕

### 14. Being and nothingness

The contingent is an existence close to nothingness. The correlation of the necessary and the contingent must be something that suggests the correlation of being and nothingness. The disjunctive affirmation that it is A, and is B, and is C, and is D, has on its reverse side the disjunctive negation that it is not A, and not B, and not C, and not D. That the absolute is a "necessary-contingent entity" 〔Tr.: 必然－偶然者〕 tells that the absolute is absolute being and at the same time is absolute nothingness. Contingency, in which, although it is also possible for there not to be A, there is A, [p. 314] [コマ 164] is nothing other than a limit-existence that stands precariously on the boundary line between being and nothingness. Possibility is the non-actual that does not have actuality, but insofar as it is equipped with a tendency towards necessity, it is something deep in the colour of being. Possibility indeed stands, in the dimension of reality, in the relation of subalternation 〔Tr.: 大小對當〕 with necessity. In contrast to this, contingency, while having actuality, insofar as it is proximate to impossibility, is something that has its fill of nothingness. Contingency stands, in the dimension of nihility 〔Tr.: 虛無性〕, in the relation of subalternation with impossibility. If the character of being common to necessity and possibility is represented by a circle, contingency is no more than the point of tangency at which the nothingness of impossibility happens to touch being (see p. 234). If the reality common to necessity and possibility is represented by a triangle, contingency is something that, while standing as the actual at the single point of the apex of the triangle, is at the same time located on the line of nothingness (see p. 236). Necessity is what has completely occupied the domain of being. Possibility stands facing being as "possible existence", that is, "possibility of coming to be", and contingency stands facing nothingness as "possibility of the opposite", that is, "possible non-existence". One who in theory and in practice always holds fast to necessity will seldom be conscious of nothingness. One who devotes his mind only to the pursuit of possibility will, merely as "privation", conceptually know nothingness [p. 315] in many cases. In contrast to this, one who has the sense 〔Tr.: 官能〕 that witnesses contingency intuits nothingness originarily 〔Tr.: 原的に〕. The wonder that accompanies chance is the emotion that, taking nothingness as the background of being, when the reason is asked concerning the transition from nothingness to being and concerning the turn from being to nothingness, moves the question itself. Chance means the possible of nothingness 〔Tr.: 無の可能〕. It is also contingency that dares the paradox of seizing impossibility from the core of nothingness and bringing it into contact with being.

![Figure 20](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-20-p315.png)

**Figure 20.** Being and nothingness: the mountain of possibility and the valley of contingency between necessity and impossibility (1948, p. 315). Letters and signs, no Japanese: N necessity; I impossibility; P possibility; C contingency; R actuality; R′ non-actuality; + domain of being; − domain of nothingness.

The relation just stated can be represented by the figure above 〔Figure 20〕. 〔Tr.: Figure on p. 315: a horizontal straight line runs from N (left) to I (right); R is marked below its left half and R′ above its right half. From N an arc marked + rises above the line and comes down to P at the midpoint, just above the line; from P the curve crosses the line to C, just below it, and from C an arc marked − runs below the line to I. An arrow on the upper arc points towards N; an arrow on the lower arc points towards I.〕 In the figure above, N represents necessity, I impossibility, P possibility, C contingency, R actuality, R′ non-actuality. + represents the domain of being, − the domain of nothingness (see pp. 238-239). R and R′ are located on the straight line, and divide the straight line equally into left and right. + and − have thickness, and divide between them the mountain and the valley shown by the curve. N is R and at the same time +, and I is R′ and at the same time −. P straddles R′ and +, and while being R′ is +. C [p. 316] [コマ 165] straddles R and −, and while being R is −. The direction of the arrows shows the direction of tendency 〔Tr.: 歸趨〕. It can also be illustrated by coordinate axes as on the left 〔Figure 21〕.

![Figure 21](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-21-p316.png)

**Figure 21.** The modalities on coordinate axes of actuality and non-actuality (1948, p. 316). Letters and signs, no Japanese, as in Figure 20; O origin; (I) to (IV) quadrants; a, b points on the curve.

〔Tr.: Figure on p. 316: coordinate axes crossing at O. The horizontal axis runs from −R (left) to +R (right), with C to the left of O and N to the right; the vertical axis runs from +R′ (top) to −R′ (bottom), with P above O and I below. The quadrants are numbered (I) upper right, (II) upper left, (III) lower left, (IV) lower right. A curve rises from the third quadrant, crosses the horizontal axis to the left of C, passes through point a in the second quadrant and point b near the vertical axis, peaks on the vertical axis above P, and falls away in the first quadrant towards the horizontal axis. Lines drop from a and from b to each axis.〕 In this figure the signs N, I, P, C, R, R′, +, − and so on have the same meaning as in the previous figure. Let O be the origin. Take two fixed straight lines ROR and R′OR′ that pass through O and intersect each other at right angles. The right of O is taken as positive and the left as negative. Also, above O is taken as positive and below as negative. Of the coordinate axes, the horizontal axis ROR represents the domain of actuality, and the vertical axis R′OR′ represents the domain of non-actuality. Necessity N is located on the horizontal axis in the positive direction, and contingency C is located in the negative direction. Possibility P is located on the vertical axis in the positive direction, and impossibility I is located in the negative direction. The first quadrant (I) and the third quadrant (III) are the field of the relation of subalternation, the fourth quadrant (IV) is the field of the relation of contrary opposition 〔Tr.: 反對對當〕, and the second quadrant (II) is the field of the relation of subcontrary opposition 〔Tr.: 小反對對當〕. If we consider the curve ab, at point a possibility is small and contingency is [p. 317] large. At point b, in contrast to that, possibility is large and contingency is small. The same relation can also be illustrated, from a somewhat different standpoint, as on the left 〔Figure 22〕.

![Figure 22](front-and-back-matter/figures/fig-22-p317.png)

**Figure 22.** Being and nothingness in the four modalities: the triangle of apodicticity, problematicity and assertoricity (1948, p. 317). Labels: 有 being; 無 nothingness; 必然 necessary; 可能 possible; 偶然 contingent; 不可能 impossible; 現實 actual; 非現實 non-actual; 確證性 apodicticity; 問題性 problematicity; 言明性 assertoricity. Horizontal labels are set right to left.

〔Tr.: Figure on p. 317: an inverted triangle. Its top side, marked 有 (being), runs from 必然 (necessary, top left) to 可能 (possible, top right); its lower vertex is 無 (nothingness). The left side, from the necessary to nothingness, is marked (確證性) (apodicticity); the right side, from the possible to nothingness, is marked (問題性) (problematicity). A line marked 現實 (actual) runs from the necessary to 偶然 (contingent), which lies on the right side near the vertex; a line marked 非現實 (non-actual) runs from the possible to 不可能 (impossible), which lies on the left side near the vertex. The two lines cross. (言明性) (assertoricity) is written in the centre.〕 In this figure, it goes without saying that the more the contingent, on the straight line of "problematicity", approaches upwards towards the possible, that is, towards being, the more the contingency of the contingent decreases, and, in contrast to that, the more it approaches downwards towards nothingness, the more contingency becomes marked. Contingency is tied to necessity by "actuality", but "being", on the contrary, ties necessity and possibility together. Possibility is bound to impossibility by "non-actuality", but "nothingness", on the contrary, envelops and extinguishes 〔Tr.: 包消〕 impossibility and contingency.

Chance is non-conceptual. It is non-relational. It is lawless, orderless, heedless, without interest. Chance has no purpose. It has no design. It has no *yukari*. Chance cannot be relied on 〔Tr.: printed 當てになら無い, the negative written with 無〕. It is blind and has no eyes. Chance, as Shakespeare says, "has no bottom". As Hege-[p. 318] [コマ 166]l says, it "has no ground". In chance, nothingness deeply encroaches on being. To that extent chance is a fragile existence. Chance merely ties a sharp-pointed, frail existence to "this place" and to "this instant". All chance holds within itself, originally, the fate of collapse and ruin. "Meeting has parting; all things pass away and perish" (*Nirvana Sutra*, vol. 2). When the actual faces nothingness, and nothingness endangers the actual, we, together with Milinda 〔Tr.: 彌蘭〕, wonder as if for the first time and utter the question "why?".

## Conclusion (結論) [コマ 166-172]

### 1. The core meaning of contingency

[p. 319] In the above, we have attempted the elucidation of contingency on the three horizons, categorical, hypothetical and disjunctive. Categorical contingency arose when, in the categorical judgement, the predicate means a non-essential mark in relation to the subject as concept. That is, it is the case where it has become clear that a certain assertoric judgement, because it lacks the identity of subject and predicate, does not have apodicticity, and accordingly does not have necessity. Hypothetical contingency arose as what stands outside the relation of ground and consequent of the hypothetical judgement. That is, it arose as what lies outside the range of the apodicticity, and accordingly the necessity, determined by the identity of ground and consequent. Disjunctive contingency can be said to arise by regarding a given categorical judgement or hypothetical judgement as one member of division 〔Tr.: 區分肢〕 of a disjunctive judgement, and thinking that besides it there still exist several [p. 320] [コマ 167] members of division. That is, by regarding an assertoric or apodictic proposition as a member of division standing in a disjunctive relation, it emphasises difference in relation to the identity of the divided concept 〔Tr.: 被區分概念〕, and at the same time problematises assertoricity (actuality) and apodicticity (necessity) into problematicity.

Categorical contingency was the contingency that, in categorical structure, a non-essential mark, that is, a contingent mark, shows in relation to the identity of the concept. And the relation between the necessary marks and the contingent marks of a concept was seen, in the distinction between analytic judgement and synthetic judgement, in the identity that forms the basis of the analytic judgement and the contingency that lurks at the root of the synthetic judgement. Also in the distinction between universal judgement and particular judgement, we learned that necessity is expressed by the universal judgement and contingency is stated by the particular judgement. Next we considered that categorical contingency appears as isolated fact, and further as exception. When the essential marks of a concept have the value of law, the contingency of a contingent mark, which has no meaning other than that of an exception to the law, particularly emphasises contingency as exceptional contingency. Then in what does the core meaning of categorical contingency lie? As can be perceived also from the fact that exceptional contingency, among categorical contingency, has a particularly marked contingency, [p. 321] the core meaning of categorical contingency came down in the end to "the individual thing and the individual event" in relation to the general concept.

Next, hypothetical contingency was the contingency that what lies outside the sphere of such a structure shows in relation to the identical necessity that the hypothetical structure of groundedness has. In the purely logical range it appeared as contingency of ground, and in the empirical world it appeared, in the form of an application of contingency of ground, as teleological contingency and causal contingency. These three modes of hypothetical contingency further each divided into negative contingency and positive contingency. Negative contingency is the case in which, concerning one event, the non-existence of groundedness, causality or purposiveness is negatively witnessed; positive contingency is the case in which one not only sees, between two or more than two events, the non-existence of a hypothetical necessary relation of groundedness, causality or purposiveness, but further goes on to witness positively the existence of some other relation. Positive contingency shows, by its positivity, a more marked contingency than negative contingency. Not only that, at the root of negative contingency there also always exists some positive contingency. Positive contingency, insofar as it has the character of relative contingency, shows the structure "the Encounter of one series with another series". 〔Tr.: 邂逅 (*kaikō*) recurs throughout the Conclusion and is rendered "Encounter" each time. The capitals are frequent here. They cannot be dropped without losing the distinction Kuki draws at p. 148, where he chooses 邂逅 over 遭遇 (*sōgū*), "encounter", as the word of his definition of contingency. See the Translator's Note.〕 And this structure was indeed the [p. 322] [コマ 168] core meaning of hypothetical contingency. Further, teleological contingency and causal contingency could be called empirical contingency as belonging to the empirical world, but on the basis of being empirical they have a temporal moment. Empirical positive contingency is something that encounters (*kaikō*, 邂逅) within time. There are two of it, simultaneous contingency and successive contingency, but the latter is reduced to the former. And insofar as simultaneity is related to spatiality, the core meaning of the contingent in the empirical world must take the form of the historical irrationality of "the Encounter in this place, at this instant".

Finally, disjunctive contingency was the contingency that each possible disjunct shows, in disjunctive structure, in relation to the identity that the whole of the possible disjuncts has. Upon coming to know the relation between contingency and possibility, the need arose to elucidate the position that contingency occupies within the system that the modalities constitute, and especially the special relation that contingency has to possibility. The minimum of possibility is the maximum of contingency. That the possibility of being 〔Tr.: 有ること〕 is small means that the possibility of not being 〔Tr.: 無いこと〕 is large. The core meaning of disjunctive contingency was to approach, as "the possible of not being" 〔Tr.: 無いことの可能〕, "the necessary of not being" 〔Tr.: 無いことの必然〕. Contingency is the actual that bears the nothingness of impossibility [p. 323] as its character. As the merely actual it appears in the present instant as if in play. To witness nothingness in the background of the actuality of a disjunct that has appeared in the present "now", and to wonder, is chance. And the emotion of wonder notifies fate to *Existenz* 〔Tr.: 實存〕. Further, the whole of the possible disjuncts means, in the eminent sense, the metaphysical absolute, and the metaphysical absolute is, in its concreteness, illuminated 〔Tr.: 開明〕 as the "necessary-contingent entity". Also, insofar as what joins the absolute and the finite one 〔Tr.: 有限者〕 is fate, fate too bears the character of the "necessary-contingent entity" and shakes the core of *Existenz*. Grasping that the correlation of the necessary and the contingent is based on the correlation of being and nothingness must form the foundation of knowledge concerning contingency.

In short, the core meaning of categorical contingency was "the individual thing and the individual event". The core meaning of hypothetical contingency was "the Encounter of one series with another series". The core meaning of disjunctive contingency was "the possible of not being". Because it is the individual thing and the individual event, it was equipped with contingent marks in relation to the general concept. Because it is the Encounter of an independent series with a series, it was outside the necessary relation of ground and consequent. Because it is the possible of not being, it ran counter to the necessity [p. 324] [コマ 169] that the whole of possibilities has. And these three meanings of the contingent are by no means separated individually, but are fused, undivided, into one. The core meaning of "the individual thing and the individual event" lies in "the Encounter of one series with another series", and the core meaning of Encounter lies in its being possible also not to encounter (*kaikō*, 邂逅), that is, in "the possible of not being". And the originary meaning of contingency that primordially determines all these is the positing of the other over against necessity as the one. Necessity is nothing other than identity, that is, the modality of the one. Contingency first exists only where there is the duality of the one and the other. That Aristotle says that the contingent exists "not as itself but as another" (οὐχ ᾗ αὐτὸ ἀλλ' ᾗ ἕτερον) (Aristoteles, *Metaphysica*, Δ 30, 1025a), and that Hegel says that the contingent is in general such as has the Ground of its existence "not in itself but in another" (nicht in sich selbst, sondern in Anderem) (Hegel, *Encyklopädie*, hrsg. v. Bolland, 1906, § 145, Zusatz S. 193), is entirely for that reason. The origin of the individual thing goes back to the dual positing of the other over against the one. Encounter is nothing other than the Encounter of two independent elements. The possible of not being presupposes duality as something based on the choice of [p. 325] the one or the other. The philosophy of the Eleatic school, which determined the meaning of being by the law of identity and regarded what runs counter to the law of identity as nothingness, began from wonder at chance and ended in a tragic refusal of the dual positing of the other. And yet we cannot but acknowledge a truth in one aspect in the philosophy of the Eleatic school. There, too, human suffering and joy lie hidden.

### 2. The internalisation of contingency

We said that the problem of contingency, because it contains the question of nothingness, is strictly a problem of metaphysics. We also said that the sciences other than philosophy as metaphysics, because they exclude the question of nothingness, do not take contingency as a problem. Contingency, as the Encounter of two independent elements in "this place" and "this instant", stands at the peril of the sharp point and faces boundless nothingness. Contingency may be thought, by sciences that take universal thought as their model and pursue only the necessity or else the probability of law, an irrationality not worth a glance. Aristotle too spoke of the "contrary-to-[p. 326] [コマ 170]reason" (παράλογος) of chance (Aristoteles, *Physica*, II, 5, 197a). Contingency forms a limit to scientific knowledge.

However, it must be known that this limit has, for theoretical existentiality 〔Tr.: 理論的實存性〕, the significance of a beginning. Empirical knowledge must start from contingency, which is the limit of knowledge, and must always be conditioned by this limit. The originary meaning of the theoretical system that gives consistency and unity to experience lies in grasping the contingency of the other and, in its concreteness, assimilating and internalising it into the identity of the one. True judgement must, in the correlation of the contingent and the necessary, stand on the contingency of fact and take the internalisation of the contingent as its task. The law of identity, the basic principle of thought, is nothing other than the principle of internalisation. To say "A is A" is nothing other than to say "I am I". The essential meaning of judgement must be to deepen the encountering (*kaikō*, 邂逅) "thou" into the "I". To identify concretely the external thou with the inner identity of the I is the idea of judgement. But it must not aim at the empty identity in Eleatic abstract universality. Internalisation by the law of identity must be a concrete internalisation conditioned by the contingency of the thou that is encountered (*kaikō*, 邂逅) as fact [p. 327]. There lies the foundation of the departmental independence of the sciences and the hierarchical organisation of scientific systems. There also lies the guarantee of the concreteness and actuality of scientific labour within one department. Mere identification, mere necessitation, by negating every thou, every contingency, leads to acosmism. The ideal that theoretical knowledge should reach must not be mere necessity. It must be a "contingent-necessary entity" that has its fill of chance and is saturated with contingency.

On the whole, insofar as the temporal character of contingency is the present as immediacy 〔Tr.: 直態〕, it is not to be wondered at that, for logic as the methodological system of necessary thought, contingency appears as irrationality. However, to make logical determination start in accordance with the immediacy of the "thou", and to grasp the idea of scientific labour in the internalisation of the "thou", is the way to bring life to logic itself and to confer concrete value on science. To keep the domain of the identity of thought, "I = I", and the immediacy of the experience of the "thou" by the "I" in as close a relation as possible, and to bring the necessary and the contingent into contact in an inseparable correlation, must be an axiomatic demand for theoretical *Existenz* that advances towards a logic of life.

[p. 328] [コマ 171] Just as contingency has no meaning for a science misled by abstract universality, so too for a rational, formal ethics that takes science as its model there will be no place to admit chance. An ethical theory that seeks to make the moral law be like a universal law of nature that permits no exceptions will, by pursuing abstract identity, arrive at "a will that wills nothing" and fall into a practical acosmism. The content of morality must be something individualised by the contingency that the present offers. The practical universality that morality takes as its task must not be abstract universality. It must be a concrete universal that, with chance as its moment, delimits the whole intensively. If there were an ethical theory that sought to unify everything into formal identity, then even if there were someone who, rebelling against its abstract universality, said, "I will lie, as Desdemona lied when dying; I will kill, as Timoleon did; I will commit suicide, as Otho did; I will enter the temple and steal, as David did; because I am hungry, I will pluck ears of grain on the Sabbath" (Jacobi, *Werke* III, 1816, S. 37), that voice would be heard as the voice of conscience crying out in the inmost depths of the human being. The practical internalisation of contingency is nothing other than the self-awareness of the relationships 〔Tr.: 間柄〕 between part and part among the countless parts in the concrete whole. The one that exists in isolation [p. 329] must, at the instant (*setsuna*, 刹那) when here and there it unexpectedly encounters (*kaikō*, 邂逅) the other, tie the whole suffering and joy of *Existenz* to internalising the external thou into the depths of the I. Chance must be made to happen to encounter (*kaikō*, 邂逅), as if falling into the depths of the I. Heidegger too says, "What is called chance can befall 〔Tr.: 偶然する〕 only towards resoluteness, from the common environing world" (Heidegger, *Sein und Zeit*, S. 300). Jaspers too says, "Beyond any thought that can be grasped, I experience terror in the limit situation, and thus experience being one with the chance that I have grasped as my own" (Jaspers, *Philosophie* II, S. 217). That the *Shuogua zhuan* says, "They observed the changes in yin and yang and established the trigrams; they brought forth the firm and the yielding and produced the lines; they accorded with the Way and its virtue and set in order what is right; they exhausted principle and fulfilled nature, and so arrived at fate 〔Tr.: 命〕," is also nothing other than an exposition of the practical internalisation of contingency. The dual relativity that brings chance into being constitutes originary sociality by disclosing intersubjectivity everywhere. Just as the meaning of judgement in theory also lay in assimilating and internalising the thou in intersubjective sociality into the concrete identity of the I that exists as *Existenz* 〔Tr.: 實存する我〕, so the meaning of action in practice also lies there. In order for morality not to be merely fictitious but to hold good in the actual as a force, [p. 330] [コマ 172] it must be something that, with the given chance as a springboard, strides loftily towards inwardness. Wonder at chance need not be founded only on the present. We can found the wonder of contingency inversely 〔Tr.: 倒逆的に〕 by the future. Contingency is the point of tangency at which impossibility touches possibility. By grasping the minute possibility within contingency and nurturing a future possibility, we can unfold the curve of action and, turning back, understand inversely the productive meaning of present contingency. We can brew "purpose without purpose" into the production of the future and bring wonder to the "instant" of Encounter. 〔Tr.: 「目的なき目的」, "purpose without purpose", is Kuki's own phrase, used in Chapter 2 for positive teleological contingency. It recalls Kant's *Zweckmäßigkeit ohne Zweck*, "purposiveness without purpose", from the Analytic of the Beautiful in the *Critique of Judgement*, but Kuki writes 目的 twice and not Kant's term, 合目的性 (purposiveness). 醸す (*kamosu*) is to brew, as sake is brewed, and so to bring something about by slow working.〕 And to emphasise the wonder of all contingency by the future is to establish the correlation "contingent-necessary", and accordingly to make contingency truly contingency. This must be the task given to the finite *Existenz* 〔Tr.: 有限なる實存者〕, and at the same time also the salvation of the finite one that exists as *Existenz* 〔Tr.: 實存する有限者〕. That the *Treatise on the Pure Land* says "Contemplating the power of the Buddha's original vow, among those who meet it, none passes by in vain" is also, in the end, probably this. What "meets" 〔Tr.: 遇ふ〕 is the contingency of the thou that encounters (*kaikō*, 邂逅) me in the present. "None passes by in vain" has meaning only as the future possibility that the I, while conditioned by the thou, has with regard to the internalisation of the thou. Where minute possibility close to the impossible [p. 331] becomes actual in contingency, gives birth, by being firmly grasped as contingency, to new possibility, and further possibility develops into necessity, there is both the original vow of the Buddha as fate and the salvation of the human being. To give the meaning of eternal fate to contingency, which holds nothingness within it and has the fate of perishing, there is no way but to make the instant live by the future. There is no way but to make the meaning of present contingency surge up by future possibility. To that "why?" of Milinda, within the sphere of theory we can only answer that contingency is the indispensable condition of concrete existence; but in the domain of practice we will be able to fill the gap of theory by giving ourselves the command "Having met, do not pass by in vain." 〔Tr.: Maraldo renders it "Let not your encounters occur in vain" (2008) and "Let not your encounters pass in vain" (*Japanese Philosophy in the Making 2*, 2019, pp. 270-71).〕

---

*End of the text (p. 331). The colophon (コマ 173) gives the first printing as 15 December 1935 and this fourth printing as 20 April 1948, Iwanami Shoten.*

---

## Appendix A. Notes on Translation Choices

These notes record the decisions taken in this translation, with the alternatives considered, so that other translators of Kuki can see where and why they might choose differently. Page references are to the 1948 printing.

### 1. The text and the two versions

The translation was made from the fourth printing of 偶然性の問題 (Iwanami Shoten, 20 April 1948; first printing 15 December 1935), using the National Diet Library's digitised images (pid/1037966). The 1935 and 1948 printings have not been compared, and the text has not yet been checked against volume 2 of the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū* (KSZ), which is the edition scholars normally cite. A concordance of 46 fixed points between the 1948 pages and KSZ vol. 2, drawn from Graham Mayeda, Takeshi Morisato and John C. Maraldo, is kept with the working files; the whole book corresponds to KSZ 2:9-260.

There are two versions. The literal version follows Kuki's sentences one by one, is the version to quote from, and is published alongside the readable text. The readable version joins his short declarative sentences into continuous argument, using reformulations such as "that is" and "this is to say" where Kuki restates, and it is meant to be read as philosophy in English. The readable version is not supposed to say anything the literal version does not.

### 2. The three modes

偶然性 is "contingency". 偶然 standing alone as a noun is "the contingent", so that "contingency" is left for 偶然性; the kinds of contingency (定言的偶然, 因果的偶然 and the rest) keep "contingency", as do the headings built on them. In the sections on history, art, fate and gambling, and where Kuki's sources have *hasard* or *Zufall*, 偶然 is "chance". In the same way 現實性 is "actuality" and 現實 "the actual". 必然性, 可能性 and 不可能性 are "necessity", "possibility" and "impossibility"; 必然, 可能 and 不可能 standing alone as nouns are "the necessary", "the possible" and "the impossible", and 存在の可能 and 無いことの可能 "the possible of existence" and "the possible of not being". X的必然 keeps "X necessity". Where Kuki quotes another author, or where 可能 means a capacity, the English is left as it was. Labels on the figures are given without the article ("contingent", "actual"). The three modes are 定言的 "categorical", 假說的 "hypothetical" and 離接的 "disjunctive". Kuki deliberately sets aside the standard Japanese logical terms 假言的 and 選言的 for the second and third (p. 8): the characters of 假說的 stress supposition, and those of 離接的 join separation (離) and connection (接). English has one word for each pair, so the change cannot be shown in the text and is explained in a footnote. 樣態 is "mode" and 樣相 or 樣相性 "modality".

### 3. Existence, being and nothingness

Kuki uses two pairs of terms that English translations often merge: 存在 and 非存在, and 有 and 無. They are kept apart here: 存在 is "existence", 非存在 "non-existence", 有 "being" and 無 "nothingness". This follows Takeshi Morisato's translation of the Introduction and agrees with Maraldo's usage. The first draft of this translation rendered both 存在 and 有 as "being", which hid the distinction on the opening page, where contingency is an existence "interposed at the surface of contact between being and nothingness" (p. 1).

The decision has consequences elsewhere:
- 眞の存在 (Kuki's gloss on Plato's ὄντως ὄν) is "true existence", and 極限的存在 is "limit-existence".
- 存在論 and 存在論的 remain "ontology" and "ontological".
- 實存, in the passages on Heidegger and Jaspers, is *Existenz*, with 實存性 "existentiality", so that it does not collide with 存在. 現存在 is "Dasein".
- 虛無性 is "nihility", to keep it apart from 無; 實在性 is "reality", and 實在理由 (*ratio essendi*) is "ground of reality".
- 個體 is "the individual", never "the individual being".
- 有る and 無い as ordinary verbs remain "to be" and "not to be".
- Chapter 3 §14, 有と無, is "Being and Nothingness".

### 4. Encounter

Kuki's central definition of contingency is 獨立なる二元の邂逅, rendered "the Encounter of two independent elements" (pp. 146-148). 二元 is "elements" because it is the plainest rendering of 元; Mayeda, working from Omodaka's French, has "two independent principles", which another translator may prefer. Kuki uses both 邂逅 and 遭遇 for the encounter of independent series of events (Cournot's *séries indépendantes*), and at pp. 146 and 148 writes 遭遇または邂逅 before choosing 邂逅 for the definition. 邂逅 is "Encounter" and 遭遇 "encounter", with the Japanese given where he sets them side by side and where 邂逅 is a verb (see the Translator's Note). 遇ふ, in the Conclusion's Buddhist passages, is "meet", kept apart from 邂逅. The closing imperative 遇うて空しく過ぐる勿れ is rendered "Having met, do not pass by in vain", which keeps as its subject the one who has met. Maraldo's "Let not your encounters occur in vain" (2008) and "Let not your encounters pass in vain" (*Japanese Philosophy in the Making 2*) are smoother but use "encounter" for 遇ふ.

Kuki also proposes a sign for contingency, )(, two arcs touching at their midpoints, which he reads as a picture of this Encounter (p. 73). It is reproduced as )( wherever he uses it.

### 5. Ground, cause and purpose

理由 is "ground", following the German *Grund*, and 根據, which Kuki also uses for *Grund*, is "Ground" (see the Translator's Note). 理由律 is "law of ground" and 充足理由律 "law of sufficient reason", to match "law of identity" (同一律) and "law of contradiction" (矛盾律); the translators first divided between "law" and "principle", and "law" was chosen throughout. 理由的偶然 is "contingency of ground", and 認識理由 and 實在理由 are "ground of knowledge" and "ground of reality". 基礎 is "basis" or "foundation", and the verbs 基礎づける and 基く are "to found" and "to rest on" or "to be based on", so that the verb "to ground" is not used for them.

In Chapter 2, 因果的 is "causal" and 目的的 "teleological", with 因果性 "causality" and 目的性 "purposiveness". 目的 is "purpose", or "end" where the sense is telos. 無目的 is "purposelessness" and 反目的 "counter-purpose". 目的なき目的, Kuki's version of a Kantian formula, is "purpose without purpose". 故意 is "intention" and 意圖 "design", kept apart because Kuki distinguishes them in discussing Aristotle's *automaton* and *tychē*. 消極的 and 積極的 are "negative" and "positive".

### 6. Logic and judgement

徵表 is "mark" (*Merkmal*). 契機 is "moment", a constitutive moment in Hegel's sense; 瞬間 is "instant", and 刹那 "instant" with the Japanese given. 規定 is "determination" and 限定 "delimitation". 決定 is "decision" or "decide" where a choice is meant and "determine" in the causal sense, with 決定論 "determinism" and 非決定性 "indeterminacy", so that "determine" still stands for both 規定 and causal 決定. In Kuki's quotations from Kant and Jaspers, and in the title of Nishida's book, 限定 keeps the settled English "determination" or "determinacy". 樣相性, modality as a category, is "Modality"; 樣相, a modality or the modalities, is "modality"; 樣態 is "mode". In Lotze's distinction, 全稱判斷 is the "universal judgement" (*universale Urteil*) and 普遍的判斷 the "generic judgement" (*generelle Urteil*); the first draft confused them (p. 7). 特稱判斷 is "particular judgement". The square of opposition follows standard English logic: 矛盾 "contradictory", 反對 "contrary", 小反對 "subcontrary" and 大小對當 "subalternation". 確證性, 言明性 and 問題性 are "apodicticity", "assertoricity" and "problematicity". Kuki's 甲, 乙 and 丙 are rendered A, B and C. His symbolic notations (Couturat's algebra in Chapter 1, C. I. Lewis's signs in Chapter 2, Oskar Becker's dot-and-stroke notation in Chapter 3) are transcribed as printed.

### 7. Probability

Kuki uses both 確率 and 蓋然性. Both are rendered "probability", since English has no settled pair that maps onto them, and the Japanese is given where he sets them side by side (p. 246). A translator who wants to keep them apart could use "probability value" for 確率.

### 8. Wonder and the emotions

驚異 is "wonder", the emotion Kuki assigns to contingency. 情緒 is "emotion" and 感情 "feeling", kept apart throughout Chapter 3 §10. 感情當價 is "affective equivalent". 不思議 is "the marvellous", so that it does not compete with "wonder".

### 9. Japanese words for chance, and poetry

Kuki builds arguments on Japanese words: *yukurinaku*, *hashinakumo* and *futo* (Chapter 2 §5), and *wakuraba ni*, *tamatama*, *tama*, *mama* and *ma* (Chapter 1 §5). These are kept in romanisation, with the sense given in the sentence where Kuki gives it and the rest in footnotes. In the readable version, the poems Kuki quotes and his classical Chinese passages (the *Liezi*, the *Nāgasena Sūtra*, the *Book of Changes*, Buddhist texts) appear in the body in English, with romanisation where the argument depends on sound (rhyme, acrostic, pivot word, palindrome); a footnote then gives Kuki's text exactly as printed, Japanese or Chinese, with our close translation. Chinese is translated as Kuki quotes it, and his kunten reading marks are not reproduced. The literal version gives the originals in the body.

### 10. Quotation, citation and emphasis

All quotations from other authors, European, Chinese and Japanese, are translated from Kuki's Japanese, not from the original texts, because his renderings may shape his argument. They should not be cited as translations of those authors. Kuki's references stay inline, as in the original. The literal version keeps them exactly as printed ("Aristoteles, Metaphysica", "Ib.", "3 Aufl."); the readable version gives authors' names and titles in their usual English form and italicises titles, keeping Kuki's editions and page numbers, and has not yet been made fully consistent in this. His emphasis marks (圏点) are rendered as italics. Japanese era dates are converted to Western years. His long dashes are rendered with commas, colons or parentheses.

Kuki's examples of race, skin colour and disability use the terms of the 1930s. They are translated plainly, without softening, and the dated Japanese terms are identified in footnotes.

### 11. Marking the translator's contributions

Every footnote in the readable version is the translator's and begins "Tr.", since Kuki gives his own references in the body of the text. Anything inserted into Kuki's text, whether a supplied word, a gloss or a note, is set in tortoise-shell brackets, 〔 〕, following the Japanese editorial convention for insertions by an editor or by the person quoting. Kuki's own sign )( was considered for this purpose and rejected, because he uses it in his argument.

### 12. Misprints, figures and open questions

Apparent misprints and doubtful references in the 1948 printing have been kept as printed and noted, among them Δ 7 for Λ 7 of the *Metaphysics* (p. 70), odds of 60,480 where Kuki's own example implies one in a million (p. 165), 大極 for 太極 (p. 305), and a cross-reference on p. 266 that does not match this pagination. Shestov's *La philosophie de la tragédie* is cited as Paris 1926 in this printing; Morisato has 1929. Kuki's 22 figures are reproduced from the National Diet Library's full-resolution scan of the 1948 printing, with contrast adjusted so that the print reads clearly and no marks removed; each has a caption and an English key to its Japanese labels, and Kuki's own references to a figure are followed by 〔Figure N〕.

Attributions and dates in the footnotes that the translators could not confirm have been left out or flagged in the working notes, and need checking before citation.

### 13. Alternatives attested in Omodaka's French

Omodaka Hisayuki's French translation has not yet been consulted. Graham Mayeda quotes it in his own English in "Is There a Method to Chance?" (2008), mainly from the Introduction and the last pages of the book. A comparison of about twenty of his quotations with this translation found agreement in substance throughout, and in three places the Japanese supports our reading against his (pp. 324, 324-325 and 330-331). Where his renderings differ they are recorded here as alternatives, marked "via Mayeda (2008), from Omodaka (1966)", since a difference may come from either translator:
- 一者: "the same", where we have "the one". Kuki defines necessity through 同一性, identity, the modality of 一者, and "the one" keeps the link to 一 that "the same" loses.
- 個物および個々の事象: "individuality and individual phenomenality", where we have "the individual thing and the individual event", which keeps Kuki's concrete nouns.
- 無いことの可能: "possible nothingness", where we have "the possible of not being", since Kuki uses the verbal 無いこと and not the noun 無.
- 形而上的理由: "metaphysical sense", where we have "metaphysical grounds", since 理由 is ground or reason.
- 實存: "existence", where we have *Existenz* (see section 3).
- 內面化: "interiorization", where we have "internalisation".
- 極微: "infinitesimal", where we have "minute".
- 二元: "principles", where we have "elements" (see section 4).

Mayeda's quotations are his English and should not be cited as evidence of Omodaka's wording.

---

## Appendix C. Concordance with the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū*

This concordance ties the pages of the 1948 printing, used in this translation, to volume 2 of the *Kuki Shūzō zenshū* (KSZ), the edition normally cited. It is built from 46 fixed points taken from Graham Mayeda, Takeshi Morisato and John C. Maraldo. It has not been checked against a physical copy of KSZ, and only rows marked HIGH or MEDIUM should be used for citation, after checking.

### Editions and sources

- **1948 printing**: 九鬼周造『偶然性の問題』, Iwanami Shoten, 1948 printing (331 pp.). Its pages are the `[p. N]` markers in our translation (`Kuki_Problem-of-Contingency_literal.md`, `Kuki_Problem-of-Contingency_translation.md`, and `ch3-parts/partH-` to `partN-literal.md`). Introduction pp. 1-11; Chapter 1 pp. 13-46; Chapter 2 pp. 47-184; Chapter 3 pp. 185-318; Conclusion pp. 319-331.
- **KSZ**: 『九鬼周造全集』 (*Kuki Shūzō zenshū*), vol. 2 (Tokyo: Iwanami Shoten, 1980; Mayeda gives 1981), as cited by Mayeda. The text runs KSZ 2:9-260.
- **French**: Kuki Shûzô, *Le problème de la contingence*, trans. Omodaka Hisayuki (University of Tokyo Press, 1966), as cited by Mayeda.

Anchors come from:
1. Graham Mayeda, *Ethics and Difference* (PhD thesis, University of Toronto, 2001), Chapter 5 endnotes, which cite "Kuki, *Le problème de la contingence*, [French page]; KSZ, vol. 2, [KSZ page]". Each note was matched to Mayeda's body passage, then to the sentence in our literal translation. Many of Mayeda's notes reproduce the Japanese passage (checked against the thesis PDF, pp. 291-295), which fixes the match; these are marked "Japanese quoted in note".
2. KSZ page breaks marked in Takeshi Morisato's translation (KSZ 2:10, 2:11, 2:12).
3. John C. Maraldo, *Frontiers of Japanese Philosophy* 2 (2008), for KSZ 2:258, 2:259, 2:260 in the Conclusion.

Where a passage crosses a 1948 page break, the 1948 column gives the range. The KSZ page is as Mayeda gives it; this file has not been checked against a physical copy of KSZ. Use only HIGH and MEDIUM rows for citation, and only after checking the page in KSZ. LOW rows are recorded for completeness.

Confidence: HIGH = a distinctive sentence clearly the same (most are confirmed by the Japanese Mayeda quotes); MEDIUM = Mayeda paraphrases, and the page is likely; LOW = doubtful.

Mayeda notes with no KSZ anchor: nn. 38-39, 63, 68, 70, 75-76, 84 (other authors or commentary); nn. 41-43 (glosses on 手間, 間 and the *ma* expressions, 1948 pp. 36-37, with no page citation); n. 65 gives only the French page (row included, no KSZ value).

| 298 | 234 | 175 | Mayeda n. 79 (Japanese quoted in note) | "Fate is an existent that is futural and at the same time originally having-been taking over its own thrownness while giving possibility to itself in finitude." | HIGH |
| 317-318 | 249 | 187 | Mayeda n. 80 (Japanese quoted in note) | "Chance is non-conceptual. It is non-relational. It is lawless, orderless, heedless, without interest..." (to Milinda's "why?", p. 318) | HIGH |
| 323 | 254 | 191 | Mayeda n. 81 | "In short, the core meaning of categorical contingency was 'the individual thing and the individual event'. The core meaning of hypothetical contingency was 'the encounter of one series with another series'..." | HIGH |
| 324 | 254-255 | 192 | Mayeda n. 82 (Japanese quoted in note) | "The core meaning of 'the individual thing and the individual event' lies in 'the encounter of one series with another series'..." | HIGH |
| 328 | 257 ff. | 194 | Mayeda n. 83 | "Just as contingency has no meaning for a science misled by abstract universality, so too for a rational, formal ethics that takes science as its model there will be no place to admit chance." | HIGH |
| 328-329 | 258 | | Maraldo (FJP 2, 2008) | "The one that exists in isolation must, at the instant when here and there it unexpectedly encounters the other, tie the whole suffering and joy of *Existenz* to internalising the external thou into the depths of the I." | HIGH |
| 330 | 259 | | Maraldo (FJP 2, 2008) | "That the *Treatise on the Pure Land* says 'Contemplating the power of the Buddha's original vow, among those who meet it, none passes by in vain' (觀佛本願力、遇無空過者)..." | HIGH |
| 330-331 | 259-260 | 194 | Mayeda n. 85 (Japanese quoted in note) | Same passage through "...there is both the original vow of the Buddha as fate and the salvation of the human being." (p. 331) | HIGH |
| 331 | 260 | | Maraldo (FJP 2, 2008) | "...by giving ourselves the command 'Having met, do not pass by in vain'" (遇うて空しく過ぐる勿れ), last sentence of the book | HIGH |

### Monotonicity check

KSZ pages increase with 1948 pages across every row except one pair:

| 1948 page | KSZ page (Mayeda) | French page | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| 51 | 51 | 31 | n. 45 |
| 53 | 50 | 32 | n. 46 |

The French pages (31, 32) run in order with the 1948 pages, so the passages are in the right sequence and one of Mayeda's KSZ numbers is wrong. The suspect value is **n. 45 (KSZ 51)**. Interpolating between the HIGH anchors at 1948 pp. 41-42 (KSZ 40) and pp. 84-85 (KSZ 73-74) puts 1948 p. 51 at about KSZ 47-48 and p. 53 at about KSZ 49, so n. 46 (KSZ 50) is within a page of expectation and n. 45 is about three pages too high. It may be a slip that repeats the 1948 page number (the passage opens 1948 p. 51). The n. 45 row is rated LOW for this reason; the passage match itself is secure.

No other row breaks the sequence. Rows that share a KSZ page (e.g. 1948 pp. 267 and 268 at KSZ 211; pp. 297 and 298 at KSZ 234; pp. 142 and 143 at KSZ 116) fit the rate of about 0.76 KSZ pages per 1948 page.


### Locating guide (for finding a page in KSZ, not for citation)

Derived from HIGH anchors only. Across the whole book the HIGH anchors fit **KSZ ≈ 0.76 × (1948 page) + 8**, within 2 pages at every HIGH anchor (the largest deviations, about 1.5 pages, fall in 1948 pp. 50-90, where KSZ runs slightly ahead of the formula). Use this to open KSZ near the right page, then find the passage.

| Part of the 1948 printing | 1948 pages | Implied KSZ vol. 2 range | HIGH anchors in range |
|---|---|---|---|
| Introduction | 1-11 | 2:9 to ~2:17 | pp. 1-4 = KSZ 9-12 (exact breaks) |
| Chapter 1. Categorical contingency | 13-46 | ~2:19 to ~2:44 | p. 30 = 32; pp. 41-42 = 40 |
| Chapter 2. Hypothetical contingency | 47-184 | ~2:45 to ~2:148 | p. 53 = 50; pp. 84-85 = 73-74; p. 89 = 77; p. 94 = 80; p. 134 = 110; pp. 142-143 = 116-117; p. 148 = 120; p. 164 = 132; pp. 168-169 = 136 |
| Chapter 3. Disjunctive contingency | 185-318 | 2:149 to ~2:250 | pp. 185-186 = 149; p. 206 = 165; pp. 212-213 = 170; pp. 220-221 = 176; pp. 236-237 = 187-188; p. 263 = 208; p. 267 = 211; p. 274 = 216; pp. 289-290 = 227-228; pp. 297-298 = 234; pp. 317-318 = 249 |
| Conclusion | 319-331 | ~2:251 to 2:260 | p. 323 = 254; p. 324 = 254-255; p. 328 = 257; pp. 328-329 = 258; p. 330 = 259; p. 331 = 260 |

The chapter-boundary values marked ~ are interpolated; KSZ may begin chapters on a new page, which would shift a boundary by a page.

## Appendix D. Index of Persons and Works Cited

This index lists the persons and works that Kuki names in *The Problem of Contingency*, with page numbers of the 1948 printing (Iwanami Shoten, fourth printing, 20 April 1948). It covers Kuki's text only; the translator's notes are not indexed. Persons named inside Kuki's quotations are included when they are historical; fictional, legendary and mythological figures are included only where Kuki discusses them, and are marked as such. Editors and translators named in Kuki's references are included. Works are given in the form Kuki cites them, editions included, and have not been checked against the editions; where a reference looks wrong it is kept as printed and marked "[as printed]". Dates and identifications are given only where certain; Shōwa-era dates are followed by the Western year in square brackets.

## Glossary

| Japanese | Reading | English |
|---|---|---|
| 偶然性 | gūzensei | contingency |
| 偶然 | gūzen | the contingent; chance (where Kuki is discussing *hasard*, *Zufall*) |
| 必然性 / 必然 | hitsuzensei / hitsuzen | necessity / the necessary |
| 可能性 / 可能 | kanōsei / kanō | possibility / the possible |
| 不可能性 / 不可能 | fukanōsei / fukanō | impossibility / the impossible |
| 必ず然か有る | kanarazu shika aru | being necessarily so |
| 偶々然か有る | tamatama shika aru | happening to be so |
| 定言的 | teigenteki | categorical |
| 假說的 | kasetsuteki | hypothetical |
| 離接的 | risetsuteki | disjunctive |
| 現實性 / 現實 | genjitsusei / genjitsu | actuality / the actual |
| 樣態 | yōtai | mode |
| 樣相 / 樣相性 | yōsō / yōsōsei | modality / Modality |
| 存在 / 非存在 | sonzai / hisonzai | existence / non-existence (存在論 ontology) |
| 有 / 無 | u / mu | being / nothingness |
| 極限的存在 | kyokugenteki sonzai | limit-existence |
| 接觸面 | sesshokumen | surface of contact |
| 根據 | konkyo | Ground |
| 基礎 | kiso | basis, foundation (基礎づける to found; 基く to rest on) |
| 理由 / 歸結 | riyū / kiketsu | ground / consequent |
| 概念 / 徵表 | gainen / chōhyō | concept / mark (*Merkmal*) |
| 契機 | keiki | moment |
| 瞬間 / 刹那 | shunkan / setsuna | instant |
| 規定 / 限定 | kitei / gentei | determination / delimitation |
| 決定 | kettei | decision; determination (in the causal sense; 決定論 determinism) |
| 全體 / 部分 | zentai / bubun | whole / part |
| 離接肢 | risetsushi | disjunct |
| 全稱判斷 | zenshō handan | universal judgement (Lotze's *universale Urteil*) |
| 普遍的判斷 | fuhenteki handan | generic judgement (Lotze's *generelle Urteil*) |
| 特稱判斷 | tokushō handan | particular judgement |
| 綜合的 / 分析的判斷 | sōgōteki / bunsekiteki handan | synthetic / analytic judgement |
| 虛無性 / 實在性 | kyomusei / jitsuzaisei | nihility / reality |
| 認識理由 / 實在理由 | ninshiki riyū / jitsuzai riyū | ground of knowledge / ground of reality (*ratio cognoscendi* / *ratio essendi*) |
| 實體 / 屬性 | jittai / zokusei | substance / attribute (accident, in Hegel and Aristotle) |
| 孤立的事實 | koritsuteki jijitsu | isolated fact |
| 個物 / 個體 | kobutsu / kotai | individual thing / individual |
| 理由律 / 充足理由律 | riyūritsu / jūsoku riyūritsu | law of ground / law of sufficient reason |
| 理由的偶然 | riyūteki gūzen | contingency of ground |
| 消極的 / 積極的 | shōkyokuteki / sekkyokuteki | negative / positive |
| 因果的 / 目的的偶然 | ingateki / mokutekiteki gūzen | causal / teleological contingency |
| 因果性 / 目的性 | ingasei / mokutekisei | causality / purposiveness |
| 無目的 / 反目的 | mumokuteki / hanmokuteki | purposelessness / counter-purpose |
| 目的なき目的 | mokuteki naki mokuteki | purpose without purpose |
| 故意 / 意圖 | koi / ito | intention / design |
| 邂逅 | kaikō | Encounter (Japanese given where Kuki pairs it with 遭遇, and where it is a verb) |
| 獨立なる二元の邂逅 | dokuritsu naru nigen no kaikō | the Encounter of two independent elements |
| 系列 | keiretsu | series |
| 交叉點 | kōsaten | point of intersection |
| 複合的偶然 | fukugōteki gūzen | compound contingency |
| 絶對的 / 相對的偶然 | zettaiteki / sōtaiteki gūzen | absolute / relative contingency |
| 同時的 / 繼起的偶然 | dōjiteki / keikiteki gūzen | simultaneous / successive contingency |
| 原始偶然 | genshi gūzen | primal contingency (*Urzufall*) |
| 一回性 | ikkaisei | once-only character |
| 確證性 / 言明性 / 問題性 | kakushōsei / genmeisei / mondaisei | apodicticity / assertoricity / problematicity |
| 驚異 | kyōi | wonder |
| 周易 | Shūeki | the *Book of Changes* (*Zhouyi*) |
| 實存 / 實存性 | jitsuzon / jitsuzonsei | *Existenz* / existentiality (kept apart from 存在) |
| 現存在 | genzonzai | Dasein (Heidegger, Jaspers) |
| 矛盾 / 反對 / 小反對 / 大小對當 | mujun / hantai / shōhantai / daishō taitō | contradictory / contrary / subcontrary opposition / subalternation |
| 確率 / 蓋然性 | kakuritsu / gaizensei | probability (both; Japanese given where Kuki sets them side by side) |
| 遊戲 | yūgi | play |
| 感情當價 | kanjō tōka | affective equivalent |
| 情緒 / 感情 | jōcho / kanjō | emotion / feeling |
| 不思議 | fushigi | the marvellous |
| 運命 / 宿命 | unmei / shukumei | fate / destiny |
| 形而上的絕對者 | keijijōteki zettaisha | the metaphysical absolute |
| 限界狀況 | genkai jōkyō | limit situation (Jaspers) |
| 遇ふ | au | meet (kept apart from 邂逅, Encounter) |
| 內面化 | naimenka | internalisation |
| 遭遇 | sōgū | encounter |
| 目撃する | mokugeki suru | to witness |
| 闡明 | senmei | elucidation |
| 形而上學 | keijijōgaku | metaphysics |
| 勝義における哲學 | shōgi ni okeru tetsugaku | philosophy in the eminent sense |
| 優勢的命名法 | yūseiteki meimeihō | naming from the predominant feature (*denominatio a potiori*) |
